## 导读

> 本导读整理自 Socratopia 网站本书介绍页：https://www.socratopia.app/library/close-listening-mozart-en

Mozart usually arrives in pieces — Eine kleine Nachtmusik at a dinner party, the Queen of the Night's coloratura blast on a streaming algorithm, a slow movement that plays during a film scene you barely register. This book is the friend who walks you through the whole composer instead. Twenty-three chapters cover Mozart's thirty-five years and roughly fifty anchor works, from the seventeen-year-old's first dark-key symphony to the eight bars of the Lacrimosa he finished before dying. You leave with a working ear for the catalog, not a list of greatest hits.

The volume is documented-conservative where popular Mozart isn't. When Amadeus diverges from the historical record, Chapter 20 names the divergence in three columns — scholarly record, film invention, genuine gray zone — and offers no verdict. The Requiem chapter says exactly what's Mozart, what's Süssmayr, and where Robert Levin's 1991 completion does it differently. The Mozart-Haydn friendship is anchored in the February 1785 letter where Haydn told Leopold his son was "the greatest composer known to me either in person or by reputation." The book trusts you with the ambiguities.

What it actually teaches: how to follow Mozart's wind writing (the clarinets and oboes in K. 491; the basset horns of "O Isis und Osiris"); how to track the dark-key signature through K. 183, K. 466, K. 491, K. 550, K. 516, and the Requiem; how to hear the seam where Mozart's autograph stops and Süssmayr's hand begins; how to listen to an opera ensemble finale — Figaro Act II runs twenty-two minutes without a break — and find specific moments (Susanna emerging from the closet, Antonio's drunken entry, the seven-voice climax) without a libretto in your lap. By Chapter 19 the seven recurring compositional features have collected into a coherent framework you can carry into any future Mozart listening.

It's structured as a friendship rather than a lecture. Each chapter ends with Conversation Seeds, not exercises — questions for talking with a learning companion who will read along with you. The book trains your ear; the conversation makes it stick.

**Target audience**：Adult amateur classical-music lovers who have completed the Close Listening primer and the Beethoven volume, and want a 35-year deep dive into Mozart through ~50 specific anchor works.

**Prerequisites**：None formal. Familiarity with the four-section listening loop introduced in the primer volume (Before You Listen / The Listening Window / What's Happening in the Music / The Stories) helps but is re-introduced in Ch 1-2.

**Study hours**：60-80 hours of listening + reading (one chapter per week sustained; one chapter per evening if pressed).

## Chapter 1: Why Mozart and Why Now

You picked up this book because Mozart has been arriving at the edges of your listening life for a while now. Maybe you started with the _Close Listening_ primer volume and met him three times — through _Eine kleine Nachtmusik_ as a sonata-form introduction, through the Piano Concerto K. 488 and the Clarinet Concerto K. 622 as your first experience of a slow movement that opens out like a window in a stone wall, and through the Queen of the Night's coloratura aria and the Requiem's Lacrimosa as your first sustained encounter with vocal music that makes you understand why people cry at opera. Maybe you finished the Beethoven volume and noticed Beethoven repeatedly bowing to Mozart — writing a cadenza for the K. 466 D-minor concerto, naming Mozart in conversation as the touchstone, taking the C-minor Piano Concerto K. 491 as a structural model for his own. Maybe you finally went to a Mozart opera in person and walked out unable to talk for twenty minutes, and you would like a friend who can help you understand what you just heard.

This is that friend. Across twenty-three chapters we are going to walk through Mozart's thirty-five years — Salzburg child prodigy, traveling-with-Leopold adolescent, Salzburg court musician chafing under an archbishop, Vienna freelance composer, husband, father, opera collaborator, late-style miracle, dying-while-composing-the-Requiem Mozart. Each stop comes with the music written at that moment and a careful look at what to listen for and how to set it up.

### 1.1 Where this volume sits in the series

The previous two _Close Listening_ volumes set up the listening posture this book inherits.

Volume One (the comprehensive primer on classical music) introduced the _four-section listening loop_ that every chapter here will repeat: **Before You Listen** sets up the work with a hook, a short listening agenda, and one or two performers worth searching for. **The Listening Window** is the second listen — the one where you know what is coming and can attend to specific moments at specific timestamps. **What's Happening in the Music** is the structural map, the form, the orchestration choices, the things a friend with score-knowledge would point out. **The Stories** is the longest section — the biographical context, the anecdotes that survive in the scholarly record, the recording history, the cross-references to other works in this book and to other volumes in the series.

Volume Two (single-composer deep dive on Ludwig van Beethoven) added a second pattern this book uses extensively: signature-spotting as a core listening skill. Across the Beethoven volume the reader learned to spot a small repertoire of Beethoven compositional fingerprints — the C-minor key as fate-key, the late-style fugues, the _attacca_ movement transitions, the heroic-period scherzo trio. The Beethoven volume converged at Chapter 19 with a synthesis that collected the signatures and showed where each anchor work sat in the catalog.

This volume does the same for Mozart, with seven recurring features instead of Beethoven's roughly five. We will collect them at Chapter 19. Each will be introduced at its first appearance with an explicit pointer forward to that synthesis, so you know from the moment of first encounter that the convergence chapter is coming.

The deeper reason for this series-wide pattern: when you carry a few stable fingerprints across many works by one composer, you stop hearing each work as a separate event and start hearing the _catalog_ — the composer's mind moving across thirty-five years, the same hand making different choices for different occasions. That is the listening posture the _Close Listening_ series is built to grow in you.

### 1.2 Why Mozart and why now

Mozart is the safest possible single-composer subject in Western music in one specific sense: the scholarly record is dense, mature, and broadly stable. Maynard Solomon's _Mozart: A Life_ (1995) is the contemporary single-volume biographical standard. Stanley Sadie's _Mozart: The Early Years, 1756-1781_ (Norton, posthumous 2006) gives the Köchel-by-Köchel scholarly framework. Cliff Eisen and Simon Keefe's _Cambridge Mozart Encyclopedia_ (2006) is the academic reference for almost any specific question. Robert W. Gutman's _Mozart: A Cultural Biography_ (1999) puts the life in cultural context. Charles Rosen's _The Classical Style: Haydn, Mozart, Beethoven_ (1971) is the analytical voice nobody has replaced. Daniel Heartz's _Mozart, Haydn and Early Beethoven, 1781-1802_ (2009) gives the Vienna-period context. Robert Levin has spent four decades on the Requiem completion problem and Mozart performance scholarship. Christoph Wolff's _Mozart at the Gateway to His Fortune_ (2012) is the late-Mozart specialist's account. And Mozart's own letters, in Robert Spaethling's edition _Mozart's Letters, Mozart's Life_ (2000), give the primary-source voice — funny, profane, demanding, devoted to his father, capable of breathtaking professional clarity.

All of these scholars largely agree on the framework. They disagree on emphasis (Solomon emphasizes the Leopold relationship more than Eisen does; Heartz puts more weight on the Vienna chamber scene than Solomon does), but they share the same chronological spine, the same canonical work-list, the same set of attested anecdotes, and the same posture of caution toward the partly-legendary early-19th-century sources.

That is the foundation this book stands on. Where the scholarly record is settled, we use it. Where it is contested — the cause of Mozart's death, the exact extent of Franz Xaver Süssmayr's contribution to the Requiem, the nature of the Mozart-Salieri relationship — we describe the contestation rather than pick a side. Where popular culture diverges from the scholarly record (the _Amadeus_ play and film being the largest case), we name the divergence.

The other reason for this book now: a reader who has worked through the comprehensive Vol. 1 and the Beethoven Vol. 2 is ready for the deep single-composer experience. Vol. 1 gives you breadth — Bach to Bartók in twelve chapters. Vol. 2 (Beethoven) gives you depth on one figure who is structurally so different from Mozart that the comparison sharpens both. Mozart's catalog is roughly twice Beethoven's in numbered works (about six hundred Köchel numbers versus Beethoven's hundred-and-thirty-eight opus numbers, plus WoO additions). Mozart's life is just over half of Beethoven's (thirty-five years versus fifty-six). The ratio is roughly four-to-one in works-per-year, which is part of why Mozart is the singular case in Western music history and why a single-composer volume on him is one of the _Close Listening_ series' core entries.

### 1.3 The 35-year arc — a one-paragraph overview

You will live the life in detail across Chapters 3 through 22. For orientation: Wolfgang Amadeus Mozart was born in Salzburg on January 27, 1756. He died in Vienna on December 5, 1791, at the age of thirty-five. The standard scholarly framework divides those thirty-five years into three periods. **Childhood prodigy (1761-1773)** : the European concert tours with his father Leopold Mozart through London, Paris, Mannheim, Vienna, then the three Italian tours that produced about two-hundred-and-fifty Köchel-numbered works by age seventeen. **Salzburg adolescent and young man (1773-1781)** : court service under Prince-Archbishop Hieronymus Colloredo, the 1777-78 Paris-and-Mannheim journey on which Mozart's mother Anna Maria Mozart died, the first masterworks (the Sinfonia Concertante K. 364, the "Jeunehomme" Piano Concerto K. 271, the early _Idomeneo_). **Vienna freelance (1781-1791)** : the break with Colloredo in May 1781, the marriage to Constanze Weber in August 1782, the friendship with Joseph Haydn, the great Vienna piano concertos, the Da Ponte operas, the late symphonies, the chamber peak, the Magic Flute, the Requiem, the death. This framework is documented across Solomon, Eisen, Sadie, Heartz, and Wolff and is the standard.

### 1.4 The Six Listening Habits to Unlearn (Mozart Edition)

Every volume in this series opens by naming the listening habits that get in the way of the music. The Mozart-specific habits are different from the Beethoven-specific habits — the cultural baggage around Mozart is unusually heavy, and most of it is later invention rather than anything the music asks of you.

**Habit 1: "Mozart sounds like background music."** This is the most common listening error, and it is half the reason a comprehensive single-composer volume on Mozart is needed in the first place. The image of Mozart as wallpaper, pretty, courtly, and harmonically untroubled, is a partial truth pulled out of context. It accurately describes some of the early divertimentos and serenades Mozart wrote as functional background music for Salzburg garden parties. It is wildly inaccurate about the Symphony No. 25 in G minor K. 183 (the early dark-key symphony Chapter 2 anchors), the Piano Concerto No. 20 in D minor K. 466 (Chapter 7), the Don Giovanni Commendatore-statue scene (Chapter 12), the Symphony No. 40 in G minor K. 550 (Chapter 10), the String Quintet in G minor K. 516 (Chapter 17), or the Requiem (Chapter 18). The repertoire we will spend most of our time inside is the opposite of wallpaper. The dark-key half of Mozart's mature output is one of the most emotionally direct bodies of music in Western art music.

**Habit 2: "Mozart was easy — it just flowed out of him."** This is the most flattering myth and the most damaging. The image of Mozart taking finished compositions directly from his mind to paper without revision originates partly with his widow Constanze Mozart's posthumous editorial choices (about which more in Chapter 20) and partly with the surviving Friedrich Rochlitz anecdote-collection from the early-19th-century _Allgemeine musikalische Zeitung_. The surviving sketches, the working manuscripts, the letters where Mozart talks about composition as labor — all show planning, revision, and effortful construction. The "miracle of unwritten manuscript" image is partly a cultural artifact, not a description of Mozart's process. The work was work.

**Habit 3: "The early prodigy works are warm-ups for the great Vienna years."** This is the easy summary, and it is wrong in specific places. The Piano Concerto No. 9 in E♭ "Jeunehomme" K. 271, composed in January 1777 when Mozart was twenty-one and still in Salzburg, is one of the great piano concertos by any composer. Charles Rosen called it "the first great Mozart concerto" — by which he meant: the first concerto where Mozart's mind is moving at full mature speed. The _Exsultate, jubilate_ motet K. 165 (1773, Mozart age seventeen, Chapter 3's anchor) is mature vocal writing. The Salzburg years are the first half of a mature creative life, not a warm-up for the Vienna decade.

**Habit 4: "Salieri killed Mozart" / "_Amadeus_ is biography."** Peter Shaffer's 1979 play and Miloš Forman's 1984 film are great works of dramatic imagination on a real biographical scaffolding. They are not history. Antonio Salieri (1750-1825) was Imperial Kapellmeister in Vienna from 1788 to 1824, a respected composer who taught Beethoven, Franz Schubert, and Franz Liszt. There is no historical evidence that Salieri poisoned Mozart. The poisoning rumor emerged in the 1820s, after Salieri's mental decline, and Salieri himself denied it publicly. Alexander Pushkin's 1830 play _Mozart and Salieri_ is a literary work that Shaffer's 1979 _Amadeus_ extends. Chapter 20 of this volume is a focused treatment of the Mozart-Salieri question, organized as a three-column observational frame: scholarly record / _Amadeus_ -film invention / genuine historical gray zone. The chapter offers no verdict because the scholarship does not.

**Habit 5: "The Requiem is Mozart's."** The Requiem in D minor K. 626 was unfinished at Mozart's death. The Introit was complete. The Kyrie was nearly complete. The Sequence from Dies Irae through Confutatis was sketched with vocal lines and bass. The Lacrimosa was sketched only eight bars in. The Offertorium and Sanctus and Agnus Dei were either fragmentary or absent. Constanze commissioned Mozart's pupil Franz Xaver Süssmayr to complete the work; Süssmayr's completion (1792) is the version normally performed. Robert Levin published an alternative completion in 1991 (premiered Stuttgart 1991; Carus-Verlag publication 1993). Chapter 18 of this volume is honest about all of this. The Requiem is in part Mozart and in part Süssmayr (and possibly other hands). Once you can hear the seam, the work becomes more interesting, not less.

**Habit 6: "I don't know which Mozart to start with."** The standard starter list, agreed across the major scholars: the Piano Concerto No. 23 K. 488; the Piano Concerto No. 24 in C minor K. 491; the Symphony No. 40 K. 550; the Requiem; Magic Flute; _Le nozze di Figaro_. If you can give six hours of careful listening to those six works, you will know whether Mozart is going to be one of the composers of your listening life. Chapter 23 ends with this starter list assembled formally.

### 1.5 Why ~50 anchor works and not 600

Mozart's Köchel catalog has roughly six hundred numbered entries — symphonies, concertos, operas, chamber works, sacred works, occasional dances, church sonatas, divertimentos, serenades, single arias, single dances. About fifty of those works carry the major creative weight, and about fifty more reward sustained attention from a curious listener. The remaining four-hundred-fifty are either occasional works Mozart composed for specific Salzburg or Vienna functions (the contredanses for Vienna court balls, the church sonatas for Salzburg liturgy, the divertimentos for garden parties) or early apprentice works (the symphonies of his eighth year, the early piano sonatas) that scholarship treats as biographical interest rather than as repertoire.

This book is organized around the fifty central works. We touch the next fifty in passing. We mostly do not discuss the apprentice and occasional repertoire, except to acknowledge it exists and to explain where in the catalog to find it if you want to explore further (Chapter 23 includes pointers).

The reason for this triage: a listening life is finite. If you can know fifty Mozart works deeply — the symphonies K. 25 and K. 36 in passing, K. 183 and K. 504 and K. 543 and K. 550 and K. 551 by ear; the concertos K. 271, K. 453, K. 466, K. 488, K. 491, K. 503, K. 595 by ear; the operas Figaro, Don Giovanni, Così, Magic Flute, _Entführung_ , and Idomeneo by character and aria; the late chamber works K. 387, K. 465, K. 515, K. 516, K. 581, K. 622 by movement; the sacred works K. 427 and K. 626 by section — you are inside Mozart's mind in a real way. The next four-hundred-fifty Köchel numbers will mean more to you because of the fifty, not less.

### 1.6 The four-section chapter pattern, refreshed

To save you the trouble of jumping back to Volume One: every chapter from Chapter 2 onward uses the same four-section structure.

**Before You Listen** (the setup): a Hook Anecdote of about a paragraph or two — the biographical or historical episode that gives this work its dramatic context. Then a short numbered list of one to three things to notice on the first listen. Then a one-sentence background note covering when the work was composed, how long it runs, and why a specific recording is recommended. Then a Search Hints line giving you two or three performers to look for in your music app (English plus Chinese, since the Socratopia companion serves both language registers).

**The Listening Window** (the second listen): timestamps to a single specifically-named Primary Recording, with what to attend to at each timestamp. Wherever a precise timing is uncertain, the cue is reframed as a score-structural moment (the recapitulation, the development's central episode) or as a listening invitation rather than presented as exact.

**What's Happening in the Music** (the structural map): the form of the work, the orchestration choices, the technical features that make this work _this work_. Form is taught only as the anchor work demands it — we will not lecture on sonata form when the work in front of us is a strophic aria.

**The Stories** (the longest section, the Socratopia companion's primary fuel): extension anecdotes from the biographical record, recording history and the major interpretive traditions, cross-piece links to other works in this volume and, where useful, to related works in adjacent Close Listening volumes (the comprehensive classical primer and the Beethoven single-composer volume). Each chapter ends with three specific chapter-end sections that replace the standard QBooks exercise block.

The chapter-end sections are: **Conversation Seeds** (three to five specific things you can talk with the Socratopia companion about); **Things to Listen For on Re-Listen** (three to five concrete observables for the second listen, timestamped or anchored to score structure); **Going Deeper** (one further-listening recommendation plus one book, film, or podcast for the curious reader). Music appreciation is not algebra, and "Concept Check / Application Problems / Think Deeper" exercise blocks do not fit the material. The three-section chapter end is the form this series uses for music.

### 1.7 A note on what this book deliberately avoids

This is not a Mozart biography. Solomon, Sadie, Eisen, Gutman, and Spaethling have all written the biography at length, in different registers, and Chapter 23 lists the recommended reading order. This is a _listening guide_ with biographical scaffolding sufficient to make each chapter's anchor work make sense.

It is also not a music-school harmonic analysis. The book is for an adult amateur classical-music lover who does not read sheet music. Where we discuss form, we discuss it in plain English — "the first theme returns in the home key" rather than "tonic recapitulation of the principal subject group." Where we discuss orchestration, we name instruments — "the clarinets enter at this moment" rather than scoring abbreviations.

The volume is neither deification ("the divine Mozart, untouchable miracle") nor deflation ("the overrated child star whose Vienna years coasted on early reputation"). Both readings have been written, often by serious people, and both are partial. The book describes what is in the music and what is in the documented life. The reader decides what kind of figure Mozart is.

This is not the _Amadeus_ volume. Where the play and film diverge from the scholarly record — and they diverge in specific, documentable places — the relevant chapter names the divergence. Chapter 5 (the _Entführung_ and the Joseph II "too many notes" line that goes back to Franz Xaver Niemetschek's 1798 first biography), Chapter 12 (Don Giovanni and the Pushkin-to-Shaffer literary genealogy), Chapter 20 (the Mozart-Salieri question), and Chapter 22 (the death and the pauper's-grave embroidery) all handle specific _Amadeus_ -film inventions directly.

### 1.8 What you will know by Chapter 23

By the time you finish this book and the recommended listening that goes with it:

You will know about fifty specific Mozart works by ear, well enough to identify any of them on the second hearing.

You will recognize the difference between Salzburg-period Mozart (lighter scoring, courtly registers, faster tempos, the early symphonic and sacred output) and Vienna-period Mozart (heavier scoring, dramatic registers, the great piano concertos and operas).

You will hear why the late piano concertos K. 466, K. 488, K. 491, K. 503, K. 595 are among the deepest concertos ever written by any composer, and why the K. 488 Adagio has stayed at the heart of the slow-movement repertoire.

You will be able to follow a Mozart opera ensemble — the multi-character interlocking finale form Mozart effectively invented (the Figaro Act II finale runs twenty-two minutes of continuous music with characters entering, exiting, and singing across each other).

You will recognize the late string quintets K. 515 and K. 516 as Mozart's chamber-music peak, and you will know why most chamber-music players who have spent time with both Mozart and Beethoven consider the quintets as central to the repertoire as Beethoven's late quartets.

You will know what is documented and what is myth about the Requiem's completion, and you will be able to follow a Robert-Levin-completion performance with knowledge of which passages are Mozart, which are Süssmayr, and which Levin has reconstructed differently from Süssmayr.

You will walk into any Mozart program, whether at a symphony concert, a recital, or an opera house, with informed anticipation rather than reverent caution.

### 1.9 Forward to Chapter 2

Chapter 2 establishes the listening loop on a single early Mozart symphony: the Symphony No. 25 in G minor K. 183, composed in October 1773 when Mozart was seventeen. Why K. 183? It is the first Mozart symphony in a minor key. It is early Mozart at his most ear-catching, well-documented in Solomon and Sadie. The dramatic minor-key opening is the form vocabulary the rest of this book will need to build on. And K. 183 launches Thread B — Mozart's compositional features — with the first appearance of the Sturm und Drang dark-key signature that will recur in K. 466 (Chapter 7), K. 491 (Chapter 7), K. 550 (Chapter 10), and K. 516 (Chapter 17). The synthesis at Chapter 19 collects all seven Mozart compositional features into one place.

Set aside about ninety minutes for Chapter 2. Read the chapter once before you listen, do the first listen, read the Listening Window, do the second listen, read the rest. The four-section pattern works best when you live inside it rather than skim.

The work ahead is long but the friend walking with you knows where the chapters are going. Welcome to the deep Mozart.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The Six Listening Habits in §1.4 — which one of those six is the one I would have walked into this book carrying, and how do I tell?"_ (Pick the one that surprised you when you read it. Often it is Habit 2 or Habit 5.)
* _"Why does the book recommend the K. 488 Adagio as one of the great slow movements in the repertoire? What specifically makes a slow movement great?"_
* _"What is the difference between the comprehensive primer volume's posture and a single-composer deep volume's posture? When would a reader pick up one over the other?"_
* _"If I have only six hours of listening to give to Mozart, which six works should I prioritize — and why those six and not a different six?"_
* _"What is at stake in the difference between 'Mozart' the scholarly figure and 'Mozart' the _Amadeus_ -film character? Why does Chapter 1 spend time on this distinction at the start of the book?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

This chapter contains no anchor work, so the listening exercise is preparation for Chapter 2. Find a recording of the Symphony No. 25 in G minor K. 183 — Karl Böhm with the Berlin Philharmonic (Deutsche Grammophon, 1968) or John Eliot Gardiner with the English Baroque Soloists (Philips, 1989) are both standard and both have been in the catalog for thirty-plus years. Listen to the first movement once, all the way through, without taking notes. Then notice three things on the second listen:

  1. The very opening: the violins play a syncopated figure (notes that come in slightly off the beat) while the violas pulse underneath. This is the rhythmic-displacement opening that catches the ear immediately.
  2. The key: G minor. Mozart wrote only two symphonies in G minor in his entire life — K. 183 (1773, the "little G minor") and K. 550 (1788, the "great G minor"). Both are central works in the catalog.
  3. The age of the composer: seventeen. Listen for the maturity of phrase and structure given that age.

These three observations are the entry point to Chapter 2.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : the Symphony No. 1 in E♭ K. 16, composed in London in early 1765 when Mozart was eight years old. It is genuinely the work of an eight-year-old — derivative of the symphonies he had heard in London (the Johann Christian Bach influence is direct and audible) but already structurally coherent. Eight minutes long. Listen to confirm the bare fact: yes, an eight-year-old wrote this.

**Read** : Robert Spaethling, _Mozart's Letters, Mozart's Life: Selected Letters_ (Norton, 2000). This is the single most-readable single-volume of Mozart in his own words. The Salzburg-era letters home from the Italian tours, the Paris-and-Mannheim journey letters, the Vienna freelance period letters, the famous 1787 letter on his father's death. About 460 pages. You do not need to read it cover to cover; dip into the years that interest you as we cover them. The Spaethling edition will appear in the further-reading section of nearly every chapter in this book.

## Chapter 2: The Listening Basics for Mozart

The first time you give a Mozart symphony the kind of attention you would give a Beethoven symphony, the music can surprise you with how much is going on. The textures are lighter than Beethoven's, which is true — Mozart's orchestra is usually smaller, his wind writing more exposed, his counterpoint more transparent. But "lighter" does not mean "less is happening." If anything, the speed of musical events is higher: more themes per minute, more harmonic moves, more dialogue between sections of the orchestra. The Mozart symphony is busy in a way the Beethoven symphony often is not.

This chapter teaches the four-section listening loop on a single early Mozart symphony — the Symphony No. 25 in G minor K. 183. It runs about twenty-five minutes in total across four movements; we will anchor on the first movement, which runs about eight minutes. The chapter is the longest you will see at the start of the book, because the listening pattern it establishes is the one every subsequent chapter will use.

### 2.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

October 5, 1773. Mozart is seventeen years old, recently returned from the third Italian tour with his father Leopold Mozart, living in the Salzburg house his family has occupied for years on the Tanzmeisterhaus (the "Dance Master's House") on Hannibalplatz. The Italian tours have ended; the hoped-for Italian court appointment, Leopold's strategic goal for at least three years, has not materialized. Mozart is back in Salzburg, in the service of the Salzburg court under Prince-Archbishop Hieronymus Colloredo, and in the next eight weeks he will compose four symphonies (K. 183, K. 199, K. 200, K. 201) that together mark a turn from the entertaining-the-court mode of the early symphonies into something more dramatically ambitious.

What changed? The conventional answer, stated cautiously across Solomon and Sadie: Mozart had absorbed the German _Sturm und Drang_ aesthetic that was sweeping European art in the early 1770s — the literary movement associated with the young Friedrich Schiller and Johann Wolfgang von Goethe, with its emphasis on stormy emotion, individual revolt, dark interiority. In music the _Sturm und Drang_ meant minor keys, abrupt dynamic contrasts, syncopated rhythms, dramatic harmonic moves. Joseph Haydn had written symphonies in this mode in the late 1760s and early 1770s (the Haydn "Sturm und Drang" symphonies Nos. 26, 39, 44, 45, 49 are the canonical Haydn examples). Mozart had certainly heard some of these or knew their reputation. K. 183 is Mozart's first symphony to take the _Sturm und Drang_ posture as its primary mode.

It is also Mozart's first symphony in a minor key. He would write only one more in his life — the Symphony No. 40 in G minor K. 550, composed in July 1788, fifteen years later. The pair K. 183 + K. 550 are the only Mozart minor-key symphonies, both in G minor, separated by half of his life. The match is not coincidental. We will return to this when Chapter 10 anchors on K. 550.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **The opening seven seconds.** The violins enter with a syncopated leaping figure, accented notes that fall on the offbeat, while the violas pulse underneath in regular eighth notes. The rhythmic displacement catches the ear immediately. You will know within seven seconds that this is not a courtly symphony.

  2. **The horn writing.** This symphony is scored unusually for four horns rather than the standard two — two crooked in G, and a second pair crooked in B-flat _alto_ (high B-flat). On valveless natural horns, having two pairs at different pitches lets Mozart cover more of the harmony and project a brighter, biting top register. Listen for the moments where the horns answer the strings or hold long sustained notes underneath the texture; Solomon, Sadie, and Charles Rosen all note the four-horn scoring as part of what gives K. 183 its dark color.

  3. **The dynamic shocks.** Listen for the sudden loud-to-soft and soft-to-loud contrasts. The _Sturm und Drang_ aesthetic prized these dynamic surprises as emotional immediacy. Mozart deploys them throughout.

#### One-sentence background

Composed in Salzburg on October 5, 1773 (the autograph manuscript is dated); approximately twenty-five minutes across four movements, the first movement runs about eight minutes; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is John Eliot Gardiner conducting the English Baroque Soloists in their Mozart symphonies cycle for Philips (recorded 1989, released across 1990-94), chosen for the period-instrument transparency and the way the four-horn scoring projects on natural horns. (Karl Böhm's 1968 Berlin Philharmonic reading on Deutsche Grammophon is the standard modern-instrument alternative if you prefer the warmer mid-century tradition.)

#### Search Hints

EN: "Mozart Symphony 25 K 183 Gardiner English Baroque Soloists" or "Mozart Symphony 25 Böhm Berlin Philharmonic" ZH: "莫扎特 第25号交响曲 K183 加德纳" 或 "莫扎特 第25号交响曲 K183 贝姆 柏林爱乐" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

### 2.2 The Listening Window

This section is for your second listen — the one where you know what is coming and can attend to specific moments. Timestamps below refer to the Gardiner / English Baroque Soloists Primary Recording named in §2.1. If you are listening to a different recording the score-structural moments still apply but the exact clock times will differ; in that case use the score-moment description rather than the timestamp.

**0:00 — The opening.** Violins play the syncopated leaping figure; violas pulse beneath in steady eighth notes. The downbeat is felt but not stated by any instrument. The texture establishes the rhythmic-displacement mood that runs through the entire movement.

**0:15 — First full orchestral entry.** Horns and oboes confirm the G minor harmony and the meter; the syncopated figure now sits inside a full orchestral context rather than as a solo string statement.

**About one-third through the exposition — the transition into the second key area.** Mozart moves from G minor to B♭ major (the relative major, which is the standard sonata-form move for a minor-key first movement of this period). The transition is briefer and more abrupt than a comparable Haydn symphony might handle it; you will feel the key-change as a release, like the sun appearing.

**The exposition's second theme in B♭ major.** Lyric line in the violins over a steady accompaniment. The mood lifts; the music sounds for a moment like the Mozart you might already know. Then the closing material of the exposition pulls back toward the minor mode and the exposition repeats.

**The development.** Mozart treats the opening syncopated figure aggressively — fragmenting it, passing it between instruments, modulating through several keys before settling on the dominant of G minor in preparation for the recapitulation. The development is short by later-Mozart standards (Mozart at seventeen has not yet developed the extended development sections that mark the great Vienna concertos), but it is structurally complete.

**The recapitulation.** The opening returns in G minor. The second-theme material that was in B♭ major in the exposition now also returns in G minor (the sonata-form rule — second-theme material in the home key on return), which casts what was lyric and bright in a darker color the second time around. Listen for this. The same melodic line in major vs. minor is one of the most direct lessons sonata form can teach a listener.

**The coda.** Brief reaffirmation of G minor. The movement ends without resolution into major — the _Sturm und Drang_ aesthetic does not permit the easy victory turn.

### 2.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The four-horn scoring

The K. 183 orchestration is two oboes, two bassoons, four horns (two in G, two in B♭), and strings. No flutes, no clarinets, no trumpets, no timpani. The four-horn choice is unusual. The standard symphony of 1773 used two horns. By adding a second pair in a different transposition, Mozart gives himself more harmonic flexibility (a horn in G and a horn in B♭ between them can cover more harmonies than two horns of the same pitch) and a denser sustained sonority for the dramatic moments. Charles Rosen identifies the four-horn scoring as one of the markers that pushes K. 183 from courtly entertainment into something more emotionally weighted; Stanley Sadie makes the same point in his Norton biography. Listen specifically for the long horn pedal notes underneath the strings in the development section — that is the four-horn payoff.

#### Sonata form, briefly

Since this is the first chapter to anchor on a sonata-form first movement, a one-paragraph refresher (any introductory text on Western form will give you a fuller treatment). A sonata-form first movement has three parts: **exposition** (presents two contrasting theme groups in two different keys, with the first in the home key — here G minor — and the second in a related key — here the relative major B♭); **development** (takes material from the exposition, fragments it, modulates through other keys); **recapitulation** (brings the material back, with the second-theme group now in the home key as well, so the movement ends with both themes in G minor rather than the original G-minor-then-B♭-major split). The form is a journey out and back, with the home key reasserted on return.

For Mozart specifically, sonata form is the basic architectural unit for the first movements of his symphonies, concertos, and chamber works. Across the next twenty chapters you will hear sonata-form first movements again and again. K. 183 is the first place where Mozart's deployment of the form takes on emotional ambition, rather than serving simply as a vessel for melodic invention.

#### Why G minor specifically?

This is where Thread B of the volume officially launches. **Thread B** is the running collection of Mozart's compositional features — patterns that recur across the catalog and that a careful listener can learn to spot. Chapter 19 §19.1 will collect all seven features into one synthesis chapter; we point forward there now so that you know from this first encounter that the convergence is coming.

The first feature is the **dark-key signature**. Mozart had certain keys he turned to when the emotional weight of a work asked for it. The two most distinctive are D minor and G minor. The D-minor works include the Piano Concerto K. 466 (Chapter 7), the Don Giovanni overture and Commendatore scene (Chapter 12), and the Requiem (Chapter 18). The G-minor works are fewer but central: this Symphony No. 25 K. 183, the Symphony No. 40 K. 550 (Chapter 10), and the String Quintet K. 516 (Chapter 17). C minor adds a smaller cluster (the Piano Concerto K. 491, Chapter 7; the Piano Sonata K. 457, Chapter 15; the Great Mass K. 427, Chapter 18). The matched pair K. 183 + K. 550, the only two Mozart symphonies in G minor separated by fifteen years and roughly half his creative life, is the most direct invitation the catalog offers to ask: what did G minor mean to Mozart?

The answer Solomon and Sadie give cautiously is: G minor is the key in which Mozart writes when he wants to take the _Sturm und Drang_ aesthetic seriously. Both G-minor symphonies open with rhythmic displacement, both use unusual scoring (K. 183's four horns; K. 550's omission of trumpets and timpani for darker color), both keep the major-key relief sections brief and conditional. Whether Mozart "felt" G minor as a personal signature in any biographical sense is a question the scholarship treats with care — we will return to it in Chapter 10. For the moment: register that K. 183's G minor is the start of a thread you will track across the volume.

#### The age question

Mozart was seventeen years and eight months old when he composed K. 183. For comparison: Beethoven at the same age (1788) was still composing court music in Bonn and would not write his first publishable mature works until five or six years later. Schubert at the same age was already composing major Lieder (1814, "Gretchen am Spinnrade") and the rate is comparable to Mozart's. Mendelssohn at seventeen composed the _Midsummer Night's Dream_ Overture (1826), one of the great teenage masterpieces in Western music; the comparison to K. 183 is direct.

The seventeen-year-old Mozart of K. 183 has been composing for thirteen years (Leopold Mozart documented his first compositions at age four). The Italian tours have given him direct exposure to Italian opera, sacred polyphony, and Italian orchestral writing; the earlier London tour exposed him to Johann Christian Bach's English style. By 1773 his compositional toolkit includes most of the European mainstream. K. 183 is what a maximally-trained seventeen-year-old does when he turns serious.

This brings us back to Habit 2 of Chapter 1 ("Mozart was easy"). K. 183 sounds inevitable on the page; it does not sound like a struggle. But the inevitability is a function of fifteen years of full-time professional training before age seventeen. The "miracle" is partly the training. We will return to this question repeatedly.

### 2.4 The Stories

#### What K. 183 is and is not

K. 183 is not Mozart's first symphony — that distinction belongs to the Symphony No. 1 K. 16, composed in London in early 1765 when Mozart was eight years old. By 1773 the symphony list has about thirty entries, depending on how you count the Italian overtures that double as symphonies and the dubious-attribution works that Sadie's Norton volume disentangles. Most of the early symphonies are short (eight to twelve minutes), three movements rather than four, scored for small forces, and primarily concerned with melodic invention rather than dramatic structure. They were composed for specific Salzburg or Italian occasions and were largely functional music.

K. 183 marks a turn. It is four movements (the standard later-Classical symphonic form), about twenty-five minutes long, scored more heavily than the early symphonies, and dramatic rather than primarily melodic in conception. Solomon describes it as the first Mozart symphony that asks the listener to follow a sustained emotional argument across its movements rather than simply enjoy a sequence of pleasant pieces. Sadie's treatment in the Norton biography emphasizes the formal sophistication of the first-movement development.

#### The four movements

The first movement (the one we anchored on) is the _Sturm und Drang_ showpiece. The second movement is an Andante in E♭ major (the relative major's parallel, an unusual modulation for the slow movement) — quieter, more lyric, with the bassoons given lovely melodic lines. The third movement is a Menuetto and Trio that returns to G minor (the minuet) but lifts into G major for the trio (the contrasting middle section) — the brief major-mode breath. The fourth movement is an Allegro in G minor that returns to the _Sturm und Drang_ posture of the first movement and brings the symphony to a properly stormy close, without the major-mode resolution that a more conventional minor-key symphony of the period might have used.

The cycle as a whole is balanced: stormy first movement, lyric breathing second movement, the brief major-mode relief in the minuet trio, stormy finale. This four-movement balance, dramatic outer movements with a contrasting lyric center, becomes the standard Mozart symphonic shape and will recur in the later symphonies including K. 543, K. 550, and K. 551.

#### Recording history briefly

K. 183 is one of the most-recorded early Mozart symphonies because it stands out clearly from the courtly entertainment of its predecessors. The recording lineage divides into two broad traditions.

The mid-century modern-instrument tradition is anchored by Karl Böhm's Berlin Philharmonic readings (Deutsche Grammophon, 1968) and Otto Klemperer's earlier Philharmonia readings (Columbia, late 1950s). These performances use the modern symphony orchestra with full string sections, vibrato in the strings, and the rounder tone of modern brass and woodwinds. The K. 183 sounds weighty, almost Beethovenian, with the strings drawing out the lyric lines.

The period-instrument Historically Informed Performance (HIP) tradition, which began in the 1970s and flowered in the 1980s and 1990s, takes a different approach. John Eliot Gardiner with the English Baroque Soloists, Christopher Hogwood with the Academy of Ancient Music, Trevor Pinnock with the English Concert, and Frans Brüggen with the Orchestra of the Eighteenth Century — these conductors use the orchestral forces of Mozart's own time (smaller string sections, gut strings, valveless natural horns, period bassoons), faster tempos closer to the original metronome marks, and less vibrato. The K. 183 in HIP-style sounds sharper, more rhythmic, with the four natural horns producing a slightly buzzy sustained sound that contemporary listeners would have recognized as the horn sound of their time. Gardiner's reading is the one this chapter anchors on, but the choice is partly preference. If you prefer the warmer modern-instrument tradition, the Böhm reading is equally legitimate and well-loved.

The more contemporary HIP recordings — Teodor Currentzis with MusicAeterna, Pablo Heras-Casado with the Freiburger Barockorchester, the Akademie für Alte Musik Berlin — are vivid and worth seeking out, though they are recent enough that critical consensus is still settling. Currentzis in particular is divisive; some listeners love the dramatic extremes, others find them mannered.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

K. 183 connects forward to three later works in this volume.

**To K. 466** (D-minor Piano Concerto, Chapter 7): the Sturm und Drang opening, the dark-key seriousness, the dynamic shocks. K. 466's first-movement opening sounds Beethovenian to most contemporary listeners, but the gesture is genealogically connected to the K. 183 we are hearing here. Beethoven himself certainly knew K. 466 — he composed his own cadenza for it around 1809.

**To K. 491** (C-minor Piano Concerto, Chapter 7): the dark-key minor-mode dramatic concerto opening. Lewis Lockwood has argued that K. 491 directly influenced Beethoven's own C-minor Piano Concerto Op. 37 — the genealogical chain runs K. 183 → K. 466 / K. 491 → Beethoven Op. 37 → the C-minor Symphony No. 5. Mozart's _Sturm und Drang_ posture in 1773 is part of why Beethoven in 1798 could write Op. 37.

**To K. 550** (G-minor Symphony No. 40, Chapter 10): the matched pair. Same key. Same composer. Fifteen years apart. K. 550 is mature, harmonically more daring, but the family resemblance to K. 183 is immediate. When you reach Chapter 10 you will hear the two G-minor symphonies side by side and the comparison will sharpen what each work is doing.

**To K. 516** (G-minor String Quintet, Chapter 17): the chamber-music peer of the G-minor symphonies. K. 516 is one of the most emotionally direct works in the chamber repertoire and the third major G-minor anchor in this volume.

The chapter at the convergence point (§19.1) collects all four G-minor works and asks what G minor meant to Mozart across the catalog. The forward pointer here is the first installment of that collection.

#### The biographical context: late 1773 in Salzburg

A brief note for the biographical thread (Thread A). October 1773 finds the Mozart family between two phases. The Italian period is over — the three tours (1769-71, 1771-72, 1772-73) are complete, the hoped-for Italian court appointment did not happen. The Salzburg court is the available employer. Prince-Archbishop Hieronymus Colloredo took office in 1772 and the Mozart-Colloredo relationship, which will rupture in May 1781 (Chapter 5), is in its early reasonably-functional phase. Wolfgang is a Salzburg court musician; Leopold continues as Vice-Kapellmeister. The composition rate is high (four symphonies in eight weeks, the _Exsultate, jubilate_ K. 165 motet completed in January 1773 in Milan, the early Salzburg piano sonatas) and the future is still open.

K. 183 is sometimes interpreted biographically as Mozart's frustration at being back in provincial Salzburg after the cosmopolitan Italian tours. Solomon treats this interpretation with care: the music does not require it, and Mozart did not leave any letter explicitly tying K. 183 to a specific frustration. Better to hear K. 183 as a young composer absorbing the _Sturm und Drang_ aesthetic he had encountered (through Haydn and through the German literary fashion) and trying it on for the first time in a major symphony. Whether Salzburg-confinement is part of why the aesthetic appealed to him is reasonable speculation, not a documented biographical claim.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The K. 183 opening with violins syncopated over viola pulse — how would you describe what that rhythmic-displacement does to the listener in the first seven seconds? What does Mozart get from putting the accents off the beat rather than on it?"_
* _"The four-horn scoring is unusual for 1773. What does the second pair of horns let Mozart do that two horns alone could not? Listen to the development section's horn pedal — does the four-horn scoring solve a specific structural problem there?"_
* _"K. 183 and K. 550 are the only two Mozart symphonies in G minor, separated by fifteen years. Why those two specifically — what makes G minor a key Mozart turns to when the work asks for emotional weight? Is the choice biographical, aesthetic, or formal?"_
* _"The Gardiner period-instrument reading vs. the Böhm modern-instrument reading — pick a specific moment (the opening, the development, the recapitulation in G minor) and describe what each performance is emphasizing differently."_
* _"How does Mozart at seventeen compare to Mendelssohn at seventeen (the Midsummer Night's Dream Overture, 1826)? Both wrote a major work at the same age. What does the comparison reveal about how teenage masterpieces become possible?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find Gardiner / English Baroque Soloists on Philips (1989) or another recording you trust. Listen to the first movement of K. 183 a second time and attend to these three concrete moments:

  1. **The very opening (0:00-0:15).** Confirm: the violins play the syncopated leaping figure, the violas pulse underneath in regular eighth notes. Notice that no instrument states the downbeat. This is the rhythmic displacement that catches the ear.

  2. **The transition into the second theme (roughly one-third through the exposition).** Mozart moves from G minor to B♭ major. Notice the change in mood — the sun appearing, briefly. Then notice what mode the same theme returns in during the recapitulation (G minor) and how that re-coloring changes the second hearing of the same melodic material.

  3. **The horn writing in the development.** Listen for the moments where the horns hold long sustained notes underneath the string figuration. This is the four-horn scoring's payoff. The natural horns on the period-instrument Gardiner recording will sound noticeably brassier and more pungent than the modern horns on the Böhm.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : pair K. 183 with the Haydn Symphony No. 39 in G minor (composed circa 1765-68), one of the canonical Haydn _Sturm und Drang_ symphonies. The Haydn predates K. 183 by about five to eight years and was widely known in central Europe by 1773. Listening to the two G-minor symphonies side by side reveals what Mozart absorbed from the Haydn-driven _Sturm und Drang_ aesthetic and what he changed.

**Read** : Charles Rosen, _The Classical Style: Haydn, Mozart, Beethoven_ (Norton, 1971; expanded edition 1997). The chapters on Mozart's symphonic and concerto writing are the analytical voice this book most often returns to. Rosen writes for the literate non-specialist (no sheet music required to follow the prose) and his treatment of _Sturm und Drang_ in early-1770s symphonic writing is the clearest in the literature. About 460 pages total; read selectively.

## Chapter 3: The Prodigy Years 1761-1773

Wolfgang Amadeus Mozart was born in Salzburg on January 27, 1756, the seventh child and second surviving child of Leopold Mozart, a Salzburg court musician and Vice-Kapellmeister, and Anna Maria Mozart née Pertl. His older sister Maria Anna ("Nannerl") was four-and-a-half years his senior and was already a prodigy at the keyboard. Wolfgang's musical talents emerged so early (Leopold Mozart records the first compositions at age four, the first public keyboard performances at age five) that the family changed shape around them. Leopold abandoned his promising career as a composer and violin pedagogue (his _Versuch einer gründlichen Violinschule_ of 1756 was a standard violin treatise for decades) to become Wolfgang's manager, teacher, and chief promoter. From 1761 to 1773, the Mozart family lived primarily as a touring concert ensemble — the prodigy children of a serious musician father, presented to the courts and concert societies of Europe.

This chapter covers those twelve traveling years and the music they produced. It anchors on the motet _Exsultate, jubilate_ K. 165, composed in Milan in January 1773 for the castrato Venanzio Rauzzini when Mozart was seventeen. K. 165 is one of the latest works of the prodigy period — composed at the end of the third Italian tour, before the family's return to Salzburg in March and Wolfgang's autumn turn into the _Sturm und Drang_ aesthetic (the Symphony K. 183 we heard in Chapter 2). The motet shows what twelve years of full-time professional training and concert touring had produced.

### 3.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

Mid-April 1770. The Mozart family (Leopold and the fourteen-year-old Wolfgang, traveling without Anna Maria or Nannerl on the first Italian tour) arrived in Rome in time for Holy Week. On Wednesday of Holy Week (April 11, 1770) they attended the Tenebrae service at the Sistine Chapel where the famed _Miserere_ by Gregorio Allegri (composed c. 1638) was sung. The _Miserere_ was a Vatican closely-guarded property: the score was forbidden to leave the Sistine Chapel, and the work was sung only during Holy Week. Copies were not permitted; performers from outside the Sistine choir were not permitted to obtain the score.

According to Leopold Mozart's letter to Anna Maria dated April 14, 1770, Wolfgang heard the _Miserere_ once at the Sistine Chapel and wrote it down from memory back at the lodging. Leopold's letter is the primary source for this story. Wolfgang returned to the Sistine the following Friday for the second Holy Week performance, made small corrections to his copy, and the transcription was complete. The transcription circulated; Charles Burney later published it.

This is the most famous prodigy-feat anecdote in Mozart's biography. Solomon treats the Leopold letter as essentially reliable. The Allegri _Miserere_ is about twelve minutes long, written in nine vocal parts across two choirs; the transcription from a single hearing (with one corrective hearing) by a fourteen-year-old is extraordinary but not impossible for a child of that training and that ear. The story is sometimes embroidered in popular telling — the most common embroidery makes the act a single-hearing memorization with no second corrective hearing, which the Leopold letter does not support. We tell it here in the form Leopold's letter actually supports: one hearing, one transcription, one corrective hearing, final version.

This is the Mozart of the prodigy years. He is fourteen. He has been traveling and performing across Europe for nine years. The Pope (Clement XIV) will confer the Order of the Golden Spur on him three months later, in Bologna on July 4, 1770.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **The opening Allegro ("_Exsultate, jubilate_ ").** A buoyant, almost theatrical entrance for the solo voice. Listen for the way Mozart writes for the upper register — the soprano voice (originally for the castrato Rauzzini, now standardly sung by a soprano) sits high and stays light. The text is a generic celebration of joy; the music is the kind of bravura writing eighteenth-century audiences attended to first for the showcase of vocal technique.

  2. **The Andante middle movement ("_Tu virginum corona_ ").** Slower, more lyric, in a contrasting key. Listen for the long sustained phrases, the way the voice is allowed to spin a single melodic idea across many measures, the way the orchestra falls back to let the voice carry the line.

  3. **The final "Alleluia" movement.** The famous one. About four minutes of vocal pyrotechnics on a single word. Listen for the long melismas (multiple notes on a single syllable), the way Mozart varies the same melodic gesture across many repetitions, the trill at the end. The "Alleluia" is a soprano showpiece that has stayed in the active recital repertoire and is sometimes excerpted from the motet for stand-alone performance.

#### One-sentence background

Composed in Milan in January 1773 (the autograph is dated January 17, 1773) for the castrato Venanzio Rauzzini, who was singing the role of Cecilio in Mozart's opera _Lucio Silla_ in Milan that season; about fifteen minutes across three movements; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Cecilia Bartoli with the Vienna Chamber Orchestra under György Fischer (Decca, 1991), a now thirty-plus-year-old reading widely held as one of the standards. (Emma Kirkby with Christopher Hogwood and the Academy of Ancient Music on L'Oiseau-Lyre / Decca, 1980, is the period-instrument alternative; both have been in catalog for decades.)

#### Search Hints

EN: "Mozart Exsultate jubilate K 165 Bartoli" or "Mozart Exsultate jubilate K 165 Kirkby Hogwood" ZH: "莫扎特 喜悦欢腾 K165 巴托莉" 或 "莫扎特 经文歌 K165 柯尔比 霍格伍德" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

### 3.2 The Listening Window

Timestamps below refer to the Bartoli / Fischer Primary Recording. Other recordings vary; use the score-structural moments if your recording is different.

**Opening Allegro — "_Exsultate, jubilate_."** Orchestral introduction sets up the key (F major) and the buoyant character. The soprano enters confidently on the opening "Exsultate" and the melismatic decoration of "jubilate" begins almost immediately. Listen for the way Mozart writes the vocal line so that the high notes always sit where the text would natural emphasize — the bravura is not arbitrary, it tracks the rhetoric of the Latin text.

**Recitative — "_Fulget amica dies_."** A short connecting recitative (declamatory speech-like vocal writing over sparse orchestral accompaniment) bridges the opening Allegro to the lyric Andante. Brief, transitional.

**Andante — "_Tu virginum corona_."** Slower tempo, lyric mood. The orchestral accompaniment thins out; the voice carries longer melodic phrases. The text praises the Virgin Mary as queen of virgins. Listen for the long sustained notes the soprano holds — this is the vocal-line writing that requires deep breath support and is one of the marks of a serious vocalist's recital repertoire.

**The "Alleluia" finale.** Tempo lifts; the meter changes. The single word "Alleluia" is set in multiple variations across about four minutes of music. Mozart writes the melismas to ascend and descend across nearly the full soprano range, with the final climactic ascent reaching the upper register and the trill that closes the work. Listen for the way the orchestra answers the voice between the vocal phrases — the orchestral interludes function as breathing space for the singer and as structural punctuation for the listener.

### 3.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The motet as a genre

A motet in the eighteenth-century Italian tradition was a sacred concert work for solo voice (or small ensemble) and orchestra, on a Latin religious text, not part of the Mass liturgy. The genre permitted virtuoso vocal writing in a way the strict liturgical Mass did not. Composers wrote motets for specific singers and for specific occasions (often a particular church or court celebration). _Exsultate, jubilate_ is a textbook example of the genre — three movements (Allegro, Andante, Allegro), Latin text, solo voice, small orchestra (two oboes, two horns, strings, organ continuo), virtuoso vocal demands.

#### Why Rauzzini specifically?

Venanzio Rauzzini (1746-1810) was one of the leading castrato singers of his generation. (A castrato was a male singer who had been castrated before puberty to preserve the soprano or mezzo-soprano vocal range while developing the chest capacity and breath support of an adult male — a practice that persisted in Italy through the early nineteenth century, primarily for sacred and operatic singing.) Mozart had composed the role of Cecilio in his opera _Lucio Silla_ for Rauzzini, who was singing the role in Milan from December 1772 through January 1773. The two had worked closely together; Mozart wrote _Exsultate, jubilate_ during the run of _Lucio Silla_ specifically for Rauzzini to sing in the church of the Theatines in Milan on January 17, 1773 (the date on the autograph).

The reason this biographical detail matters for listening: Mozart is writing for a specific voice he knows intimately. The vocal range, the agility, the breath capacity, the timbre — all of it is calibrated to Rauzzini's specific instrument. The high notes are where they are because Rauzzini could place them there reliably. The long sustained lines exist because Rauzzini could carry them. The bravura coloratura passages exist because Rauzzini was famous for his ornamental technique.

When a modern soprano sings _Exsultate, jubilate_ (which is now standard — castrati no longer exist), she is singing music written for a specific historical voice that the modern soprano voice can approximate but does not match exactly. The piece works in either voice, but careful listeners can sometimes hear the way the line wants a slightly different vocal placement than a modern soprano naturally produces.

#### The "Alleluia" and what makes it work

The "Alleluia" finale has stayed in steady circulation as a stand-alone soprano showpiece. Mozart was seventeen when he wrote it; the movement displays both prodigy facility and genuinely mature compositional sense.

What makes it work, technically: the underlying harmonic structure is simple (the movement stays primarily in the home key of F major with brief excursions to the dominant), which gives the vocal line freedom to elaborate. The melodic material is small (essentially three or four short motivic ideas) which Mozart varies across the movement; the variation creates the sense of forward motion without overwhelming the listener with new material. The orchestral accompaniment is light enough that the voice carries the full texture; the orchestra punctuates and supports rather than competing. The structural climax — the long ascending melisma to the high note and the final trill — arrives where the listener's ear expects a climax, after about four minutes of preparation.

What makes it work, rhetorically: the single word "Alleluia" is the perfect text for the genre. It has no semantic content that needs to be communicated through prosody; it is pure praise, pure exclamation. The vocal elaboration becomes the meaning. Mozart matches text to musical procedure in a way that feels inevitable.

The seventeen-year-old Mozart who wrote the "Alleluia" was already in command of the most important composer skill: matching musical idea to musical occasion. The "Alleluia" is exactly what an "Alleluia" is supposed to do.

#### The prodigy-trajectory question

Chapter 1's Habit 3 ("The early prodigy works are warm-ups for the great Vienna years") gets its first specific challenge here. K. 165 is genuinely mature work. The vocal writing is professional; the structure is sound; the matching of musical means to text and occasion is fully accomplished. A pseudonymous performance of the "Alleluia" attributed to an unknown contemporary composer of the early 1770s would not be marked out as immature. The seventeen-year-old Mozart is already operating at full professional level in vocal writing.

Where the early Mozart and the Vienna Mozart differ is not in technical command. The difference is in the depth of emotional or structural ambition the works take on. K. 165 is a brilliant motet; it is not aiming at the depth of the Requiem K. 626 (Chapter 18) or the _Et incarnatus est_ of the Great Mass K. 427 (Chapter 18 again). The Vienna Mozart writes works that ask more difficult questions; the Salzburg-era Mozart writes works that perform their tasks beautifully. The progression is from professional brilliance to artistic depth, not from amateur to professional. Hold this distinction — we will return to it.

### 3.4 The Stories

#### The European concert tours 1761-1769

Leopold Mozart's plan was to display the prodigy children to the European courts and concert societies. The major tours of the prodigy period:

**The Munich and Vienna tour (January-October 1762).** Wolfgang age six. Performances at the Bavarian court in Munich and the Habsburg court in Vienna under Empress Maria Theresa. The famous Vienna anecdote (Wolfgang jumping onto Maria Theresa's lap, refusing the Archduchess Marie Antoinette's hand and instead asking to marry her) comes from this trip. The Marie Antoinette episode is partly attested in the Nannerl Mozart memoir that Friedrich Schlichtegroll printed in 1793 (Nannerl's recollection at a remove of thirty years), and partly elaborated in later popular biography.

**The Grand Tour (June 1763-November 1766).** Three and a half years. Munich, Augsburg, Stuttgart, Mannheim, Frankfurt, Brussels, Paris (five months in 1763-64; another five months in 1766), London (fifteen months from April 1764 to July 1765), the Netherlands, Switzerland. The longest single residence was London, where Mozart at age eight composed his first symphony K. 16 (early 1765) under the influence of Johann Christian Bach (the youngest son of Johann Sebastian Bach), then resident in London as music master to Queen Charlotte. The Mozart-J.C. Bach friendship was significant: the two performed together at the keyboard, and J.C. Bach's symphonic and operatic styles influenced young Wolfgang's compositional development for years.

The London visit also produced Nannerl Mozart's memoir of her brother as a child — the warm-natured, even-tempered boy who loved his father, who took pranks well, who wept when his father was ill. The Nannerl memoir is one of the principal primary sources for the young Mozart's personality, and Solomon treats it carefully but largely with credence.

**The first Italian tour (December 1769-March 1771).** Wolfgang age thirteen-and-a-half to fifteen. Leopold and Wolfgang traveled alone (Anna Maria and Nannerl stayed in Salzburg). The tour reached Verona, Mantua, Milan, Bologna, Florence, Rome (the _Miserere_ episode, Holy Week 1770), Naples, and returned through Rome (where the Pope conferred the Order of the Golden Spur on July 4, 1770) and Bologna (where Wolfgang was admitted to the Accademia Filarmonica in October 1770 after passing a strict counterpoint examination — at age fourteen and a half, two years below the minimum permitted age). The first opera, _Mitridate, re di Ponto_ K. 87, premiered in Milan on December 26, 1770, for the Carnival season.

**The second Italian tour (August-December 1771).** Brief return to Milan for the premiere of the _serenata_ _Ascanio in Alba_ K. 111 in October 1771, composed for the wedding festivities of Archduke Ferdinand and Maria Beatrice d'Este.

**The third Italian tour (October 1772-March 1773).** Return to Milan for the premiere of _Lucio Silla_ K. 135 on December 26, 1772. This is the tour during which _Exsultate, jubilate_ K. 165 was composed (January 1773). When the tour ended in March 1773 and the Mozarts returned to Salzburg, the Italian project was effectively over. The hoped-for Italian court appointment had not materialized; the major Italian cities had been visited; Wolfgang was seventeen and the prodigy phase was concluding.

#### What the prodigy years produced

By age seventeen, Mozart had produced approximately 250 Köchel-numbered works. The categories: about twenty-five symphonies (K. 16 through approximately K. 200), the early Italian-tour operas (_Mitridate_ , _Ascanio in Alba_ , _Lucio Silla_ , the early sacred works including the Mass in C minor K. 139 "Waisenhausmesse" composed at age twelve in 1768 for the consecration of the Waisenhauskirche in Vienna), the early piano sonatas and violin sonatas, the early divertimentos and serenades, the early concert arias for various singers Mozart met on tour, the early sacred motets including K. 165.

Most of this catalog is not standard repertoire today. The early symphonies are recorded by completist labels (the Christopher Hogwood Academy of Ancient Music cycle, the Trevor Pinnock English Concert cycle, the Ton Koopman Amsterdam Baroque cycle) but are programmed in concert only rarely. The early operas have specialist advocates (René Jacobs has championed them in recent decades) but are not opera-house staples. The early sacred works are studied more than performed.

What is standard repertoire from the prodigy period: K. 165 (the "Alleluia" finale especially), the Symphony K. 16 (occasionally programmed as the curiosity of an eight-year-old's first symphony), the Symphony K. 183 (Chapter 2, the dramatic turn of late 1773), and isolated arias from the Italian-tour operas. The bulk of the prodigy output is biographical interest rather than active concert life.

The prodigy years matter less for what they produced in repertoire than for what they did to Mozart's compositional development. By age seventeen he had heard, in person and in the original orchestral colors, the major styles of European music of his time — French opera in Paris, English keyboard and symphonic music in London, German _galant_ style in Mannheim and Munich, Italian opera in Milan and Rome and Naples, Italian sacred polyphony in Rome and Bologna. His compositional toolkit included most of the European mainstream. When he turned seriously to a single project — the K. 183 symphony of October 1773, the _Idomeneo_ opera of 1781 — the toolkit was ready.

#### The Leopold-Wolfgang relationship in this period

The prodigy years are the warmest phase of the Leopold-Wolfgang relationship. Leopold is teacher, manager, traveling companion, business agent, and primary letter-writer (Wolfgang adds his own postscripts to many of the family letters from this period, but the principal voice is Leopold's). The letters home to Anna Maria and Nannerl in Salzburg — written from Vienna, from Paris, from London, from the Italian cities — are the principal primary source for the prodigy years and are extensively quoted in Solomon and in Gutman.

The dynamic that will rupture the relationship a decade later (the 1781 Vienna break, Chapter 5) is not yet present. The young Wolfgang is content to be guided; Leopold's investment in the prodigy project is being rewarded by the European reception. The conflicts will start in the late 1770s — the Paris-and-Mannheim journey of 1777-78 (Chapter 4), where Wolfgang first traveled without Leopold and where Anna Maria died, is the inflection point. In 1773, when K. 165 was composed, the family is still functioning as Leopold designed it.

#### Recording history of K. 165

K. 165 has been recorded extensively because the "Alleluia" finale is a recital staple. The standard readings:

Cecilia Bartoli's reading with the Vienna Chamber Orchestra under György Fischer (Decca, 1991) is widely held as the standard contemporary modern-instrument reading. Bartoli's coloratura technique handles the elaborate ornamentation comfortably, and the Vienna Chamber Orchestra provides supple accompaniment.

Emma Kirkby's reading with the Academy of Ancient Music under Christopher Hogwood (L'Oiseau-Lyre / Decca, 1980) is the standard period-instrument reading. Kirkby's voice is lighter and the period-instrument accompaniment is leaner; the reading aims at the original sound-world.

Kiri Te Kanawa with the English Chamber Orchestra under Colin Davis (Philips, 1981) is the warmer, more lyric reading from the same era. Te Kanawa's voice has more vibrato and a heavier tone than Kirkby's; the reading is closer to the standard concert-recital posture.

Edita Gruberová with the Wiener Symphoniker under Leopold Hager (Teldec, 1986) is the coloratura-soprano showcase reading; Gruberová's technical command is extraordinary.

Numerous other readings exist. The work is in catalog from nearly every major soprano of the past sixty years.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The Allegri _Miserere_ memorization at fourteen — what makes it a believable feat for a child of that training, and what is the difference between the version Leopold's letter actually attests (one hearing, one corrective second hearing) and the embroidered popular version (single hearing, no corrective)? Why does the embroidered version persist in popular telling?"_
* _"K. 165 is genuinely mature vocal writing at seventeen. What specifically distinguishes mature vocal writing from prodigy showpiece? Pick a specific passage in the 'Alleluia' or in the Andante and describe what makes it work."_
* _"Mozart wrote K. 165 for the castrato Venanzio Rauzzini in January 1773. A modern soprano voice approximates but does not exactly match the castrato instrument. What does the modern listener gain and lose in hearing K. 165 sung by a soprano rather than its original voice?"_
* _"The prodigy years produced about 250 Köchel works but only a handful are in standard repertoire today (K. 165 most of all). Why? What makes a work survive into the standard concert life of two centuries later, and why do most of the prodigy works not survive that test?"_
* _"Compare the Bartoli (1991) modern-instrument reading with the Kirkby/Hogwood (1980) period-instrument reading. Pick a specific moment in the 'Alleluia' and describe what each performance is emphasizing. Is the difference in vocal weight, in tempo, in orchestral color, or in something harder to name?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find Bartoli / Fischer on Decca (1991) or Kirkby / Hogwood on L'Oiseau-Lyre (1980). Listen to the full motet a second time and attend to three concrete moments:

  1. **The opening of "_Exsultate, jubilate_ " (about the first thirty seconds).** Notice how Mozart writes the vocal entry so that the first phrase establishes both the key (F major) and the buoyant character. The soprano enters on a confident upward leap and the melisma on "jubilate" begins almost immediately.

  2. **The Andante middle movement "_Tu virginum corona_."** Listen specifically for the orchestral thinning — the strings drop to lighter accompaniment, the winds drop out, the voice carries the line over sparse harmonic support. Notice the length of the sustained vocal phrases and the way the singer's breath structures the music.

  3. **The "Alleluia" trill at the very end.** The final cadence ascends to the high note and resolves with a trill (a rapid alternation between two adjacent notes). The trill is the moment singers are listened for most closely in this piece. Notice both how the singer executes it and how the orchestral accompaniment settles into the final tonic chord beneath it.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : pair K. 165 with the opera _Lucio Silla_ K. 135 (December 1772) — specifically the aria "Pupille amate" sung by the Cecilio character, also composed for Venanzio Rauzzini and only weeks before K. 165. The two works are by the same composer at the same age for the same voice. Hearing them side by side gives you a fuller sense of Mozart's vocal writing for Rauzzini specifically. Cecilia Bartoli has also recorded _Lucio Silla_ arias; the Patricia Petibon recital and the Edita Gruberová readings also include this material.

**Read** : Stanley Sadie, _Mozart: The Early Years, 1756-1781_ (Norton, posthumous 2006). This is the scholarly standard for the prodigy and Salzburg-young-man years. Sadie's treatment of the Italian tours, the _Miserere_ episode, the Bologna Accademia Filarmonica examination, and the third Italian tour during which K. 165 was composed is detailed and reliable. About 650 pages; Chapter 23 of this volume gives the recommended reading order for the full Sadie catalog.

---

## Chapter 4: Salzburg + First Vienna Trip 1773-1781

The eight years between the return from the third Italian tour in March 1773 and the rupture with Prince-Archbishop Hieronymus Colloredo in May 1781 are the years in which Wolfgang Amadeus Mozart turned from a prodigy into a composer. The prodigy years (Chapter 3) had built the toolkit: full European exposure to French, English, German, and Italian styles; full professional vocal and instrumental writing capacity; the early symphonies; the early operas; the early sacred works. The Vienna freelance years (Chapter 5 onward) would bring the great Vienna piano concertos, the Da Ponte operas, the late symphonies, the chamber peak. The 1773-1781 Salzburg years are the transition — the years in which Mozart took the toolkit and started using it to make works that asked harder questions than the early ones had asked.

This chapter anchors on two of those harder-question works. The first is the **Piano Concerto No. 9 in E♭ "Jeunehomme" K. 271** (January 1777), composed in Salzburg when Mozart was twenty-one. The second is the **Sinfonia Concertante for Violin and Viola in E♭ K. 364** (1779), the homecoming work from the disastrous 1777-78 Paris-and-Mannheim journey during which Anna Maria Mozart died. The pair frame the two halves of the chapter: the breakthrough of K. 271 in early 1777, the journey and its loss across late 1777 and 1778, and the homecoming maturity of K. 364 in 1779.

### 4.1 Before You Listen — The Two Works in Context

#### Hook Anecdote (K. 271)

January 1777. Mozart is two days short of his twenty-first birthday. A French keyboard virtuoso named Victoire Jenamy, the daughter of the Paris-based ballet master and choreographer Jean-Georges Noverre, is passing through Salzburg. (Jean-Georges Noverre was an old friend of the Mozart family from Leopold Mozart's Paris contacts during the 1763-64 Grand Tour; Victoire Jenamy was at this point in her early thirties and an accomplished pianist.) Mozart writes her a piano concerto — the Concerto No. 9 in E♭, K. 271, completed January 1777 — for her to play in Salzburg.

For more than two centuries the dedicatee was misidentified as a "Mlle Jeunehomme" — a name no one could place historically because she did not exist under that name. The Salzburg court records, the Leopold Mozart correspondence, the contemporary references — all of them were searched, and "Mlle Jeunehomme" produced no biographical traces. It was the Austrian music scholar Michael Lorenz who, in research published in 2003-2005, identified the dedicatee as Victoire Jenamy through the Noverre family connection. "Jeunehomme" was a long-standing misreading or mishearing of "Jenamy." Modern programming and scholarship use the correct attribution; the traditional nickname "Jeunehomme" remains attached to the work in popular usage because two centuries of program-note convention is hard to displace.

Charles Rosen, writing in _The Classical Style_ before the Lorenz correction, called K. 271 "the first of Mozart's masterpieces." The judgment has held. The work is the place where Mozart's concerto writing turned from the brilliant entertainment of the early concertos (K. 175, K. 238, K. 246) into something structurally and emotionally ambitious.

#### Hook Anecdote (K. 364)

July 3, 1778. Paris. Mozart, aged twenty-two, is in the city with his mother Anna Maria Mozart. The Paris-and-Mannheim journey is in its eleventh month.
