The trip had been Leopold's strategic project: Wolfgang, in his early twenties, would travel to the major German musical center (Mannheim, then arguably the leading orchestral establishment in Europe) and to Paris, would meet patrons, would secure a court appointment, would launch the career-after-Salzburg that Leopold's prodigy investment had been building toward. Leopold remained in Salzburg under Colloredo's service; Anna Maria traveled with Wolfgang as chaperone and companion.

The journey had not gone as planned. The Mannheim residence (October 1777 to March 1778) had produced no court appointment despite Mozart's evident impression on the Mannheim musicians. The Paris residence (from March 1778) had produced the Symphony No. 31 K. 297 "Paris" and some keyboard sonatas, but no patronage breakthrough. Anna Maria had been ill since June. On the night of July 3, 1778, she died.

Mozart was alone in the Paris lodging when she died. He wrote two letters that night — one to Leopold and one to the family friend Abbé Joseph Bullinger in Salzburg. The letter to Leopold did not tell him directly that Anna Maria had died; it said she was very ill. The letter to Bullinger asked Bullinger to prepare Leopold for the news. The deferred-disclosure was a kindness — Mozart was twenty-two, far from home, and breaking the news to his father required preparation Mozart could not provide directly across the postal distance. The two letters survive and are extensively quoted in Solomon and in Robert Spaethling's letters edition.

Mozart returned to Salzburg in January 1779. The Sinfonia Concertante K. 364 was composed there later that year. The slow movement, an Andante in C minor (the parallel minor of the E♭ home key), is conventionally heard as bearing the emotional weight of the journey just completed; §4.5 takes up the biographical reading at length.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen (K. 271)

  1. **The opening (about the first minute).** The full orchestra plays a confident E♭ major flourish, and then, almost unprecedentedly for a concerto of this period, the piano enters in the second measure with a brief responding phrase before the orchestra finishes its statement. The structural innovation is detailed in §4.2.

  2. **The C-minor slow movement.** A turn to the parallel minor key for the slow movement is unusual in a major-key concerto. The mood is more intense, more dramatic, more aria-like. Listen for the way Mozart writes the piano as if it were a singer — long sustained phrases, vocal-style ornamentation.

  3. **The finale.** A rondo (a form in which a recurring main theme alternates with contrasting episodes), but with a Menuetto inserted as one of the episodes — another structural unconventionality. The mood lifts back to E♭ major and to confidence; the wind writing in the Menuetto episode is particularly Mozart-distinctive.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen (K. 364)

  1. **The opening orchestral introduction (about the first two minutes).** Mozart gives the orchestra a full sonata-form exposition before the soloists enter, but the orchestral writing is unusually substantial — the wind writing especially carries weight, with the oboes and horns trading material across the texture. Listen for the moment around the one-minute mark where the strings drop out and the winds carry a quiet, sustained chord.

  2. **The dual-soloist dialogue.** The violin and viola enter together and from that point function as a single composite voice. Listen for how Mozart distributes the material between them — the violin takes the upper register, the viola the lower, but the parts are equal in interest. The viola is not a secondary instrument here; in this concerto it is the violin's equal partner.

  3. **The C-minor Andante (the second movement).** The emotional center of the work. Listen for the way the orchestra darkens at the entrance — flutes drop out, the strings move into a darker registration — and for the way the two soloists carry an extended dialogue across the movement. The C-minor mode persists almost throughout; brief major-mode breath is followed by return to the minor.

#### One-sentence background (K. 271 and K. 364)

**K. 271** : Salzburg, January 1777; about thirty-two minutes across three movements. **Primary Recording** : Mitsuko Uchida with the English Chamber Orchestra under Jeffrey Tate (Philips, 1986). Alternatives: Alfred Brendel / Neville Marriner (Philips, 1976), warmer older modern-instrument reading; Robert Levin / Christopher Hogwood (L'Oiseau-Lyre / Decca, 1996), period-instrument.

**K. 364** : Salzburg, summer 1779 (no securely dated autograph); about thirty minutes across three movements. **Primary Recording** : Anne-Sophie Mutter and Bruno Giuranna with the Philharmonia Orchestra under Riccardo Muti (EMI, 1983). Alternatives: Itzhak Perlman / Pinchas Zukerman / Zubin Mehta (DG, 1982); Christian Tetzlaff / Tabea Zimmermann / Paavo Järvi (Virgin / Erato, 2005).

#### Search Hints

EN (K. 271): "Mozart Piano Concerto 9 K 271 Uchida Tate" or "Mozart Jeunehomme Concerto Brendel Marriner" EN (K. 364): "Mozart Sinfonia Concertante K 364 Mutter Giuranna Muti" or "Mozart K 364 Perlman Zukerman Mehta" ZH (K. 271): "莫扎特 第9号钢琴协奏曲 K271 内田光子 泰特" 或 "莫扎特 让纳米协奏曲 K271 布伦德尔" ZH (K. 364): "莫扎特 交响协奏曲 K364 慕特 朱拉纳 穆蒂" 或 "莫扎特 K364 帕尔曼 祖克曼" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

### 4.2 The Listening Window — K. 271

Timestamps below refer to the Uchida / Tate Primary Recording.

**0:00-0:10 — The opening.** Orchestra plays the opening E♭ major flourish; piano answers within two seconds. Mozart sets up an exchange — orchestra states, piano responds, orchestra continues — instead of the standard concerto opening in which the orchestra plays a complete exposition (two to three minutes) before the soloist enters. After the piano's brief response, the orchestra carries on with the full exposition while the piano sits silent. The "interjection-then-silence-then-extended-orchestral-exposition" pattern is what makes K. 271's opening unique.

**The piano's full entry.** When the piano enters fully for the second time, the soloistic material begins. The writing across the first movement is virtuosic but conversational with the orchestra — the dialogue model Rosen identifies as a Mozart concerto signature.

**The cadenza near the end of the first movement.** A cadenza is a passage near the end of a concerto movement in which the soloist plays alone, traditionally improvising on the movement's main themes; Mozart's own cadenzas for K. 271 survive and are normally played. Mitsuko Uchida plays the Mozart cadenza on this recording.

**The C-minor Andante (the second movement).** The mood shifts immediately into a darker register. The orchestra establishes the C-minor harmony; the piano enters singing a long, lyric line that wanders chromatically across the harmonic space. Listen for the piano writing's vocal quality — the phrasing breathes the way a singer would breathe. About midway through, the orchestra has a brief solo passage in which the strings play _con sordino_ (with mutes), giving an even quieter, more intimate texture. The piano returns over this hushed accompaniment.

**The rondo finale.** Lively tempo, E♭ major confidence. The main rondo theme is bouncy and clearly stated by the piano. The recurring rondo theme alternates with contrasting episodes. The most-discussed structural feature is the Menuetto episode about midway through — Mozart inserts a complete Menuetto (a slower, courtly triple-meter dance) into the middle of the rondo, then returns to the fast rondo material afterward. The unconventionality keeps the form fresh; listen for the moment the tempo drops and the Menuetto begins. The wind writing in this episode is particularly Mozart-distinctive — the oboes carry the principal melodic line.

### 4.3 The Listening Window — K. 364

Timestamps below refer to the Mutter / Giuranna / Muti Primary Recording.

**0:00-2:00 — The orchestral exposition (first movement).** Substantial — Mozart gives the orchestra a full sonata-form statement before the soloists enter. The wind writing is unusually weighted: listen for the moment about one minute in when the strings drop to soft accompaniment and the winds carry a sustained chord. The orchestral material establishes the dual character of the work — confident E♭ major outer mood, but with darker hints that will return in the slow movement.

**The soloists' entry.** Violin and viola enter together. The traditional concerto form would have them enter sequentially (violin first as the higher and more conventionally soloistic instrument); Mozart enters them together as equal partners. This is the work's structural signature — the two instruments are not soloist and accompanist, they are two soloists in dialogue.

**The development section.** Mozart takes the opening material into more remote keys before returning to E♭ for the recapitulation. The dialogue between the two soloists intensifies; listen for the moments when one soloist completes a phrase the other has started.

**The C-minor Andante (the second movement, the emotional center).** The mood-change is immediate. The orchestra darkens — flutes drop out, the strings move into a lower registration. The first soloist (the violin) enters with a long aria-like phrase; the viola answers. The Andante is essentially a vocal duet for two instrumental voices, and the soloists trade and overlap material for the rest of the movement. Listen specifically for the way Mozart writes the viola's part with the same expressive weight as the violin's — the viola, normally a supporting instrument in concertos and chamber works, here carries half the emotional weight.

The slow movement is roughly ten minutes — the largest single span of sustained minor-mode music in the chamber-concerto repertoire up to that date, and the most directly emotional thing Mozart had yet written. (The biographical reading of K. 364 as the homecoming work, taken up in §4.5, asserts itself most strongly here.)

**The finale (Rondo, Presto, E♭ major).** The mood lifts back to the home key and confidence. The rondo theme is bouncy and brief; the two soloists trade it back and forth across the orchestra. The movement closes the work with restoration of the opening confidence, but the listener carries the C-minor Andante through the finale — once heard, the slow movement is the work's center, and the finale functions as the recovery rather than the destination.

### 4.4 What's Happening in the Music

#### Why K. 271 is the breakthrough

Rosen's claim has held because of structural specifics that can be named. Three features mark K. 271 as the turning point.

**First, the piano's early entry.** The standard concerto opening of 1777 was the orchestral exposition followed by the soloist's first entry — a procedure inherited from Vivaldi through J.C. Bach and codified in dozens of concertos by C.P.E. Bach, Mozart's earlier teachers, and Mozart himself in his first eight piano concertos. K. 271 breaks the procedure on the second measure. The piano's interjection is brief (about three seconds of music), but its placement reorganizes the listener's expectation. From that opening, K. 271 announces that it will not be a standard concerto.

**Second, the slow movement's emotional ambition.** The C-minor Andante is not merely a slow movement — it is an aria for piano, with the piano writing modeled on operatic vocal writing of the period. The chromatic lines, the long sustained phrases, the way the piano "breathes" — all of it is borrowed from Mozart's operatic vocabulary. K. 271's slow movement is a piano singing. The earlier concerto slow movements had been more conventionally instrumental.

**Third, the finale's structural unconventionality.** The Menuetto inserted into the rondo is not a small flourish — it is a complete dance movement embedded in the larger rondo structure. The procedure is unprecedented in Mozart's concertos to this point and rare in the concerto literature generally. Mozart at twenty-one has the structural confidence to interrupt one form with another and then resume.

The three features arrive together. The earlier eight concertos had moments — a clever orchestration here, a brief modulation there — but K. 271 is the first work in which Mozart's concerto thinking operates at full mature speed across all three movements.

#### The Sinfonia Concertante genre

A _sinfonia concertante_ is a hybrid form combining concerto and symphony — two or more soloists with orchestra, conceived more symphonically than the standard concerto. The form was a French-Mannheim innovation of the 1760s-70s. Mozart encountered it during the Paris-and-Mannheim journey (the K. 297b for winds, attributed to him but with disputed authorship, dates from the Paris months) and brought it home to Salzburg as a form he wanted to work in. K. 364 is the surviving major Mozart sinfonia concertante. The choice of violin and viola as the two soloists is an artistic statement — Mozart could have written for violin and cello (the more common pairing) or two violins (the brighter pairing). Choosing the viola gives the work a darker overall color and lets the slow movement reach the depth it reaches. The viola is also Mozart's own instrument — he played it in the Haydn-Mozart quartet evenings of 1784-85.

#### The slow-movement-as-work's-heart pattern

A second feature in Thread B begins here: the **slow-movement-as-work's-heart pattern**. Mozart's mature works frequently place their emotional center in the slow movement rather than in the dramatic outer movements that the Beethoven tradition would later make the structural focus. The pattern starts here with K. 271's and K. 364's C-minor Andantes and continues across the catalog: the K. 488 Adagio, the K. 595 B-flat major Larghetto, the K. 581 D major Larghetto, the K. 622 slow movement.

#### The Mozart wind-writing signature

Listen to the orchestral exposition of K. 364 and to the K. 271 rondo's Menuetto episode and you will hear what Rosen and Stanley Sadie both identify as the **Mozart wind-writing signature** : oboes, clarinets (when available), bassoons, and horns carry melodic material, answer the soloists in dialogue, and take solo turns within the orchestral tutti rather than playing a background-supporting role. The K. 364 orchestral exposition's wind passages and the K. 271 rondo Menuetto's oboe writing are early-mature instances; the feature recurs in the late piano concertos (K. 595), the late symphonies (especially K. 543), and the chamber works with clarinet (K. 581, K. 622).

### 4.5 The Stories

#### Salzburg under Colloredo, 1773-1781

Prince-Archbishop Hieronymus Colloredo took office as Archbishop of Salzburg in 1772. The Mozart-Colloredo relationship across the next nine years is the principal biographical fact of the Salzburg-young-man period. Colloredo was an Enlightenment reformer — he reduced the size of the court music establishment, restricted the elaborateness of liturgical music, cut what he considered to be unnecessary ceremonial expenditure. For a Salzburg court musician whose father had spent his career building family prosperity around the court music establishment, Colloredo's reforms were professional contraction.

Mozart's titles across the period: court Konzertmeister (the leading violinist of the court orchestra) from 1772; court organist from 1779; throughout, a salaried musician whose duties included regular sacred works for the Salzburg cathedral liturgy. The salary was modest, the duties unceasing, and the career ceiling at Salzburg low. The 1777-78 Paris-and-Mannheim journey was the family's strategic attempt to find a post outside Salzburg; its failure is part of why Mozart's professional frustration accumulated through the late 1770s and finally produced the 1781 rupture.

#### The 1777-78 Paris-and-Mannheim journey

Mozart and Anna Maria left Salzburg on September 23, 1777. The plan was Mannheim first (the Mannheim court orchestra was the leading European orchestral establishment, and the court had recently acquired the Elector Palatine Karl Theodor whose musical patronage was substantial), then Paris (where Leopold had Mozart's contacts from the 1763-64 Grand Tour and where the _Concert Spirituel_ concert series provided commission and performance opportunities).

**Mannheim (October 30, 1777 - March 14, 1778).** Mozart impressed the Mannheim musicians — Karl Stamitz, Christian Cannabich (the concertmaster, who became a close friend), Franz Xaver Richter — and met the Weber family, three musical daughters among them: the soprano Aloysia (with whom Mozart fell in love), her older sister Josepha, and the younger Constanze (who would in 1782 become Mozart's wife). Mozart absorbed the Mannheim orchestral style firsthand, with its famous "rocket" crescendos and precision wind writing, but no court appointment was forthcoming.

**The Aloysia Weber episode.** Mozart wanted to take Aloysia to Italy to launch her opera career; Leopold, alarmed, ordered Wolfgang to Paris immediately. The attachment dissolved on Wolfgang's return through Munich in late 1778. (Aloysia later married the actor Joseph Lange and became Mozart's sister-in-law in 1782.) The episode is the first major Leopold-Wolfgang disagreement and an early sign of the rupture to come in 1781.

**Paris (March 23 - September 26, 1778).** Mozart and Anna Maria moved to Paris. The Paris months produced the Symphony No. 31 K. 297 "Paris" (June 1778, commissioned by the _Concert Spirituel_ and performed in Paris on June 18, 1778) and several keyboard sonatas (K. 310 A minor among them — we return to K. 310 in Chapter 15) and the disputed Sinfonia Concertante for Winds K. 297b. The court appointment did not materialize. Anna Maria fell ill in June 1778. She died on July 3, 1778.

**The return.** Mozart spent the rest of summer and autumn 1778 in Paris arranging Anna Maria's affairs; in October he started homeward. He lingered in Strasbourg, Mannheim again, and Munich (where he saw Aloysia and was rebuffed) before reaching Salzburg in mid-January 1779.

#### K. 364 as homecoming work

The Sinfonia Concertante K. 364 was composed in Salzburg in 1779, in the months after Mozart's return (no securely dated autograph survives). Mozart left no letter or marginal note tying the C-minor Andante to Anna Maria's death; the biographical reading is plausible and consistent with his pattern of writing works of unusual emotional weight after significant personal events, but it is interpretation rather than fact. Solomon's treatment is the standard cautious one.

Hear K. 364 on its own terms first — the slow movement is one of Mozart's emotional peaks regardless of whether the biographical reading holds. Then, if you want to bring the biography into the listening, the C-minor Andante is the place to hear what mourning sounded like to a twenty-three-year-old composer ten months after his mother's death in a foreign city.

#### K. 271 and the structural innovation question

Why did Mozart in January 1777 — six months before the Paris-and-Mannheim journey, three years before _Idomeneo_ , four years before the Vienna break — write a concerto with the structural confidence of K. 271? We do not know; Leopold's correspondence records no compositional reflection from Wolfgang about why K. 271 broke the conventions it broke. The Victoire Jenamy connection gives a possible occasion but not a structural cause.

What can be said: by January 1777 Mozart had completed eight piano concertos and five violin concertos (K. 207, K. 211, K. 216, K. 218, K. 219), and had absorbed the European concerto tradition through Vivaldi, J.C. Bach (the London friendship of 1764-65), and C.P.E. Bach. The conventions were thoroughly known to him. K. 271 reads like the work of a composer who had decided that what convention required was not what the concerto could be — the breaking of the opening exposition, the parallel-minor slow movement, the embedded Menuetto are not exploratory choices but confident structural decisions. The conviction will carry across all twenty-seven of his subsequent piano concertos.

#### The Mozart-Leopold rupture, in seed form

The Paris-and-Mannheim journey was the first major friction point in the Leopold-Wolfgang relationship. The Aloysia Weber episode produced Leopold's first sustained letters of disapproval; the Paris financial difficulties produced second-guessing about Wolfgang's career management; Anna Maria's death produced a wound the family could not close. The rupture that will come in 1781 (Chapter 5) is not yet fully present, but the seeds are visible in the 1777-78 correspondence — extensively documented in Solomon.

#### Recording history brief

Beyond the Primary Recordings named in §4.1, the K. 271 reference cycles also include Daniel Barenboim, Murray Perahia (English Chamber Orchestra, Sony 1975-84), and Maria João Pires; for K. 364, older long-standing references include Yehudi Menuhin / Rudolf Barshai and Arthur Grumiaux / Arrigo Pelliccia (Concertgebouw, Philips 1960s).

### Conversation Seeds

* _"K. 271's piano entry in the second measure — what does Mozart gain structurally and rhetorically by breaking the orchestral-exposition convention right at the opening? Why was this such a noticeable departure in 1777?"_
* _"The slow movement of K. 364 is conventionally heard as carrying the weight of Anna Maria Mozart's death in Paris ten months earlier. Mozart left no annotation connecting the movement to that biographical event. How do you decide as a listener whether to bring the biography into the listening, and what does the music gain or lose from that decision?"_
* _"Why did Mozart choose violin and viola for the K. 364 dual-soloist pairing rather than violin and cello (the more common chamber pairing) or two violins? What does the viola contribute to the work's color that the other choices would not have?"_
* _"The 1777-78 Paris-and-Mannheim journey failed at its strategic goal (no court appointment was secured) and ended in Anna Maria's death. But the journey produced major works (the Paris Symphony K. 297, the A-minor Piano Sonata K. 310, and indirectly the homecoming K. 364). How do you weigh a journey that fails biographically but succeeds creatively?"_
* _"Charles Rosen called K. 271 'the first great Mozart concerto.' If you accept the judgment, what specifically is Rosen pointing at? If you resist the judgment, which earlier Mozart concerto would you nominate instead and why?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find Uchida / Tate (K. 271) and Mutter / Giuranna / Muti (K. 364). Listen to both works a second time and attend to four concrete moments:

  1. **K. 271 opening (first thirty seconds).** Confirm: the orchestra plays a confident E♭ flourish, the piano enters with a brief responding phrase in the second measure, the orchestra continues. The exchange-pattern is the work's structural signature.

  2. **K. 271 C-minor Andante.** Listen for the piano's vocal-style writing — the long phrases, the chromatic descents, the moments where the piano "breathes." Notice the _con sordino_ string passage (about midway through the movement) where the orchestra plays with mutes for an even quieter accompaniment.

  3. **K. 271 finale's Menuetto episode.** The tempo drops from the rondo's lively pace into the slower triple-meter Menuetto. Listen for the oboe writing in the Menuetto — the Mozart wind-writing signature in early-mature form.

  4. **K. 364 C-minor Andante.** Listen specifically for the way the viola is given equal expressive weight with the violin. The slow movement is essentially a vocal duet for two instrumental voices. Notice the moment around the middle of the movement where one soloist completes a phrase the other has started — the conversational chamber-style dialogue is the work's center.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : pair K. 364 with the Symphony No. 31 K. 297 "Paris" (June 1778), composed for the Paris _Concert Spirituel_ during the same journey. K. 297 is the brighter, more confident product of the Paris months — composed before Anna Maria's death, written to impress a Paris audience. Hearing K. 297 alongside K. 364 gives you both halves of the journey: the bright surface of the Paris court appointment ambition (K. 297) and the homecoming weight of what the journey actually produced (K. 364). The Karl Böhm / Berlin Philharmonic and the Trevor Pinnock / English Concert recordings of K. 297 are both standard.

**Read** : Maynard Solomon, _Mozart: A Life_ (HarperCollins, 1995). The chapters on the 1777-78 journey, Anna Maria's death, and the Salzburg homecoming are the standard secondary source for understanding what K. 364 carries.

---

## Chapter 5: The 1781 Vienna Break + Marriage to Constanze 1782

In the spring and summer of 1781, Mozart's life reorganized itself. He moved from Salzburg to Vienna; he broke with his employer of nine years, Prince-Archbishop Hieronymus Colloredo; he set up as a freelance composer in a city that had no precedent for a freelance composer of his ambition; and within fourteen months he married Constanze Weber over Leopold Mozart's strong opposition. Within five years (by the spring of 1786), the Vienna freelance career would have produced the Haydn Quartets, the great Vienna piano concertos K. 466 and K. 491 and K. 488, and the first Da Ponte opera _Le nozze di Figaro_. The 1781 break is the inflection point of Mozart's adult life.

This chapter anchors on the opera that came directly out of the break — _Die Entführung aus dem Serail_ K. 384 (premiered Vienna, July 16, 1782), which Mozart was actively composing through the months of the Colloredo rupture and the early Vienna freelance establishment. Within _Entführung_ we anchor on a single aria: "Martern aller Arten," the great showpiece aria for the soprano Konstanze (the character, not Mozart's wife, though the name-pairing was probably not lost on Mozart). The aria is about ten minutes long, is one of the most technically demanding arias in the soprano repertoire, and is the place in _Entführung_ where Mozart's mature vocal writing first reaches the level we will hear in the Da Ponte operas.

### 5.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

May 9, 1781. The Vienna residence of Count Karl Joseph Felix Arco, chamberlain to Prince-Archbishop Hieronymus Colloredo. Mozart has come to deliver, for the second or third time, his formal request for release from Colloredo's service. The first attempts had been rebuffed; Colloredo refused to release him. On this day the conversation between Mozart and Count Arco escalated. According to Mozart's own letter to Leopold (May 9 and 12, 1781), Count Arco ended the meeting by kicking Mozart literally out of the room — a kick on the backside as Mozart was leaving.

The letter is Mozart's own first-person account, and Solomon, Sadie, Eisen, and Gutman all treat it as essentially reliable. The kick is a small fact and is partly a face-saving description (whether Count Arco's gesture was a contemptuous physical kick or a metaphorical one varies in the retellings), but the formal break with Colloredo's service is the underlying historical reality, and the letter's tone, outraged, exhilarated, and free, is the principal voice of the new Mozart. He is twenty-five years old. He has been a Salzburg court musician for nine years. He has just left it.

Mozart took a room in Vienna with the Weber family — Cäcilia Weber (the mother) and her three musical daughters Aloysia (now married to the actor Joseph Lange), Josepha, and Constanze. The Webers had moved from Mannheim to Vienna in 1779. The young Mozart who had been infatuated with Aloysia three years earlier (Chapter 4's Mannheim episode) was now living in the same house as her younger sister Constanze. Within fourteen months, Mozart and Constanze would marry.

The freelance career began immediately. By summer 1781, Mozart was giving piano lessons, performing at private concerts, and composing on commission. The opera commission for _Die Entführung aus dem Serail_ arrived in late summer 1781 from the Vienna National-Singspiel theater company under Joseph II's patronage; Mozart was composing the opera through the autumn 1781 and spring 1782 in the same months as his relationship with Constanze was deepening and as Leopold's letters from Salzburg were registering increasing disapproval. The opera premiered on July 16, 1782; the wedding to Constanze followed on August 4, 1782, in St. Stephen's Cathedral in Vienna. Leopold's blessing arrived only after the wedding had taken place.

This chapter is set in those fourteen months, May 1781 to August 1782, in which Mozart restructured his life and produced his first Vienna-period mature work.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **The orchestral introduction (about the first ninety seconds).** Before Konstanze sings a note, the orchestra plays an unusually substantial introduction featuring four concertante soloists — flute, oboe, violin, and cello — who weave around each other in dialogue. This is the longest orchestral introduction Mozart had yet written for any aria, and it announces that "Martern aller Arten" is not a standard rage aria but something structurally more ambitious.

  2. **The vocal range.** Konstanze's part climbs into the upper soprano register repeatedly. The high notes are placed at moments of textual emphasis ("Martern aller Arten" = "tortures of every kind"; the text is the captive Konstanze's defiant declaration that she will withstand any torture rather than surrender). Listen for how Mozart matches the high notes to the rhetorical climaxes of the German text.

  3. **The four-soloist concertante texture.** The flute, oboe, violin, and cello function as a chamber-music quartet within the aria, with each instrument given solo material that interacts with the soprano line. Listen for how the four instruments trade material across the aria — the texture is closer to a concerto than to a standard aria orchestration.

#### One-sentence background

Composed in Vienna, autumn 1781 through spring 1782 (premiered July 16, 1782); the aria "Martern aller Arten" runs about ten minutes within the larger opera; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Edita Gruberová as Konstanze with the Bayerischer Rundfunk Orchestra under Karl Böhm (Deutsche Grammophon, 1980), part of Böhm's complete _Entführung_ widely held as the modern reference recording. (Diana Damrau with the Akademie für Alte Musik Berlin under René Jacobs on Harmonia Mundi, 2015, is the more recent period-instrument alternative; Christine Schäfer with the Mahler Chamber Orchestra under Daniel Harding on Virgin Classics, 2002, is an additional alternative with strong reviews.)

#### Search Hints

EN: "Mozart Entführung K 384 Gruberová Böhm" or "Mozart Martern aller Arten Damrau Jacobs" ZH: "莫扎特 后宫诱逃 K384 格鲁贝罗娃 贝姆" 或 "莫扎特 各种各样的酷刑 达姆劳 雅各布斯" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

### 5.2 The Listening Window

Timestamps below refer to the Gruberová / Böhm Primary Recording. The aria sits in Act II of _Entführung_ ; if you are listening to a complete recording the aria's start is the entry of the four-instrument concertante introduction; in excerpt recordings the aria starts at the orchestral first chord.

**0:00-0:20 — The opening orchestral chord and the start of the concertante introduction.** Full orchestra plays a confident opening chord; then the four solo instruments enter — flute, oboe, violin, cello — and begin the concertante exchange. The four-instrument texture is established immediately.

**The continuation of the orchestral introduction.** The four soloists trade material across the orchestral background. The flute carries the highest line, the oboe the middle, the violin a soloistic line, and the cello provides both bass support and occasional melodic interjections. The pattern of dialogue among the four instruments will continue through the aria, with the soprano joining as the fifth voice.

**Konstanze's first vocal entry.** The soprano enters with the opening words "Martern aller Arten" ("Tortures of every kind"). The vocal line sits in the middle register at first and climbs as the text intensifies. Listen for how Mozart paces the climb — the high notes are not constant but arrive at specific rhetorical moments.

**The middle section of the aria.** Konstanze's text shifts to defiance: she will not surrender, she will withstand any pain rather than betray her beloved Belmonte. The vocal writing intensifies — longer melismas, higher tessitura, more rhythmic urgency. The four soloists continue trading material across the orchestral texture; listen for the moments where the flute or oboe answers a phrase the soprano has just sung.

**The climax.** The aria builds to a final defiant declaration. The soprano reaches her highest sustained notes; the orchestra and the four soloists provide the supporting climax; the harmonic resolution arrives only at the final cadence.

**The aria's closing.** The orchestral writing recedes; the soprano completes her defiant statement; the four soloists return briefly for a closing exchange and the orchestra closes the aria.

The aria is one of the most technically demanding in the soprano repertoire. Sopranos who can sing it credibly — with the vocal range, the breath capacity for the long melismas, the dramatic command of the German text, and the chamber-musician sense to balance with the four instrumental soloists — are a small group. The Primary Recording's Edita Gruberová is one of the post-1970 sopranos most associated with the role.

### 5.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The _Singspiel_ genre

_Die Entführung aus dem Serail_ is a _Singspiel_ — a German-language opera with spoken dialogue interleaved with sung numbers, distinguished from the Italian _opera seria_ (sung throughout, mythological subjects, formal arias) and the Italian _opera buffa_ (sung throughout, comic contemporary subjects). The _Singspiel_ tradition was vernacular German theater — Vienna theaters, Hamburg theaters, the traveling theatrical companies that performed in German-speaking provincial cities. The genre had relatively modest formal ambitions in the early 1780s; Mozart's _Entführung_ is the work that elevated the _Singspiel_ into a major operatic form.

Joseph II's National-Singspiel project — the Habsburg emperor's attempt in the early 1780s to establish a German-language opera tradition that could compete with the dominant Italian opera at the imperial court — was the commissioning context for _Entführung_. Joseph II wanted Vienna to have its own German opera repertoire; he commissioned major composers to write _Singspiels_ for the National-Singspiel theater company; Mozart's _Entführung_ was the most successful of these commissions and remained in the repertoire well past the National-Singspiel project's dissolution.

The dramatic structure of _Entführung_ : Belmonte, a Spanish nobleman, with his servant Pedrillo, arrives at the Turkish palace of Pasha Selim to rescue his betrothed Konstanze and her maid Blonde, who were captured by pirates and sold to the Pasha's harem. The Pasha desires Konstanze but will not coerce her; he has been kind to her in a paternal way. Belmonte and Pedrillo attempt to rescue Konstanze and Blonde and to bring them away by ship at night. The rescue is detected by the harem overseer Osmin; the four are captured. Pasha Selim, despite discovering that Belmonte's father is his old enemy, grants the captives their freedom rather than take revenge. The opera ends with the four lovers reunited and the Pasha's nobility affirmed.

#### "Martern aller Arten" in dramatic context

The aria is in Act II. Konstanze has been pressed by Pasha Selim to accept his love. She refuses. The Pasha, frustrated, threatens her with torture. "Martern aller Arten" is Konstanze's response — a declaration that she will withstand any torture rather than surrender her fidelity to Belmonte. The aria is the principal vocal-dramatic statement of Konstanze's character and is the moment in _Entführung_ where the work asks the listener to take Konstanze seriously as a heroine rather than as a captive sympathetic figure.

The aria's structural unconventionality — the four-instrument concertante orchestration, the ten-minute length, the unusual cadenza-like passages for the four soloists — is partly Mozart writing for the technical capacities of the soprano Caterina Cavalieri (1755-1801), who created the role of Konstanze in the 1782 premiere. Cavalieri was the lead soprano of the National-Singspiel company and one of Mozart's preferred Viennese sopranos. The vocal demands of "Martern aller Arten" are tailored to her capacities, much as the Rauzzini-tailored writing in _Exsultate, jubilate_ K. 165 (Chapter 3) was tailored to that specific castrato. The aria is also long — about ten minutes — which is unusual for a _Singspiel_ aria of the early 1780s. The length is part of why the aria stops the dramatic action: the music asks the audience to dwell in Konstanze's interior state rather than to follow the plot forward.

#### The Joseph II "too many notes" line

The most-quoted anecdote attached to _Entführung_ is the line attributed to Emperor Joseph II after the premiere: "Too many notes, my dear Mozart." Mozart's reply, in the same tradition, was "Exactly as many notes as are necessary, Your Majesty."

The line is from Franz Xaver Niemetschek's _Leben des K. K. Kapellmeisters Wolfgang Gottlieb Mozart_ — the first published Mozart biography, first edition 1798, expanded second edition 1808. Niemetschek had known the Mozart family (he had befriended Constanze and helped raise the Mozart children after Mozart's death) and the biography is part of the contemporary primary-source record. But the "too many notes" anecdote is told in the biography at second hand — Niemetschek did not witness the exchange, and his source is unattributed. Solomon's treatment treats the anecdote as "semi-legendary" — the kind of biographical detail that may capture something true about Joseph II's reception of _Entführung_ but that cannot be confirmed in the form Niemetschek gives.

The popular "_Amadeus_ " film (Peter Shaffer's 1979 play and Miloš Forman's 1984 film) dramatizes the "too many notes" line as a pivotal scene in Mozart's Vienna reception. The line in the film is part of the broader _Amadeus_ -style invention layered over the historical record. The line itself traces to Niemetschek; the film's dramatization is Shaffer's. Whether Joseph II actually said anything like the phrase is unknowable.

The Niemetschek line, true or embroidered, captures something true about _Entführung_ 's actual scoring. The opera is unusually densely orchestrated for the _Singspiel_ genre of 1782. The four-instrument concertante of "Martern aller Arten" is a representative example. If Joseph II found _Entführung_ "too many notes," he would have meant something like: this is denser writing than the _Singspiel_ tradition normally provided, and it asks more sustained attention from the audience than the genre had previously required. The judgment, even if invented, would have been accurate as a description of what Mozart had done.

#### The Mozart wind-writing signature, continued

Chapter 4 launched the wind-writing thread with the K. 364 orchestral exposition and the K. 271 rondo Menuetto. _Entführung_ continues the thread. The four-instrument concertante of "Martern aller Arten" gives unusual prominence to the flute and oboe; the rest of _Entführung_ is similarly wind-heavy, with the famous "Janissary" orchestration (added percussion — piccolo, triangle, cymbals, bass drum — to evoke a stylized Turkish military band) bringing additional wind colors into the orchestral palette throughout the opera. The orchestration of _Entführung_ is part of what made the work feel novel to Vienna audiences in 1782.

The wind-writing signature will recur in the late piano concertos (Chapter 8), the late symphonies (Chapters 9-10), and most centrally in the chamber works (Chapter 17, Clarinet Quintet K. 581 and Clarinet Concerto K. 622). Chapter 19 §19.2 collects the wind-writing instances.

### 5.4 The Stories

#### The Vienna freelance economics

Mozart's decision to break with Colloredo and set up as a freelance composer in Vienna was, at the time, unprecedented for a composer of his ambition. The standard career path for a leading composer in the 1780s was court appointment (an _Hofkapellmeister_ position at one of the German or Italian courts) or church appointment (the equivalent positions at the major cathedrals). Joseph Haydn at this period held the appointment at the Esterházy court; Antonio Salieri held the imperial Kapellmeister appointment in Vienna; the major German composers nearly all held court positions. A freelance career meant: no salary, no benefits, no court patronage; income depended on subscription concerts (Mozart's own Vienna concert series, in which he would announce a concert, sell subscriptions to a wealthy audience, and pocket the net), opera commissions (paid per opera, irregularly), keyboard teaching (a steady income source through the 1780s), and publication royalties (modest at this period).

The Vienna freelance economics of 1781-86 were good for Mozart. The Vienna economy was prosperous, the aristocratic audience was wealthy and musically engaged, the demand for new keyboard concertos and chamber works was high. Mozart's income across this period was substantial — by some estimates among the highest earned by any Vienna musician — and the family lived well, with multiple residences across the city, a carriage, fine clothing, and a billiard table that Mozart loved.

The economics darkened after 1788. The Austro-Turkish War (1788-91) contracted the Vienna economy; the aristocratic patrons reduced their spending; Mozart's subscription concerts attracted smaller audiences; the begging letters to Michael Puchberg (a Mason friend whom Mozart asked repeatedly for loans across 1788-91) document the financial pressure. The K. 550 G-minor Symphony and the K. 551 Jupiter (Chapter 10) were composed in the summer of 1788 under this financial pressure. By 1791 the situation was easing somewhat — Mozart had taken on additional teaching, the publication income was growing, and the Magic Flute (Chapter 14) was a popular and financial success — but the recovery was cut short by his death in December 1791.

The freelance career, in retrospect, was both a success (the Vienna years produced the great Vienna repertoire) and a precarious financial arrangement that the late-1780s economic contraction nearly broke. Without the 1781 break, the Vienna repertoire would not exist; with the 1781 break, Mozart's family was exposed to economic risk that a court appointment would have insulated them from. The tradeoff is one of the central facts of Mozart's adult life.

#### Constanze Weber and the marriage

Constanze Weber (1762-1842) was nineteen when Mozart moved into the Weber household in May 1781; Mozart was twenty-five. The romance developed across summer and autumn 1781. Leopold Mozart, receiving letters in Salzburg, was alarmed — the Weber family was poor (Cäcilia Weber was widowed; the husband had died in 1779), Constanze had no dowry, and Leopold considered the Weber sisters generally unsuitable matches for Wolfgang. Leopold's letters across late 1781 and early 1782 are extensively quoted in Solomon and Gutman; the tone is concerned, controlling, and increasingly critical.

The marriage took place on August 4, 1782, at St. Stephen's Cathedral. Leopold's formal blessing arrived after the wedding had taken place; the relationship between Wolfgang and Leopold was never fully repaired thereafter. Solomon argues that the marriage was the formal completion of the rupture begun with the Vienna break — Wolfgang's choice of Constanze over Leopold's preferences was the moment Wolfgang stopped being his father's project and became his own person.

The marriage itself, by all available evidence, was warm. Mozart's letters to Constanze from his various travels (the 1789 trip to Berlin, the 1790 trip to Frankfurt for the imperial coronation, the 1791 trip to Prague for the premiere of _La clemenza di Tito_) are affectionate and substantively warm; the letters to her from his deathbed in late November and early December 1791 are the principal primary source for Mozart's domestic life. They had six children, of whom two survived to adulthood (Karl Thomas Mozart, born 1784, lived until 1858; Franz Xaver Wolfgang Mozart, born July 1791, lived until 1844 and was himself a composer and pianist).

Constanze's role in shaping Mozart's posthumous image — through her cooperation with the early biographers Niemetschek and Georg Nikolaus von Nissen (her second husband, whom she married in 1809), through her decisions about which manuscripts to preserve, through her management of Mozart's publication estate after 1791 — is one of the principal topics of late-twentieth-century Mozart scholarship. We return to this in Chapter 20 (the Mozart-Salieri question) and Chapter 22 (Mozart's death and its biographical aftermath).

#### Joseph Haydn enters Mozart's life

The Mozart-Haydn friendship began in 1781-84 Vienna. Joseph Haydn was twenty-four years Mozart's senior and stood as the leading composer in Europe — his Esterházy court appointment had been the foundation of his career since 1761, and his published symphonies and quartets had circulated across Europe through the 1760s and 1770s. By 1781, when Mozart arrived in Vienna, Haydn was already the elder statesman of central European composition.

The two composers met in Vienna in the early 1780s — the exact first meeting is not documentable, but by 1784 the relationship was established. The famous Haydn-Mozart quartet evenings at Mozart's apartment, with Haydn and Karl Ditters von Dittersdorf playing the violin parts, Mozart playing viola, and Johann Baptist Vanhal playing cello, are well-attested. The evenings produced the readings of Mozart's "Haydn" Quartets K. 387, 421, 428, 458, 464, 465 (Chapter 16), composed 1782-1785 and dedicated formally to Haydn in the 1785 publication.

Haydn's response to Mozart — as Haydn would soon famously tell Leopold in February 1785, calling Wolfgang "the greatest composer known to me either in person or by reputation" — is recorded in Leopold's letter to Nannerl Mozart of February 16, 1785 (the full quotation appears in Chapter 16 in the context of the Haydn Quartets). The statement is a foundational passage in Mozart biography. As a contemporaneous record by Leopold's hand it is documentary rather than legendary, and it carries weight because Haydn was the elder, the more-established figure, the composer with nothing to gain by praising a younger rival.

The Mozart-Haydn friendship is the most generous senior-junior composer relationship in eighteenth-century musical biography. It runs in parallel to the more strained Mozart-Salieri relationship (Chapter 20) and is the principal positive professional relationship of Mozart's Vienna years.

#### _Entführung_ 's reception and afterlife

_Entführung_ was a popular success at its July 1782 premiere and remained in the Vienna repertoire through Mozart's lifetime — by 1785 it had been performed across the German-speaking world, in translation in Prague and in Mannheim and in Salzburg, and was Mozart's most-performed opera. The popular success was a major financial and reputational gain for the new Vienna freelance Mozart; the opera commission economics of the 1780s meant a successful opera launched a career.

In the modern repertoire, _Entführung_ sits in the second tier of Mozart's operas — performed regularly but less central than the three Da Ponte operas (Figaro, Don Giovanni, Così) and the Magic Flute. The reasons are partly structural (the spoken-dialogue _Singspiel_ format reads as old-fashioned to modern audiences accustomed to through-composed opera), partly dramatic (the Turkish-captive plot is awkward in twenty-first-century reception), and partly canonical (the Da Ponte operas have the cultural primacy that crowds _Entführung_ into a supporting role). The "Martern aller Arten" aria, however, remains in active concert and recital life — singers who can sing it credibly are still tested by it, and the aria is included in nearly every major soprano recital that touches the Mozart repertoire.

#### Recording history of "Martern aller Arten" and _Entführung_

The Karl Böhm complete _Entführung_ (Bayerischer Rundfunk Orchestra, Deutsche Grammophon, 1980) is the standard modern-instrument complete recording. Edita Gruberová as Konstanze is the central performance; Francisco Araiza as Belmonte and Kurt Moll as Osmin complete the principal cast. The Böhm reading is the chapter's Primary Recording for "Martern aller Arten."

Other standard complete _Entführungs_ : Sir Colin Davis with the Royal Opera House Covent Garden (Philips, 1979) with Christiane Eda-Pierre; the older Josef Krips reading with the Vienna Philharmonic (1966) with Anneliese Rothenberger; the more recent René Jacobs with the Akademie für Alte Musik Berlin (Harmonia Mundi, 2015) with Robin Johannsen and Maximilian Schmitt, the leading period-instrument complete reading.

For the "Martern aller Arten" aria as a recital piece (apart from the complete opera), the standard recordings come from sopranos who built recital repertoire around the Mozart vocal showpieces: Edita Gruberová (multiple recital recordings), Diana Damrau (recent), Natalie Dessay (now retired but in recital recordings from the 2000s), Christine Schäfer (multiple readings), and historically Lucia Popp and Elisabeth Schwarzkopf (older readings from the 1960s-70s).

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The May 1781 break with Colloredo — Mozart's first letter to Leopold about the rupture is exhilarated, not anxious. What does the freelance choice look like from Mozart's side, and what does it look like from Leopold's side? Both perspectives are visible in the surviving letters."_
* _"'Martern aller Arten' is structurally unconventional — ten minutes long, with a four-instrument concertante introduction and texture. What does Mozart gain dramatically and musically by writing this aria at this length and with this orchestration, rather than as a standard rage aria? What does the audience have to do differently as listeners?"_
* _"The Joseph II 'too many notes' line traces to Niemetschek's 1798 biography. Solomon treats it as 'semi-legendary' — possibly capturing something true about Joseph's reception but not securely attested in its specific form. How does a careful reader of biography hold a story like this — neither fully accepting it as historical fact nor dismissing it as pure invention?"_
* _"The Mozart-Haydn friendship is one of the most generous senior-junior composer relationships in eighteenth-century music. Haydn was twenty-four years older and the established composer; Mozart was the younger, less-established figure. What does it mean that Haydn told Leopold in 1785 that Mozart was 'the greatest composer known to me'? What does the statement cost Haydn to make, and what does it give Mozart?"_
* _"The Vienna freelance career was a success creatively (it produced the great Vienna repertoire) and was financially precarious by 1788-89. If Mozart had accepted a court appointment instead in 1781 — say, the Mannheim position he had hoped for in 1777-78, or a position at one of the smaller German courts — what works in the catalog might never have been composed? The freelance choice and the great Vienna repertoire are causally connected; trace the connection."_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find Gruberová / Böhm (Deutsche Grammophon, 1980) or Damrau / Jacobs (Harmonia Mundi, 2015). Listen to "Martern aller Arten" a second time and attend to three concrete moments:

  1. **The orchestral introduction (the first ninety seconds).** Confirm: the four solo instruments — flute, oboe, violin, cello — enter and begin trading material across the orchestral background. The four-instrument concertante texture is established before the soprano sings a note.

  2. **The first vocal entry.** Listen for the soprano's opening phrase on "Martern aller Arten." The vocal line sits in the middle register and climbs as the text intensifies. Notice how Mozart places the high notes at moments of textual emphasis rather than scattering them through the vocal line.

  3. **The aria's interior — the dialogue between soprano and the four soloists.** Listen for the moments where the flute or oboe answers a phrase the soprano has just sung. The four-instrument texture works as a chamber-music quintet within the aria, with the soprano as the fifth voice. The dialogue model is the part of the aria that gives it the structural ambition Joseph II (if Niemetschek's anecdote captures anything true) noticed.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : pair "Martern aller Arten" with the Act II quartet "Ach Belmonte! Ach mein Leben!" (No. 16), whose interior "Welch ein Geschick! O Qual der Seele!" section voices the four lovers' shock at the discovery of their rescue plot. The quartet is a different mode of vocal-dramatic writing — four voices interlocking rather than the solo aria's interior monologue — and shows Mozart's ensemble writing already approaching the Da Ponte finales to come. The Böhm complete recording includes both numbers.

**Read** : the Robert Spaethling letters edition (_Mozart's Letters, Mozart's Life_ , Norton, 2000) — specifically the letters from May through August 1781 (the Colloredo break and the Vienna establishment) and from May through August 1782 (the _Entführung_ completion and the marriage). These letters are the principal primary source for the events of this chapter, and Spaethling's translations preserve the tone of Mozart's German — the exhilaration, the defiance toward Leopold, the warm correspondence with Constanze. About forty pages of letters from this fifteen-month period; the stretch of correspondence biographers return to most often.
