## Chapter 6: The Early Vienna Piano Concertos K. 413-415 + K. 449-453

Between the spring of 1782 and the spring of 1786, Mozart composed fifteen piano concertos for his own Vienna concert use — the longest sustained burst of concerto writing by any composer in the Western tradition. The fifteen run from K. 413, 414, and 415 (composed for the 1782-83 subscription concert series, completed roughly by Christmas 1782) through the great late works K. 488, K. 491, and K. 503 (March and December 1786). The remaining twelve Mozart piano concertos divide unevenly across his earlier years (Chapter 4 anchored on K. 271 of January 1777) and his last five years (K. 537 of 1788 and K. 595 of 1791, which Chapter 8 will pair with K. 488 and K. 503). The Vienna freelance career that Chapter 5 established is, in concrete repertoire terms, principally the piano concerto career.

This chapter anchors on the first six of those fifteen Vienna concertos — K. 413 in F major, K. 414 in A major, and K. 415 in C major (the 1782-83 trio); K. 449 in E♭ major, K. 450 in B♭ major, K. 451 in D major, K. 452 quintet (for piano and winds — strictly not a concerto, but composed in the same months), K. 453 in G major (the 1784 series). We anchor most closely on K. 453 in G major (April 12, 1784), partly because its finale theme is the one Mozart's pet starling famously learned to sing back to him, and partly because the concerto sits at the warm center of the early-Vienna group.

The chapter is a Part III opener and a transitional one: it sets up the structural and economic context for the minor-key concertos K. 466 and K. 491 (Chapter 7) and the late piano concertos K. 488, K. 503, K. 537, K. 595 (Chapter 8).

### 6.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

May 27, 1784. Mozart's Vienna household ledger — the small book in which he recorded musical incoming and outgoing for the household — records a purchase: a pet starling. The price was thirty-four kreuzer. Mozart added a marginal note: the starling sang back to him a melody. The melody Mozart wrote down in the ledger is recognizably the principal theme of the finale of the Piano Concerto No. 17 in G major K. 453 — the theme-and-variations finale of the concerto Mozart had completed about six weeks earlier (April 12, 1784).

The story carries documentary weight because the ledger entry survives in Mozart's own hand and because the starling could have learned the theme either from Mozart whistling it at home (the K. 453 finale theme is whistleable and Mozart certainly hummed and whistled his own music around the household) or from one of Mozart's keyboard rehearsals. The bird then sang the theme back, with (according to Mozart's marginal note) a small rhythmic alteration. The transposition of one note from a G-natural to a G-sharp is recorded in the ledger.

The starling lived in the Mozart household until June 4, 1787, three years and a week, at which point it died and Mozart wrote and performed a brief comic funeral for it (the funeral poem survives in his hand). The starling story is a small but charming biographical detail; Stanley Sadie, Maynard Solomon, and Cliff Eisen all treat the ledger entry as authentic.

For this chapter the starling matters because it dates the K. 453 finale theme securely. The theme was completed and familiar to the household by mid-May 1784. The concerto premiered in the Vienna subscription series in spring 1784. Mozart was twenty-eight; he had been a Vienna freelance composer for three years; the great Vienna piano concerto run was at its peak.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **The opening of the first movement (about the first ninety seconds).** Orchestra plays the first-theme group, a clean major-key melodic statement with light wind decoration. Listen for the wind writing — the oboes and flutes carry distinct melodic material from the strings, not merely supporting them. The wind-writing signature continues from K. 364 (Chapter 4) and _Entführung_ (Chapter 5) and is now a regular fixture.

  2. **The second movement (Andante, C major, the parallel relative).** Slower tempo, but not in a minor key — Mozart in this concerto reserves the dramatic emotional turn for the third movement rather than the second. The Andante is lyric and conversational; the piano and orchestra trade material across the movement in an easygoing chamber-music register.

  3. **The third movement (Allegretto, G major, theme-and-variations).** A buoyant theme is followed by a series of variations, then a closing coda. Listen for the theme itself at the start of the movement — this is the theme the starling sang back. Then notice how Mozart varies it across the movement (different ornamentation, different orchestral colors, different rhythmic energy) before bringing it back as a culmination.

#### One-sentence background

Composed in Vienna, completed April 12, 1784 (the autograph is dated); about thirty minutes across three movements; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Mitsuko Uchida with the English Chamber Orchestra under Jeffrey Tate (Philips, 1989), part of the Uchida complete Mozart concerto cycle. (Murray Perahia with the English Chamber Orchestra on Sony Classical, c. 1977-78, is the warmer alternative from the late 1970s; Géza Anda with the Camerata Salzburg on Deutsche Grammophon, 1960s, is the older classic reading still in catalog.)

#### Search Hints

EN: "Mozart Piano Concerto 17 K 453 Uchida Tate" or "Mozart Concerto K 453 Perahia" ZH: "莫扎特 第17号钢琴协奏曲 K453 内田光子 泰特" 或 "莫扎特 K453 佩拉希亚" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

### 6.2 The Listening Window — K. 453

Timestamps below refer to the Uchida / Tate Primary Recording.

**0:00-1:30 — The orchestral exposition (first movement).** Substantial — Mozart gives the orchestra a full statement before the piano enters, as he had returned to doing since K. 271's unconventional early entry seven years before. The orchestral material establishes the G-major home key, the first-theme group, the lyric second-theme group, and the transitional material. Listen for the wind writing — oboes and flutes have melodic material that is independent of the strings rather than reinforcing them.

**The piano's first entry.** The piano enters with a statement of the first theme, decorated and elaborated. Mozart's mature pattern in the Vienna concertos: the piano enters with the same opening material the orchestra has already played, but in an ornamented and personalized way. The pattern signals that the piano is the protagonist who will now make the orchestral material her own.

**The exposition's continuation through the second-theme group.** Mozart moves to the dominant key (D major) for the second-theme group; the piano and orchestra trade material across the modulation. The piano writing is virtuosic but always conversational with the orchestra.

**The development.** Mozart takes the opening material through several keys before returning to G major for the recapitulation. The wind writing is especially prominent in the development — listen for the moments where the oboes or flutes carry a melodic line over a sparser string accompaniment.

**The cadenza.** Mozart wrote two cadenzas for K. 453's first movement; Uchida plays one of the Mozart cadenzas on this recording. The cadenza extends the soloist's first-movement statement and resolves into the orchestra's closing material.

**The Andante (second movement).** C major. The piano and orchestra trade material in an easygoing lyric register. Listen for the opening orchestral phrase — a quiet, sustained line that the piano then takes up and elaborates. The Andante is one of the most conversational slow movements in the Mozart concerto cycle, with neither dramatic turn nor extended emotional climax. It is Mozart at his most relaxed and serene.

**The Allegretto finale (third movement).** Back to G major and a buoyant theme. The theme itself, about thirty seconds long, is the one the starling sang back. After the theme is presented, Mozart writes a series of variations. The pattern: each variation takes the theme and re-decorates it (different ornamentation, different orchestral color, different rhythmic energy). After several variations, the music shifts gears for a more contrapuntal central section, and then returns to the theme for the closing variation and coda.

The theme-and-variations finale is structurally simpler than a sonata-form or rondo finale, but it gives Mozart room to display the variation-form skills he had been developing across the Salzburg years. Listen specifically for the moments when the same theme returns in different orchestral colors — sometimes piano alone, sometimes piano with strings, sometimes piano with full orchestra including winds. The orchestral colors are the variations' main expressive medium.

### 6.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The Vienna subscription concerto economics

Mozart's Vienna piano concertos of 1782-86 were not commissioned by patrons in the older court sense. They were composed for Mozart's own subscription concert series — a Vienna 1780s innovation in which a composer-performer would announce a series of concerts, sell subscriptions to a wealthy audience, and pocket the net of ticket sales minus orchestra and venue costs. The subscription concerto was therefore a piece composed specifically for Mozart-the-pianist to perform in front of a Vienna audience who had paid subscriptions to hear him play.

The economics were favorable for Mozart in the early-to-mid 1780s. The 1784 subscription series — for which K. 449, K. 450, K. 451, K. 453 (and K. 452, the quintet for piano and winds) were composed — was financially the most successful of Mozart's Vienna concert seasons. The subscription list survives in part; the Vienna aristocracy was well-represented. The audience expectations shaped the concertos: they wanted virtuoso piano writing, but also substantial structural ambition (the audience was musically literate and would not be satisfied with empty show); they wanted moderate length (a concert of the period included multiple works and the piano concerto was one item among several); they wanted the wind-writing distinctiveness that had become Mozart's recognized signature.

Mozart calibrated the concertos accordingly. K. 413, K. 414, K. 415 (the 1782-83 trio) were composed with optional reduced scoring — the autograph indicates that the concertos could be performed "_a quattro_ " (with strings only, omitting the winds) for chamber-style performance in private homes. This was a publication strategy: the concertos would sell to amateur pianists across the German-speaking world if they could be performed without the full orchestra. K. 449 in E♭ (the first concerto of the 1784 series) is similarly small-scale, with strings and modest wind writing only. K. 450, K. 451, K. 453 step up — these are full-orchestra concertos with more substantial wind writing and longer formal scope.

The progression across the 1782-86 concertos is from chamber-style small-scale concertos to full-orchestra concertos to (in 1785-86) the great minor-key and late concertos that Chapter 7 and Chapter 8 anchor on. The trajectory is one of formal ambition expanding under the conditions of the Vienna subscription concert economy.

#### The theme-and-variations finale

The K. 453 finale is the first major appearance in this volume of the **theme-and-variations form** as a finale structure. The form is simple in concept: a self-contained theme is presented, then varied multiple times, then often returned to in some final form. The form was used as standalone keyboard music (the Mozart variation sets on popular tunes — the "Ah, vous dirai-je, Maman" variations K. 265 of 1781-82, which is the variation set on the tune known to English-speakers as "Twinkle, Twinkle, Little Star") and as finales for sonatas, concertos, and chamber works.

For Mozart specifically, the theme-and-variations finale is one of his recurring structural choices — alongside the rondo finale (which K. 453 does not use), the sonata-form finale, and the hybrid forms. K. 453's theme-and-variations finale will be paired in Chapter 15 with the first movement of the Piano Sonata No. 11 in A major K. 331 ("Alla Turca"), which is also a theme-and-variations movement. The form returns in chamber music (variations within the Haydn Quartets, the Clarinet Quintet, the late string quintets) and in operatic ensembles (the variations on a Mozart theme in _Don Giovanni_ 's catalogue aria).

The **theme-and-variations form** is the fourth feature in this volume's Thread B (after the dark-key signature from Chapter 2, the slow-movement-as-heart pattern from Chapter 4, and the wind-writing signature from Chapter 4 and _Entführung_ in Chapter 5). Chapter 19 §19.4 will collect the variation-form works.

#### Why the Vienna concertos sound different from K. 271

K. 271 (Chapter 4) was a structural breakthrough — Mozart at twenty-one writing a concerto with structural confidence that broke the conventions of his earlier work. The Vienna concertos K. 413-453 are not structural breakthroughs in the same sense. They are mature works by a composer who has fully consolidated his concerto language. What changed?

Three things. First, the orchestral writing is more sophisticated. The Vienna orchestra of the early 1780s — the players Mozart conducted in his subscription concerts — was a more accomplished body than the Salzburg court orchestra that played K. 271. Mozart wrote for the players he had. The wind writing of K. 453 is fuller and more independently melodic than the wind writing of K. 271 partly because the Vienna players could execute it.

Second, the audience expectations are higher. The Vienna subscription audience expected musical substance; they would not pay subscriptions for empty showpieces. The K. 453 first-movement development section, the K. 453 Andante's conversational depth, the K. 453 finale's variation-form sophistication — all of these reflect what the audience was paying to hear.

Third, Mozart's own keyboard playing had developed. He was twenty-eight in 1784 (against twenty-one in 1777) and his own pianism, by all contemporary accounts, was at its peak in the Vienna years. The piano writing in K. 453 is technically more demanding than in K. 271, partly because Mozart himself could execute the more demanding writing.

The Vienna concertos therefore sound different from K. 271 not because Mozart's compositional language had restructured, but because his consolidation had deepened. K. 271 had opened the territory; the Vienna concertos extend and deepen it.

#### The Mozart-and-his-starling story, structurally

The starling-ledger entry of May 27, 1784 is one of the small biographical details that captures something true about Mozart's musical mind. The K. 453 finale theme is whistleable — it is a clean, brief, memorable melodic shape that a bird could in principle learn to imitate. The fact that Mozart composed themes a starling could whistle is part of why the K. 453 finale is so accessible to amateur listeners; the same melodic clarity that made the theme bird-imitable makes it ear-catching to a Vienna audience.

Solomon and Sadie both make a small structural argument from the starling episode: Mozart's compositional process worked partly through melodic invention that was singable and whistleable in its initial form. The complex harmonic and orchestral writing came later, around clear melodic kernels. The starling-imitable theme is the kernel of K. 453's finale; the variations that follow are the orchestral elaborations around the kernel.

The argument can be over-extended (not all of Mozart's mature themes are bird-whistleable — the K. 466 D-minor concerto's opening theme is harmonically dependent on its accompanying figures and is not easily extractable as a sung melody), but for K. 453's finale theme the argument fits. The theme is a clean kernel; the music is the variations around it.

### 6.4 The Stories

#### The 1784 subscription season

The 1784 Vienna subscription season — for which Mozart composed K. 449 (February 9), K. 450 (March 15), K. 451 (March 22), K. 453 (April 12), and K. 452 (March 30; the quintet for piano and winds) — was the most concentrated single-season output of Mozart's freelance career. Five major works in nine weeks, all premiered in Mozart's own Vienna subscription concerts that spring.

The subscription list for 1784 included substantial portions of the Vienna aristocracy. Mozart's own household ledger records subscription income from across the spring. The financial returns were substantial; the family lived well that year (the Mozarts moved into a larger apartment in the Domgasse near St. Stephen's Cathedral in September 1784, which is now the Mozart-Haus museum). The professional reputation peaked: Mozart was at this point the leading composer-pianist in Vienna, with subscription income and teaching income and publication income all running at high levels.

The 1784 season represents the high point of the Vienna freelance career in financial terms and in concentrated output. The subsequent seasons (1785, 1786) would produce the great minor-key concertos K. 466 and K. 491 and the K. 488 and K. 503, but the concentrated output of 1784 was not repeated. By 1786 Mozart had moved on to the opera commissions (Figaro premiered in May 1786), and the piano concerto output was reduced. By 1788 the financial pressures had begun. The 1784 spring season was the Vienna piano concerto career's brightest moment.

#### Why K. 449 was small-scale

K. 449 in E♭ was composed for the pianist Barbara ("Babette") Ployer, a private student of Mozart's whose father Gottfried von Ployer was a Vienna aristocrat and Habsburg court official. The Ployers commissioned K. 449 (and the later K. 453, also for Babette Ployer) for private performance in their Vienna home and country residence.

Because K. 449 was composed for private performance with whatever musicians the Ployers could assemble, Mozart scored it for piano with strings, two oboes, and two horns — the wind parts marked "_ad libitum_ " (optional). The concerto could be performed with strings only, in chamber-music style, if the wind players were not available. The reduced-scoring option made the concerto practical for the private-performance setting.

K. 450 (March 15), K. 451 (March 22), and K. 453 (April 12), the next three concertos of the spring, are full-orchestra concertos, with required wind parts and more substantial orchestration. Mozart had moved from the private-performance K. 449 to the public-concert K. 450/451/453 within five weeks. The progression reflects the shift in Mozart's compositional focus from the Ployer commission to his own subscription series.

K. 453, the chapter's principal anchor, was also commissioned by the Ployers (also for Babette Ployer) and was first performed at the Ployer country house in summer 1784 — a private premiere before the Vienna subscription performance later in the year. The concerto is therefore a private-commission piece that grew into a full-orchestra concerto in the public-concert mode; the two performance contexts, the Ployer country house and the Vienna subscription series, are the work's original homes.

#### K. 452 (the piano-and-winds quintet) as a chamber-music interlude

K. 452 — the Quintet in E♭ for piano and winds (oboe, clarinet, bassoon, horn), composed March 30, 1784 — sits in this chapter because it was composed in the same nine weeks as K. 449-453 and reflects Mozart's compositional thinking of the moment. It is a chamber-music quintet in which the piano is a chamber-music partner to four wind players, rather than a concerto with a soloist set against an orchestra.

Mozart himself called K. 452 "the best work I have so far written in my entire life" in a letter to Leopold of April 10, 1784 (eleven days after the quintet's composition and approximately ten months before the famous Haydn statement of February 1785). The judgment was made in the heat of compositional satisfaction and Mozart certainly later wrote works he would have ranked higher (the K. 466 D-minor concerto, the Haydn Quartets, the Requiem); but the letter is the most-quoted Mozart self-assessment of his own work and is taken as a window into how Mozart valued his chamber-music writing for winds specifically.

The wind-writing signature first traced in Chapter 4 and continued in Chapter 5 reaches one of its peaks in K. 452. The four wind players are each given material of independent interest; the piano serves as an integrating chamber-music partner rather than a competing soloist. The work runs about twenty-five minutes and has remained a standard of the Mozart chamber repertoire. If you have time after the K. 453 listening, K. 452 is the natural pair — the same Mozart of the same nine weeks in a different ensemble setting.

#### Recording history of K. 453

K. 453 is one of the most-recorded Mozart concertos. The reference cycles all include it: Mitsuko Uchida with the English Chamber Orchestra under Jeffrey Tate (Philips, 1985-91; the chapter's Primary Recording), Murray Perahia with the English Chamber Orchestra (Sony, 1975-84), Alfred Brendel with the Academy of St Martin in the Fields under Neville Marriner (Philips, 1970s-80s), Daniel Barenboim with the Berlin Philharmonic (multiple labels), Maria João Pires with the Chamber Orchestra of Europe under Claudio Abbado (Deutsche Grammophon, 1990s), and on period instruments Robert Levin with the Academy of Ancient Music under Christopher Hogwood (L'Oiseau-Lyre / Decca, 1990s).

Géza Anda with the Camerata Salzburg (Deutsche Grammophon, 1960s) is the older classic reading that has stayed in catalog. Friedrich Gulda with the Vienna Philharmonic under Claudio Abbado (Deutsche Grammophon, 1975) is another older reading worth knowing. Lif Ove Andsnes with the Norwegian Chamber Orchestra (EMI, 2006) is the more recent reading that has gathered strong reviews.

The Uchida / Tate reading is chosen as the Primary Recording for its consistent balance of soloist-and-orchestra dialogue, the chamber-music sense of the conversational passages, and the warm but not heavy modern-instrument orchestral color. Uchida's playing is unfussy and clear; she lets Mozart's invention carry the work without imposing interpretive weight.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The starling-ledger story of May 27, 1784. Solomon and Sadie both treat it as authentic. What does the story tell you about how Mozart's themes worked — about the relationship between melodic clarity and orchestral elaboration? Is the K. 453 finale theme bird-imitable in the way Mozart's K. 466 D-minor concerto opening is not?"_
* _"K. 449 in E♭ was composed for private performance with optional wind parts. K. 450, K. 451, K. 453 (the next three concertos of the same spring) are full-orchestra concertos with required winds. Why did Mozart progress from one to the other in five weeks? What did the shift cost him compositionally, and what did it gain him?"_
* _"Mozart in April 1784 called the K. 452 piano-and-winds quintet 'the best work I have so far written in my entire life.' Two weeks later he completed K. 453 — the piano concerto we are anchoring on. Why would Mozart value the quintet above the concerto? What does K. 452's wind chamber-music writing offer that the concerto's wind writing does not?"_
* _"The 1784 Vienna subscription season produced five major concertos (and the K. 452 quintet) in nine weeks. The financial returns were strong, the professional reputation was high. Why was the concentrated output of 1784 not repeated in 1785 or 1786? What changed in Mozart's compositional priorities across those next two years?"_
* _"K. 453's finale is a theme-and-variations rather than a rondo or sonata-form finale. What does the variation-form choice give Mozart structurally that a rondo would not have? Listen specifically for the way the same theme returns in different orchestral colors across the variations — what is the orchestral coloring doing as a variation device?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find Uchida / Tate (Philips, 1989) or Perahia (Sony, 1977-78). Listen to K. 453 a second time and attend to three concrete moments:

  1. **The first movement's orchestral exposition (the first ninety seconds).** Confirm: the wind writing is independently melodic from the strings — the oboes and flutes carry distinct material rather than reinforcing the string texture. The wind-writing signature continues from K. 364 and _Entführung_.

  2. **The Andante (second movement) opening.** Listen for the quiet sustained orchestral phrase that opens the movement, then the piano's elaborated response. The conversational lyric register sets the second-movement mood, which stays in major-key easygoing throughout.

  3. **The finale theme (the starling-imitable theme at the start of the third movement).** Listen specifically for the theme itself — its clean shape, its memorability, its whistleable quality. Then listen for how the same theme returns in different orchestral colors across the variations: piano alone, piano with strings, piano with full orchestra including winds. The orchestral coloring is the variations' main expressive medium.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : the Quintet in E♭ for piano and winds, K. 452 (March 30, 1784) — Mozart's own most-praised work as of April 1784, composed thirteen days before K. 453. The standard recordings: Vladimir Ashkenazy with the London Wind Soloists (Decca, 1965), Alfred Brendel with members of the Academy of St Martin in the Fields (Philips, 1980s), Mitsuko Uchida with the Vienna Wind Soloists (Philips, 1990). The quintet runs about twenty-five minutes and is the chamber-music pair to K. 453.

**Read** : Charles Rosen, _The Classical Style: Haydn, Mozart, Beethoven_ (Norton, 1971; expanded edition 1997). Chapter 5 ("Mozart") includes Rosen's treatment of the Vienna piano concertos as the form's mature flowering. Rosen on the concerto-as-dialogue is the analytical voice this book most often returns to and the source-of-record for how to listen to the Vienna concertos.
