K. 488 (March 1786) is the high peak of the 1784-86 concentrated output; K. 503 (December 1786) closes the peak; K. 595 (January 1791) opens the final year. How does the late piano concerto cycle change across these three works? What is K. 595 doing that K. 488 and K. 503 are not?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find Perahia K. 595 (Sony, c. 1982) and Perahia K. 503 (Sony, c. 1980). Listen to both works and attend to four concrete moments:

  1. **K. 595 Larghetto opening.** Confirm: strings and winds together carry the opening theme in E♭ major (the subdominant of the home key B♭ — a conventional slow-movement key, but the writing inside is what distinguishes it). The theme is one of Mozart's simplest melodic lines with no virtuoso decoration.

  2. **K. 595 Larghetto's middle section.** Listen for the modulation through related keys before the return to E♭. The harmonic shift is gentle — no abrupt change, no minor-mode darkening, no dynamic surge. The new key arrives under the same quiet texture that established the opening.

  3. **K. 503 first-theme construction.** Listen for the dotted-rhythm fanfare gesture at the very opening, then the lyric continuation, then the wind responses. The first theme is a constellation of related figures rather than a single melodic line.

  4. **K. 503 development section.** Listen for the harmonic adventurousness — Mozart takes the first-theme material through more remote keys than the development of K. 466 or K. 488 had used. The harmonic palette is part of K. 503's architectural ambition.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : return to K. 488 in A major. Pairing the F-sharp minor Adagio with the K. 595 Larghetto here gives the slow-movement-as-heart pattern in its clearest form. Standard recordings of K. 488: Mitsuko Uchida with Jeffrey Tate (Philips, 1985), Murray Perahia (Sony, 1977), Maria João Pires with Abbado (Deutsche Grammophon, 1990s).

**Read** : Christoph Wolff, _Mozart at the Gateway to His Fortune: Serving the Emperor, 1788-1791_ (Norton, 2012). Wolff's argument is that Mozart's late period was not the financial decline that earlier biography sometimes portrayed but a transition during which Mozart was repositioning himself as a court composer (he was appointed Imperial Chamber Composer in December 1787) and was on the verge of greater stability when he died. The book is the principal late-period scholarly source and treats K. 595, K. 622, the Magic Flute, and the Requiem as a cluster. About 280 pages.

---

## Chapter 9: The "Prague" Symphony No. 38 K. 504 + Symphony No. 39 in E♭ K. 543

Mozart composed five symphonies in his last six years (1786-1791): No. 38 "Prague" K. 504 (December 1786), No. 39 in E♭ K. 543 (June 1788), No. 40 in G minor K. 550 (July 1788), No. 41 in C major "Jupiter" K. 551 (August 1788), and a sixth that does not survive in symphonic form (sometimes the early K. 444 "No. 37," which is actually mostly Michael Haydn with a slow introduction by Mozart, is counted; modern numbering treats the five 1786-88 symphonies as the late symphonic output and stops counting at K. 551 No. 41). The five late symphonies, three of them composed in roughly seven weeks in the summer of 1788, are the symphonic cycle Mozart actually completed; the late piano concerto cycle (Chapter 8) ran in parallel with it.

This chapter anchors on K. 504 "Prague" and K. 543. Chapter 10 anchors on K. 550 and K. 551 "Jupiter." The four-chapter Part IV of this volume covers the four late symphonies in detail and gives the reader the symphonic Mozart at his mature peak. K. 504 sits chronologically with K. 503 (the concerto, completed three weeks earlier in December 1786 — the same month produced both the great C-major piano concerto and the great D-major symphony); K. 543 sits as the first of the summer-1788 trilogy.

### 9.1 Before You Listen — Two Works in Context

#### Hook Anecdote (K. 504)

January 1787. Mozart and Constanze traveled from Vienna to Prague to attend performances of _Le nozze di Figaro_ , which had premiered in Vienna nine months earlier (May 1, 1786) and had been received in Prague that autumn with such acclaim that the Prague public was, by some contemporary accounts, more enthusiastic about _Figaro_ than the Vienna public had been. Mozart's letter to Gottfried von Jacquin (January 15, 1787) describes the Prague reception: he found himself a celebrity in the city, with Prague audiences whistling _Figaro_ arias in the streets. The Prague residency lasted about a month.

During the Prague visit, Mozart conducted a performance of _Figaro_ at the National Theater and also conducted a concert of his own works on January 19, 1787, that included the new Symphony No. 38 in D K. 504 — composed in Vienna in early December 1786 and brought to Prague for performance. The symphony was so received in Prague that it became known as the "Prague" symphony from that point forward. (Mozart did not name it; the popular nickname attached after the Prague reception.)

The Prague residency had broader consequences. The director of the Prague theater, Pasquale Bondini, commissioned Mozart for a new opera for the upcoming season. The commission produced _Don Giovanni_ K. 527, which premiered in Prague on October 29, 1787 (Chapter 12). The Mozart-Prague relationship that the January 1787 visit established was one of the principal professional relationships of Mozart's last five years; Prague's musical culture took to Mozart in a way the Vienna culture sometimes did not, and Mozart was reciprocally warm toward Prague.

#### Hook Anecdote (K. 543)

June 26, 1788. Mozart completed the autograph of Symphony No. 39 in E♭ K. 543 — the first of the famous summer-1788 symphony trilogy. Mozart was thirty-two; he had three-and-a-half years remaining to live. The Vienna economy was contracting under the Austro-Turkish War (declared February 1788); Mozart had moved his family from the central Domgasse apartment to a less expensive lodging in the Alsergrund suburb on June 17, 1788; his first letter to Michael Puchberg requesting a loan was written June 17, 1788 — the same week the family moved and nine days before he completed K. 543's autograph.

K. 543 is the calmest of the three summer-1788 symphonies. Where K. 550 in G minor (completed July 25, 1788, Chapter 10) is dark and rhythmically urgent, and K. 551 "Jupiter" in C major (completed August 10, 1788, Chapter 10) is structurally ambitious and contrapuntally elaborate, K. 543 in E♭ sits between as a more lyric, more expansive, more wind-rich symphonic statement. The contrasts among the three suggest Mozart's compositional planning: he wrote three symphonies in seven weeks not because he had three identical ideas but because he had three different symphonic projects in mind and executed them in succession. Whether the three were intended as a cycle (some scholars argue for this, citing Mozart's frequent practice of composing related works in groups — the Haydn Quartets, the late piano trios K. 542/548/564, the Prussian Quartets) or as three independent works that happened to fall in the same seven weeks (other scholars argue this — there is no surviving letter or compositional document that mentions a cycle plan) is a contested question. We return to it in Chapter 10.

The documented mystery of the summer-1788 symphonies: there is no clear contemporary record that Mozart heard any of the three performed in his lifetime. Some scholars argue for likely performances based on indirect evidence (a Vienna concert announcement that might have included one of them; a Frankfurt or Berlin performance during Mozart's 1789-90 travels); other scholars argue from the absence of clear evidence that the three may have remained unperformed during Mozart's life. The contested-ness of this question is its own commentary on the contracting Vienna concert economy of 1788 — the era when Mozart had been able to premiere new symphonies in his own subscription concerts was already past, and the three new symphonies of summer 1788 had no obvious performance venue waiting for them.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen (K. 504)

  1. **The slow introduction (the first two-and-a-half minutes).** Mozart opens K. 504 with one of his longest and most elaborate slow introductions — Adagio, full orchestra including trumpets and timpani, harmonically adventurous, structurally substantial. Listen for the chromatic descents in the lower strings, the wind responses, the dramatic dynamic contrasts. The slow introduction sets up the Allegro main movement with structural weight rather than mere ceremonial gesture.

  2. **The Allegro main movement opening.** After the slow introduction, the main Allegro launches in D major with an urgent, syncopated string figure that contrasts with the slow introduction's gravity. The transition from slow introduction to Allegro is a hinge moment Mozart had not attempted at this scale before.

  3. **The three-movement form.** K. 504 has only three movements, fast, slow, fast, with no minuet. This is unusual for a Mozart late symphony (K. 543, K. 550, K. 551 all have four movements with minuets). The three-movement form is partly an inheritance from Italian symphonic practice (Italian opera overtures of the period were often in three-movement form) and partly a structural choice to keep the symphony tightly focused. Listen for the absence of the minuet — the structural sense the symphony has from being four movements compressed into three.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen (K. 543 menuetto + finale)

  1. **The Menuetto (third movement) opening.** Strong rhythmic statement in E♭ major. The minuet is more rhythmically vigorous than a typical late-Mozart minuet — closer to a peasant dance than a courtly dance. Listen for the moment the contrasting middle section (the Trio) begins; the trio features the clarinets prominently in the famous K. 543 clarinet writing.

  2. **The Trio of the Menuetto.** Clarinets carry the principal melodic line. The clarinet writing in K. 543 is famous within the Mozart symphonic literature — the instrument was a relatively recent addition to the standard symphonic complement (it had been added in the 1780s, and Mozart was among the first composers to give the clarinet substantial symphonic writing), and K. 543's Trio is one of the principal pieces of evidence for what the clarinet could do in symphonic context.

  3. **The finale (fourth movement).** Allegro in E♭ major, sonata-form with a brief and lively first theme. The finale runs about six minutes and is one of Mozart's most exuberant symphonic finales. Listen for the wind writing throughout and for the contrapuntal complexity in the development section.

#### One-sentence background (K. 504)

Completed December 6, 1786 (autograph dated); about thirty minutes across three movements, the first movement (slow introduction + Allegro) runs about fourteen minutes; the **Primary Recording** for K. 504 in this chapter is Sir Charles Mackerras with the Scottish Chamber Orchestra (Telarc, 1986), part of Mackerras's complete Mozart late symphonies cycle widely held as one of the modern references. (Karl Böhm with the Berlin Philharmonic on Deutsche Grammophon, 1960s, is the warmer older alternative; John Eliot Gardiner with the English Baroque Soloists on Philips, 1990, is the period-instrument alternative.)

#### One-sentence background (K. 543)

Completed June 26, 1788; about thirty minutes across four movements; the **Primary Recording** for K. 543 in this chapter is Sir Charles Mackerras with the Scottish Chamber Orchestra (Telarc, 1986). (Karl Böhm with the Berlin Philharmonic on Deutsche Grammophon, 1960s, is the modern-instrument alternative; Trevor Pinnock with the English Concert on Deutsche Grammophon Archiv, 1990s, is the period-instrument alternative.)

#### Search Hints

EN (K. 504): "Mozart Symphony 38 Prague K 504 Mackerras Scottish Chamber" or "Mozart Prague Symphony Böhm Berlin" EN (K. 543): "Mozart Symphony 39 K 543 Mackerras" or "Mozart Symphony 39 Böhm" ZH (K. 504): "莫扎特 第38号交响曲 布拉格 K504 麦凯拉斯" 或 "莫扎特 布拉格交响曲 贝姆" ZH (K. 543): "莫扎特 第39号交响曲 K543 麦凯拉斯" 或 "莫扎特 第39号交响曲 贝姆" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

### 9.2 The Listening Window — K. 504 First Movement

Timestamps below refer to the Mackerras Primary Recording.

**0:00-2:30 — The slow introduction (Adagio).** Full orchestra including trumpets and timpani. The texture is harmonically adventurous and dramatically substantial. Listen for the chromatic descending figures in the lower strings, the wind responses that decorate them, the dynamic contrasts that build through the introduction. The slow introduction is structurally substantial — not a brief ceremonial gesture but a fully developed opening statement.

**The transition into the Allegro.** Mozart shifts from the slow introduction into the fast main movement through a brief preparatory passage. The transition is one of the most-discussed moments in his symphonic writing.

**The Allegro main movement's first-theme group.** Urgent syncopated string figure in D major. The first theme is rhythmically driven rather than melodically lyric; the syncopation provides the propulsive energy that will carry the main movement.

**The exposition's second-theme group.** Lyric major-key theme in A major (the dominant). Brief contrast to the first theme's urgency. K. 504 has no clarinets in its scoring — the wind complement is two flutes, two oboes, two bassoons, two horns, and two trumpets with timpani. Listen for the oboes and bassoons carrying the second-theme material; the wind writing has the Mozart conversational dialogue character without the clarinet color that K. 543 and the late piano concertos rely on.

**The development.** Mozart takes the first-theme material through several keys and develops the syncopated rhythmic figure in various orchestral colors. The development is structurally substantial.

**The recapitulation.** First-theme group returns in D major; second-theme group returns in D major (the home-key rule).

**The coda.** Substantial — Mozart closes the first movement with the architectural weight the slow introduction and exposition had established.

### 9.3 The Listening Window — K. 543 Menuetto and Finale

Timestamps below refer to the Mackerras Primary Recording.

**The Menuetto (third movement) opening.** Strong rhythmic statement in E♭ major. The minuet is vigorous rather than courtly — closer to a peasant-dance energy than a court-ball mannerism. Mozart in the late symphonies sometimes uses the minuet as a place for rhythmic vigor rather than as a stylized courtly interlude.

**The Trio of the Menuetto.** The contrasting middle section. Clarinets carry the principal melodic line — the famous K. 543 clarinet writing. The instrument was a relatively recent symphonic addition (the clarinet had been added to the standard symphonic complement in the 1780s), and Mozart's writing for it in K. 543's Trio is one of the principal early examples of substantial symphonic clarinet writing. The Trio's mood contrasts sharply with the minuet's vigor — quieter, more lyric, more reflective.

**Return to the Menuetto after the Trio.** Standard _da capo_ return — the minuet is played again from the beginning, completing the ternary form of the movement.

**The finale (fourth movement) opening.** Allegro in E♭ major. Brief, lively first theme. The finale is sonata-form but runs at high tempo; the structural compression is one of the markers of Mozart's late symphonic finales.

**The finale's development.** Mozart develops the brief first-theme material contrapuntally; listen for the moments of imitation between sections of the orchestra. The contrapuntal complexity is less elaborate than what K. 551's Jupiter finale will display (Chapter 10), but it is a clear pointer toward that complexity.

**The finale's closing material.** The finale reaches its closing material with full orchestral participation; the closing is one of the most exuberant in the Mozart symphonic literature.

### 9.4 What's Happening in the Music

#### Why the slow introduction in K. 504

K. 504's slow introduction is structurally substantial — about two-and-a-half minutes long, the longest in any Mozart symphony. The slow introduction as a symphonic device was a Haydn innovation; Haydn had used substantial slow introductions in many of his "London" symphonies (the symphonies composed for London in 1791-95, after Mozart's death), but in the 1786 period the slow-introduction practice was already established. Mozart in K. 504 takes the device to a new structural weight.

What the slow introduction does for K. 504: it establishes the symphony's harmonic ambition and dramatic seriousness before the main movement begins. The chromatic descents, the wind responses, the harmonic exploration — all of these are structural material that the main Allegro will then engage. The slow introduction is not a preface to be skipped over; it is the symphony's opening statement and the rest of the work flows from it.

The slow-introduction device will return in K. 543's first movement (a brief Adagio introduction precedes the Allegro) but not in K. 550 (which opens directly with the famous Allegro) and not in K. 551 (which similarly opens directly with the Allegro). K. 504's slow introduction is the most fully developed in the late symphonic cycle.

#### The clarinet writing in K. 543

The clarinet was added to the standard symphonic complement in the 1780s. Before the 1780s, the standard symphonic wind complement was flutes, oboes, bassoons, and horns (plus trumpets and timpani for fuller scoring); the clarinet was an instrument used mainly in military bands and in _Harmoniemusik_ (wind-ensemble music for outdoor and ceremonial use). The transition of the clarinet from those contexts into the standard symphonic complement was an 1780s development.

Mozart was among the principal composers who normalized the clarinet's symphonic role. Of the last three symphonies, K. 543 and K. 550 (the revised version) use clarinets prominently; K. 504 of December 1786 has none, and K. 551 ("Jupiter") also omits them. The K. 543 Trio is the passage analysts usually point to for the clarinet, but the clarinet writing runs throughout K. 543 — in the first-movement Allegro, in the slow-movement Andante, in the finale.

Mozart's late clarinet works extend the K. 543 clarinet writing: the Clarinet Quintet K. 581 (1789, Chapter 17) and the Clarinet Concerto K. 622 (October 1791) are the principal clarinet works in Mozart's catalog. Both were composed for Anton Stadler (1753-1812), the leading clarinetist of Vienna in the 1780s-90s and one of Mozart's preferred wind-soloist collaborators. The K. 543 clarinet writing is the first major step in Mozart's clarinet career; the K. 581 and K. 622 are the destinations.

The wind-writing signature collected as Thread B in this volume continues here. K. 543's clarinet writing is one of the principal late-period instances of the wind-writing thread. Chapter 19 §19.2 collects the wind-writing instances.

#### The Prague reception and what it meant

The January 1787 Prague visit established a Mozart-Prague relationship that lasted through Mozart's life. Prague received Mozart with enthusiasm that Vienna sometimes withheld; the Prague theater commissioned _Don Giovanni_ (which premiered October 29, 1787, Chapter 12); Mozart returned to Prague for the _Don Giovanni_ premiere; Mozart returned again to Prague in 1791 for the premiere of _La clemenza di Tito_ K. 621 (September 6, 1791) — the last opera Mozart completed before his death.

The Prague-Vienna comparison is a recurring theme in Mozart scholarship and in popular Mozart biography. The simple version: Vienna had a more sophisticated and demanding audience but was also more politicized (the Italian-opera faction at the imperial court resisted Mozart's German-style writing); Prague had a more enthusiastic and less politicized audience that took to Mozart's music more directly. The simple version is partly true and partly oversimplified; Vienna also received Mozart enthusiastically much of the time, and Prague had its own opera politics. But the Prague relationship was warm and the city's musical culture is the principal documented case of a non-Vienna European city that took Mozart as one of its own composers.

K. 504 carries the Prague nickname not because it was composed for Prague (it was composed in Vienna in early December 1786, before the January 1787 Prague visit) but because it was performed at Mozart's Prague concert on January 19, 1787 and was received in Prague with the same enthusiasm that _Figaro_ had been receiving. The nickname stuck because of the Prague performance, not the composition. The naming pattern — the nickname attaches to the performance rather than the composition — is common for Mozart works (the K. 537 "Coronation" nickname from the 1790 Frankfurt coronation festival, the K. 543 work without a nickname despite its prominence, the K. 271 "Jeunehomme" misattribution that became a nickname).

#### The summer-1788 trilogy question

The three summer-1788 symphonies, K. 543 (June 26), K. 550 (July 25), and K. 551 (August 10), were composed in roughly seven weeks. Whether they were intended as a connected cycle is contested.

The arguments for a cycle: (a) Mozart frequently composed related works in groups (the Haydn Quartets of 1782-85 are six quartets composed as a set; the late piano trios K. 542/548/564 of 1788 are three trios composed in close succession; the Prussian Quartets K. 575/589/590 of 1789-90 are three quartets composed for the King of Prussia as a set); (b) the three symphonies have complementary characters (K. 543 lyric, K. 550 dark and rhythmically urgent, K. 551 structurally ambitious and contrapuntal) that suggest deliberate contrast within a unified plan; (c) the three were composed in quick succession with no other major work intervening; (d) some scholars (Daniel Heartz, Christoph Wolff) argue for the cycle reading.

The arguments against a cycle: (a) no surviving letter or compositional document mentions a cycle plan; (b) Mozart sometimes composed in close succession without a unified intention (his concertos were sometimes grouped by circumstance rather than by plan); (c) the three symphonies were not published as a set in Mozart's lifetime (K. 543 was published by Hoffmeister in 1789; the others later); (d) other scholars (Maynard Solomon is the principal advocate) treat the three as independent works that happened to fall in close succession.

The scholarship has not settled the question; the compositional context is consistent with either reading. What can be said: the three summer-1788 symphonies as a group constitute one of the principal compositional events in Mozart's career and in the history of the late-eighteenth-century symphony, whether or not Mozart consciously planned them as a cycle. Chapter 10 (which anchors on K. 550 and K. 551) returns to the cycle question.

### 9.5 The Stories

#### The K. 504 composition in December 1786

Mozart was extraordinarily busy in late 1786. _Le nozze di Figaro_ had premiered in Vienna in May 1786 and was being prepared for Prague performance in the autumn. The piano concertos K. 491 (March 1786) and K. 503 (December 4, 1786) framed the year. K. 504 was completed December 6, 1786 — two days after K. 503. The Symphony K. 504 was therefore composed simultaneously with or immediately after the Piano Concerto K. 503; the same Mozart of December 1786 was producing both works.

What this fact tells us about the late peak: Mozart's compositional capacity in late 1786 was at one of its highest concentrations. Two major works (K. 503 and K. 504) within the same week is not normal compositional output for any composer; for Mozart it was the working pattern of late 1786. The Prague trip of January 1787 brought K. 504 to its first major audience and established the Mozart-Prague relationship; the subsequent _Don Giovanni_ commission grew from the January 1787 visit.

#### The K. 543 composition in June 1788

The K. 543 composition was completed June 26, 1788. The Vienna context was deteriorating: the Austro-Turkish War had been declared in February 1788; the Vienna economy was contracting; Mozart's subscription concert series had become harder to fill; the family had moved to a less expensive lodging on June 17, 1788; the first Puchberg loan letter was written June 17, 1788.

The simultaneity of the financial pressures and the K. 543 composition is sometimes biographically read as Mozart writing the late symphonies under financial duress. The reading is partly true: the Vienna economy was contracting, the household finances were tighter than they had been in 1784-86, and Mozart was reaching out to friends for loans. But the K. 543 itself is not a work of duress; it is a structurally serene, lyric symphony with extended wind writing and considerable formal expansiveness. Mozart did not compose like a man under duress; he composed like a man at the height of his powers, who was also dealing with financial pressure that was real but did not penetrate the compositional work.

The relationship between Mozart's life circumstances and his late compositional output is one of the principal interpretive questions of his biography. The conservative reading (which Solomon advances and Wolff develops): Mozart's late compositional output comes from a composer whose powers were at their height and whose life circumstances were difficult but not yet catastrophic. The romantic reading (which earlier biography sometimes favored): Mozart in his last years was a tragic figure whose work bears the weight of his life suffering. The conservative reading has prevailed in late-twentieth-century and twenty-first-century Mozart scholarship; the romantic reading persists in popular biography but is harder to support from the surviving compositional and biographical record.

#### Recording history of K. 504 and K. 543

The standard cycles all include both symphonies. The references:

Sir Charles Mackerras with the Scottish Chamber Orchestra (Telarc, 1986, the chapter's Primary Recording) is the leading modern reference. Mackerras's readings are tempo-historical informed but use modern instruments; the result is a clear-textured, rhythmically vital reading that respects the late-Mozart structural ambition.

Karl Böhm with the Berlin Philharmonic (Deutsche Grammophon, 1960s) is the mid-century reference for both symphonies. Böhm's readings are warmer, more vibrato-rich, more architecturally weighted than the Mackerras readings — the Vienna tradition's late-twentieth-century continuation.

Trevor Pinnock with the English Concert (Deutsche Grammophon Archiv, 1990s) is the period-instrument cycle. Pinnock's readings recover the original sound-world; the wind writing of K. 543 is particularly clear on period instruments where the clarinets project more sharply than modern instruments allow.

John Eliot Gardiner with the English Baroque Soloists (Philips, 1990) is the period-instrument alternative to Pinnock; Gardiner's readings are more dramatic and rhythmically pointed.

Otto Klemperer with the Philharmonia Orchestra (Columbia / EMI, 1960s) is the older monumental reading — slower tempos, fuller orchestral weight, the mid-century European tradition at its most architecturally serious. Klemperer's Mozart symphonies divide listeners (some find them too heavy for Mozart's lighter textures; others find the architectural weight revelatory), but the readings have stayed in catalog and are worth knowing.

Bruno Walter with the Vienna Philharmonic and later the Columbia Symphony Orchestra (Columbia / Sony, 1930s-50s) is the historic reference. Walter was a Mozart specialist of the early-twentieth-century Vienna tradition; his readings carry the warmth of the central European Mozart performing tradition before the HIP revolution. Walter's K. 543 is particularly cherished by listeners who prefer the older Vienna sound.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"K. 504 'Prague' is a three-movement symphony with no minuet — unusual for a Mozart late symphony. What does the absence of the minuet do structurally? Does the three-movement form make K. 504 feel more focused than the four-movement K. 543, K. 550, K. 551, or does it feel incomplete? How does the slow introduction compensate (if it does) for the missing minuet?"_
* _"The K. 543 Trio gives the clarinets the principal melodic line — the most prominent symphonic clarinet writing of the mid-1780s. Mozart was among the first composers to write substantially for the clarinet in symphonic context. Listen to the Trio and identify what the clarinet's sound contributes to the music that an oboe or flute would not have contributed. What does the new instrument's tonal color let Mozart do?"_
* _"The Mozart-Prague relationship was one of the principal professional relationships of Mozart's last five years. Prague's musical culture took to Mozart with enthusiasm that Vienna sometimes withheld. What does it mean for a composer's career to have a second city — a city other than the principal place of residence — that receives him as one of its own? How does the Prague reception of K. 504 and Figaro and Don Giovanni shape the Mozart catalog we have?"_
* _"The summer-1788 symphony trilogy (K. 543, K. 550, K. 551) — three symphonies in seven weeks — is one of the principal compositional events in Mozart's career. Whether the three were intended as a unified cycle is contested. What evidence would settle the cycle question if we could find it? What does it matter whether the three were planned as a cycle or composed in close succession without a unified plan?"_
* _"K. 504 was composed simultaneously with or immediately after K. 503 (the piano concerto, completed two days earlier). The same Mozart of early December 1786 produced both works. What does that simultaneity tell you about Mozart's compositional process — does he have one project at a time or several? Listen to K. 503 and K. 504 in the same sitting and notice what is shared and what is distinct between the two works."_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find Mackerras / Scottish Chamber Orchestra (Telarc, 1986) or Böhm / Berlin Philharmonic (Deutsche Grammophon, 1960s). Listen to both symphonies and attend to four concrete moments:

  1. **K. 504 slow introduction (the first two-and-a-half minutes).** Confirm: full orchestra including trumpets and timpani; chromatic descending figures in the lower strings; wind responses that decorate them; dynamic contrasts that build through the introduction. The slow introduction is structurally substantial rather than ceremonial.

  2. **K. 504 transition into the Allegro.** Listen for the moment Mozart shifts from the slow introduction's gravity into the main movement's syncopated urgency. The transition is the structural hinge of the symphony.

  3. **K. 543 Menuetto and Trio.** Confirm: the minuet is vigorous (closer to peasant-dance than courtly-dance energy); the Trio features the clarinets carrying the principal melodic line. The K. 543 clarinet writing in the Trio is among the principal early examples of substantial symphonic clarinet writing.

  4. **K. 543 finale opening.** Brief, lively first theme in E♭ major. Listen for the contrapuntal moments in the development section — the imitation between sections of the orchestra. The contrapuntal complexity is less elaborate than what K. 551 Jupiter's finale will display (Chapter 10) but is a clear pointer toward it.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : pair K. 504 with the Piano Concerto K. 503 (December 4, 1786) — the work Mozart completed two days before K. 504. The same Mozart of December 1786 produced both. Hearing the two together gives a sense of his compositional thinking at the late 1786 peak. The Murray Perahia K. 503 (Sony, c. 1980) and the Mackerras K. 504 (Telarc, 1986) provide the listening pair.

**Read** : Daniel Heartz, _Mozart, Haydn and Early Beethoven, 1781-1802_ (Norton, 2009). The chapters on Mozart's late symphonies place K. 504 and K. 543 in the context of the 1780s symphonic tradition — the Haydn symphonies that preceded them, the Vienna concert culture that received them, the publication history. Heartz is the principal Vienna-period contextualizer in this volume's voice DNA and the late-symphony chapters of his book are the standard treatment.

## Chapter 10: Symphony No. 40 in G minor K. 550 + Symphony No. 41 "Jupiter" K. 551

The final two Mozart symphonies — No. 40 in G minor K. 550 (completed July 25, 1788) and No. 41 in C major "Jupiter" K. 551 (completed August 10, 1788) — were composed within sixteen days of each other and within seven weeks of K. 543 (Chapter 9, completed June 26, 1788). The three summer-1788 symphonies constitute Mozart's symphonic peak and were the last symphonies he composed; he had three-and-a-half years remaining to live and would not return to the symphonic genre, though he would compose major works in nearly every other form before his death in December 1791.

This chapter anchors on K. 550's first movement (the famous opening with violin theme over viola accompaniment) and on K. 551's fourth-movement finale (the five-theme contrapuntal Jupiter finale that Charles Rosen identified as one of the structural marvels of the Classical period). Both works run about thirty minutes in complete performance; the first movement of K. 550 runs about eight minutes; the Jupiter finale runs about eight to nine minutes. These two anchors give the listener the dark-key climax of the volume (K. 550 in G minor is the third major G-minor anchor after K. 183 of Chapter 2 and pointing toward K. 516 of Chapter 17) and the structural-marvel climax of the symphonic literature.

### 10.1 Before You Listen — Two Works in Context

#### Hook Anecdote (K. 550)

July 25, 1788. Mozart completed the autograph of Symphony No. 40 in G minor K. 550. He was thirty-two and a half years old, living in the Alsergrund suburb apartment the family had moved to on June 17, 1788; the household's financial pressures were sharper than they had been in the high-peak 1784-86 years; Mozart had written his first Puchberg loan letter five-and-a-half weeks earlier. K. 550 is therefore composed against a background of contracting Vienna economic conditions and tightening household budgets.

K. 550 is Mozart's second symphony in G minor. The first was the "little G minor" K. 183 of October 1773 (Chapter 2) — composed fifteen years earlier when Mozart was seventeen. The matched pair K. 183 + K. 550 — the only two Mozart symphonies in G minor, separated by half of his creative life — invites comparison: what did fifteen years do to Mozart's understanding of G minor?

The answer can be heard. K. 183 is the seventeen-year-old's _Sturm und Drang_ posture — dramatic, syncopated, rhythmically urgent, structurally compact. K. 550 is the mature composer's working of the same key into a more harmonically adventurous, more contrapuntally elaborated, more emotionally sustained dramatic argument. Both are great works in G minor; they are different works that share a key. The pair, taken together, is the most direct invitation Mozart's catalog offers to ask what G minor meant to him across his life. Charles Rosen and Stanley Sadie both make the K. 183 / K. 550 pairing the central case for the dark-key signature in Mozart.

The famous opening of K. 550 — violin theme over viola accompaniment in repeated eighth notes — is one of the most-recognized openings in Western art music. Listen for it specifically; it is the opening of one of the few classical-music passages an ordinary educated listener can identify by ear even without classical-music training.

#### Hook Anecdote (K. 551)

August 10, 1788. Sixteen days after K. 550, Mozart completed the autograph of Symphony No. 41 in C major K. 551. The "Jupiter" nickname, like the "Prague" nickname for K. 504, was not Mozart's; it attached to the work later. The earliest known attribution of the "Jupiter" nickname to the symphony is in Vincent Novello's 1829 publication; the nickname's origin has been traced to Johann Peter Salomon (the London impresario who brought Haydn to England in 1791-92 and 1794-95), though the exact moment of naming is not securely documented. Whoever named it, the nickname captured the symphony's expansive, ceremonial, C-major confidence — appropriate to the king of the gods.

The K. 551 finale is the symphony's most-discussed movement. It is structured as a sonata-form Allegro with five thematic subjects that Mozart treats contrapuntally — each subject is presented, each is combined with the others in various pairings, and at the end of the movement all five subjects are stated simultaneously in invertible counterpoint (each subject can be moved to any position in the texture and the harmony still works). The technical accomplishment is what Rosen called one of the structural marvels of the Classical period; the rhetorical accomplishment is the sense of inevitability the finale carries — five themes that turn out to have been designed all along to combine, arriving at their combination as if it were predestined.

The Jupiter is the last symphony Mozart composed. He would have been thirty-two and a half. He would not write another symphony in the three-and-a-half years before his death.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen (K. 550 first movement)

  1. **The opening (the first thirty seconds).** Violins enter immediately with the famous theme — a chromatic descending figure decorated with grace notes — while the violas pulse beneath in repeated eighth notes. No slow introduction; the symphony opens directly with the theme. The accompaniment-and-melody texture is one of the simplest possible openings; the simplicity is part of why the opening is so memorable.

  2. **The orchestration.** K. 550 omits trumpets and timpani — unusual for a late Mozart symphony in a minor key. Mozart later revised K. 550 to add clarinets to the wind complement (the revision is conventionally heard in modern performances; both the original-no-clarinets version and the revised-with-clarinets version are in catalog). The leaner orchestration (no trumpets and timpani; sometimes no clarinets) gives K. 550 its darker, more chamber-like color compared with K. 504, K. 543, and K. 551.

  3. **The first-theme's emotional weight.** The chromatic descending figure is one of the rhetorical conventions of grief in the eighteenth-century musical vocabulary — the chromatic-descent figure that this volume's Thread B has been tracking. K. 550's first theme is built around the chromatic descent. Listen for the moments of grief-rhetoric: the descending half-step intervals, the grace-note decorations, the syncopation that throws the rhythm slightly off-balance.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen (K. 551 fourth-movement finale)

  1. **The first subject.** A four-note rising figure — C, D, F, E (in scale degrees: do-re-fa-mi). The figure is brief and clean. Listen for it; you will hear it return many times across the finale, both alone and combined with the other subjects.

  2. **The five subjects entering.** Across the first few minutes of the finale, Mozart introduces five distinct thematic subjects. The first (the four-note rising figure) is the most prominent; the others are subsidiary but each is given its own moment of clear statement. Listen for each subject's first appearance.

  3. **The closing combination.** At the end of the finale, all five subjects sound simultaneously — in invertible counterpoint, meaning each subject can appear in any voice (soprano, alto, tenor, bass) and the harmony still works. The closing combination is the moment of structural revelation; you will recognize subjects you have heard separately now coming together. The technical feat is the passage analysts return to whenever they discuss late-eighteenth-century counterpoint.

#### One-sentence background (K. 550)

Completed July 25, 1788 (autograph dated); about thirty minutes across four movements, the first movement runs about eight minutes; the **Primary Recording** for K. 550 in this chapter is Sir Charles Mackerras with the Scottish Chamber Orchestra (Telarc, 1986). (Karl Böhm with the Berlin Philharmonic on Deutsche Grammophon, 1960s, is the warmer older alternative; Bruno Walter with the New York Philharmonic on Columbia/Sony, 1953, is the classic mid-century reading; John Eliot Gardiner with the English Baroque Soloists on Philips, 1990, is the period-instrument alternative.)

#### One-sentence background (K. 551)

Completed August 10, 1788 (autograph dated); about thirty minutes across four movements, the finale runs about eight to nine minutes; the **Primary Recording** for K. 551 in this chapter is Sir Charles Mackerras with the Scottish Chamber Orchestra (Telarc, 1986). (Otto Klemperer with the Philharmonia Orchestra on Columbia/EMI, 1962, is the monumental mid-century alternative; Leonard Bernstein with the New York Philharmonic on Columbia/Sony, 1960s, is the warmer modern-instrument alternative; Trevor Pinnock with the English Concert on Archiv, 1990s, is the period-instrument alternative.)

#### Search Hints

EN (K. 550): "Mozart Symphony 40 K 550 Mackerras Scottish Chamber" or "Mozart Symphony 40 Böhm Berlin Philharmonic" EN (K. 551): "Mozart Symphony 41 Jupiter K 551 Mackerras" or "Mozart Jupiter Klemperer Philharmonia" ZH (K. 550): "莫扎特 第40号交响曲 K550 麦凯拉斯" 或 "莫扎特 第40号交响曲 贝姆 柏林爱乐" ZH (K. 551): "莫扎特 第41号交响曲 朱庇特 K551 麦凯拉斯" 或 "莫扎特 朱庇特 克伦佩勒" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

### 10.2 The Listening Window — K. 550 First Movement

Timestamps below refer to the Mackerras Primary Recording.

**0:00-0:20 — The opening.** Violas pulse beneath in repeated eighth notes; violins enter on the second beat with the chromatic descending figure decorated with grace notes. No slow introduction; the symphony opens directly with the famous theme. The opening's accompaniment-and-melody texture is one of the simplest possible symphonic openings.

**The continuation of the first-theme group.** The first theme is restated and developed across the orchestral exposition. The wind writing is more restrained than in K. 543 — clarinets (in the revised version) and oboes carry occasional melodic interjections but the texture is dominated by strings. The chromatic descending figure recurs in many guises.

**The exposition's second-theme group.** Mozart moves to B♭ major (the relative major) for the second theme. The contrast in mood is brief; the major-key relief is conditional, and the music returns to G minor before the exposition closes.

**The development.** Mozart takes the first-theme material through several keys before returning to G minor for the recapitulation. The chromatic descending figure dominates the development; Mozart explores its harmonic possibilities through extended modulation.

**The recapitulation.** First-theme group returns in G minor; second-theme group returns in G minor (the home-key rule, casting what had been B♭ major's brightness back into the dark home key).

**The coda.** Brief and dark. The G-minor mode is reaffirmed without major-mode resolution.

### 10.3 The Listening Window — K. 551 Fourth-Movement Finale

Timestamps below refer to the Mackerras Primary Recording.

**0:00-0:20 — The first subject.** Four-note rising figure: C, D, F, E. The figure is stated cleanly in the strings. The clean simplicity of the figure is part of its structural power — Mozart can combine it with the other subjects only because it is brief and unambiguous.

**The introduction of subsequent subjects.** Across the first few minutes of the finale, four more thematic subjects are introduced. Each gets its moment of clear statement. The five subjects are: (1) the four-note rising figure; (2) a stepwise descending line; (3) a syncopated rising figure; (4) a falling-fifth gesture; (5) a closing figure. Listen for each as it enters.

**The development.** Mozart takes the subjects through various keys and combinations. The contrapuntal density increases through the development as subjects are paired and re-paired.

**The recapitulation.** The five subjects return in C major (the home-key rule). The contrapuntal density continues to build.

**The closing combination.** At the end of the finale, all five subjects sound simultaneously in invertible counterpoint. The moment is the structural revelation that has been prepared by the entire movement. Listen for the moment all five voices enter their final combination; the texture becomes dense but the underlying harmonic logic remains clear. The Jupiter finale's closing combination is the moment Mozart pulls together what he had been building.

**The coda.** Brief affirmation of C major. The movement ends with full orchestral force.

### 10.4 What's Happening in the Music

#### Why K. 550 is the dark-key climax

K. 550 is the third major G-minor work in this volume (after K. 183 of Chapter 2; before K. 516 of Chapter 17). It is the most mature G-minor symphony in Mozart's catalog and is the principal piece of evidence for the dark-key signature this volume has been tracking.

What makes K. 550 the dark-key climax of the symphonic cycle: the chromatic descending figure is the principal thematic material across all four movements (not just the first), giving the symphony a sustained grief-rhetoric register that no other Mozart symphony matches. The orchestration is leaner than the other late symphonies — no trumpets and timpani, contributing to the chamber-like darker color. The first-movement opening's accompaniment-and-melody simplicity directs attention to the chromatic line rather than to orchestral elaboration. The slow movement (Andante in E♭ major) and the minuet (G minor) maintain the dark register; the finale (Allegro assai in G minor) returns to the urgency of the first movement and closes without major-mode resolution.

The matched pair K. 183 + K. 550, fifteen years apart in the same key, is the most direct evidence the catalog offers for the dark-key signature as a sustained compositional concern across Mozart's life. Solomon, Sadie, and Rosen all make this matched pair central to the argument for G minor as a Mozart signature.

K. 550 is the dark-key climax of the symphonic literature in this volume. The thread will continue in K. 516 (Chapter 17, the chamber-music G-minor peer to K. 550) and will be collected in Chapter 19 §19.1.

#### Why the Jupiter finale is a structural marvel

The Jupiter finale's five-theme invertible counterpoint is the technical accomplishment the work is best known for. The procedure — multiple thematic subjects combined simultaneously in counterpoint with each subject preserved as a recognizable line — is called "invertible counterpoint" because the subjects can be moved to any voice in the texture and the harmony still works. The technique has a long contrapuntal-music tradition (Johann Sebastian Bach wrote extensively in invertible counterpoint, particularly in _The Art of Fugue_ and the _Musical Offering_), but its use in a sonata-form Allegro finale of a Classical-era symphony is rare. Charles Rosen, in _The Classical Style_ , identified the Jupiter finale as one of the structural marvels of the period for combining the contrapuntal technique with the sonata-form structure.

What makes the procedure marvelous and not merely impressive: the five subjects sound natural and lyric when stated separately. They do not sound like contrapuntal exercises; they sound like ordinary melodic material. When they combine at the finale's close, the combination feels inevitable rather than forced — as if Mozart had designed the subjects all along to fit together, which is what he had in fact done. The compositional planning required to write five subjects that each sound natural alone and that combine in invertible counterpoint is itself a structural marvel; the rhetorical force of the combination — the sense that these five themes have arrived at their predestined union — is what gives the finale its emotional force beyond its technical accomplishment.

The Jupiter finale is the last symphonic finale Mozart composed; it is the work in which his symphonic writing reached its most ambitious structural form. Charles Rosen's identification of it as one of the structural marvels of the Classical period is the standard scholarly view and has not been seriously contested.

#### The summer-1788 trilogy question, revisited

Chapter 9 raised the question of whether the three summer-1788 symphonies were intended as a unified cycle. Reviewing now with all three works in mind:

K. 543 (E♭ major, June 1788) is the lyric, expansive, wind-rich symphony. K. 550 (G minor, July 1788) is the dark, rhythmically urgent, leaner-textured symphony. K. 551 (C major, August 1788) is the structurally ambitious, contrapuntal, ceremonial symphony.

The three have complementary characters that suggest deliberate contrast. If the three were intended as a cycle, the cycle would be a three-mood symphonic statement — lyric, dark, ceremonial — across three different keys (E♭ major, G minor, C major) and three different orchestral characters. The matched-trilogy hypothesis has the appeal of structural symmetry; it accounts for the complementary characters.

But the absence of any surviving Mozart letter or document referring to the three as a cycle, and the absence of a Mozart-arranged premiere of all three together in his lifetime, makes the cycle hypothesis contestable. Mozart frequently composed in groups (the Haydn Quartets, the Prussian Quartets, the piano trios K. 542/548/564); but he did not always conceive groups as performance cycles. The Haydn Quartets were a publication set (six quartets dedicated to Haydn, published together in 1785); they were not a performance cycle in the sense that all six were normally performed in a single sitting. The three summer-1788 symphonies could similarly have been three works composed in succession for various potential performance contexts (a planned Vienna subscription concert that did not materialize, possible Frankfurt or Leipzig performances during Mozart's 1789-90 travels) without being intended for performance together as a cycle.

The honest position remains: we do not know whether Mozart intended the three as a cycle. The compositional context is consistent with either reading. Modern programming sometimes performs the three together (the trilogy as concert program); sometimes performs them separately. Both are legitimate.

#### The performance question

There is no clear contemporary record that Mozart heard any of the three summer-1788 symphonies performed in his lifetime. The argument for likely performances: Mozart announced a Vienna concert in June 1788 (an academy concert at the Casino in Vienna) that may have included new symphonic material; the 1789 Mozart trip to Berlin and Dresden produced concert appearances that may have included new symphonies; the 1790 trip to Frankfurt for the coronation festival included Mozart's own concerts that may have included the late symphonies. The argument for likely non-performance: the surviving concert announcements and contemporary reviews from these events do not specifically name K. 543, K. 550, or K. 551; the references are vague and do not allow secure identification of which symphonies were performed.

What we can say: the summer-1788 symphonies entered the active concert repertoire after Mozart's death rather than during his lifetime. The 1790s and early 1800s saw multiple performances; by the early nineteenth century the late three were standard repertoire. The fact that Mozart may not have heard them is one of the small but striking facts of his late biography — the symphonies he composed at the height of his powers may not have been performed in his lifetime by anyone, including himself.

If the non-performance reading is correct (the conservative reading among the contested possibilities), it sharpens the question of why Mozart composed them. The answer scholars favor: Mozart composed them because the compositional impulse required them, not because a specific performance occasion required them. The freelance composer's compositional life sometimes ran ahead of the performance economics; the summer-1788 symphonies may be the principal example of this gap in Mozart's career.

### 10.5 The Stories

#### The 1788 Vienna context

The economic and biographical context of 1788 in Vienna has been touched on in Chapters 8 and 9. To consolidate: the Austro-Turkish War was declared February 1788; the Vienna economy contracted across spring and summer 1788; aristocratic subscription audiences for concerts reduced their spending; Mozart's own subscription concert series had effectively ceased by 1789. Mozart's first Puchberg loan letter was written June 17, 1788 (the day the family moved to the Alsergrund apartment). The summer-1788 symphonies were composed in this context.

The economic pressures of 1788 did not break Mozart's compositional output — the three symphonies are evidence that the compositional work continued at the highest level — but they did reshape his professional life. The piano concerto career effectively ended (K. 537 of February 1788 was the second-to-last piano concerto; K. 595 of January 1791 would be the last). The subscription concert series ended. The opera commissions (Don Giovanni 1787, Così fan tutte 1790, Magic Flute 1791) became the principal vehicle of Mozart's compositional public life. Teaching income became more central. The Imperial Chamber Composer appointment of December 1787 (a salaried court position) provided a baseline income that softened the contraction but did not fully compensate for the lost subscription income.

The years 1788-91 were therefore a transitional professional period for Mozart. The compositional output remained at the highest level; the financial picture tightened; the freelance career model that had worked in 1782-86 was no longer adequate. The summer-1788 symphonies sit at the start of this transition.

#### The K. 550 clarinet revision

Mozart originally composed K. 550 without clarinets in the wind section — the wind complement was flutes, oboes, bassoons, and horns. Sometime after the original composition (the exact date is contested; conventional dating places the revision in 1791, late in Mozart's life), Mozart revised K. 550 to add two clarinets to the wind complement. The revision is mostly an adjustment of the oboe parts (the clarinets take over some of the original oboe material; the oboes are reduced or eliminated in the corresponding passages); the underlying compositional structure is unchanged.

Modern performances normally play the revised version with clarinets. Some period-instrument recordings (Christopher Hogwood's Academy of Ancient Music reading; some others) record both versions for comparison. The clarinet revision softens the wind color slightly and gives the work a marginally richer wind texture; the underlying dark-key character is unchanged.

Both versions are legitimate Mozart. The clarinet revision is part of the work's authorial history; the original-no-clarinets version represents the work as Mozart first composed it. Most recordings use the revised version; if you have the option to hear both, the comparison is illuminating.

#### Recording history of K. 550 and K. 551

Both symphonies have accumulated deep recording histories. The standard references:

Karl Böhm with the Berlin Philharmonic and the Vienna Philharmonic (Deutsche Grammophon, 1960s-70s) is the warmer mid-century European tradition reference for both works.

Sir Charles Mackerras with the Scottish Chamber Orchestra (Telarc, 1986, the chapter's Primary Recording) is the leading modern reference. Mackerras's readings are tempo-historical informed but use modern instruments.

Trevor Pinnock with the English Concert (Deutsche Grammophon Archiv, 1990s) is the period-instrument cycle reference.

John Eliot Gardiner with the English Baroque Soloists (Philips, 1990) is the period-instrument alternative to Pinnock; Gardiner's readings are more dramatic.

Otto Klemperer with the Philharmonia Orchestra (Columbia/EMI, early 1960s) is the monumental mid-century alternative — slower tempos, fuller orchestral weight. Klemperer's K. 551 in particular is famous for its architectural seriousness and is one of the central twentieth-century interpretations of the Jupiter.

Leonard Bernstein with the New York Philharmonic and the Vienna Philharmonic (Columbia/Sony 1960s; Deutsche Grammophon 1980s) is the warmer modern-instrument alternative.

Bruno Walter with the New York Philharmonic (Columbia/Sony, 1953) is the historic reference for K. 550. Walter's reading is one of the most cherished by listeners who prefer the older Vienna tradition.

Nikolaus Harnoncourt with the Concertgebouw Orchestra (Teldec, 1991) is the modern period-instrument-aware reading on a major modern orchestra; Harnoncourt's K. 550 and K. 551 are admired for the rhythmic clarity and the chamber-music balance.

George Szell with the Cleveland Orchestra (Columbia/Sony, 1960s) is the precision-and-clarity reference; Szell's K. 551 is one of the most architecturally clean readings in the catalog.

The Teodor Currentzis / MusicAeterna readings (Sony, 2014) are excellent and now well within the ten-year safeguard threshold; they may be recommended without qualification.

### Conversation Seeds

How does the mature K. 550 work the same dark key that the seventeen-year-old K. 183 had first explored?"_

Does the contrapuntal density obscure the underlying logic, or does the logic remain audible?"_

If Mozart composed K. 543, K. 550, K. 551 without a specific performance occasion in view, what does that tell you about his compositional motivation in the late freelance period? Why would a composer in financial pressure write three symphonies for no specific performance?"_

Solomon and Rosen both make the pair central to the dark-key argument. What does it mean for a key to function as a composer's signature? Is the relationship between Mozart and G minor a deliberate compositional choice, an unconscious gravitational pull, or some combination?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find Mackerras / Scottish Chamber Orchestra (Telarc, 1986). Listen to K. 550's first movement and K. 551's finale a second time and attend to four concrete moments:

  1. **K. 550 opening (the first thirty seconds).** Confirm: violas pulse beneath in repeated eighth notes; violins enter on the second beat with the chromatic descending figure decorated with grace notes. The accompaniment-and-melody texture is one of the simplest possible symphonic openings; the simplicity directs attention to the chromatic line.

  2. **K. 550 development section.** Listen for the chromatic descending figure as it dominates the development through extended modulation. Mozart takes the figure through many keys; the modulation is the structural argument of the development.

  3. **K. 551 finale, the introduction of the five subjects.** Listen for the four-note rising figure (the first subject) at the very opening. Then listen for the subsequent four subjects as they enter across the first few minutes. The five subjects are each given their moment of clear statement before the combinations begin.

  4. **K. 551 finale closing combination.** At the end of the finale, all five subjects sound simultaneously in invertible counterpoint. Try to hear at least two of the five subjects as distinct lines within the combination. The closing combination is the structural revelation the entire finale has been building toward.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : pair K. 550 with the String Quintet in G minor K. 516 (1787, Chapter 17 of this volume). K. 516 is the chamber-music peer of K. 550 in G minor; the two works share the dark-key signature and the chromatic-descending material. The Pinchas Zukerman / Guarneri Quartet recording of K. 516 (Sony, 1991) and the Hagen Quartet recording (Deutsche Grammophon, 1998) are both standard. Hearing K. 550 and K. 516 in the same sitting gives the fullest sense of Mozart in G minor.

**Read** : Charles Rosen, _The Classical Style: Haydn, Mozart, Beethoven_ (Norton, 1971; expanded edition 1997). Chapter 5 ("Mozart") includes Rosen's treatment of the Jupiter finale's five-theme combination as a structural marvel. Rosen's analytical voice is the source-of-record for both K. 550 and K. 551.

## Chapter 11: _Le nozze di Figaro_ K. 492 (1786)

_Le nozze di Figaro_ premiered at the Vienna Burgtheater on May 1, 1786. It was Mozart's first collaboration with the librettist Lorenzo Da Ponte and the first of the three Mozart-Da Ponte operas (_Don Giovanni_ of 1787 and _Così fan tutte_ of 1790 would complete the trilogy). It launched what is conventionally called the Da Ponte trilogy — three Italian-language _opera buffa_ (comic opera) collaborations that together form a coherent three-opera sequence by a single composer-librettist pair.

_Figaro_ sits at the structural and emotional center of Mozart's mature operatic output. It is the opera in which Mozart's distinctive ensemble-finale form first appears in full development (the Act II finale runs about twenty minutes of continuous music with characters entering, exiting, and singing across each other; the Act IV finale runs about twenty minutes more in a similarly continuous structure). It is the opera in which Mozart's mature vocal characterization first reaches its full range — multiple distinct characters each given music that distinguishes them as specific personalities rather than as type characters. And it is the opera that established Mozart's reputation in Vienna and across the German-speaking world as the leading composer of his generation in the _opera buffa_ genre.

This chapter anchors on the two great ensemble finales, Act II and Act IV, as the principal structural anchor points. The chapter does not attempt to walk through the full opera scene by scene (a complete _Figaro_ runs about three hours and contains thirty-some musical numbers); a complete-opera walkthrough belongs to a different kind of book. The Conversation Seeds and Going Deeper sections point toward other parts of the opera worth following.

### 11.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

April 1786. Vienna. The Viennese censor's office was, according to Lorenzo Da Ponte's later memoirs, deeply uncertain about the planned premiere of Mozart's setting of Pierre-Augustin Caron de Beaumarchais's play _La Folle Journée, ou Le Mariage de Figaro_. Beaumarchais's play (first performed in Paris in April 1784, two years before the Mozart-Da Ponte opera) was politically explosive in its original French version — it portrayed a servant outwitting his aristocratic master in romantic intrigue, and the play was widely understood as a critique of aristocratic privilege in the years before the French Revolution. The Habsburg Emperor Joseph II had banned the Beaumarchais play from performance in Vienna in 1785, presumably for its political content.

The Mozart-Da Ponte opera adaptation removed most of the explicitly political content while preserving the dramatic structure (the servant Figaro and his fiancée Susanna outwitting Count Almaviva's attempts to exercise the _droit du seigneur_ on Susanna; the Countess and Cherubino subplots). Da Ponte's memoirs describe a personal visit to the Emperor in which Da Ponte made the case for permitting the opera; Joseph II read the libretto and approved it for performance. The opera premiered at the Burgtheater on May 1, 1786 under Mozart's own direction from the keyboard.

The Vienna reception was favorable but not overwhelming — the opera was performed nine times in 1786 at the Burgtheater, which was respectable but not the runaway success Mozart and Da Ponte had hoped for. The Prague reception in winter 1786-87 (anchored in Chapter 9 as part of the K. 504 "Prague" symphony context) was much warmer — Prague took _Figaro_ enthusiastically and it became one of the principal works the Prague theater associated with Mozart. The Prague success contributed to the _Don Giovanni_ commission for the next year.

The Da Ponte memoirs (the _Memorie_ of 1823-30, written when Da Ponte was in his seventies and living in New York where he had emigrated) are partly reliable and partly self-aggrandizing; the Joseph II audience for the _Figaro_ approval is reasonably attested in the broader Vienna theater records of the period.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen (Act II finale)

  1. **The opening.** The Act II finale begins with the Count knocking on the closet door, demanding that the Countess open it — he is convinced a man is hidden inside. (The Countess's two arias bracket different acts: "Porgi, amor" opens Act II; "Dove sono i bei momenti" comes in Act III.) Listen for how Mozart starts the finale at a specific dramatic moment and builds from there.

  2. **The continuous form.** The Act II finale runs about twenty minutes without breaks. Characters enter and exit; the musical material shifts as new characters arrive; the harmonic and rhythmic structure continually adjusts. The finale is one continuous musical span rather than a sequence of separate numbers. Listen for the moments of arrival and departure — each new character's entry produces a musical shift.

  3. **The ensemble layering.** By the end of the Act II finale, seven characters are singing simultaneously in interlocking parts. Listen for how Mozart distributes the lines — each character has their own dramatic concern (the Count is suspicious, Figaro is calculating, Susanna and the Countess are scheming, Marcellina and Bartolo and Don Basilio are pursuing their own subplots), and the music distinguishes the characters' simultaneous concerns through registration, rhythm, and melodic contour.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen (Act IV finale)

  1. **The garden scene setup.** Act IV takes place in the Almaviva castle garden at night. The Countess and Susanna have switched dresses; the Count, Cherubino, Figaro, and others are in the garden in various states of confusion about who is who. The finale starts with the various deceptions in play and works toward the reconciliation at the end.

  2. **The Count's plea.** Near the end of the finale, the Count discovers the deception (the "Susanna" he has been pursuing is actually his wife the Countess in disguise) and asks his wife for forgiveness. The Countess's response — "Più docile io sono, e dico di sì" ("I am more docile, and say yes") — is the opera's emotional turning point. The music shifts at this moment into a quiet, sustained, deeply lyric register that contrasts with the comic chaos that has preceded it.

  3. **The closing ensemble.** All the characters join in a closing affirmation of the reconciliation. The music returns to the bright D-major key in which the opera ends. Listen for the way the closing ensemble combines the multiple characters' voices into a single resolving statement — the structural reversal of the Act II finale's accumulating ensemble.

#### One-sentence background

Composed in Vienna, autumn 1785 through spring 1786 (premiered May 1, 1786); the complete opera runs about three hours; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Sir Charles Mackerras with the Scottish Chamber Orchestra (Telarc, 1994), the modern reference complete recording with a strong international cast (Bryn Terfel as Figaro, Alison Hagley as Susanna, Renée Fleming as the Countess). (Karl Böhm with the Deutsche Oper Berlin on Deutsche Grammophon, 1968, is the warmer mid-century alternative with Hermann Prey, Edith Mathis, and Gundula Janowitz; Sir John Eliot Gardiner with the English Baroque Soloists on Archiv, 1993, is the period-instrument alternative.)
