EN: "Mozart Figaro K 492 Mackerras Terfel" or "Mozart Le nozze di Figaro Böhm Prey Mathis" ZH: "莫扎特 费加罗的婚礼 K492 麦凯拉斯 特菲尔" 或 "莫扎特 费加罗的婚礼 贝姆" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

### 11.2 The Listening Window — Act II Finale

The Act II finale begins with the Count returning home to discover that Cherubino (a young page in the Count's household, who has been pursuing the Countess and getting into various scrapes) has been hidden in the Countess's bedroom. The Count, suspicious of his wife, demands to know what is in the locked closet adjacent to her bedroom. The finale proper begins here.

**The Count's demand and the Countess's evasions.** Count Almaviva, suspicious, knocks on the closet door and demands that the Countess open it. The Countess, having helped Cherubino into the closet earlier, attempts to evade. The musical texture is duet — Count and Countess — with the Count's musical material assertive and the Countess's evasive.

**Susanna's secret entry into the closet.** While the Count is fetching tools to break down the door, Susanna (the Countess's maid and Figaro's fiancée) slips into the closet to take Cherubino's place. Cherubino jumps from the window into the garden below. When the Count returns and breaks open the closet door, Susanna emerges — to the Count's confusion. The musical texture is duet again — Count and Susanna — with the Count's confusion now the principal dramatic material. _Listening anchor:_ the door swings open and Susanna walks out unhurriedly to a sudden bright shift in the orchestra; she addresses the Count and Countess with mock-formal politeness ("Signore! Cos'è quel stupore?" — "My lord! Why so astonished?"). The musical reversal — from the agitated buildup of the Count's tool-fetching threat to the lyric calm of Susanna's emergence — is one of the clearest pivot points in the finale, and the easiest to track on first listen.

**The Count's apology and the Countess's reproach.** The Count, having found Susanna in the closet and not Cherubino, must apologize to his wife for having suspected her. The Countess reproaches him. The musical texture is trio — Count, Countess, Susanna — with the three characters' concerns now interleaving.

**The Gardener Antonio's entrance.** The gardener Antonio bursts in to complain that someone has jumped from the window into the garden and trampled his carnations. The Count's suspicions are re-aroused; Figaro must explain (it was him, he says, jumping from the window). The musical texture is quartet — Count, Countess, Susanna, Figaro — with Figaro's improvised explanations the principal dramatic material. _Listening anchor:_ Antonio's arrival is one of the easiest tempo shifts in the finale to hear. The continuous ensemble has been building in a quick triple meter; Antonio interrupts in a heavier duple, the orchestral texture thickens, and his complaint ("Ah, signor… signor…") tumbles out in lurching, half-drunk phrases. Listen for the orchestral pivot at his entry — a clear stop-and-restart that resets the finale's momentum and gives Figaro his improvisation cue.

**The arrival of Marcellina, Bartolo, and Don Basilio.** Three additional characters arrive, each with their own dramatic concerns. Marcellina is pursuing a marriage contract with Figaro (long story; the marriage contract is the principal Act III subplot); Bartolo is supporting Marcellina; Don Basilio is the local music master and intriguer. The texture expands to seven voices — Count, Countess, Susanna, Figaro, Marcellina, Bartolo, Basilio — all singing simultaneously in interlocking parts. The finale's accumulating ensemble reaches its full seven-voice texture.

**The closing of Act II.** The seven characters' simultaneous singing builds to a climax; the Count's suspicions are now uncontainable; the Act curtain falls on the seven-voice ensemble with all dramatic threads suspended. The unresolved structural tension is the principal dramatic engine of Act III, which will follow.

The Act II finale runs approximately twenty minutes as a single continuous musical span. It is one of the principal pieces of evidence for what Charles Rosen and Daniel Heartz both called the Mozart ensemble finale — the multi-character interlocking form that Mozart developed in his late operas and that became one of his distinctive operatic devices.

### 11.3 The Listening Window — Act IV Finale

The Act IV finale takes place in the Almaviva castle garden at night. Multiple deceptions are in play: the Countess and Susanna have switched dresses so that the Count will pursue the wrong "Susanna" (actually his wife in disguise) and reveal his attempted infidelity. Figaro, not in on the deception, briefly believes Susanna has betrayed him. Cherubino is also in the garden. The various confusions resolve across the finale.

**The opening — Figaro's misperception.** Figaro, observing what he believes to be Susanna receiving the Count's attentions, falls into jealous despair. The musical material is dark, almost minor-key.

**Figaro's discovery and the deception's resolution for him.** Susanna reveals herself to Figaro (in her own voice, in a moment of recognition). Figaro joins the deception against the Count. The musical material shifts to a teasing, mock-jealous register as Figaro plays along with the disguise game.

**The Count's pursuit of the disguised Countess.** The Count, believing he is courting Susanna, pursues the woman in Susanna's dress (actually the Countess). The musical material is lighter and more lyric, building toward the deception's climax.

**The Count's discovery.** The Count discovers his wife in Susanna's dress. The deception is revealed. The Count has been caught attempting infidelity with what he believed to be Susanna. The musical material pauses; the dramatic tension reaches its peak.

**The Count's plea for forgiveness.** The Count kneels and asks the Countess for forgiveness: "Contessa, perdono!" ("Countess, forgive me!"). The Countess's response — "Più docile io sono, e dico di sì" ("I am more docile, and say yes") — carries the structural weight of the entire opera. The music shifts at this moment into a quiet, sustained, deeply lyric register. The forgiveness is granted; the dramatic tension dissolves into reconciliation.

The Countess's forgiveness aria is structurally and emotionally the core of the entire opera. The Count has been pursuing, scheming, threatening, suspecting through four acts. The Countess has been the wronged party who has been quietly maintaining her dignity. At the moment of forgiveness, the music carries the full weight of the dramatic situation — a marriage that has been damaged but might be repaired, an injury that has been suffered but is being released. The music's quietness and lyric depth are the rhetorical means by which the moment achieves its weight.

**The closing ensemble.** All the characters join in a closing affirmation of the reconciliation. The music returns to D major and to confident closing material. The closing ensemble combines the multiple characters' voices into a single resolving statement.

The Act IV finale runs approximately twenty minutes, similar in scale to the Act II finale. The structural reversal — Act II's accumulating ensemble of unresolved tension versus Act IV's dissolving ensemble of reconciliation — is one of the principal architectural achievements of the opera.

### 11.4 What's Happening in the Music

#### Lorenzo Da Ponte (1749-1838) as librettist

Lorenzo Da Ponte was the Imperial Poet (the official librettist of the Vienna court theaters under Emperor Joseph II) from 1783 to 1790. He had been born in Veneto, Italy, in 1749; his early life included priesthood (he was an ordained Catholic priest), an exile from Venice for various scandals, residence in Vienna from 1781, and the imperial appointment. His operatic libretti for Mozart (_Figaro_ 1786, _Don Giovanni_ 1787, _Così fan tutte_ 1790) and for other composers including Antonio Salieri (_Axur, re d'Ormus_ 1788) and Vicente Martín y Soler (_Una cosa rara_ 1786) established his reputation as one of the major libretto writers of the period.

Da Ponte's distinctive qualities as a librettist: he wrote Italian verse of high formal quality (he was a poet of substantial training, not merely a theatrical hack); he had a sharp dramatic sense (the Mozart-Da Ponte operas have plot structures that work as drama as well as music); he was politically supple (the _Figaro_ adaptation that removed the politically dangerous elements of Beaumarchais's play while preserving the dramatic engine was Da Ponte's work). His relationship with Mozart across the three operas was collaborative and apparently warm; the two worked closely on libretto revisions during composition.

Da Ponte's post-Mozart career is one of the curiosities of opera history. He was dismissed from the Vienna court in 1790 (following Emperor Joseph II's death and the political shift under Leopold II); he moved to London in 1792 where he worked at the King's Theatre; he moved to New York in 1805 where he ran a grocery store and eventually a millinery shop, taught Italian at Columbia College, and helped establish the first Italian opera company in New York (the Italian Opera House, opened in 1833). He died in New York in 1838 at age eighty-nine. His memoirs (_Memorie_ , 1823-30) are partly reliable and partly self-aggrandizing but are a principal source for the early-1780s and 1786-90 Vienna operatic culture.

#### The Mozart ensemble finale form

The Mozart ensemble finale is the structural innovation for which the Mozart-Da Ponte operas (and the earlier _Le nozze di Figaro_ especially) are best known in operatic history. The form had antecedents — Italian _opera buffa_ composers including Pasquale Anfossi, Giovanni Paisiello, and Domenico Cimarosa had been developing multi-character ensemble finales across the 1760s and 1770s — but Mozart's _Figaro_ finales (Act II and Act IV) are the works in which the ensemble finale form reached its mature compositional development.

The structural principles of the Mozart ensemble finale: (a) the finale is a single continuous musical span rather than a sequence of separate numbers; (b) the finale typically begins with a small ensemble (duet or trio) and expands as new characters arrive; (c) each new character's entry produces a musical shift — new tempo, new key, new texture; (d) by the finale's climax, all the principal characters are singing simultaneously in interlocking parts; (e) each character's musical material is distinctive enough that the listener can hear the multiple voices as a multi-character ensemble rather than as a single chorus; (f) the finale typically closes with a structural resolution that brings the dramatic threads to a (provisional, in the case of Act II; final, in the case of Act IV) point of arrival.

The Act II _Figaro_ finale and the Act IV _Figaro_ finale together constitute the principal early example of the Mozart ensemble finale. The form will recur in _Don Giovanni_ (Chapter 12, particularly the Act II finale supper scene), in _Così fan tutte_ (Chapter 13), and in _Die Zauberflöte_ (Chapter 14, though the _Singspiel_ form modifies the ensemble structure somewhat). Chapter 19 §19.5 collects the ensemble finale instances as one of the Thread B features.

#### Why the Countess's forgiveness moment matters

The Countess's forgiveness — "Più docile io sono, e dico di sì" — is the emotional center of _Figaro_ and is often cited as the principal moment in which Mozart's operatic mature style first reached its full emotional depth. The musical means are simple: the texture quiets, the harmonic register softens, the vocal line spans a long lyric phrase that gathers all the previous dramatic tension into a single moment of resolution.

What makes the moment work dramatically: the Countess has been the wronged party through four acts. She has maintained dignity, planned the disguise scheme, faced humiliation, prayed for renewed love. At the moment of the Count's discovery and plea, she does not refuse, she does not lecture, she does not extract conditions. She forgives. The forgiveness is freely given.

The music carries the moment's weight by being quiet rather than triumphant. A composer of lesser dramatic sense might have written the forgiveness as a major-key fanfare of reconciliation; Mozart writes it as a sustained, lyric, almost private response. The intimacy of the music is the dramatic point — the forgiveness is intimate, between husband and wife, not a public ceremony. The closing ensemble that follows brings the public reconciliation; the Countess's intimate forgiveness has earned it.

The Countess in _Figaro_ is the role through which Mozart's mature operatic understanding of female character first reaches full development. The Countess is not a type (the noble wife, the wronged spouse) but a specific person — emotionally complex, dramatically capable, vocally distinctive. The Mozart-Da Ponte operas would continue to develop multiple female characters of similar specificity (Donna Anna and Donna Elvira and Zerlina in _Don Giovanni_ ; Fiordiligi and Dorabella and Despina in _Così fan tutte_). The Countess is the first of this lineage.

#### Cherubino as Mozart's first ambiguous character

Cherubino — the young page, sung by a soprano or mezzo-soprano in trouser-role tradition (a female singer playing a male character) — is one of Mozart's most distinctive vocal characterizations. The role's vocal range and tessitura match the adolescent boy's voice the character represents; the trouser-role convention allows a female singer to play the boy with vocal flexibility a young male singer would lack. Cherubino's aria "Voi che sapete" (Act II) is a standard mezzo-soprano recital piece.

Cherubino's dramatic function is as the embodiment of adolescent eros — the young page is pursuing every woman in the household (the Countess, Susanna, the gardener's daughter Barbarina) in undifferentiated romantic enthusiasm. The trouser-role convention adds a layer of theatrical play; the audience watches a woman playing a boy pursuing women, with the gender-play partly the dramatic point. _Figaro_ is the first Mozart opera to deploy this kind of vocal-gender ambiguity as a dramatic resource; _Idomeneo_ (Chapter 13, the 1781 opera seria) had a different vocal convention (castrato male roles being sung by women in modern revivals), and the Mozart-Da Ponte operas developed the trouser role most extensively.

### 11.5 The Stories

#### _Figaro_ in Vienna and Prague — the two receptions

_Figaro_ premiered in Vienna on May 1, 1786 and was performed nine times that year. The Vienna reception was favorable but not overwhelming; the opera was not the runaway success Mozart and Da Ponte had hoped for. Several factors contributed to the muted reception: Vincente Martín y Soler's _Una cosa rara_ (also with a Da Ponte libretto, premiered November 17, 1786) became Vienna's overwhelming hit of the 1786-87 season and crowded _Figaro_ out of the public attention; the Vienna Italian-opera audience had complex political and aesthetic tastes that did not always favor Mozart's denser scoring; the _Figaro_ dramatic content, even with the political elements removed, was still topically pointed for Vienna audiences.

_Figaro_ opened in Prague in December 1786 and was received with the enthusiasm that became the legendary Mozart-Prague relationship. By Mozart's January 1787 visit to Prague (Chapter 9), the city was reportedly whistling _Figaro_ arias in the streets. The Prague theater director Pasquale Bondini commissioned _Don Giovanni_ on the strength of the _Figaro_ success — the commission that produced the second Da Ponte opera.

The two-city reception is sometimes used in popular biography to argue that Vienna was hostile to Mozart and Prague was warm. The fuller picture is more nuanced — Vienna received _Figaro_ favorably (nine performances in a season is a respectable run), and Prague's enthusiasm was partly a function of the city's smaller and less politicized musical economy. But the contrast is real: Prague embraced Mozart in a way Vienna sometimes withheld, and the Prague enthusiasm shaped Mozart's late operatic career (the _Don Giovanni_ commission, the 1791 _La clemenza di Tito_ commission for the Bohemian coronation festival).

#### The Beaumarchais source and the political dimension

Pierre-Augustin Caron de Beaumarchais (1732-1799) was a French playwright whose Figaro plays — _Le Barbier de Séville_ (1775) and _La Folle Journée, ou Le Mariage de Figaro_ (premiered Paris 1784) — were among the most-discussed dramatic works of the pre-Revolutionary French theater. The _Marriage of Figaro_ play was widely read as a critique of aristocratic privilege — particularly the _droit du seigneur_ (the legal right of the local lord to sleep with a peasant bride on her wedding night, a real medieval practice that was largely defunct by the eighteenth century but that retained symbolic force). The play's Figaro speech in Act V — the "What have you done [as an aristocrat] to deserve so many privileges?" monologue — was directly understood as anti-aristocratic critique and was central to the play's pre-Revolutionary political reception.

The Mozart-Da Ponte adaptation removed most of the explicitly political content. The Figaro speech of Beaumarchais's Act V was cut; the _droit du seigneur_ element was retained but presented as the Count's personal failing rather than as a systemic aristocratic injustice; the broader social-class commentary was softened. The dramatic engine (servant outwitting master in romantic intrigue) was preserved; the political polemic was attenuated.

What remains in the opera is still socially pointed by 1786 standards. The Count's attempted exercise of _droit du seigneur_ over Susanna is shown as morally wrong; the servants (Figaro, Susanna, the household women) are shown as more morally astute than their master; the Countess's forgiveness of the Count is granted but not earned by his behavior. The Mozart-Da Ponte _Figaro_ is not a revolutionary opera in the sense Beaumarchais's play was a revolutionary play, but it is not a politically neutral opera either. It is sharp comedy with a social-class edge.

The political dimension of _Figaro_ — what it preserved from Beaumarchais and what it modified — is one of the principal topics of late-twentieth-century _Figaro_ scholarship. Daniel Heartz, Cliff Eisen, and Robert W. Gutman all treat the question; the scholarly consensus is that the opera is socially aware but politically softened, and that the softening was both a censorship necessity and a dramatic choice that focused the work on the personal-emotional dimensions rather than the political ones.

#### Recording history of _Figaro_

_Figaro_ has a deep recorded catalogue. The complete-recording references:

Sir Charles Mackerras with the Scottish Chamber Orchestra (Telarc, 1994, the chapter's Primary Recording) features Bryn Terfel as Figaro, Alison Hagley as Susanna, Renée Fleming as the Countess, Cecilia Bartoli as Cherubino, Alastair Miles as the Count. The reading is tempo-historical informed but uses modern instruments; the cast is one of the strongest international assemblies of the 1990s.

Karl Böhm with the Deutsche Oper Berlin (Deutsche Grammophon, 1968) is the warmer mid-century reference. Hermann Prey as Figaro, Edith Mathis as Susanna, Gundula Janowitz as the Countess, Tatiana Troyanos as Cherubino, Dietrich Fischer-Dieskau as the Count. The Vienna-tradition warmth is at its peak; the cast is one of the standard reference assemblies of the LP era.

Sir John Eliot Gardiner with the English Baroque Soloists (Archiv, 1993) is the period-instrument complete reference. The reading uses period orchestra and tempos consistent with 1786 practice; the dramatic vitality is higher than some period-instrument readings achieve.

Sir Colin Davis with the Royal Opera House Covent Garden (Philips, 1971) is the older modern-instrument reference. Geraint Evans as Figaro, Mirella Freni as Susanna, Jessye Norman as the Countess, Yvonne Minton as Cherubino, Wladimiro Ganzarolli as the Count.

Erich Kleiber with the Vienna Philharmonic (Decca, 1955) is the historic reference. The mono recording is well-preserved; the Vienna-tradition reading is one of the standard classic _Figaros_ of the LP era.

Claudio Abbado with the Vienna Philharmonic (Deutsche Grammophon, 1994) and Riccardo Muti with the Vienna Philharmonic (EMI, 1986, the bicentennial year of the opera) are additional modern references worth knowing.

The Carlo Maria Giulini Philharmonia recording (EMI, 1959-60) with Eberhard Wächter as the Count and Elisabeth Schwarzkopf as the Countess is the historic mid-twentieth-century reference for the principal-singer level.

René Jacobs's _Figaro_ with the Concerto Köln (Harmonia Mundi, 2003) is the contemporary period-instrument alternative to Gardiner; the reading is highly dramatic and has gathered strong reviews.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The Mozart ensemble finale — the multi-character interlocking form that the Act II and Act IV _Figaro_ finales most fully develop — was the structural innovation that made Mozart's late operatic style distinctive. What does the form let Mozart do dramatically that a sequence of separate arias and ensembles would not let him do? Listen to the Act II finale and trace the moments where new characters enter and the music shifts; what is the cumulative effect of the seven-voice closing texture?"_
* _"The Countess's forgiveness moment in the Act IV finale — 'Più docile io sono, e dico di sì' — is the emotional center of the opera. Mozart writes the forgiveness as a quiet, lyric, sustained moment rather than as a triumphant major-key fanfare. What does the quiet-rather-than-triumphant choice do dramatically? How does the music's intimacy carry the dramatic weight that a louder treatment would have squandered?"_
* _"Lorenzo Da Ponte adapted Pierre-Augustin Caron de Beaumarchais's politically explosive play into an opera libretto that the Vienna censor would permit. The adaptation removed most of the explicitly political content while preserving the dramatic engine. What did the political softening cost the opera dramatically, and what did it gain? Is the Mozart-Da Ponte _Figaro_ a politically neutral comedy or a socially-aware comedy with the political polemic softened?"_
* _"Cherubino — the young page sung by a soprano or mezzo-soprano in trouser-role tradition — is one of Mozart's most distinctive vocal characterizations. The trouser-role convention creates a layer of theatrical gender-play (a woman playing a boy pursuing women) that adds dramatic complexity. Listen to Cherubino's 'Voi che sapete' in Act II and identify what the role's vocal characterization gains from the trouser-role convention. Would the role work in the same way if it were composed for a young tenor or boy soprano?"_
* _"_Figaro_ premiered in Vienna in May 1786 and was received favorably but not overwhelmingly; the Prague reception in winter 1786-87 was warmer and led to the _Don Giovanni_ commission. What does the two-city reception tell you about the operatic markets Mozart was working in? Why did Prague embrace Mozart in a way Vienna sometimes withheld? Was the Vienna reception of _Figaro_ a failure, a moderate success, or something more complex?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find Mackerras / Scottish Chamber Orchestra (Telarc, 1994) or Böhm / Deutsche Oper Berlin (Deutsche Grammophon, 1968). Listen to the Act II finale and Act IV finale a second time and attend to four concrete moments:

  1. **Act II finale opening (the Count's demand at the closet door).** Confirm: the finale begins with the Count knocking and demanding entry; the Countess's evasions follow. The duet texture establishes the Count-Countess relationship at the finale's start; this will expand as new characters arrive.

  2. **Act II finale's seven-voice climax.** Listen for the moment when all seven characters (Count, Countess, Susanna, Figaro, Marcellina, Bartolo, Don Basilio) are singing simultaneously. Each character has their own dramatic concern; the music distinguishes the simultaneous concerns through registration, rhythm, and melodic contour. The unresolved tension is the principal dramatic engine carrying into Act III.

  3. **Act IV finale's Countess forgiveness.** Listen specifically for the moment when the Count kneels with "Contessa, perdono!" and the Countess responds "Più docile io sono, e dico di sì." The music shifts at this moment into a quiet, sustained, deeply lyric register. The forgiveness moment is the emotional center of the opera.

  4. **Act IV finale's closing ensemble.** All characters join in a closing affirmation. The music returns to D major and to confident closing material. Listen for how the closing ensemble combines the multiple characters' voices into a single resolving statement — the structural reversal of the Act II finale's accumulating ensemble.

  5. **Act III sextet "Riconosci in questo amplesso" (recognition scene).** In the middle of Act III, the long-running Marcellina subplot resolves: Marcellina's attempt to enforce her marriage contract against Figaro collapses when she discovers Figaro is her own long-lost son (Bartolo is his father). The sextet is the recognition scene — six characters absorbing the revelation. _Listening anchor:_ the dramatic hinge is the small two-note exchange when Marcellina embraces Figaro and the others react. The Count and Don Curzio (the lawyer) repeat an incredulous "Sua madre?" — "His mother?" — and Bartolo and Marcellina answer "Sua madre!" — "His mother!" — each time on the same two-note figure. The figure is short, simple, immediately memorable; once you hear it once, you can track it as it passes between the characters. Susanna arrives midway, misreads the embrace as betrayal (she sees Figaro in Marcellina's arms and assumes the worst), and slaps Figaro before learning the truth — the orchestra accents her sudden "Alto, alto, signor Conte" entry as the texture lifts into her outburst, and that outburst opens the second half of the sextet.

  6. **Act II "Voi che sapete" (Cherubino's canzona).** Early in Act II, the page Cherubino sings this short song for the Countess and Susanna in the Countess's chamber — a canzona he has composed himself, performed within the drama as an embedded musical number. _Listening anchor:_ B-flat major, a moderate tempo, lyric mezzo-soprano line. The accompaniment is the signature. Pizzicato strings carry a steady, almost guitar-like ostinato underneath (Susanna in the staging mimes accompanying Cherubino on a guitar), and a small wind ensemble — flute, oboe, clarinet, bassoon, and horn, with clarinet and bassoon especially audible — weaves an obbligato around the vocal line. Listen for the opening words ("Voi che sapete che cosa è amor / Donne, vedete s'io l'ho nel cor" — "You who know what love is, ladies, see whether I have it in my heart") and for the way the wind obbligato shadows the singer phrase by phrase. The aria is short, self-contained, and the easiest way to hear Mozart's adolescent-eros characterization of Cherubino in compact form.

  7. **Act IV "Deh vieni, non tardar" (Susanna's garden aria).** Late in Act IV, before the finale proper, Susanna sings this aria from the garden — ostensibly to her supposed lover, but actually knowing Figaro is eavesdropping and that the disguise game is in motion. _Listening anchor:_ the orchestral texture is the easiest signature in the opera. Pizzicato strings carry the accompaniment (plucked, not bowed — the unmistakable "guitar serenade" sound) and a small wind group (flute, oboe, bassoon) weaves an obbligato around the soprano line. F major, slow tempo, long lyric phrases. Listen for the opening words ("Deh vieni, non tardar, o gioia bella" — "Come, do not delay, my lovely joy") and for the way Susanna's voice floats above the pizzicato bed without ever competing with it. The aria is the lyric calm before the finale's confusions.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : pair the Act II and Act IV _Figaro_ finales with the Act II finale of _Don Giovanni_ K. 527 (Chapter 12 of this volume) — the supper scene with the Commendatore's statue. The two finales are the principal evidence for the Mozart ensemble finale form across the Da Ponte trilogy; hearing them together gives the fullest sense of how Mozart developed the form. The Karl Böhm _Don Giovanni_ (Deutsche Grammophon, 1977) with Sherrill Milnes and the Riccardo Muti _Don Giovanni_ (EMI, 1991) are both standard.

**Read** : Daniel Heartz, _Mozart's Operas_ (University of California Press, 1990). The chapters on _Figaro_ are the standard treatment of the opera's compositional and reception history. Heartz on the ensemble finale form is the principal scholarly account; Heartz on the Beaumarchais source and the political dimensions of the adaptation is the most-cited treatment of those topics. About 400 pages total; the _Figaro_ chapters are roughly eighty pages.

---

## Chapter 12: _Don Giovanni_ K. 527 (1787)

_Don Giovanni_ — Mozart's K. 527, the second Mozart-Da Ponte collaboration after _Le nozze di Figaro_ — premiered at the National Theater in Prague on October 29, 1787. The opera was commissioned by the Prague theater director Pasquale Bondini in the wake of the Prague _Figaro_ success of late 1786 (Chapter 11); Mozart composed it across the summer and autumn of 1787 and traveled to Prague in October to direct the premiere. The Prague premiere was a critical success; the Vienna production followed on May 7, 1788 with some modifications (a different ending was substituted, with the Don Giovanni-going-to-hell scene followed by a moralizing ensemble of the surviving characters; modern productions typically perform the Prague version of the ending or a hybrid).

_Don Giovanni_ sits at the center of the Mozart opera catalog by most aesthetic measures. It is the work in which the Mozart ensemble finale form (Chapter 11) reached its dramatic peak in the Act II supper scene with the Commendatore's statue; it is the work in which the dark-key signature (Chapter 7) found its most direct operatic expression in the D-minor overture and Commendatore scene; it is the work that brought the chromatic-descent figure into the operatic register; and it is the work that has generated an exceptionally large scholarly and critical literature for a Mozart opera. Søren Kierkegaard's _Either/Or_ (1843) treats _Don Giovanni_ as the philosophical center of the Don Juan myth; E.T.A. Hoffmann's 1813 novella "Don Juan" treats it as a central work of the Romantic operatic imagination; modern scholarship from Maynard Solomon, Cliff Eisen, Daniel Heartz, and others continues to engage the opera as a standing point of interpretive debate in Western opera.

This chapter anchors on three musical moments: the Overture (about six minutes), the Catalogue Aria "Madamina, il catalogo è questo" (about six minutes), and the Act II finale supper scene (about fifteen minutes). The chapter does not attempt a complete-opera walkthrough; the Conversation Seeds and Going Deeper point toward additional moments worth following.

### 12.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

October 28, 1787, Prague. The day before the premiere. The famous anecdote about the Overture: Mozart had not yet written it as of the day before the premiere, and composed the entire overture overnight, with Constanze keeping him awake by telling him stories or by providing him coffee. The Overture was rehearsed by the orchestra for the first time on the day of the premiere, sight-reading.

The anecdote is partly attested in Constanze Mozart's later recollections to Georg Nikolaus von Nissen (her second husband, who incorporated her recollections into his 1828 Mozart biography) and partly in Friedrich Rochlitz's anecdote-collection for the _Allgemeine musikalische Zeitung_ in the early nineteenth century. Solomon and Sadie both treat the overnight-overture story with care — Rochlitz's anecdote collection is known to contain inventions, and Constanze's recollections at thirty-plus years' remove may have been shaped by her interest in showing Mozart as the spontaneous genius. The story may capture something true (the Overture was composed late in the compositional process; Mozart may have completed it within the last few days before the premiere) but its specific form (overnight, with Constanze's coffee, single sight-reading by the orchestra) is partly legendary.

The Overture's musical content is what matters most. It opens with the slow introduction in D minor — the dark-key opening that will return in the Act II finale supper scene when the Commendatore's statue arrives at Don Giovanni's house. The D-minor opening of the Overture is therefore not merely a dramatic opening for the audience; it is structurally connected to the opera's climactic scene. Mozart in the Overture is presenting in compressed form the harmonic and dramatic vocabulary that the opera will develop.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen (Overture)

  1. **The opening D-minor chord and the slow introduction (the first three minutes).** Full orchestra plays the opening chord; the slow introduction in D minor develops across the next three minutes. The harmonic language is unusually dark for an opera overture of the period — sustained minor mode, chromatic descents in the lower strings, dramatic dynamic contrasts. Listen for the chromatic descending figures; these will return in the Commendatore scene.

  2. **The transition into the fast Allegro.** Mozart shifts from the D-minor slow introduction into a D-major Allegro (D-major, the parallel major key, is the standard relief from D-minor's gravity). The transition is a pivot point in the operatic overture literature — the audience moves from the supernatural-grave D-minor opening to the comic-energetic D-major main movement that will set up the opera's _dramma giocoso_ register.

  3. **The Allegro main movement.** Lively D-major orchestral writing; the energy contrasts with the slow introduction's gravity. The Overture closes without resolution; it leads directly into the opera's first scene (Leporello's complaining monologue outside Donna Anna's house). Listen for the open-ended close — Mozart does not provide a clean cadence; the music opens into the dramatic action.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen (Catalogue Aria)

  1. **The setup.** Leporello (Don Giovanni's servant) is explaining to Donna Elvira (one of Don Giovanni's former lovers, currently pursuing him for marriage promises he has reneged on) the extent of his master's romantic conquests. The aria is a list: how many women Don Giovanni has seduced, in which countries, in which categories. The dramatic effect is comic — Leporello is a _buffa_ character (a comic servant), and the cataloguing format is mock-bureaucratic.

  2. **The "1003" line.** Leporello sings that in Spain alone Don Giovanni has seduced "ma in Ispagna son già mille e tre" — "but in Spain already a thousand and three." The "1003" is the aria's punchline. The number is specific (one thousand and three; not "a thousand"; not "more than a thousand"), and Da Ponte's libretto choice of the specific odd number is part of the comic effect — Leporello is keeping precise count.

  3. **The aria's musical structure.** The aria is divided into two sections — a faster opening (the recitation of categories) and a slower middle section (Leporello expanding on Don Giovanni's preferences across types). Listen for the shift in tempo and mood between the two sections; the slower middle section gives the aria its dramatic and musical depth beyond the comic catalogue.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen (Act II finale supper scene)

  1. **The opening of the supper scene.** Don Giovanni is at dinner in his palace, served by Leporello. A small wind ensemble plays popular tunes of the period (Mozart includes a quotation from his own _Le nozze di Figaro_ — the aria "Non più andrai," which the audience would have recognized immediately — and quotations from Vicente Martín y Soler's _Una cosa rara_ and Giuseppe Sarti's _Fra i due litiganti il terzo gode_). Listen for the _Figaro_ quotation; it is a moment of metatheatrical comedy where Mozart references his own previous opera.

  2. **Donna Elvira's entrance.** Donna Elvira enters and pleads with Don Giovanni to change his life — to repent, to give up his licentious career. Don Giovanni mocks her. Elvira leaves; her departing scream signals that something has happened outside (she has encountered the Commendatore's statue arriving at the house).

  3. **The Commendatore's statue arrives.** Knocking at the door. The Commendatore (a statue of the man Don Giovanni killed in Act I) enters. The chromatic D-minor music of the Overture's slow introduction returns — now in dramatic context. The Commendatore demands that Don Giovanni repent. Don Giovanni refuses. The Commendatore drags Don Giovanni down to hell. The chromatic descents and the D-minor harmony from the Overture are now functioning as the operatic representation of supernatural judgment.

#### One-sentence background

Composed in Vienna and Prague, summer-autumn 1787 (Prague premiere October 29, 1787); the complete opera runs about three hours; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Karl Böhm with the Vienna Philharmonic and the Vienna State Opera Chorus (Deutsche Grammophon, 1977), featuring Sherrill Milnes as Don Giovanni, Walter Berry as Leporello, Anna Tomowa-Sintow as Donna Anna, Teresa Zylis-Gara as Donna Elvira, Edith Mathis as Zerlina, Peter Schreier as Don Ottavio, John Macurdy as the Commendatore. (Sir Charles Mackerras with the Scottish Chamber Orchestra on Telarc, 1994, is the modern reference complete recording with Bo Skovhus as Don Giovanni; Riccardo Muti with the Vienna Philharmonic on EMI, 1991, is the alternative reading with William Shimell as Don Giovanni and Cheryl Studer as Donna Anna.)

#### Search Hints

EN: "Mozart Don Giovanni K 527 Böhm Vienna Philharmonic" or "Mozart Don Giovanni Mackerras Scottish Chamber" ZH: "莫扎特 唐璜 K527 贝姆 维也纳爱乐" 或 "莫扎特 唐璜 麦凯拉斯" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

### 12.2 The Listening Window — The Overture

Timestamps below refer to the Böhm Primary Recording.

**0:00-0:10 — The opening.** Full orchestra plays the opening D-minor chord with timpani. The opening sonority is an immediately identifiable signature of the opera.

**The slow introduction.** Across the next three minutes, Mozart develops the D-minor material. Chromatic descending figures in the lower strings; wind responses; dramatic dynamic contrasts. The slow introduction's harmonic language is unusually dark for an opera overture of the period — sustained minor mode, with brief modulations that do not break the D-minor gravity. The slow introduction is structurally the operatic representation of the Commendatore's supernatural authority; the music's vocabulary will return when the Commendatore arrives at the Act II finale.

**The transition into the Allegro.** Mozart shifts from the D-minor slow introduction into a D-major Allegro. The transition itself is a brief moment of harmonic preparation; the listener moves from gravity to energy.

**The Allegro main movement.** Lively D-major writing. The Allegro is in sonata form and develops thematic material that connects to the comic energy of the opera's first scenes. Listen for the wind writing — the _dramma giocoso_ (the mixed seria-buffa genre Mozart and Da Ponte chose for the opera) is signaled musically by the contrast between the dark slow introduction and the lively Allegro.

**The closing of the Overture.** The Allegro does not close with a clean cadence; it leads directly into the opera's first scene. The open-ended close is structural — the Overture is not a self-standing work but the opening of the opera, and the music opens into the dramatic action without break.

### 12.3 The Listening Window — The Catalogue Aria

Timestamps below refer to the Böhm Primary Recording.

**The setup.** Leporello and Donna Elvira have met in front of Don Giovanni's house in Seville. Leporello has just been speaking with his master; Donna Elvira has been pursuing Don Giovanni for the marriage promises he has reneged on. Leporello, in an attempt to console Elvira (or perhaps to deflect her anger from his master), launches into the Catalogue Aria.

**The opening of the aria.** "Madamina, il catalogo è questo / delle belle che amò il padron mio" — "Madame, here is the catalogue of the beauties whom my master has loved." Leporello presents a list. The musical character is comic, light, with the orchestra providing bouncy accompaniment.

**The geographic catalogue.** Leporello enumerates the countries: Italy, Germany, France, Turkey, and Spain. Each country has a different count: in Italy, six hundred and forty; in Germany, two hundred and thirty-one; in France, a hundred; in Turkey, ninety-one; and in Spain... already a thousand and three ("ma in Ispagna son già mille e tre"). The "1003" line is delivered as the climax of the geographic catalogue; the specificity of the odd number is part of the comic effect.

**The transition into the slower middle section.** The music shifts tempo and mood. The aria's first half (the comic catalogue) gives way to the second half (Leporello expanding on Don Giovanni's preferences). The shift is a structural marker — the aria is not a single comic catalogue but a two-section study of the seducer's psychology. _Listening anchor:_ the textual hinge is unmissable. After the comic Allegro catalogue closes, the orchestra drops into a slower 3/4 minuet-like Andante and Leporello begins "Nella bionda egli ha l'usanza" — "With the blonde he has the habit [of praising her gentleness]." That phrase is the exact pivot from the geographic catalogue (countries and counts) to the categorical catalogue (hair color, build, season, station). The vocal writing softens and becomes more lyric, almost insinuating; the orchestra accompanies more delicately. If you hear "Nella bionda," you have arrived at the aria's second section.

**The categorical catalogue.** Leporello describes Don Giovanni's preferences: among blondes he praises their gentleness; among brunettes their constancy; among the white he praises their sweetness; in winter he likes the plump; in summer the slender; the tall, the small, the noble, the common — Don Giovanni has loved them all. The categorical catalogue is more reflective than the geographic catalogue; the music's slower tempo and more lyric writing give the aria its dramatic depth.

**The closing.** "Purché porti la gonnella, / voi sapete quel che fa" — "Provided they wear a skirt, you know what he does." The aria closes with a return to the comic register. The dramatic effect: Donna Elvira (and the audience) understands that Don Giovanni is not selective in his seductions — he pursues women as a category rather than as specific individuals. The understanding is partly comic (the cataloguing format played for laughs) and partly bleak (Don Giovanni's pursuit of women is impersonal).

### 12.4 The Listening Window — Act II Finale Supper Scene

Timestamps below refer to the Böhm Primary Recording. The Act II finale runs about twenty-five minutes total; we anchor on the supper-scene portion of about fifteen minutes.

**The setup.** Don Giovanni is at dinner in his palace. Leporello serves. A small wind ensemble (the on-stage _Harmoniemusik_ band) plays popular tunes of the period.

**The on-stage wind ensemble.** Listen for the three tunes the wind ensemble plays. The first is from Vicente Martín y Soler's _Una cosa rara_ (the Vienna hit of 1786-87 that had crowded _Figaro_ out of the Vienna theaters); the second from Giuseppe Sarti's _Fra i due litiganti il terzo gode_ (another popular opera of the period); the third is "Non più andrai" from Mozart's own _Le nozze di Figaro_. The _Figaro_ quotation is metatheatrical comedy — Mozart references his own previous opera, and the audience would have recognized it immediately. Leporello in the staging makes a verbal comment about the _Figaro_ tune; the moment is one of the principal pieces of evidence for the Mozart-Da Ponte sense of comic self-reference.

**Donna Elvira's entrance.** Donna Elvira enters the dining hall and pleads with Don Giovanni to change his life — to repent his seductions and to live a better life. The music shifts as Elvira enters; her vocal writing is lyric and impassioned. Don Giovanni mocks her plea, inviting her to join him at dinner. Elvira refuses and leaves; the music shifts back to the dining-hall texture.

**Elvira's departing scream.** From off-stage, Donna Elvira screams. Don Giovanni sends Leporello to investigate. Leporello also screams — he has seen the Commendatore's statue arriving at the house.

**The knocking and the Commendatore's entrance.** Heavy knocking at the door. The chromatic D-minor music of the Overture's slow introduction returns. The Commendatore (the statue of the man Don Giovanni killed in Act I) enters and addresses Don Giovanni. The Commendatore's voice, a deep bass, carries the supernatural authority that the D-minor harmonic language signals. _Listening anchor (the trombones):_ trombones are silent through almost the entire opera; Mozart reserves them for the Commendatore's supernatural appearances (the brief Act I graveyard scene and this Act II scene). When the knocking begins and the slow D-minor music returns, listen for the trombone choir entering with the wind chords — a sonority the rest of the opera has not given you. The trombones are the orchestral marker that the supernatural has arrived; once you have heard them once, the rest of the scene is unmistakably their world. _Listening anchor (the descending bass line):_ underneath the opening of the scene, the lower strings move stepwise downward through a long chromatic descent — half-step by half-step, slow and inevitable. The descent is the harmonic engine of the Commendatore's authority; it gives the scene its sense of being dragged down rather than freely moving.

**The Commendatore's demand.** "Don Giovanni, a cenar teco m'invitasti, e son venuto" — "Don Giovanni, you invited me to dine with you, and I have come." The Commendatore demands that Don Giovanni repent. Don Giovanni refuses ("No, no!"). The exchange repeats — the Commendatore demands repentance, Don Giovanni refuses. The dramatic stakes mount with each exchange.

**The chromatic descent and the descent to hell.** The Commendatore takes Don Giovanni's hand. The chromatic-descending figures in the orchestra intensify; the harmony moves through dark modulations. The Commendatore drags Don Giovanni down to hell. The chorus of demons (offstage) provides the supernatural backing.

**The Vienna ending vs the Prague ending.** In the Prague version of the opera (the original), Don Giovanni's damnation is the dramatic conclusion; the opera ends with this scene. In the Vienna version (1788, with some modifications by Mozart), Don Giovanni's damnation is followed by a moralizing ensemble of the surviving characters who comment on the just fate of the libertine. Modern productions usually perform the Prague ending (the dramatic ending without the moralizing) or a hybrid; the listening anchor here treats the supper-scene damnation as the structural ending of the Act II finale.

The chromatic-descent figure — first launched in K. 491's wind writing (Chapter 7), now central to the Commendatore scene — receives one of its most direct operatic uses in Mozart's catalog here. The chromatic descent functions as the operatic representation of supernatural judgment. Chapter 19 §19.6 will collect the chromatic-descent instances under Thread B; the Commendatore scene is one of the principal anchors.

### 12.5 What's Happening in the Music

#### The _dramma giocoso_ genre

_Don Giovanni_ is designated _dramma giocoso_ — "comic drama," a hybrid genre that combines elements of _opera seria_ (serious opera, with high-style aria writing and noble characters) and _opera buffa_ (comic opera, with quick-paced ensembles and _buffo_ characters). The genre designation was conventional for late-eighteenth-century Italian operas with mixed registers; _Don Giovanni_ 's mixed character — the seria-style Donna Anna and Donna Elvira and Don Ottavio set against the buffa-style Leporello and Zerlina and Masetto — is the genre's distinctive feature.

What the _dramma giocoso_ gives Mozart: dramatic range. Donna Anna's grand grief at her father's murder (Act I, the recitative and aria "Or sai chi l'onore") is sung in seria style — long lyric phrases, formal aria structure, noble register. Leporello's Catalogue Aria is sung in buffa style — patter, comic patter rhythm, mock-bureaucratic register. The two styles coexist within the same opera, and the contrast between them is part of the dramatic argument. _Don Giovanni_ is structurally one of Mozart's most ambitious operas precisely because it works across the full range of late-eighteenth-century vocal styles.

#### The D-minor harmonic argument

_Don Giovanni_ 's D-minor harmonic argument runs across the entire opera. The Overture opens in D minor; the Commendatore's first appearance in Act I (when Don Giovanni kills him in a duel) is in D minor; the Act II finale supper scene returns to D minor when the Commendatore's statue arrives. The D-minor key is therefore the operatic representation of the Commendatore's supernatural authority — first as victim of murder, then as agent of judgment.

The intervening sections of the opera move through other keys (the F major of the Catalogue Aria, the various keys of the Act I ensembles, the D major and G major of the seduction scenes), but the structural framing is D minor. The opera opens in D minor and closes in D minor; the rest is the comic-romantic intrigue that takes place within that supernatural framing.

The dark-key signature reaches one of its principal operatic peaks in _Don Giovanni_. The D-minor key joins K. 466 (Chapter 7) and the Requiem K. 626 (Chapter 18) as the principal D-minor anchors in this volume.

#### The chromatic-descent figure as supernatural-judgment vocabulary

The chromatic-descent figure — a descending sequence of half-steps, often in the lower strings or bassoons — is one of the rhetorical conventions of grief, judgment, and supernatural authority in the eighteenth-century musical vocabulary. Johann Sebastian Bach used the figure extensively in the cantatas and Passions (the "Crucifixus" of the B-minor Mass is one of the most-cited Bach uses); Henry Purcell used it in _Dido and Aeneas_ ("When I am laid in earth"); the figure was a shared convention across the Baroque and into the Classical period.

Mozart's use of the chromatic-descent figure in the Commendatore scene is one of the principal Classical-period applications of the convention to supernatural-judgment material. The chromatic descents in the lower strings as the Commendatore demands Don Giovanni's repentance carry the conventional weight of grief-and-judgment — the listener trained in the eighteenth-century musical vocabulary recognizes the convention immediately and feels its emotional charge.

The figure will return in the Requiem K. 626's Lacrimosa (Chapter 18), where it functions as the operatic-style mourning vocabulary in liturgical context. The Commendatore scene and the Lacrimosa together are the two principal Mozart uses of the chromatic-descent figure.

#### The Mozart ensemble finale, extended

The Act II finale supper scene extends the Mozart ensemble finale form that Chapter 11's _Figaro_ finales had introduced. The form here takes on supernatural-dramatic content that the _Figaro_ finales had not contained; the _Figaro_ finales had been social-comedy ensembles (the seven-voice climax of _Figaro_ Act II is a Vienna household's confused suspicions). The _Don Giovanni_ Act II finale is a supernatural-judgment scene (the Commendatore's statue drags the libertine down to hell). The form is the same; the content is different.

What the form accomplishes here: it gives the supernatural scene the same continuous-musical-span structure that the comic finales of _Figaro_ had used. The Commendatore's arrival is not a separate set-piece; it is embedded in a continuous musical span that begins with the dining-hall scene and runs through the damnation without break. The musical continuity gives the scene its dramatic inevitability; the audience cannot escape into a recitative or a separate aria before the judgment arrives.

The Commendatore scene is one of the principal anchors of the ensemble finale tradition that Chapter 11 introduced.

### 12.6 The Stories

#### The Prague premiere

The October 29, 1787 Prague premiere was a critical and popular success. The Prague theater had become Mozart's principal operatic home through the _Figaro_ enthusiasm of 1786-87; _Don Giovanni_ 's premiere consolidated that relationship. Mozart conducted the premiere from the keyboard; the principal singers included Luigi Bassi (the Italian baritone for whom the role of Don Giovanni had been composed), Felice Ponziani as Leporello, Teresa Saporiti as Donna Anna, Caterina Micelli as Donna Elvira, Caterina Bondini (the wife of the theater director Pasquale Bondini) as Zerlina, Antonio Baglioni as Don Ottavio, and Giuseppe Lolli as the Commendatore.

The Prague reception was enthusiastic. Mozart's letters from Prague to Gottfried von Jacquin in Vienna describe the success; the Prague newspapers and theater records register the strong reception. The opera entered the Prague repertoire immediately and remained there.

#### The Vienna production

The Vienna production followed on May 7, 1788 at the Burgtheater. The Vienna cast was different (Francesco Albertarelli as Don Giovanni; Francesco Bussani as Leporello; Aloysia Lange — Mozart's sister-in-law, the soprano whose earlier connection with Mozart in 1777-78 had been the Mannheim infatuation of Chapter 4 — as Donna Anna; Caterina Cavalieri as Donna Elvira). The Vienna production made some modifications to the Prague score: a new ensemble was added for Donna Elvira ("In quali eccessi, o numi"), a new duet was added for Leporello and Zerlina ("Per queste tue manine"), and the ending was modified to include the moralizing ensemble of surviving characters after Don Giovanni's damnation.

The Vienna reception was more mixed than the Prague reception. The Vienna audience apparently found the dark D-minor supernatural content unsettling; Emperor Joseph II is reported to have said something like "the opera is divine, perhaps even more beautiful than _Figaro_ , but it is not the food for the teeth of my Viennese" (the attribution is from Da Ponte's memoirs and is partly self-aggrandizing). The Vienna run was reasonable but did not match the Prague enthusiasm.

#### _Don Giovanni_ in subsequent reception

_Don Giovanni_ has been a standing object of interpretive debate in the Western operatic tradition since the early nineteenth century. The principal threads of reception:

E.T.A. Hoffmann's 1813 novella "Don Juan" treats the opera as the central work of the Romantic operatic imagination — Don Giovanni as the demonic hero whose pursuit of the absolute is its own moral category. The Hoffmann reading shaped nineteenth-century reception of the opera and is one of the principal Romantic-era interpretations.

Søren Kierkegaard's _Either/Or_ (1843, Volume I) treats Mozart's _Don Giovanni_ as the philosophical center of the Don Juan myth — the seducer as the embodiment of the immediate-sensual life that Kierkegaard's "aesthete" represents. Kierkegaard's reading is one of the most extended philosophical engagements with any opera in the Western tradition; his argument that music is the medium uniquely suited to representing the immediate-sensual (because music is itself temporal and non-conceptual) makes _Don Giovanni_ the case in point.

Twentieth- and twenty-first-century reception has continued to debate Don Giovanni's moral status (is he a libertine to be condemned, a tragic hero to be pitied, an existential figure beyond moral categories?), the gender dynamics of the seductions (Donna Anna's relationship to her father's murder and to Don Giovanni's pursuit of her is one of the most-debated single dramatic questions in the opera), and the role of the supernatural ending (does Don Giovanni's damnation work as moral judgment, or does it work as dramatic catharsis only?). Maynard Solomon's treatment in _Mozart: A Life_ gives an overview of the modern reception; Cliff Eisen and Daniel Heartz both add scholarly depth.

The opera's contested reception is part of its life. _Don Giovanni_ asks the listener to engage the central questions of seduction, judgment, and dramatic morality without offering a settled interpretive position. The dramatic ambiguity is built in.

#### Recording history of _Don Giovanni_

_Don Giovanni_ has a deep recorded catalogue. The complete-recording references:

Karl Böhm with the Vienna Philharmonic (Deutsche Grammophon, 1977, the chapter's Primary Recording) features Sherrill Milnes as Don Giovanni. The reading is warm and architecturally weighted; the cast is one of the strongest mid-century international assemblies.

Sir Charles Mackerras with the Scottish Chamber Orchestra (Telarc, 1994) is the modern reference. Bo Skovhus as Don Giovanni, Renée Fleming as Donna Anna, Anne Sofie von Otter as Donna Elvira, Bryn Terfel as Leporello.

Riccardo Muti with the Vienna Philharmonic (EMI, 1991) is the alternative modern reading. William Shimell as Don Giovanni, Cheryl Studer as Donna Anna, Susanne Mentzer as Donna Elvira, Samuel Ramey as Leporello.

Carlo Maria Giulini with the Philharmonia Orchestra (Columbia/EMI, 1959) is the historic mid-twentieth-century reference. Eberhard Wächter as Don Giovanni, Joan Sutherland as Donna Anna, Elisabeth Schwarzkopf as Donna Elvira, Giuseppe Taddei as Leporello.

Wilhelm Furtwängler with the Vienna Philharmonic (EMI, 1953-54, recorded from Salzburg Festival performances) is the historic reference for the dramatic-conducting tradition. Cesare Siepi as Don Giovanni.

Sir John Eliot Gardiner with the English Baroque Soloists (Archiv, 1994) is the period-instrument complete reference. Rodney Gilfry as Don Giovanni.

René Jacobs with the Freiburger Barockorchester (Harmonia Mundi, 2007) is the contemporary period-instrument alternative; the reading is dramatic and detailed.

The Sir Colin Davis Royal Opera House Covent Garden recording (Philips, 1973) with Ingvar Wixell as Don Giovanni is another older modern-instrument reference.

The Daniel Barenboim Berlin Philharmonic recording (Erato, 1991) with Ferruccio Furlanetto as Don Giovanni is another modern alternative.

### Conversation Seeds

Listen to the Overture's first three minutes and then to the Commendatore's entry in the Act II finale. What does the structural connection do for the audience? Why does Mozart use the same harmonic vocabulary to open the opera and to close the dramatic action?"_
* _"The Catalogue Aria's "ma in Ispagna son già mille e tre" (the '1003' line) is the aria's comic punchline. The specificity of the odd number is part of the comic effect. Is the audience meant to laugh at Don Giovanni or at Leporello's catalogue or at both?"_

The metatheatrical comedy (Mozart references his own previous opera) is unusual for the period. What does the self-reference accomplish dramatically? Does it work as a moment of comic relief before the supernatural ending, or does it work as a structural connection between the two Da Ponte operas?"_

The dramatic ambiguity is built in — the opera does not resolve Don Giovanni's moral status. Is Don Giovanni a libertine to be condemned, a tragic hero to be pitied, or something else? How does Mozart's music position the character relative to these readings?"_
* _"The dramatic effect of the chromatic-descent figure in the Commendatore scene relies on the eighteenth-century musical convention — descending half-steps as the conventional vocabulary of grief and supernatural judgment. The convention had been used by Bach in the Passions, by Purcell in _Dido and Aeneas_ , and across the Baroque generally. Mozart's deployment of the convention in the Commendatore scene is one of the principal Classical-period applications. What does the convention give Mozart that purely original material would not have given him?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find Böhm / Vienna Philharmonic (Deutsche Grammophon, 1977) or Mackerras / Scottish Chamber (Telarc, 1994). Listen to the Overture, the Catalogue Aria, and the Act II finale supper scene a second time and attend to four concrete moments:

  1. **Overture opening (the first ten seconds).** Confirm: full orchestra plays the opening D-minor chord with timpani. The opening sonority is an immediately identifiable signature of the opera; the slow introduction's D-minor gravity sets up the opera's harmonic framing.

  2. **Catalogue Aria's country count, building to "1003."** Listen for the geographic enumeration in sequence — "in Italia seicento e quaranta" (in Italy, six hundred and forty), "in Almagna duecento e trentuna" (in Germany, two hundred and thirty-one), "cento in Francia, in Turchia novantuna" (a hundred in France, ninety-one in Turkey), and then the punchline "ma in Ispagna son già mille e tre" (but in Spain already a thousand and three). Each country gets its own brief musical phrase; the counts accumulate; "mille e tre" lands as the comic climax of the geographic catalogue. Notice how the music's tempo and orchestration shift at the moment.

  3. **The transition into the slower middle section of the Catalogue Aria.** Listen for the moment the music shifts from the comic geographic catalogue into the more lyric categorical catalogue. The two-section structure gives the aria its dramatic depth beyond the comic surface.

  4. **The Commendatore's entry in the Act II finale.** Listen for the moment the chromatic D-minor music of the Overture's slow introduction returns, and for the trombone choir entering with the wind chords (the trombones are silent through almost the entire opera; the Commendatore is what they are for). The Commendatore's voice, a deep bass, carries the supernatural authority that the D-minor harmonic language signals. The chromatic-descending figures in the lower strings intensify as the scene builds toward the damnation.

  5. **The three on-stage bands at the Act I finale (masked-ball scene).** Late in the Act I finale, Don Giovanni hosts a masked ball at his palace. Three small on-stage ensembles play simultaneously in three different dance meters: a minuet in 3/4 (for the aristocratic guests — Don Ottavio, Donna Anna, Donna Elvira, in masks), a contredanse in 2/4 (which Don Giovanni dances with Zerlina), and a fast German dance (_Teitsch_) in 3/8 (which Leporello forces on Masetto to keep him away from the others). Mozart layers the three meters on top of each other in a single texture. _Listening anchor:_ once the dancing begins, listen for the moment when the second and then the third band enter — the rhythmic field thickens audibly, and you can briefly hear three independent dance rhythms competing for ear-space. The three-meter simultaneity is a standout technical feat of late-eighteenth-century opera. It ends abruptly when Zerlina's scream from offstage breaks the dance.

  6. **"Là ci darem la mano" (Act I, Don Giovanni-Zerlina duet).** Shortly after the Catalogue Aria, Don Giovanni encounters the peasant bride Zerlina on her wedding day to Masetto and sets about seducing her in the space of a single duet. _Listening anchor:_ the duet is in A major, at a graceful Andante. Don Giovanni opens with a smooth, gallant phrase ("Là ci darem la mano, là mi dirai di sì" — "There we shall join hands, there you will say yes"); Zerlina answers in the same shape but with the words inverted into hesitation ("Vorrei e non vorrei" — "I would and I would not"). For the first half of the duet the two voices alternate phrase-for-phrase — Don Giovanni proposing, Zerlina answering — and the orchestra keeps the two characters audibly separate. The turning point is the shift into 6/8 Allegretto and the joined "Andiam, andiam, mio bene" ("Let us go, let us go, my love"), where the two voices finally sing together in parallel thirds. Once you hear the meter change and the voices coalescing in thirds, you have heard the seduction completed musically — the orchestra's role through the alternating section had been to give Zerlina's resistance its shape, and that resistance dissolves into joined motion at the meter change.

  7. **Donna Anna's "Or sai chi l'onore" (Act I).** Donna Anna's great Act I aria to Don Ottavio — she has just realized that Don Giovanni, the masked stranger who attacked her on the night of her father's murder, was also her father's killer. _Listening anchor:_ the aria is in D major (the parallel-major of the opera's framing D minor), at a vigorous Allegro tempo, and is built around a martial dotted rhythm in the orchestra. The dotted figure is the principal signature; once you hear it, you can track it through the orchestral interludes between Donna Anna's phrases. The aria's vehemence — Donna Anna demanding that Don Ottavio swear vengeance — is the seria-style counterweight to Leporello's buffa-style Catalogue Aria that follows it in the act.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : pair the _Don Giovanni_ Act II finale supper scene with the Requiem Lacrimosa (Chapter 18 of this volume). Both works use the chromatic-descent figure as the principal rhetorical material for grief and supernatural judgment. The _Don Giovanni_ scene is the operatic application; the Lacrimosa is the liturgical application. Hearing them together gives the fullest sense of how Mozart used the chromatic-descent figure across the operatic and liturgical registers. The Karl Böhm _Don Giovanni_ (Deutsche Grammophon, 1977) and the Christopher Hogwood Requiem (L'Oiseau-Lyre / Decca, 1984) provide the listening pair.

**Read** : Søren Kierkegaard, _Either/Or, Volume I_ (1843), specifically the section "The Immediate Erotic Stages, or the Musical Erotic" — Kierkegaard's extended engagement with _Don Giovanni_ as the central work of the Don Juan myth. Kierkegaard's argument that music is uniquely suited to representing immediate-sensual experience makes _Don Giovanni_ the case in point. The reading is dense philosophical writing and not light reading; about eighty pages in the standard English translations. The reading is a standard critical engagement with the opera.

## Chapter 13: _Così fan tutte_ K. 588 (1790) + _Idomeneo_ K. 366 (1781)

_Così fan tutte_ (K. 588, premiered Vienna Burgtheater, January 26, 1790) is the third and last of the Mozart-Da Ponte collaborations. _Idomeneo_ (K. 366, premiered Munich, January 29, 1781) is Mozart's principal opera seria, composed in the months before the May 1781 Vienna break (Chapter 5) and predating the Da Ponte collaboration by five years. The two operas sit at different ends of Mozart's mature operatic career (_Idomeneo_ at the start, _Così_ near the end) and represent different genres (opera seria, opera buffa with Da Ponte irony). Pairing them in a single chapter compresses two important works into one chapter; the choice reflects this volume's overall plan to cover the opera catalog in four chapters rather than six, and it lets us engage two operas that work in different operatic registers without giving each a separate chapter.

The chapter's principal _Così_ anchor is the "Soave sia il vento" trio from Act I (about three minutes), one of the most-loved Mozart ensembles. For _Idomeneo_ the chapter anchors on three dramatic moments — Idomeneo's "Fuor del mar," the quartet "Andrò ramingo e solo," and Elettra's final mad scene "D'Oreste, d'Aiace" — that together give the clearest picture of what mature Mozart opera seria can do. The _Idomeneo_ treatment is more compact than the Da Ponte chapters but is anchored, not merely contextual.

### 13.1 Before You Listen — _Così fan tutte_

#### Hook Anecdote

Late 1789. Vienna. Emperor Joseph II (whose reign was ending — he would die February 20, 1790, three weeks after the _Così_ premiere) commissioned Mozart and Da Ponte to write a new opera. The libretto's premise was unusual and partly scandalous: two young Italian officers, Ferrando and Guglielmo, make a wager with the older cynic Don Alfonso that their fiancées (the sisters Fiordiligi and Dorabella) will remain faithful to them during a fabricated absence. The officers feign a military departure and return in disguise to test the women's fidelity by attempting to seduce each other's fiancées. Both women, after initial resistance, accept the disguised suitors. The wager is revealed; the women are humiliated; the four reconcile (in some interpretations) or the reconciliation is left dramatically unstable (in others).

The premise was widely understood as cynical even by 1790 standards. The title — _Così fan tutte_ , "thus do they all" (meaning, all women behave this way) — was a generalizing claim that nineteenth-century audiences found increasingly difficult to accept. Across the nineteenth century _Così_ was performed less frequently than the other two Da Ponte operas; some nineteenth-century productions modified the libretto to soften the cynicism. The opera's reception was rehabilitated in the twentieth century, beginning roughly with the 1934 Glyndebourne production under Fritz Busch and continuing through the work's centrality in the late-twentieth-century opera repertoire. _Così_ is now performed as frequently as _Figaro_ and _Don Giovanni_ , and its dramatic ambiguities are treated as part of its complexity rather than as defects.

Whether _Così_ is a comedy or a tragedy, a misogynist text or a critique of male presumption, a cynical exercise or a tender examination of human inconstancy — these are open interpretive questions. The opera does not resolve them. The music carries the dramatic weight; the libretto provides the framing; the interpretive choices belong to directors and listeners.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen ("Soave sia il vento")

  1. **The dramatic setup.** The trio occurs in Act I. Fiordiligi and Dorabella are watching from the dockside as Ferrando and Guglielmo (their fiancés, in the staged "military departure") sail away. Don Alfonso (who has organized the deception) stands with them. The three voices sing the trio, two women's voices and one bass, wishing safe passage to the departing ships.

  2. **The musical character.** Slow tempo (the marking is _Andante_); E major (a warm, lyric key). The three voices weave around each other in close harmony with sustained vocal lines. The orchestra provides quiet accompaniment — strings predominantly, with light wind decoration. The trio's mood is sublimely lyric; commentators routinely reach for the word "sublime" when describing it.

  3. **The dramatic irony.** The two women are sincerely wishing safety to the men they believe to be sailing away to war. The men are not sailing away to war — they are about to return in disguise to test the women's fidelity. Don Alfonso, the cynic, is singing the trio with the women in apparent sincerity while knowing the deception is in progress. The dramatic irony (the audience knows what the women do not) gives the lyric music a layer of complication that pure sincere lyric writing would not have. The trio is sublime, but it is sublime over a foundation of deception.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen (Idomeneo)

  1. **The dramatic problem.** _Idomeneo_ , king of Crete, is caught in a storm returning from the Trojan War and vows to Neptune that, if he is spared, he will sacrifice the first person he meets on landing. The first person he meets is his own son Idamante. The opera's entire dramatic action turns on the impossibility of the vow — a father commanded by a god to kill his son, a son discovering that his father wants to evade him, a kingdom learning that its king's salvation will cost a royal life. Mozart's task in the score is to make the vow felt as a real weight rather than as a plot device; the principal arias and ensembles are the means.

  2. **The orchestration and the chorus.** _Idomeneo_ is more densely orchestrated than any opera Mozart had written to date, with substantial wind parts and an unusually heavy use of chorus. The chorus is a real dramatic actor — the Cretan people respond to the storm, to the sea monster Neptune sends as punishment, to the announcement of the impending sacrifice — rather than the static frame of conventional opera seria. Listen for the chorus entries; they carry as much dramatic weight as any individual aria.

  3. **The opera seria genre and its late position.** _Idomeneo_ is opera seria — the serious operatic genre with mythological subjects, high-style aria writing, formal alternation of recitatives and arias, and noble characters. The genre had been the leading European opera form through the early-to-mid eighteenth century but was being displaced by opera buffa (the comic genre Mozart-Da Ponte would write in) and by the German _Singspiel_ tradition during the 1780s. _Idomeneo_ is therefore one of the late high-period opera seria works — composed at a moment when the genre was losing dominance but still commanded major commissions. The Munich court for the 1780-81 Carnival season was the commissioner; Mozart traveled from Salzburg to Munich in November 1780, the premiere was January 29, 1781, and the score is the last opera Mozart wrote before the May 1781 Vienna break (Chapter 5).

#### One-sentence background (_Così_)

Composed in Vienna, late 1789-early 1790 (premiered January 26, 1790); the complete opera runs about three hours; the **Primary Recording** for the "Soave sia il vento" trio is from the Karl Böhm complete _Così fan tutte_ with the Philharmonia Orchestra (Columbia/EMI, 1962) featuring Elisabeth Schwarzkopf as Fiordiligi, Christa Ludwig as Dorabella, and Walter Berry as Don Alfonso. The Böhm 1962 reading is a touchstone among twentieth-century _Cosìs_. (Sir John Eliot Gardiner with the English Baroque Soloists on Archiv, 1992, is the period-instrument alternative; Riccardo Muti with the Vienna Philharmonic on EMI, 1982-83, is the modern-instrument alternative.)

#### One-sentence background (_Idomeneo_)

Composed in Munich and Salzburg, autumn 1780 through January 1781 (premiered Munich January 29, 1781); the complete opera runs about three hours; the **Primary Recording** for _Idomeneo_ is Sir Colin Davis with the Royal Opera House Covent Garden (Philips, 1991) featuring Anthony Rolfe Johnson as Idomeneo and Anne Sofie von Otter as Idamante. (Karl Böhm with the Staatskapelle Dresden on Deutsche Grammophon, 1977-78, is the older alternative; Sir John Eliot Gardiner with the English Baroque Soloists on Archiv, 1990, is the period-instrument alternative.)
