### 13.2 The Listening Window — "Soave sia il vento"

Timestamps below refer to the Böhm Primary Recording. The trio runs approximately three minutes.

**0:00-0:15 — The orchestral introduction.** Strings in soft sustained chords; the tempo is Andante. The harmonic language is E major — warm, lyric, settled. The orchestral introduction is brief; the trio enters within fifteen seconds.

**Fiordiligi's entry.** The first soprano voice enters on the words "Soave sia il vento" — "May the wind be gentle." The vocal line is sustained and lyric; the writing is close to the most directly lyric Mozart vocal writing in the repertoire. Listen for the breath support — the phrase is long and the singer must carry it without strain.

**Dorabella's entry.** The second soprano voice (Dorabella is sometimes mezzo-soprano in modern casting) joins on "tranquilla sia l'onda" — "may the wave be tranquil." The two women's voices weave together in close harmony — thirds and sixths, the conventional close-harmony intervals for two-voice lyric writing of the period.

**Don Alfonso's entry.** The bass voice (Don Alfonso) joins on "ed ogni elemento" — "and every element." The three voices are now in trio texture, two sopranos and one bass, with the women's voices carrying the upper lines and Don Alfonso providing the bass foundation.

**The closing.** The trio extends across a sustained lyric arc and closes on a quiet cadence in E major. The orchestral accompaniment recedes; the three voices settle into the final chord. The trio's closing is one of the quietest in the Mozart opera literature.

The "Soave sia il vento" trio is approximately three minutes long. Its musical means are simple — slow tempo, warm key, close-harmony three-voice texture, restrained accompaniment. The sublime effect is built on the simplicity rather than against it. The dramatic irony — that the two women are wishing safety to fiancés who are about to deceive them — gives the music its weight beyond the surface lyric beauty.

### 13.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### Why "Soave sia il vento" is so loved

The trio is often cited as one of the most perfect single ensembles in the Mozart opera catalog. The reasons:

The vocal writing is exquisite. The two sopranos' lines weave together in close harmony with phrasing that allows both singers to display their lyric capacities. The bass line provides foundation without overwhelming the upper voices. The three-voice texture has become a model for chamber-music-within-opera writing.

The orchestral writing is restrained. Mozart uses strings primarily, with light wind decoration only. The orchestral accompaniment supports the voices rather than competing with them. The texture is transparent throughout.

The harmonic language is warm and settled. E major is one of the lyric, warm keys in the standard tonal repertoire; Mozart in the trio uses it without modulation, sustaining the home key for the trio's entire duration. The harmonic constancy gives the trio its sense of stillness.

The dramatic timing is right. The trio occurs at Act I scene 5 — past the opening exposition but before the dramatic complications of the disguise scheme have begun. The audience knows what is about to happen (Ferrando and Guglielmo will return in disguise to test the women); the women do not. The trio's lyric sincerity rests on the women's belief that the departure is real; the audience's awareness of the deception gives the trio its dramatic depth.

The combination — exquisite vocal writing, restrained orchestral writing, warm harmonic language, dramatic timing — produces a moment that is more than the sum of its parts. The trio is one of those rare musical moments where the dramatic and musical means align to produce something the listener experiences as sublime.

#### _Così fan tutte_ in the operatic repertoire history

_Così fan tutte_ premiered January 26, 1790. The reception in Vienna was favorable but the Emperor's death three weeks later (February 20, 1790) disrupted the season. The opera was performed ten times in early 1790 and a few more times later in the year; subsequent Vienna productions were fewer than the _Figaro_ and _Don Giovanni_ productions of the same period.

The nineteenth-century reception of _Così_ was complicated. The libretto's premise — that all women will yield to seduction under sufficient pressure — was widely understood as cynical and was sometimes considered offensive. Some nineteenth-century productions modified the libretto to soften the cynicism (the two officers might be redirected to swap their original partners permanently, or the wager might be reframed as a test the women pass rather than fail). Other productions simply omitted the work. _Così_ was performed across the nineteenth century but less frequently than _Figaro_ and _Don Giovanni_ and with periodic alterations.

The twentieth-century rehabilitation of _Così_ is conventionally dated to the 1934 Glyndebourne Festival production under Fritz Busch, which presented the original libretto without modification and treated the dramatic ambiguities as part of the work's complexity. Subsequent productions (Karl Böhm at the Salzburg Festival; the modern productions of the postwar era; the Glyndebourne tradition continuing) consolidated _Così_ in the standard repertoire. By the late twentieth century _Così_ was performed as frequently as the other Da Ponte operas; the dramatic ambiguities (is the libretto misogynist? is the reconciliation sincere or unstable? is the music's beauty in tension with the libretto's cynicism?) were treated as the work's interpretive openness rather than as defects.

The question of how to interpret _Così_ is now one of the principal questions in Mozart opera scholarship and direction. Modern productions vary widely: some emphasize the comedy; some emphasize the tragedy; some present the reconciliation as genuine; some present it as forced. Mozart's music does not resolve the interpretive question; the libretto leaves the resolution open; the directorial choice carries the interpretive weight.

#### _Idomeneo_ and opera seria

_Idomeneo_ is Mozart's principal opera seria — the high-period serious genre with mythological subjects, formal aria-recitative alternation, noble characters, and elaborate vocal display. Mozart had composed earlier opera seria works (_Mitridate_ of 1770, _Lucio Silla_ of 1772 — the Italian-tour operas from Chapter 3); _Idomeneo_ is the mature opera seria work and is considered the principal evidence for what Mozart could do in the genre.

The plot: Idomeneo, king of Crete, is returning from the Trojan War. Caught in a storm at sea, he vows to Neptune that if he survives he will sacrifice the first person he meets on landing. He meets and recognizes his son Idamante. The vow's terrible consequence drives the opera's dramatic action. Subplot: Idamante is loved by two princesses — Ilia (a captive Trojan princess) and Elettra (the daughter of the murdered Agamemnon). The opera's resolution involves Neptune's intervention (the sacrifice is averted), Idomeneo's abdication in favor of Idamante, the marriage of Idamante and Ilia, and Elettra's defeated madness scene.

The Munich commission context: the Munich court for the 1780-81 Carnival season commissioned Mozart to write a new opera seria for the Elector Karl Theodor's court. Mozart traveled from Salzburg to Munich in November 1780 to oversee rehearsals; the premiere occurred January 29, 1781. The opera was a critical success at Munich but did not lead to the Munich court appointment Mozart had hoped for. He returned to Salzburg in March 1781 and proceeded to Vienna in time for the May 1781 break with Colloredo.

The historical position of _Idomeneo_ in Mozart's catalog: it is the principal opera before the Vienna freelance period (Chapter 5) and the principal evidence for what Mozart could have done in the opera seria tradition if he had stayed in that genre. The shift after 1781 to opera buffa with Da Ponte (_Figaro_ 1786, _Don Giovanni_ 1787, _Così_ 1790) and to _Singspiel_ (_Entführung_ 1782, _Magic Flute_ 1791) was a generic redirection; opera seria as a form was being displaced across the 1780s-90s, and Mozart's late operatic output reflected the shift. _Idomeneo_ therefore stands as the road not taken — Mozart's mature opera seria, composed at the moment before the redirection.

_Idomeneo_ has stayed in the operatic repertoire as a specialist work. It is performed less frequently than the Da Ponte operas or _Magic Flute_ , but the major opera houses program it periodically and the work has had champions throughout the twentieth and twenty-first centuries (Karl Böhm, Sir Colin Davis, Sir John Eliot Gardiner, René Jacobs all conducted notable productions). The work's orchestral richness and the dramatic intensity of Elettra's mad scene are particularly admired.

#### Three Idomeneo moments — listening anchors

_Idomeneo_ is too large a work for a complete-opera walkthrough in a shared chapter, but three moments give the most direct evidence of what Mozart's mature opera seria can do. Each is found near the center of one of the three acts; together they show how Mozart handles the dramatic problem (the vow, the impossible obligation, the moral collapse) across the opera's arc.

**"Fuor del mar" (Idomeneo's great Act II aria).** Idomeneo has reached land safely and is now confronting the full weight of his vow. The aria is his self-reproach and his desperate search for an evasion: the sea has spared him, but the storm inside is worse than the storm outside. _Listening anchor:_ the aria is in D major at a vigorous Allegro, and is one of the most-virtuosic tenor arias Mozart ever wrote — long coloratura runs, wide leaps, sustained high notes, all in the seria-tenor display tradition. (Mozart wrote two versions: a more demanding original for the Munich tenor Anton Raaff, who was sixty-six at the premiere, and a slightly simplified version that some modern productions adopt.) Listen for the running figures in the strings under the vocal line — they are the orchestral image of the sea Idomeneo has just escaped, and they keep pushing back against the singer's attempted composure. The aria is the show-piece of the tenor role; if you have heard "Fuor del mar," you have heard the principal vocal evidence of what Mozart could ask of an opera-seria tenor.

**The quartet "Andrò ramingo e solo" (Act III).** The quartet is one of Mozart's greatest dramatic ensembles in any genre and the structural and emotional center of the opera. The dramatic situation: Idamante (Idomeneo's son) is leaving Crete because his father has rejected him without explanation (Idomeneo cannot reveal the vow); Ilia (the captive Trojan princess who loves Idamante) cannot bear his departure; Elettra (the second princess, who also loves Idamante) sees her hopes failing; Idomeneo watches his son leave knowing he himself is the cause. Four characters, four irreconcilable positions, one slow ensemble. _Listening anchor:_ the texture is the opposite of the _Figaro_ finale form — not accumulating ensemble of comic confusion, but a slow lyric quartet in which four characters each sustain their own line and the four lines knot together without resolving. The key is E♭ major (warm, lyric), the tempo is slow, the orchestration is restrained. Listen for Idamante's opening phrase ("Andrò ramingo e solo" — "I will go wandering and alone") and for the way each of the other three characters answers him with their own response. The ensemble is not a stretto or a climax; it is a held suspension. It ends without resolution — the four characters' situations are still impossible at the quartet's close, and the dramatic tension passes unresolved into the next scene.

**Elettra's "D'Oreste, d'Aiace" (Act III, the final mad scene).** Elettra has lost. Idamante will marry Ilia, not her; her own claim is denied; the Furies of her brother Orestes' madness are her inheritance. The aria is one of the most ferocious soprano showpieces in the repertoire — Elettra invoking the avenging spirits of her family's curse and collapsing into madness. _Listening anchor:_ the aria is in C minor at a driving Allegro assai, and the vocal writing is a sustained explosion — leaping wide intervals, repeated stabbing figures, rapid runs that climb into the upper register and then crash down. The orchestral writing is dense and dark; the strings drive the texture, the winds answer with shrieking figures. Listen for the moment Elettra names her brother Orestes and the half-brother Ajax ("D'Oreste, d'Aiace ho in seno i tormenti" — "In my breast I have the torments of Orestes and of Ajax"). The aria ends with the singer's collapse; in most productions Elettra is dragged from the stage as the orchestra hammers out the closing measures. The mad scene is the price the opera exacts for the resolution — Idamante and Ilia get their happiness only because Elettra has been destroyed.

For the biographical context of _Idomeneo_ 's composition (autumn 1780 through January 1781), the chapter directs readers back to Chapter 5 of this volume (the 1781 Vienna break, which followed the Munich premiere by four months). The compositional ambitions of _Idomeneo_ — large-scale opera seria, dense orchestration, extensive chorus writing — reflected Mozart's hope for a Munich court appointment; the appointment did not materialize, and the redirection to Vienna freelance status produced the operatic output we have today.

### 13.4 The Stories

#### Da Ponte and the _Così_ libretto

The libretto for _Così fan tutte_ was originally composed for Antonio Salieri (the other principal Vienna operatic composer of the period), who began setting it but then abandoned the project. The libretto then passed to Mozart, who composed the opera across late 1789-early 1790. The Salieri-then-Mozart transfer is occasionally cited in popular biography but is not central to the work's interpretive history; Da Ponte's text is the libretto Mozart set, and the music is fully Mozart's.

The libretto's premise — the wager, the disguise, the two-sister fidelity test — has antecedents in earlier Italian comic theater and in _commedia dell'arte_ traditions. The specific framing of the wager (the older cynic Don Alfonso betting the two younger officers that their fiancées will yield) is Da Ponte's contribution. The libretto's title comes from a line within the libretto itself (Don Alfonso uses the phrase as his conclusion); the title's generalizing claim ("thus do they all") was understood as cynical even in 1790.

#### The dramatic ambiguity of the reconciliation

The opera's ending is dramatically ambiguous. The disguise is revealed; the women have yielded to the disguised suitors; the men are angry but also have their own complicity in the deception. The libretto provides a quick "reconciliation" — the four are brought together for a closing ensemble. But the reconciliation is dramatically unstable. Will Fiordiligi and Guglielmo continue their engagement, knowing that Fiordiligi yielded to the disguised Ferrando? Will Dorabella and Ferrando continue, knowing that Dorabella yielded more quickly than her sister? Will the four marry as originally planned, or will they reconfigure their relationships, or will none of them marry?

The libretto does not resolve the question; the closing ensemble's text is general affirmation without specific resolution. Directorial choices vary: some productions present the reconciliation as genuine and the marriages as proceeding as originally planned; others present the reconciliation as forced and the marriages as compromised; others have the four couples swap permanently (Fiordiligi marries the now-revealed Ferrando, Dorabella marries Guglielmo); still others present the four as separated, the original engagements broken.

The musical writing in the closing ensemble does not adjudicate. Mozart's music is harmonically settled (E♭ major closing material) but is brief and emotionally muted. The composer does not provide either the triumphant reconciliation that would settle the question affirmatively or the unstable dissolution that would settle it negatively. The interpretive openness is built in.

#### Recording history of _Così_

The complete-recording references:

Karl Böhm with the Philharmonia Orchestra (Columbia/EMI, 1962, the chapter's Primary Recording for the trio) features Elisabeth Schwarzkopf as Fiordiligi, Christa Ludwig as Dorabella, Hanny Steffek as Despina, Alfredo Kraus as Ferrando, Giuseppe Taddei as Guglielmo, Walter Berry as Don Alfonso. The Schwarzkopf-Ludwig pairing is a benchmark soprano-mezzo combination in the Mozart opera recording history. Böhm's reading is warm and architecturally weighted.

Karl Böhm with the Vienna Philharmonic (Deutsche Grammophon, 1974) is the second Böhm complete _Così_ — Gundula Janowitz as Fiordiligi, Brigitte Fassbaender as Dorabella, Reri Grist as Despina, Peter Schreier as Ferrando, Hermann Prey as Guglielmo, Rolando Panerai as Don Alfonso. The Vienna-tradition warmth is at its peak.

Sir John Eliot Gardiner with the English Baroque Soloists (Archiv, 1992) is the period-instrument complete reference. Amanda Roocroft as Fiordiligi, Rosa Mannion as Dorabella, Eirian James as Despina, Rainer Trost as Ferrando, Rodney Gilfry as Guglielmo, Claudio Nicolai as Don Alfonso.

Riccardo Muti with the Vienna Philharmonic (EMI, 1982-83) is the alternative modern-instrument reading. Margaret Marshall as Fiordiligi, Agnes Baltsa as Dorabella.

Sir Charles Mackerras with the Scottish Chamber Orchestra (Telarc, 1993) is the modern reference with Felicity Lott as Fiordiligi, Marie McLaughlin as Dorabella.

Otto Klemperer with the Philharmonia Orchestra (EMI, 1971) is the older monumental reading. Margaret Price as Fiordiligi, Yvonne Minton as Dorabella.

Daniel Barenboim with the Berlin Philharmonic (Erato, 1989) is another modern alternative.

The Joseph Krips Vienna State Opera recording (Decca, 1955) is the historic reference with Lisa Della Casa as Fiordiligi.

#### Recording history of _Idomeneo_

The complete-recording references:

Sir Colin Davis with the Royal Opera House Covent Garden (Philips, 1991, the chapter's Primary Recording) features Anthony Rolfe Johnson as Idomeneo, Anne Sofie von Otter as Idamante (sung by a mezzo-soprano in the trouser-role convention; the role was originally composed for a castrato), Sylvia McNair as Ilia, Hillevi Martinpelto as Elettra.

Karl Böhm with the Staatskapelle Dresden (Deutsche Grammophon, 1977-78) is the older modern-instrument reference. Wieslaw Ochman as Idomeneo, Peter Schreier as Idamante (sung by a tenor in some performance traditions; the Idamante role can be cast as either mezzo-soprano or tenor depending on the version).

Sir John Eliot Gardiner with the English Baroque Soloists (Archiv, 1990) is the period-instrument reference. Anthony Rolfe Johnson as Idomeneo, Anne Sofie von Otter as Idamante.

René Jacobs with the Freiburger Barockorchester (Harmonia Mundi, 2008) is the contemporary period-instrument alternative.

The Sir Colin Davis BBC Symphony Orchestra recording (Philips, 1971) is an earlier Davis reading worth knowing.

The Nikolaus Harnoncourt Mozarteum Orchestra Salzburg recording (Teldec, 1980) is another period-instrument-aware alternative.

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find Böhm / Philharmonia Orchestra (Columbia/EMI, 1962). Listen to the "Soave sia il vento" trio a second time and attend to three concrete moments:

  1. **The orchestral introduction (the first fifteen seconds).** Confirm: strings in soft sustained chords; tempo Andante; harmonic language E major (warm, lyric, settled). The orchestral introduction is brief and prepares the trio's quiet entry.

  2. **The three-voice weaving.** Listen for how the two soprano voices (Fiordiligi and Dorabella) move together in close harmony — thirds and sixths — while Don Alfonso's bass provides the foundation. The chamber-music-within-opera texture has become a textbook example of the form.

  3. **The closing cadence.** The trio extends across a sustained lyric arc and closes on a quiet E-major cadence. The orchestral accompaniment recedes; the three voices settle into the final chord. The closing is one of the quietest in the Mozart opera literature.

For _Idomeneo_ , find Davis / Royal Opera House Covent Garden (Philips, 1991) or Gardiner / English Baroque Soloists (Archiv, 1990). The three structural anchors are:

a. **"Fuor del mar" (Act II).** The tenor showpiece — listen for the orchestral sea-figures running under the vocal line and for the long coloratura runs that test the tenor's upper register.

b. **The quartet "Andrò ramingo e solo" (Act III).** Slow, E♭ major, four characters' lines held in suspension. The ensemble ends without resolution; listen for the way the four positions remain irreconcilable at the close.

c. **Elettra's "D'Oreste, d'Aiace" (Act III).** C-minor mad scene, driving Allegro assai. Listen for the stabbing orchestral figures and the wide leaps in the vocal line; the aria ends with Elettra's collapse.

Three additional _Così_ moments worth anchoring on a re-listen of the complete opera:

  4. **Fiordiligi's "Come scoglio" (Act I).** Fiordiligi's first big aria — her defiant declaration of constancy to her absent fiancé Guglielmo, sung to reject the disguised Ferrando's advances. _Listening anchor:_ the vocal line is built on extreme intervallic leaps — wide jumps from the chest register up to the top of the soprano range and back down — that are the aria's signature. The leaps are deliberately exaggerated; Mozart is writing parody-seria, gently mocking Fiordiligi's overstated determination through music that is too grand for its actual emotional ground. The orchestral writing is full and brassy. Listen for the leaps — they are the easiest fingerprint of the aria.

  5. **Fiordiligi's "Per pietà, ben mio, perdona" (Act II).** Fiordiligi's second big aria — her conflicted reckoning with her growing attraction to the disguised Ferrando. The aria is the dramatic counterweight to "Come scoglio": where the Act I aria was overstated defiance, the Act II aria is genuine inward struggle. _Listening anchor:_ the obbligato writing for the two horns. Mozart gives the horns an extended, prominent role across the aria — long lyric phrases that wind around the soprano line. Listen for the horn entries; the horn-and-soprano dialogue is what gives the aria its specific emotional color and its slight melancholy weight.

  6. **The closing "Tutti accusan le donne" sextet.** Near the end of the opera, before the final reconciliation, Don Alfonso delivers his moral: "Tutti accusan le donne, ed io le scuso" — "Everyone accuses women, and I excuse them" — leading into the cynical conclusion that "così fan tutte." _Listening anchor:_ the texture is mock-solemn — all six characters joining in close harmony on a slow, deliberate setting of the title phrase. The music carries the weight of an oratorio chorale, while the words deliver the libretto's most pointed cynicism. The mismatch between music and meaning is the moment that most modern productions hinge their interpretation on; listen for it and decide what you think Mozart is doing.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : pair "Soave sia il vento" with the Act II "Per pietà, ben mio, perdona" — Fiordiligi's great Act II aria of conflicted fidelity. The aria is the dramatic counterpart to the trio: the trio shows the women in unaware sincerity; the aria shows Fiordiligi in self-conscious struggle with her growing attraction to the disguised Ferrando. Hearing the trio and the aria together gives a fuller sense of _Così_ 's dramatic complexity. The Böhm 1962 complete recording includes both.

**Read** : Cliff Eisen and Simon P. Keefe (eds.), _The Cambridge Mozart Encyclopedia_ (Cambridge University Press, 2006), particularly the _Così fan tutte_ entry. The Encyclopedia entry gives the scholarly survey of the work's compositional context, the Da Ponte libretto, the reception history (the nineteenth-century complications and the twentieth-century rehabilitation), and the modern interpretive debates. The Encyclopedia is the principal academic reference on Mozart and is the standard treatment for any specific question about the catalog. About 700 pages total; specific entries are typically two to ten pages.

---

## Chapter 14: _Die Zauberflöte_ K. 620 (1791)

_Die Zauberflöte_ (_The Magic Flute_ , K. 620) premiered at the Freihaus-Theater auf der Wieden in Vienna on September 30, 1791. It was Mozart's last completed opera (_La clemenza di Tito_ K. 621 premiered in Prague on September 6, 1791 — three weeks before the _Magic Flute_ premiere; the chronology of composition for the two late operas overlaps, but _La clemenza_ was a quickly-composed coronation commission for the Bohemian throne while the _Magic Flute_ was the slower-developed Vienna _Singspiel_ project that occupied Mozart across spring and summer 1791). The librettist was Emanuel Schikaneder, the theater impresario who ran the Freihaus-Theater and who created the role of Papageno for himself to perform.

The _Magic Flute_ is the principal Mozart late-style work in the opera category, alongside the Clarinet Concerto K. 622 (October 1791) and the Requiem K. 626 (autumn 1791, Chapter 18). The three late works share several concrete features the volume tracks under the late-style transparency heading: wind writing that gives one player at a time the leading line (Sarastro's "O Isis" supported by a wind chorale of basset horns and bassoons; the K. 622 first-movement clarinet against light strings); strophic or near-strophic melodic lines (Papageno's verses; Sarastro's "In diesen heil'gen Hallen"); and harmonic moves that arrive without the dynamic surge or chromatic intensification typical of the Da Ponte operas.

The opera is also one of Mozart's principal Masonic works. Mozart had been a Mason from December 1784 (initiated into the Vienna Lodge "Zur Wohltätigkeit"); the _Magic Flute_ 's libretto contains Masonic symbolism throughout (the trials of fire and water, the brotherhood of the priesthood, the number-three structures, the _animae duae sed cor unum_ ("two souls but one heart") formulations); the music similarly contains Masonic gestures (the three opening chords in E♭ major; the three-flat key signature; the wind writing for the priests' chorus). The Masonic content is well-attested in the surviving Mozart-Schikaneder libretto materials and in the modern scholarship of Cliff Eisen, Maynard Solomon, and others.

This chapter anchors on six musical moments in _Magic Flute_ : the Queen of the Night's two arias ("O zittre nicht" from Act I and "Der Hölle Rache" from Act II), Sarastro's two Act II arias ("O Isis und Osiris" and "In diesen heil'gen Hallen"), Papageno's "Der Vogelfänger bin ich ja," and the Act II finale (with several anchor points inside it, including the Pamina-Tamino reunion, the flute solo through the trials, and the Papageno-Papagena duet). The chapter does not attempt a complete-opera walkthrough; the dramatic complexity of the _Magic Flute_ 's plot (the rescue narrative that turns into an initiation narrative) deserves a fuller treatment than this chapter can give.

### 14.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

Spring and summer 1791. Vienna. Mozart was in the last year of his life (he would die December 5, 1791, at age thirty-five). The Vienna economic situation had partly recovered from the 1788-89 contraction; teaching income was steady; the Imperial Chamber Composer appointment (December 1787) provided a baseline salary. The Magic Flute commission from Schikaneder was a different kind of project from the Burgtheater opera commissions of the Da Ponte trilogy — Schikaneder's Freihaus-Theater was a suburban Vienna popular theater catering to a broader audience than the imperial Burgtheater. The audience was less aristocratic, less Italian-opera oriented, more interested in _Singspiel_ — German-language opera with spoken dialogue interleaved with sung numbers, the genre Mozart had used in _Entführung_ of 1782 (Chapter 5).

The Schikaneder collaboration was probably warm. Emanuel Schikaneder (1751-1812) was a theater impresario, actor, and singer whose company had been performing at the Freihaus-Theater since 1789. He was also a fellow Mason (initiated into the Salzburg Lodge in 1778; Mozart and Schikaneder may have known each other from the Salzburg years). Schikaneder wrote the libretto and created the role of Papageno (the bird-catcher) for himself to perform in the premiere; the role's character — folkloric, comic, the working-class everyman — matches Schikaneder's own theatrical persona.

The _Magic Flute_ was a popular success at its September 30, 1791 premiere. Mozart conducted the premiere; subsequent performances were directed by others. The opera was performed twenty times in October 1791 alone and reached its hundredth performance within just over a year. The popularity continued through the nineteenth century and into the present; the _Magic Flute_ is among the most-performed operas in the standard repertoire and is the principal Mozart opera many listeners encounter first.

Mozart's letters from late 1791 mention the _Magic Flute_ repeatedly. The October 7, 1791 letter to Constanze (from Vienna, while Constanze was at Baden taking the waters) describes Mozart attending a performance at the Freihaus-Theater and watching the audience's enthusiastic response. The mood of the letter is contented; Mozart had no premonition that he had two months to live, and the _Magic Flute_ 's success was the principal professional pleasure of his last months.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen (Queen of the Night "O zittre nicht")

  1. **The dramatic setup.** Act I. The Queen of the Night addresses Tamino (the young prince who has just been rescued from a serpent by the Three Ladies). She asks Tamino to rescue her daughter Pamina, who has been (according to the Queen's narrative) abducted by Sarastro. The aria is a presentation of the rescue mission — the Queen establishing the dramatic premise of the opera as Tamino (and the audience) will initially understand it.

  2. **The vocal range.** The aria climbs into the upper soprano register repeatedly. The Queen of the Night is one of the highest-tessitura roles in the standard repertoire; the high F (and in "Der Hölle Rache" the high F6) is the technical challenge that defines who can sing the role. Listen for how Mozart paces the climb — the high notes arrive at moments of textual emphasis rather than scattered through the line.

  3. **The aria's two-section structure.** The aria has a slow opening section (the Queen's lyric appeal to Tamino's compassion) and a faster closing section (the Queen's promise of Pamina as Tamino's bride if he succeeds in the rescue). Listen for the structural shift; the two-section form is one of the operatic conventions of the period and gives the Queen's aria its dramatic range.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen (Sarastro "O Isis und Osiris")

  1. **The bass voice register.** Sarastro is sung by a deep bass. The aria sits in the bass voice's lower-middle register and asks for sustained tone production rather than virtuoso display. The aria's solemnity is built on the bass voice's gravity.

  2. **The wind writing.** The accompaniment uses two basset horns (the deep-toned tenor clarinet Stadler also played) and two bassoons to give the aria its solemn, almost liturgical color — the same Masonic-funeral sonority Mozart had used in K. 477. Listen for the wind writing; the strings are present but the foreground texture is carried by the wind chorale, with one or two winds visible at any moment rather than the full orchestra in tutti.

  3. **The prayer character.** The text is a prayer to the Egyptian gods Isis and Osiris to grant wisdom and strength to the two initiates Tamino and Pamina. The aria's character is prayerful, ceremonial, unhurried.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen (Papageno "Der Vogelfänger bin ich ja")

  1. **The popular-theater character.** Papageno is the bird-catcher, the comic working-class figure who serves as Tamino's companion in the initiation narrative. The aria is Papageno's self-introduction — a strophic song (multiple verses set to the same music) with the characteristic folkloric melodic shape Mozart used for popular-theater characters.

  2. **The pan-pipe motif.** Papageno plays a small set of pan-pipes; Mozart writes a recognizable five-note motif for the pan-pipes that punctuates the aria's verses. Listen for the motif; it returns several times in the opera and serves as Papageno's recurring musical tag.

  3. **The strophic form.** The aria has multiple verses, all set to the same music. The strophic form is the conventional popular-song structure of the period (and remains the conventional structure for most contemporary popular songs); its use in the _Magic Flute_ contrasts with the through-composed aria forms of the Da Ponte operas and is one of the markers of the _Singspiel_ popular-theater tradition.

#### One-sentence background

Composed in Vienna, spring through summer 1791 (premiered September 30, 1791); the complete opera runs about three hours; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Sir Georg Solti with the Vienna Philharmonic and the Vienna State Opera Chorus (Decca, 1969), featuring Cristina Deutekom as the Queen of the Night, Pilar Lorengar as Pamina, Stuart Burrows as Tamino, Hermann Prey as Papageno, and Martti Talvela as Sarastro. (Karl Böhm with the Berlin Philharmonic on Deutsche Grammophon, 1964, is the warmer mid-century alternative with Roberta Peters as the Queen of the Night and Fritz Wunderlich as Tamino; Sir John Eliot Gardiner with the English Baroque Soloists on Archiv, 1995, is the period-instrument alternative; Claudio Abbado with the Mahler Chamber Orchestra on Deutsche Grammophon, 2005, is the more recent modern reading.)

#### Search Hints

EN: "Mozart Magic Flute K 620 Solti Vienna" or "Mozart Zauberflöte Böhm Wunderlich" ZH: "莫扎特 魔笛 K620 索尔蒂 维也纳" 或 "莫扎特 魔笛 贝姆" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

### 14.2 The Listening Window

Timestamps below refer to the Solti Primary Recording. The Window covers five moments across the opera; we treat each briefly rather than walking through the complete work.

#### "O zittre nicht" (Act I, Queen of the Night)

The aria occurs early in Act I. Tamino has been rescued from the serpent by the Three Ladies; the Three Ladies have brought him to the Queen of the Night, who addresses him in this aria.

**The orchestral introduction.** Brief; the orchestra establishes the B♭ minor harmonic field (the Queen's principal key) and prepares the soprano entry.

**The slow opening section.** The Queen sings of her grief over her daughter Pamina's abduction. The vocal writing is lyric and sustained; the soprano's range sits in the middle register at first and climbs as the dramatic intensity builds.

**The transition into the faster closing section.** The aria shifts tempo and key. The Queen offers Tamino her daughter Pamina as bride if he succeeds in the rescue. The vocal writing becomes more elaborate; the upper register is more prominent.

**The closing.** The Queen reaches the upper register's climactic notes and closes the aria. The dramatic mission is now Tamino's; the Queen has framed the rescue narrative as the opera's principal action — though as the opera progresses (and Tamino discovers that Sarastro's brotherhood is the morally legitimate authority and the Queen is actually the deceptive figure) the framing will be reversed.

#### "Der Hölle Rache" (Act II, Queen of the Night)

The Act II aria occurs after Pamina has been brought to Sarastro's temple precinct. The Queen of the Night appears to Pamina and demands that Pamina kill Sarastro. The aria is the Queen's command — vehement, vengeful, and technically formidable: it requires a soprano who can place high coloratura at sustained vehemence.

The aria climbs to F6 (high F, the F above high C) at its most-famous staccato passage. The vocal writing is virtuosic coloratura — rapid scale figures, leaping intervals, sustained high notes. Only sopranos with exceptional upper-register capacity can sing the role, which sits among the principal showcase parts in the standard repertoire.

_Listening anchor (the F6 staccato passage):_ the aria's signature passage is a fast detached run that climbs the staccato arpeggio and lands on the high F at the top, repeats, lands again. There are two such bursts of high-F staccato in the aria, set against the words "Hört der Mutter Schwur" / "der Mutter Schwur" ("Hear the mother's oath") — the Queen at her most vehement. On first listen the easiest way to find them is to wait for the orchestra to drop out into thin accompaniment under the soprano: the staccato climbing figures appear precisely at those moments of orchestral thinness, and the F6 is the top of each climb. The two passages are about thirty seconds apart and frame the aria's central section.

The Solti reading's Cristina Deutekom handles the role; other classic Queen of the Night recordings include Edda Moser (with Wolfgang Sawallisch), Sumi Jo (with Sir Charles Mackerras), Diana Damrau (more recent), and historically Roberta Peters (with Böhm) and Erika Köth.

#### "O Isis und Osiris" (Act II, Sarastro)

The aria occurs in Act II as Sarastro prepares to lead the brotherhood in the initiation trials for Tamino and Pamina. Sarastro addresses the Egyptian gods Isis and Osiris, asking for their blessing on the initiation.

The aria is short: about three minutes. The bass voice sits in the lower-middle register and sustains the prayer character through long phrases. The wind writing — two basset horns and two bassoons — provides the solemn accompaniment, the same somber wind color Mozart had used in the _Maurerische Trauermusik_ K. 477. The harmonic language is F major, settled and unhurried.

The aria is a standard-repertoire bass piece, programmed on most bass recital programs that include Mozart. The classic recordings include Kurt Moll (with Solti), Martti Talvela (with Solti), Gottlob Frick (with various conductors), Cesare Siepi (with various conductors), and historically Alexander Kipnis.

#### "In diesen heil'gen Hallen" (Act II, Sarastro)

Sarastro's second great Act II aria, addressed to Pamina after the Queen of the Night has demanded that Pamina kill him. Where the Queen has just sung of vengeance, Sarastro responds with the brotherhood's principle: within the sacred halls, vengeance has no place; the brotherhood's response to enmity is to lead the enemy to love. The aria is the philosophical answer to "Der Hölle Rache" — the two arias stand in deliberate contrast, the dark vengeance of the Queen against the serene mercy of Sarastro.

_Listening anchor:_ the aria is in E major, slow tempo, two strophic verses to the same music. The bass voice descends to a low F at the close of each verse on the word "Freund" ("friend") — one of the lowest sustained notes in the standard bass repertoire and the aria's vocal signature. The orchestral writing is restrained: strings primarily, with light wind support. Listen for the simplicity of the melodic line — Sarastro's wisdom is presented through music that is calm, unornamented, almost folksong-like. The aria's brevity (about three and a half minutes for both verses) and its low-register cadence are what give the aria its specific gravity.

#### "Der Vogelfänger bin ich ja" (Act I, Papageno)

The aria occurs early in Act I, shortly after Tamino's rescue from the serpent. Papageno enters and introduces himself in song. He is the bird-catcher in the Queen of the Night's service; the song is his trade-introduction and his self-portrait.

The aria is strophic — multiple verses to the same music. The melodic shape is folkloric and immediately memorable; the pan-pipe motif (five descending notes) punctuates the verses. The accompaniment is light — strings primarily, with light wind decoration.

The Solti reading's Hermann Prey is a frequently cited Papageno. Other classic Papagenos include Walter Berry (with Karl Böhm), Erich Kunz (with Wilhelm Furtwängler), Christian Gerhaher (more recent), and historically Hans Hotter.

#### The Act II finale

The Act II finale is one of the longest in the Mozart opera literature, running about forty minutes. The finale covers the completion of the initiation trials, the reconciliation of the dramatic conflicts, the defeat of the Queen of the Night and the Three Ladies, and the closing celebration of light over darkness.

For the chapter's listening anchor, we focus on five structural moments within the finale:

**The Pamina-Tamino reunion.** Just before the trials begin, Pamina — having been told by the Three Boys that Tamino still loves her, after she had despaired and nearly killed herself — is reunited with Tamino at the entrance to the trial precinct. _Listening anchor:_ the orchestra modulates upward into a warm major key as Pamina catches sight of Tamino; her first phrases ("Tamino mein! O welch ein Glück!" — "My Tamino! O what joy!") are sustained and lyric. The dramatic shift from her preceding suicide scene to the reunion is a central emotional moment — the music carries the full weight of her near-death and her return. Listen for the moment Pamina takes Tamino's hand and tells him that she will lead him through the trials; her vocal line opens out into long lyric phrases that the orchestra supports with quiet sustained chords. This is the emotional center of the finale.

**Tamino's flute solo through the trials of fire and water.** Tamino and Pamina, joined hand in hand, proceed through the symbolic ordeals. Tamino plays the magic flute — Mozart writes a real flute solo for the orchestral flute, representing the on-stage instrument Tamino is supposed to be playing — and the flute's melody guides them through. _Listening anchor:_ the orchestral texture during the trials is unusual. Two trumpets and timpani sustain a slow march-like rhythm (the ceremonial-trial sonority); the solo flute plays a simple stepwise melody above; lower strings provide minimal support. The voices enter only sparingly — Tamino and Pamina each sing brief lyric phrases between the flute solo passages. Listen for the contrast between the heavy trumpet-and-timpani solemnity and the floating flute melody on top; the texture has no real parallel elsewhere in the opera.

**The defeat of the Queen of the Night.** The Queen, the Three Ladies, and Monostatos (the Moor in Sarastro's service who has betrayed the brotherhood to the Queen) attempt to break into the temple. They are repulsed by the brotherhood's chant; the harmonic shift represents the defeat of the dark forces.

**The Papageno-Papagena duet "Pa-pa-pa-pa-pa-Papagena!"** After Papageno has nearly hanged himself out of despair at being alone and the Three Boys have intervened to bring him Papagena (his promised partner), the two finally meet. The duet is the comic counterpart to the Tamino-Pamina reunion. _Listening anchor:_ Papageno (baritone) starts the stuttering "Pa- pa- pa- pa-" trying to get out his beloved's name; Papagena (light soprano, the soubrette role) answers him in kind; the two toss the syllables back and forth across the orchestra until the names finally come out whole ("Papageno!" "Papagena!"). The duet is in G major, fast tempo, light orchestration; the comic stutter is unmistakable on first listen.

**The closing celebration.** Sarastro and the brotherhood celebrate the success of the initiation. The harmonic language returns to E♭ major (the opera's home key, the Masonic three-flat signature). The closing chorus is celebratory and unhurried.

### 14.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The late-style transparency

_Magic Flute_ is one of the principal late-style transparency works in Mozart's catalog. Concrete points of comparison: Papageno's "Der Vogelfänger" is strophic with light string accompaniment and a five-note wind motif; "O Isis und Osiris" sets a solo bass against a wind chorale (two basset horns and two bassoons) with the strings reduced to harmonic support. Compare with _Don Giovanni_ 's second-act finale where strings, full winds, brass, and timpani together drive the Commendatore scene; or with _Figaro_ 's Act II finale where six voices stack against the full orchestra.

The _Magic Flute_ 's harmonic language is also more settled: E♭ major as the home key, the principal arias in clearly diatonic keys without extensive modulation, and the few modulations (e.g., the lift into the warm major key as Pamina sees Tamino in the Act II finale) arrive without the chromatic intensification that announces the _Don Giovanni_ harmonic shifts. The late style here is what Mozart's compositional thinking had arrived at — not the dark-key tragic register of his peak operas but a lighter, more direct, more lyric register.

The late-style transparency feature reaches one of its principal operatic peaks in _Magic Flute_. Combined with K. 595's Larghetto (Chapter 8) and the Clarinet Concerto K. 622, the _Magic Flute_ completes the late-style cluster. Chapter 19 §19.7 collects the late-style transparency works.

#### The Masonic content

Mozart was a Mason from December 1784 (initiated into the Vienna Lodge "Zur Wohltätigkeit"; the Lodge merged with two others in 1786 to form the new Lodge "Zur neugekrönten Hoffnung"). Schikaneder was also a Mason. The _Magic Flute_ libretto and music contain Masonic symbolism throughout.

The principal Masonic elements: the three opening chords in E♭ major (the Masonic "knocking" gesture, signifying admission to the lodge); the E♭-major home key with its three flats (the Masonic number three); the initiation narrative (Tamino and Pamina pass through trials of fire and water, symbolic of Masonic initiation rites); the brotherhood of the priesthood (Sarastro's followers represent the Masonic brotherhood); the contrast between dark and light (the Queen of the Night versus Sarastro, representing ignorance versus enlightenment in Masonic terms); the wind writing for the priests' chorus (Mozart had composed several Masonic funeral works including the _Maurerische Trauermusik_ K. 477 of 1785, which used the same kind of solemn wind writing).

The Masonic content was openly displayed at the 1791 premiere; the opera was not a coded or covert Masonic work but an openly Masonic one. The Vienna audience of 1791 (including the Masonic membership and the broader theatrical public) would have recognized the symbolism. The Masonic dimension is therefore one of the principal interpretive frameworks for the opera and is well-established in modern scholarship.

For listeners who are not Masonic-familiar: the Masonic content is part of the opera's intellectual depth but does not require Masonic knowledge to appreciate the work. The _Magic Flute_ operates on multiple levels — as a popular-theater fairy tale (the dragon, the magic flute, the bird-catcher, the comic chase scenes), as a Masonic initiation drama (the trials, the brotherhood, the enlightenment theme), as a personal-romantic love story (Tamino and Pamina's developing love), and as a music-theatrical entertainment. The first level requires no specialized knowledge; the deeper levels enrich the experience for the informed listener.

#### The opera as Singspiel

_Magic Flute_ is _Singspiel_ — German-language opera with spoken dialogue interleaved with sung numbers. The genre is the same as _Entführung_ (Chapter 5) but the dramatic stakes and the musical sophistication are higher. _Magic Flute_ is the principal _Singspiel_ in the standard repertoire and is the work in which the genre reached its highest formal development.

The _Singspiel_ tradition's structural features: the spoken dialogue is in German and is often closer to popular-theater speech patterns than to literary verse; the sung numbers include arias, ensembles, choruses, and dance numbers; the alternation between speech and song creates a different dramatic rhythm than the through-composed Italian opera of the Da Ponte trilogy. _Magic Flute_ uses the _Singspiel_ alternation throughout but increases the musical density of the sung numbers to a level that approaches the Italian-opera tradition.

The _Singspiel_ tradition matters historically because it became the foundation for the nineteenth-century German opera tradition — Carl Maria von Weber, Heinrich Marschner, Albert Lortzing, and ultimately Richard Wagner all built on the _Singspiel_ foundation. _Magic Flute_ is one of the principal works the nineteenth-century German operatic tradition drew on as a model; Wagner's _Die Meistersinger von Nürnberg_ (1868) is a particularly clear descendant of the _Magic Flute_ 's genre and dramatic structure.

#### The Queen of the Night as vocal showcase

The Queen of the Night is one of the principal vocal showcase roles in the standard repertoire. The two arias — "O zittre nicht" (Act I) and "Der Hölle Rache" (Act II) — together cover an unusually wide range of soprano writing and require unusual technical capacity. The role's high F (and the famous high F6 in "Der Hölle Rache") is the technical signature; sopranos who can place these notes reliably are a small group.

The role was created by Josepha Hofer, Mozart's sister-in-law (Constanze Mozart's older sister, the soprano Josepha Weber who had married the violinist Franz de Paula Hofer in 1788). Josepha Hofer was an established Vienna soprano of the period; Mozart wrote the role for her specific vocal capacities, much as he had written for Catarina Cavalieri (Konstanze in _Entführung_) and for the various other specific singers across his operatic catalog.

The role's vocal demands have made it one of the principal showcase parts for coloratura sopranos through the nineteenth and twentieth centuries. The Queen of the Night appears in nearly every soprano coloratura recital; the role has been recorded by every major coloratura soprano of the twentieth and twenty-first centuries. Recorded Queens of the Night include Erna Berger, Rita Streich, Erika Köth, Roberta Peters, Cristina Deutekom (the Solti Primary Recording), Edda Moser, Lucia Popp, Sumi Jo, Edita Gruberová, Natalie Dessay, and Diana Damrau.

### 14.4 The Stories

#### The Schikaneder collaboration

Emanuel Schikaneder (1751-1812) was the librettist and producer of _Magic Flute_ and created the role of Papageno for himself to perform. His career: he had been a touring actor and singer across German-speaking Europe in the 1770s; he became a theater impresario in the 1780s; he took over the Freihaus-Theater auf der Wieden in Vienna in 1789. The Freihaus-Theater was a suburban popular theater catering to a broader audience than the imperial Burgtheater; Schikaneder's company performed _Singspiel_ , comedies, and popular spectacles.

The Mozart-Schikaneder collaboration on _Magic Flute_ probably began in spring 1791. Schikaneder wrote the libretto; Mozart composed the music; both worked in close consultation as the composition progressed. The libretto contains elements from multiple sources: the fairy-tale tradition (the dragon, the rescue, the magic instruments), the Enlightenment-philosophical tradition (the wisdom-versus-ignorance theme), the Masonic tradition (the initiation rites, the brotherhood), and the popular-theater tradition (Papageno's comic interludes, the chase scenes, the bird-catcher's family).

The collaboration was apparently warm. Mozart's letters from late 1791 mention Schikaneder favorably; the popular success of the opera benefited both Mozart and Schikaneder. After Mozart's death in December 1791, Schikaneder continued to produce the opera at the Freihaus-Theater; he wrote a sequel libretto (_Das Labyrinth_ , 1798, set by Peter von Winter) that did not achieve the original's success.

Schikaneder built the Theater an der Wien in 1801 (the Vienna theater that opened with a production of his sequel _Das Labyrinth_ and that became one of the principal Vienna opera venues for the next century). He died in Vienna in 1812 in financial difficulty.

#### The 1791 premiere and its reception

The premiere on September 30, 1791 at the Freihaus-Theater auf der Wieden was a popular success. The opera was performed twenty times in October 1791 alone; Mozart attended at least one performance (the October 7 performance, mentioned in his letter to Constanze) and conducted the premiere itself. The popular reception was strong; the opera entered the Vienna repertoire immediately and remained there.

The critical reception was more mixed. Some Vienna critics found the libretto's mix of fairy-tale, philosophical, and Masonic elements incoherent; others praised the music and accepted the libretto's eclecticism. The Goethe enthusiasm for _Magic Flute_ (Goethe began writing a sequel libretto in 1795, _Der Zauberflöte zweiter Theil_ , that he never completed) is one of the principal early literary endorsements of the work; Goethe saw in the _Magic Flute_ a serious philosophical drama that the popular-theater format had obscured but not eliminated.

By the early nineteenth century _Magic Flute_ was being performed across German-speaking Europe and was settling into permanent place in the standard repertoire. The popularity has continued through the present.

#### _Magic Flute_ in the cultural-context history

_Magic Flute_ has been read as multiple things across two centuries of reception:

As a fairy tale: the dragon, the rescue, the magic instruments, the bird-catcher, the happy ending. The fairy-tale reading is accessible to children and is the principal way _Magic Flute_ is often introduced to younger audiences.

As a Masonic initiation drama: the trials, the brotherhood, the enlightenment theme. The Masonic reading is the principal scholarly reading and is well-established in modern Mozart scholarship.

As an Enlightenment philosophical drama: the wisdom-versus-ignorance theme, the brotherhood of humanity, the celebration of light over darkness. The Enlightenment reading overlaps with the Masonic reading and was the principal Goethe reading.

As a problematic representation of gender and race: the Queen of the Night's portrayal as the deceptive woman, Monostatos's portrayal as the Moor-traitor in Sarastro's service, the brotherhood's exclusively male character. The critical reading has emerged in late-twentieth-century reception and is now part of the standard interpretive landscape; modern productions sometimes engage these problems directly through directorial choices, sometimes pass over them.

As a music-theatrical entertainment: the popular-theater scale, the strophic songs, the chase scenes, the pan-pipe motifs. The entertainment reading is the surface level and is what most audiences engage on first encounter.

All five readings are present in the work; the work supports multiple interpretations simultaneously. _Magic Flute_ asks the listener to engage multiple levels at once rather than settling into a single interpretive position.

#### Recording history of _Magic Flute_

A handful of complete recordings cover the principal traditions:

**Solti / Vienna Philharmonic (Decca, 1969)** — the chapter's Primary Recording; Cristina Deutekom (Queen), Pilar Lorengar (Pamina), Stuart Burrows (Tamino), Hermann Prey (Papageno), Martti Talvela (Sarastro). Dramatically intense and orchestrally rich.

**Böhm / Berlin Philharmonic (DG, 1964)** — the warmer mid-century reference; Roberta Peters, Evelyn Lear, Fritz Wunderlich, Dietrich Fischer-Dieskau, Franz Crass. The Wunderlich Tamino is the cycle's calling card.

**Klemperer / Philharmonia (Columbia/EMI, 1964)** — the monumental alternative; Lucia Popp (Queen), Gundula Janowitz, Nicolai Gedda, Walter Berry, Gottlob Frick.

**Gardiner / English Baroque Soloists (Archiv, 1995)** — the period-instrument complete reference; Cyndia Sieden, Christiane Oelze, Michael Schade, Andreas Schmidt, Harry Peeters.

**Abbado / Mahler Chamber Orchestra (DG, 2005)** — the more recent modern-instrument reference; Erika Miklósa, Dorothea Röschmann, Christoph Strehl, Hanno Müller-Brachmann, René Pape.

Historic curiosities worth seeking out for specific voices: Furtwängler's 1949-50 Salzburg Vienna Philharmonic recording (Erich Kunz's Papageno); Christie / Les Arts Florissants (Erato, 1995) for a second period-instrument reading.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The _Magic Flute_ operates on multiple levels — as a fairy tale, as a Masonic initiation drama, as an Enlightenment philosophical work, and as a popular-theater entertainment. Most operas operate on one or two levels at a time. What does Mozart and Schikaneder gain by writing an opera that operates on four levels at once? Does the multi-level structure produce coherence or incoherence? Is the work better understood as a unified vision or as a multi-vocal text?"_
* _"The Queen of the Night's two arias span the soprano voice's full virtuoso range. The Act I 'O zittre nicht' has a slow opening section and a faster closing; the Act II 'Der Hölle Rache' is vehement coloratura with the famous high F6. Listen to both arias and identify how the Queen's vocal characterization changes across the opera. Is the Queen the same character in Act II as in Act I, and how does the music distinguish (or not distinguish) the two appearances?"_
* _"The Masonic content of _Magic Flute_ was openly displayed at the 1791 premiere; the Vienna audience would have recognized the symbolism. Modern audiences typically have no Masonic familiarity and engage the symbolism only through scholarly commentary. What is lost when the Masonic content becomes invisible to the listener? What is gained when the work is approached without the Masonic framework — as a fairy-tale and a personal-romantic love story rather than as a Masonic initiation drama?"_
* _"Papageno is the comic working-class figure created by Schikaneder for himself to perform. The role's musical character is folkloric — strophic songs, the pan-pipe motif, the popular-theater scale. Papageno's relationship to the more elevated characters (Tamino, Pamina, Sarastro) is one of the dramatic axes of the opera. What does the inclusion of the folkloric register accomplish in an opera that also includes the Queen of the Night's coloratura virtuosity and Sarastro's bass solemnity? Is _Magic Flute_ a popular-theater opera that has been elevated, or an elevated opera that has incorporated popular-theater elements?"_
* _"_Magic Flute_ is the late-style transparency work in the operatic register, alongside K. 595's Larghetto (Chapter 8) and the Clarinet Concerto K. 622. Listen to Sarastro's 'O Isis und Osiris' (bass voice supported by a wind chorale of basset horns and bassoons, strings as background) alongside the Don Giovanni Commendatore scene (strings, full winds, brass, and timpani driving together). Both are bass-register moments of weight in late Mozart, but the orchestration weight is on opposite sides. Does _Magic Flute_ feel less dramatic than the Da Ponte operas, or differently dramatic?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find Solti / Vienna Philharmonic (Decca, 1969) or Böhm / Berlin Philharmonic (Deutsche Grammophon, 1964). Listen to five concrete observables:

  1. **"Der Hölle Rache" — the F6 staccato passages (Act II).** Wait for the two moments in the aria's central section when the orchestra thins out and the soprano launches a fast detached climbing run that peaks on the high F, set to "Hört der Mutter Schwur." The two bursts frame the aria's central section and are the technical signature of the role.

  2. **Sarastro's "In diesen heil'gen Hallen" — the low F on "Freund" (Act II).** Listen for the bass voice's descent to a low F at the close of each strophe on the word "Freund." The aria is the calm philosophical answer to the Queen's vengeance aria heard a few minutes earlier; the low cadences give it its specific gravity.

  3. **Papageno "Der Vogelfänger bin ich ja" (Act I).** Listen for the pan-pipe motif (five descending notes) that punctuates the verses. The strophic form gives the aria its popular-song character; the folkloric melodic shape is immediately memorable.

  4. **Act II finale — the Pamina-Tamino reunion and the flute solo through the trials.** Listen for the modulation upward into the warm major key as Pamina sees Tamino ("Tamino mein!"), then for the unusual texture of the trial sequence — the trumpet-and-timpani solemnity supporting the floating flute melody above.

  5. **Act II finale — the Papageno-Papagena duet ("Pa-pa-pa-pa-pa-Papagena!").** Listen for the stuttering antiphonal exchange — Papageno (baritone) and Papagena (light soprano) tossing the syllables back and forth in G major until the full names finally come out.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : pair _Magic Flute_ with the Clarinet Concerto K. 622 (October 1791) and the Requiem K. 626 (autumn 1791) — the three late-style works composed in Mozart's last year. The three together constitute the late-style cluster; hearing them in proximity gives the fullest sense of what Mozart's compositional thinking had arrived at in his final months. Standard recordings of K. 622: Sabine Meyer with the Berlin Philharmonic under Claudio Abbado (EMI, 1998), Karl Leister with the Berlin Philharmonic, the Anthony Pay reading on period clarinet with the Academy of Ancient Music.

**Read** : Maynard Solomon, _Mozart: A Life_ (HarperCollins, 1995), specifically the chapters on 1791 — the _Magic Flute_ composition, the Masonic content, the late-style transparency, and the death. Solomon's treatment of the _Magic Flute_ puts the work in the context of Mozart's late biographical year and is one of the standard accounts. About forty pages on the late period; the full biography is about six hundred pages and Chapter 23 of this volume gives the recommended reading order.

---

## Chapter 15: Mozart at the Piano Alone — Selected Piano Sonatas + Violin Sonatas

Mozart composed eighteen piano sonatas across his life — from the early Salzburg sonatas of 1774-75 (K. 279-284) through the late Vienna sonatas (K. 533, K. 545, K. 570, K. 576 of 1788-91). The middle ten sonatas form the core of the catalog. Mozart also composed thirty-five violin-and-piano sonatas, of which the Vienna-period sonatas K. 296 through K. 547 are the standard repertoire entries.

This chapter anchors on four piano sonatas: **No. 8 in A minor K. 310** (1778, the Paris-period sonata composed near the time of Mozart's mother's death), **No. 11 in A major K. 331** (1783, the "Alla Turca" finale and the theme-and-variations first movement), **No. 14 in C minor K. 457** (1784), and **No. 16 in C major "Sonata facile" K. 545** (1788). Brief audition: the **Violin Sonata K. 379** in G major (1781, the Vienna chamber-music intimacy of the violin-and-piano duos). The four anchor sonatas together span Mozart's mature piano writing — from the Paris-period grief sonata of his early twenties through the Vienna teaching-piece simplicity of his last years.

This chapter has uneven density by design: the K. 310 and K. 331 sections are substantive (the principal sonata anchors); the K. 457 and K. 545 sections are briefer; the violin sonata K. 379 section is the briefest.

### 15.1 Before You Listen — The Four Sonatas in Context

#### Hook Anecdote (K. 310)

Summer 1778. Paris. Mozart was twenty-two; he was in the Paris months of the Paris-and-Mannheim journey we covered in Chapter 4, where Anna Maria's July 3 death is narrated in full. Mozart composed the Piano Sonata in A minor K. 310 during the Paris summer — the autograph is not securely dated, but the work belongs to the Paris months either around or shortly after that loss.

K. 310 is the first of Mozart's two minor-key piano sonatas (the other is K. 457 in C minor of 1784). The A-minor key, the relentless rhythmic drive of the first movement, the chromatic darkness of the slow movement, the agitated finale — all are unusual for Mozart's piano-sonata writing of the period (the other Paris-era sonatas K. 309, K. 311, K. 330 are in major keys and have a lighter character). The work is conventionally heard as the Paris-period grief sonata, bearing the weight of Anna Maria's death and the journey's broader disappointments.

As with K. 364 (Chapter 4, the homecoming Sinfonia Concertante), Mozart left no annotation tying K. 310 to a specific biographical event. The reading of K. 310 as grief writing is interpretive rather than annotated. Solomon's treatment is the standard cautious one — the reading is plausible and consistent with Mozart's compositional pattern of writing emotionally weighted works in proximity to significant personal events, but it is not directly attested.

#### Hook Anecdote (K. 331)

  1783. Vienna. Mozart had been a Vienna freelance composer for two years and was at the start of his great Vienna keyboard career. The Piano Sonata in A major K. 331 was probably composed during a summer 1783 trip to Salzburg (the only Salzburg trip Mozart made after the 1781 break; he traveled with Constanze to introduce her to Leopold, who received her coolly). The sonata's three movements — a theme-and-variations first movement, a Menuetto-and-Trio second movement, and the famous "Alla Turca" finale — make it the Mozart piano sonata most listeners will recognize on first hearing.

The "Alla Turca" finale has stayed in active concert circulation since its first publication. The Turkish-style rhythm and percussion-imitating writing (the "Janissary" octaves in the bass evoking the stylized Turkish military band sound that _Entführung_ 's orchestration had also used) made the finale a popular concert piece almost immediately. The "Alla Turca" has been transcribed for orchestra, jazz band, ringtones, and nearly every conceivable performing medium; the melody is one of the few classical tunes an ordinary educated listener can identify by ear.

K. 331's first movement is a theme-and-variations — a structural choice that connects to the K. 453 finale (Chapter 6) and confirms the theme-and-variations form as one of Mozart's recurring compositional choices (collected in Chapter 19 §19.4).

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen (K. 310 first movement)

  1. **The opening.** Right hand plays the first theme in A minor with a relentless eighth-note rhythmic figure in the left hand underneath. The texture is severe — no decorative figuration in the right hand, no major-mode breathing in the harmonic language. The first movement opens with structural austerity that the rest of the movement will work out.

  2. **The chromatic intensity.** The first-theme group contains chromatic descending figures (the same harmonic vocabulary that later returns in K. 491 of Chapter 7 and in the Don Giovanni Commendatore scene of Chapter 12). K. 310 is one of the earliest places in Mozart's catalog where the chromatic descent operates as primary thematic material rather than as occasional decoration.

  3. **The relentlessness.** The first movement has very little breathing space; the eighth-note rhythmic drive continues nearly without break for the duration of the movement. The relentlessness is the principal expressive means; the listener cannot escape the drive into a calmer passage.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen (K. 331)

  1. **The opening theme of the first movement.** The first theme is one of Mozart's simplest and most lyric — a sixteen-measure period in A major with a memorable melodic shape and a clean, balanced structure. The theme is followed by six variations of increasing complexity and a closing coda. Listen for the theme itself at the start; you will hear it repeatedly through the variations.

  2. **The variations' technique.** Each variation takes the theme and re-decorates it. Variation I adds right-hand ornamentation; Variation II adds left-hand figuration; Variation III moves to the minor mode; Variation IV adds harmonic complexity; Variation V shifts to a slower tempo (Adagio); Variation VI breaks the steady tempo entirely and turns Allegro, with the most elaborate figuration of the set. The progression is one of the standard theme-and-variations procedures of the period and gives the first movement its expressive arc.

  3. **The "Alla Turca" finale.** The third movement opens with the famous Turkish-style march figure. The Janissary-percussion octaves in the bass imitate the stylized Turkish military band sound; the right hand carries the principal melody. The finale runs about three minutes.

#### One-sentence background (K. 310)

Composed in Paris, summer 1778; about twenty minutes across three movements; the **Primary Recording** for K. 310 in this chapter is Mitsuko Uchida (Philips, 1989), part of Uchida's complete Mozart piano sonata cycle widely held as one of the modern references. (Alfred Brendel on Philips, 1980s, is the warmer modern-instrument alternative; Maria João Pires on Deutsche Grammophon, 1990s, is the lyric alternative; Murray Perahia on Sony Classical, 1980s-90s, is another modern reference.)

#### One-sentence background (K. 331)

Composed probably during the 1783 Salzburg trip (date contested; some scholars date to 1781 in Paris); about twenty minutes across three movements; the **Primary Recording** for K. 331 in this chapter is Mitsuko Uchida (Philips, 1990), same complete cycle as K. 310. (The other piano sonata cycle references include Daniel Barenboim on EMI, 1980s; Alfred Brendel; András Schiff on Decca, 1990s.)

#### Search Hints

EN (K. 310): "Mozart Piano Sonata 8 K 310 A minor Uchida" or "Mozart K 310 Brendel" EN (K. 331): "Mozart Piano Sonata 11 K 331 A major Alla Turca" or "Mozart K 331 Uchida" ZH (K. 310): "莫扎特 第8号钢琴奏鸣曲 K310 内田光子" 或 "莫扎特 a小调奏鸣曲 布伦德尔" ZH (K. 331): "莫扎特 第11号钢琴奏鸣曲 K331 土耳其进行曲" 或 "莫扎特 K331 内田光子" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

### 15.2 The Listening Window — K. 310 First Movement

Timestamps below refer to the Uchida Primary Recording.

**0:00-0:30 — The opening.** Right hand plays the first theme in A minor with a relentless eighth-note rhythmic figure in the left hand underneath. The texture is severe — no decorative figuration in the right hand, no major-mode breathing.

**The first-theme group's continuation.** The first theme is restated and developed across the opening section. Chromatic descending figures appear in the harmonic language; the eighth-note rhythmic drive continues.

**The exposition's second-theme group.** Mozart moves to C major (the relative major) for the second theme. The contrast is brief; the major-key relief is conditional, and the music returns to A minor before the exposition closes.

**The development.** Mozart takes the first-theme material through several keys before returning to A minor for the recapitulation. The eighth-note rhythmic drive continues throughout the development; the structural severity is unrelieved.

**The recapitulation and coda.** First-theme group returns in A minor; second-theme group also returns in A minor (the home-key rule). The coda reaffirms A minor without major-mode resolution; the movement ends without consolation.

The K. 310 first movement is approximately seven minutes long. Its severity is the principal expressive means; the listener experiences the relentlessness as the work's emotional weight. Whether or not the biographical reading (as the Paris grief sonata) is accepted, the music's severity is the principal fact about the work.

### 15.3 The Listening Window — K. 331

Timestamps below refer to the Uchida Primary Recording.

**The theme of the first movement.** A sixteen-measure period in A major. The theme is one of Mozart's simplest and most lyric — a clean, balanced melodic shape. Listen for the theme itself; you will hear it repeatedly through the variations.

**Variation I.** Right-hand ornamentation added to the theme. The basic melodic shape remains recognizable; the decoration is the new compositional material.

**Variation II.** Left-hand figuration added. The texture shifts: the right hand carries a simplified version of the theme; the left hand provides moving figuration that fills in the harmonic spaces.

**Variation III.** Minor-mode variation. The theme is recast in A minor; the harmonic mood darkens; the figuration continues but with the minor-mode coloring. The minor-mode variation provides the central darker turn within the major-key first movement.

**Variation IV.** Return to A major; the harmonic complexity expands; the figuration becomes more elaborate.

**Variation V (Adagio).** The tempo slows to Adagio; the variation is the most lyric and emotionally expansive of the set. The slower tempo gives the variation the central reflective moment of the first movement.

**Variation VI (Allegro).** After Variation V's slowdown to Adagio, Variation VI shifts up to Allegro — faster than the original theme. The figuration becomes the most elaborate of the set; the tempo change provides the closing climax of the first-movement variations and pulls the listener clean out of the Andante grazioso frame.

**The closing coda.** A brief coda brings the first movement to its close in A major.

**The second movement (Menuetto and Trio).** Standard Menuetto-and-Trio structure. The minuet is in A major and is lyric and lightly ornamented; the Trio in D major provides the contrasting middle section; the _da capo_ return brings back the minuet.

**The "Alla Turca" finale (third movement).** The famous Turkish-style march. The opening features the Janissary-percussion octaves in the bass imitating the stylized Turkish military band sound. The right hand carries the principal melody in A minor (the parallel minor of the home A-major key); the rhythmic energy is propulsive. The middle section moves to A major for contrast; the A-minor march material returns; and the closing coda turns to **A major** for a brilliant Janissary-style flourish that sends the sonata out in the home key's parallel major.

### 15.4 What's Happening in the Music

#### K. 310 and the Paris-period grief reading

The biographical reading of K. 310 as the Paris grief sonata is one of the principal interpretive conventions of the work. The reading rests on three pieces of circumstantial evidence: the composition date (Paris summer 1778, in the months of or immediately after Anna Maria's death); the minor key (one of Mozart's two minor-key piano sonatas in his entire catalog); the musical severity (the relentlessness, the chromatic descents, the lack of major-mode breathing). The reading is plausible.

The reading is also not directly annotated. Mozart left no surviving letter or marginal note tying K. 310 to his mother's death. The composition date is partly inferred (the autograph is not securely dated; the Paris months are the best estimate). Other Paris-period works (the Symphony No. 31 K. 297 "Paris" of June 1778, the keyboard sonatas K. 309 and K. 311 of late 1777-early 1778) do not display the same dark character; K. 310's severity is therefore singular to it.

In short: the K. 310 biographical reading is the conventional interpretation, is plausible, and is not directly attested. Hearing K. 310 with the biographical reading in mind gives the music a particular emotional weight; hearing it without the biographical reading still gives the music its severity. The work survives either interpretive frame.

K. 310 is the principal minor-key piano sonata in Mozart's catalog (with K. 457 of 1784 as its later peer). The piece has a steady place in concert programming and recital repertoire. The Mitsuko Uchida reading is one of the standard contemporary interpretations.

#### K. 331 and the theme-and-variations form

K. 331's first movement is the principal theme-and-variations movement in the Mozart piano sonata catalog. The form had appeared in K. 453's finale (Chapter 6) and would appear in standalone variation sets (the "Ah, vous dirai-je, Maman" variations K. 265 of 1781-82) and across chamber works. K. 331's first movement is the form's principal appearance in the solo piano sonata genre.

What the variation form gives the first movement: a structural unity built on melodic clarity. The theme is simple and memorable; the variations develop its possibilities. The form's accessibility (the listener can hear the theme reappear in each variation) made K. 331 a standard pedagogical sonata — students learning the sonata could follow the variation structure clearly, and the variation technique was a foundational skill for keyboard composition and improvisation.

The theme-and-variations form will return briefly in the late string quintets and clarinet works (Chapter 17).

#### The "Alla Turca" finale and the _Janissary_ tradition

The "Alla Turca" finale uses the _Janissary_ musical tradition — the stylized European representation of Turkish military band music. The Janissaries were the elite Turkish infantry corps; their military bands (with characteristic percussion — bass drum, cymbals, triangle, sometimes Turkish crescent) had been encountered by European audiences through Ottoman military campaigns and through diplomatic exchanges in the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries. The Janissary musical style entered European composition through _alla turca_ movements that imitated the percussion sound through piano writing (octaves in the bass mimicking the bass drum, repeated short rhythmic figures mimicking the percussion patterns) or through actual added percussion in operas with Turkish subject matter.

Mozart's _Entführung_ (Chapter 5) uses Janissary orchestration with added percussion (piccolo, triangle, cymbals, bass drum). The "Alla Turca" finale of K. 331 uses the keyboard imitation of the Janissary sound — the bass octaves and the rhythmic patterns evoke the percussion without actually requiring percussion. The keyboard _alla turca_ tradition would continue in other composers (Beethoven's _Ruins of Athens_ of 1811 has a Turkish march; the _Marche turque_ tradition extended through the nineteenth century into popular concert programs).

The "Alla Turca" finale's popularity has been continuous since the sonata's first publication. The melody has been transcribed, arranged, and quoted across two centuries of musical culture.

#### K. 457 in C minor briefly

The Piano Sonata No. 14 in C minor K. 457 was composed in Vienna in October 1784. It is the second of Mozart's two minor-key piano sonatas (K. 310 being the first). The work was published in 1785 paired with the Fantasy in C minor K. 475 (an introductory free-fantasia piece composed in 1785), and the pair K. 475 + K. 457 is often performed and recorded together.

K. 457 has the structural severity of K. 310 but works in a more rhetorical-virtuoso register. The first movement opens with a stark unison statement; the slow movement is in E♭ major (the relative major) and is lyric; the finale returns to C minor with dramatic urgency. The work is one of the principal pieces of evidence for Mozart's mature minor-key writing in the solo piano genre and is in the standard recital repertoire.

K. 457 paired with K. 475 makes a substantial recital program (about twenty-five minutes for the pair). The Mitsuko Uchida cycle includes both; the Alfred Brendel cycle is another standard reference.

#### K. 545 "Sonata facile" briefly

The Piano Sonata No. 16 in C major K. 545 was composed in Vienna in June 1788. Mozart's own catalog of his works entered K. 545 with the note "_per principianti_ " ("for beginners") and the work is typically known as the "Sonata facile" (the "easy sonata").
