It is the Mozart piano sonata pedagogy returns to most consistently; nearly every piano student of the past two centuries has played the first movement at some point in their training.

The "Sonata facile" character is real — the technical demands are modest compared with K. 310 or K. 457 — but the compositional craft is not modest at all. The first movement's opening is a textbook example of clean sonata form; the slow movement (G major Andante) is lyric and well-shaped; the finale is a brief rondo. The work is what Mozart could write for a pedagogical occasion without compromising the craft.

K. 545 is mentioned here for completeness; its pedagogical centrality makes it one of the works most readers will already know on first encounter with the Mozart piano sonata literature.

#### The violin sonatas briefly

Mozart composed about thirty-five violin-and-piano sonatas across his career. The early ones (K. 6-15 of 1762-66, written for the prodigy tours) are violin obbligato to keyboard with the keyboard carrying most of the substantive material. The Mannheim-Paris sonatas (K. 296 of 1778; K. 301-306 of 1778) and the Vienna sonatas (K. 376-380 of 1781; K. 454 of 1784; K. 481 of 1785; K. 526 of 1787; K. 547 of 1788) develop the violin's role into an equal chamber partner with the keyboard.

The Vienna violin sonatas K. 376-380 (1781) were among the first published works Mozart issued after the Vienna freelance establishment. The sonatas were composed for and dedicated to Josepha Auernhammer, a Vienna pianist Mozart had taught.

**K. 379 in G major** (1781, the volume's brief audition work for the violin sonatas) is the work in the K. 376-380 set most often singled out. The sonata has two movements (Adagio-Allegro for the first, Andantino-Allegretto for the second) and is approximately seventeen minutes long. The chamber-music intimacy of the violin-and-piano duo is at its mature stage here; the violin is the keyboard's partner rather than its decoration. Listen briefly to K. 379 to hear what Mozart's chamber-music violin writing of 1781 sounds like. Recommended recordings: Anne-Sophie Mutter with Lambert Orkis on Deutsche Grammophon (2006); Itzhak Perlman with Daniel Barenboim on Deutsche Grammophon (1980s); Arthur Grumiaux with Walter Klien on Philips (1960s).

### 15.5 The Stories

#### Mozart's keyboard playing

Mozart was one of the leading keyboard performers of his time. His public performance career began in childhood (the prodigy tours of Chapter 3 displayed his keyboard playing as the principal attraction) and continued through the Vienna freelance years (the great Vienna piano concertos of Chapters 6-8 were composed for Mozart's own performance). The piano sonatas are the solo-piano repertoire that Mozart composed for his own use, for pedagogical purposes (the sonatas for his students), and for publication.

Contemporary accounts of Mozart's playing emphasize several features: clean rapid passagework, expressive phrasing in slow movements, idiomatic use of the keyboard's dynamic capacities, and improvisatory facility (the cadenzas in the concertos were improvised in performance; Mozart's keyboard improvisations drew particularly strong contemporary praise). The piano sonatas reflect Mozart's compositional thinking about what the solo piano could do.

On the Walter fortepiano Mozart played at home — discussed in Chapter 8 — the keyboard writing sounds transparent and rhythmically clear in passages that would sound thick or sustained on a modern grand. Period-instrument performances of the sonatas (Malcolm Bilson, Andreas Staier, Kristian Bezuidenhout) recover the original sound-world; modern-instrument performances (Uchida, Brendel, Pires, Barenboim) give a richer sound at the cost of original transparency.

#### The keyboard sonata as a genre in the late eighteenth century

The keyboard sonata as a publishable genre had been developed through the mid-eighteenth century by Carl Philipp Emanuel Bach, Johann Christian Bach (whose London friendship with the young Mozart in 1764-65 was an early influence), Joseph Haydn (whose keyboard sonatas were widely known), and others. By the 1780s the sonata was a standard publication category; composers wrote sonatas for the publication market as well as for personal and pedagogical use.

Mozart's keyboard sonatas occupy a specific position in the genre. They are technically accomplished but not at the limit of virtuosity (Mozart's piano concertos pushed the virtuoso envelope further than his sonatas did); they are expressively varied (the minor-key sonatas K. 310 and K. 457 contrast sharply with the major-key sonatas K. 332 and K. 333); they are pedagogically useful (the "Sonata facile" K. 545 is the principal explicit pedagogical sonata, but many of the others have been used as teaching pieces). The position is mainstream rather than extreme; Mozart in the sonatas is writing for the publishable repertoire rather than for the extreme limits of his own performance capacities.

#### Recording history of the piano sonatas

The complete-cycle references for the Mozart piano sonatas:

Mitsuko Uchida (Philips, 1980s-90s) is the leading modern reference. Uchida's playing is unfussy, clear, and rhythmically vital; her readings respect the score and the keyboard idiom.

Alfred Brendel (Philips, 1970s-80s) is the older modern-instrument reference. Brendel's readings are warmer and more expressive than Uchida's; the Brendel cycle has been in catalog for decades.

Maria João Pires (Deutsche Grammophon, 1990s) is the lyric alternative. Pires's playing emphasizes the melodic line and the chamber-music transparency.

András Schiff (Decca, 1990s) is the alternative modern reference; Schiff's readings are detailed and structurally clear.

Daniel Barenboim (EMI, 1980s) is another standard cycle.

Murray Perahia (Sony Classical, 1980s-90s) is admired for the warm lyric character.

On period instruments: Malcolm Bilson (Hungaroton, 1980s-90s), Andreas Staier (Harmonia Mundi, various decades), Kristian Bezuidenhout (Harmonia Mundi, 2010s) are the principal references. The fortepiano readings recover the original sound-world; the lighter touch and quicker decay give the music a different character from the modern-grand readings.

For K. 310 specifically: the Lipatti recording (Columbia, 1950s) is the historic reference. Dinu Lipatti's playing is restrained and emotionally intense; the recording has been continuously available in remastered form since the 1950s.

For K. 331 specifically: the Horowitz late recordings (Deutsche Grammophon and Sony, 1980s) include K. 331 and are admired for Horowitz's late-period clarity in the Mozart literature.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"K. 310 is one of Mozart's two minor-key piano sonatas (K. 457 being the other). The biographical reading of K. 310 as the Paris grief sonata, composed near the time of Anna Maria Mozart's death, is plausible but not directly attested. How does the biographical reading shape your experience of the music? If the biographical reading were definitively disproven (if scholars discovered a Mozart letter showing K. 310 was composed in different circumstances), would the music still carry the same emotional weight?"_
* _"The 'Alla Turca' finale of K. 331 is one of the few classical melodies an ordinary listener can hum from memory. The Janissary musical tradition — the stylized European representation of Turkish military band music — gave Mozart the rhythmic and percussion vocabulary the finale uses. What does the _alla turca_ tradition give Mozart that purely Classical-period material would not have given him? Is the finale's popularity built on the Turkish-style novelty or on the underlying compositional craft?"_
* _"K. 331's first movement is a theme-and-variations rather than a sonata-form. The variation form gives the movement a structural unity built on melodic clarity — the listener can follow the theme through each variation. What does the variation form let Mozart do that sonata form would not have let him do? Why did Mozart choose the variation form for K. 331's first movement specifically?"_
* _"The 'Sonata facile' K. 545 is the principal Mozart pedagogical sonata — nearly every piano student plays the first movement at some point in their training. The technical demands are modest; the compositional craft is not. What does it mean for a great composer to write deliberately accessible material? Is K. 545 a compromise, an exercise, or a serious work in a different register?"_
* _"Mozart composed about thirty-five violin-and-piano sonatas across his life — a substantial chamber-music output that is less central to his repertoire than the piano sonatas. Why does the violin sonata catalog get less attention than the piano sonata catalog? Listen briefly to K. 379 and consider whether the relative neglect is a fair reflection of the music's quality or a function of programming traditions."_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find Uchida (Philips, 1989-90). Listen to K. 310's first movement and K. 331's full sonata and attend to four concrete moments:

  1. **K. 310 opening (the first thirty seconds).** Confirm: right hand plays the first theme in A minor with the relentless eighth-note rhythmic figure in the left hand underneath. The texture is severe and unrelieved; the relentlessness is the principal expressive means.

  2. **K. 310 chromatic descents.** Listen specifically for the chromatic descending figures in the first-theme group. The chromatic descent is one of the rhetorical conventions of grief; K. 310 is one of the earliest places in Mozart's catalog where the figure operates as primary thematic material.

  3. **K. 331 theme of the first movement.** Listen for the simple sixteen-measure theme. The theme is balanced and memorable; you will hear it return in each of the six variations.

  4. **K. 331 "Alla Turca" finale.** Listen for the Janissary-percussion octaves in the bass at the opening. The keyboard imitation of the Turkish military band sound is the principal compositional device; the right hand carries the melody over the percussion-evoking bass.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : pair K. 310 with the Symphony No. 31 K. 297 "Paris" (June 1778) — the Paris-period symphony composed in the same months. The two works together give a fuller sense of Mozart's Paris-period writing: K. 297 is the bright surface of the Paris court appointment ambition; K. 310 is the darker interior of the journey. Standard recordings of K. 297: Karl Böhm with the Berlin Philharmonic, Trevor Pinnock with the English Concert, Sir Charles Mackerras with the Scottish Chamber Orchestra.

**Read** : Robert Spaethling, _Mozart's Letters, Mozart's Life: Selected Letters_ (Norton, 2000). The Paris-period letters (March-September 1778) are the principal primary source for the journey and for the months around Anna Maria's death. The July 3, 1778 letter to Leopold (in which Mozart describes Anna Maria as very ill but does not tell Leopold she has died) and the July 9, 1778 letter to Abbé Bullinger (in which Mozart asks Bullinger to prepare Leopold for the news) are among the most-quoted Mozart letters and the principal evidence for Mozart's emotional state during the Paris summer. About forty pages of letters from this five-month period.

---

## Chapter 16: The "Haydn" String Quartets K. 387, 421, 428, 458, 464, 465 (1782-1785)

Mozart composed twenty-three string quartets across his career. The first ten (K. 80, K. 155-160, K. 168-173) were composed during the Salzburg years 1770-1773 — a series of early quartets that absorbed the Italian and Viennese quartet traditions and that are part of the prodigy-and-young-Mozart output. The next six — the "Haydn" Quartets K. 387, 421, 428, 458, 464, 465 — were composed in Vienna across 1782-1785 and were published in September 1785 with a formal dedication to Joseph Haydn. The last seven quartets are the late Vienna works: the single Hoffmeister Quartet K. 499 (1786), and the three Prussian Quartets K. 575, 589, 590 (1789-90) composed for King Friedrich Wilhelm II of Prussia.

This chapter anchors on two of the Haydn Quartets: the K. 465 "Dissonance" (composed 1785, the last of the six, named for its famous dissonant slow introduction) and the K. 387 in G major (composed 1782, the first of the six, the most contrapuntally elaborate in its finale). The pair frames the Haydn Quartets — K. 387 at the start, K. 465 at the end — and gives the reader the structural shape of what the six-quartet set accomplished as a unified compositional project.

### 16.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

February 12, 1785. Vienna. Leopold Mozart had arrived for his extended visit (Chapter 7's K. 466 premiere context). On the evening of February 12, Joseph Haydn was at Mozart's apartment for a quartet evening. Three of the six Haydn Quartets — K. 387, K. 421, K. 428 — had been completed; the remaining three, K. 458, K. 464, and K. 465, were in progress or recently finished. Haydn played the violin parts in the quartets at the evening; Mozart played the viola; the other parts were played by Karl Ditters von Dittersdorf (the established Vienna composer and violinist) and Johann Baptist Vanhal (another Vienna composer-cellist).

The quartet evening that night, or one closely connected to it, produced the famous Haydn statement to Leopold Mozart. Leopold's letter to Nannerl Mozart of February 16, 1785 records Haydn's words: "_Ich sage Ihnen vor Gott, als ein ehrlicher Mann, ihr Sohn ist der grösste Componist, den ich von Person und dem Namen nach kenne; er hat Geschmack, und über das die grösste Compositionswissenschaft._ " — "I tell you before God and as an honest man, your son is the greatest composer known to me either in person or by reputation; he has taste and, beyond that, the greatest knowledge of composition."

The statement is a foundational passage in Mozart biography. It is attested by Leopold's contemporary letter to his daughter (not later recollection or anecdote collection) and is therefore primary-source evidence rather than legendary. Haydn was twenty-four years Mozart's senior, the leading composer in Europe, and had nothing professional to gain by praising the younger composer. The statement stands as a strikingly generous senior-junior composer compliment for the period.

The Mozart-Haydn quartet evenings at Mozart's apartment across 1784-85 are well-attested. The cast was usually Haydn, Mozart, Dittersdorf, and Vanhal; the repertoire was Mozart's quartets in progress and Haydn's recent quartets. The evenings were among the principal contexts in which the Haydn Quartets were first played; the dedication to Haydn (in the published set of September 1785) was a public acknowledgment of what the evenings had produced creatively for Mozart.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen (K. 465 "Dissonance")

  1. **The Adagio slow introduction (the first thirty seconds).** The work opens with a slow introduction in which the four instruments enter on dissonant chords that resolve only with delay. The cello and viola enter first with a steady accompaniment figure; the second violin and first violin enter on top with a chromatic line that crosses against the lower instruments to produce a series of sharply dissonant chords. The dissonances resolve eventually into the C-major harmony that the Allegro will establish, but the slow introduction's dissonance is the work's most-discussed compositional feature and the source of the "Dissonance" nickname.

  2. **The transition into the Allegro.** After the dissonant Adagio, the Allegro launches in clean C-major harmony. The contrast between the dissonant introduction and the clean main movement is the work's dramatic hinge; Mozart presents an extreme moment of dissonance, then resolves it into the mainstream harmonic language.

  3. **The first-movement Allegro.** The Allegro is structurally a standard sonata-form first movement; the harmonic language is clear; the four instruments share thematic material in the dialogue model that became the standard for the late-Classical string quartet. The slow introduction was the special feature; the Allegro is the work in mainstream style.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen (K. 387 finale)

  1. **The four subjects.** The K. 387 finale is one of Mozart's most contrapuntally elaborate quartet finales. The movement combines sonata-form structure with extensive fugal counterpoint; four melodic subjects are presented and combined across the movement. Listen for the first subject's clean shape at the opening; subsequent subjects will be introduced and combined.

  2. **The contrapuntal density.** Across the movement, the four instruments engage in interlocking counterpoint — each playing a distinct melodic line that combines with the others. The texture is denser than the K. 465 first movement; the contrapuntal complexity is the principal compositional feature.

  3. **The closing combination.** At the end of the finale, Mozart combines the subjects in the kind of multi-voice counterpoint that anticipates the K. 551 Jupiter finale (Chapter 10). The K. 387 finale is in many ways the chamber-music ancestor of the Jupiter; the two works are structurally connected through their contrapuntal procedures.

#### One-sentence background (K. 465)

Composed in Vienna, completed January 14, 1785; about thirty minutes across four movements, the first movement's Adagio introduction runs about one minute and the Allegro about ten; the **Primary Recording** for K. 465 in this chapter is the Quartetto Italiano (Philips, 1966-71), part of the Italiano cycle widely held as one of the modern references. (The Hagen Quartett on Deutsche Grammophon, 1991, is the alternative modern reading; the Alban Berg Quartett on EMI, 1980s, is another reference; the Quatuor Mosaïques on Astrée / Naïve, 1990s, is the period-instrument alternative.)

#### One-sentence background (K. 387)

Composed in Vienna, completed December 31, 1782; about thirty minutes across four movements; the **Primary Recording** for K. 387 in this chapter is also the Quartetto Italiano (Philips, 1966-71), same cycle as K. 465. (The Hagen Quartett, the Alban Berg Quartett, the Quatuor Mosaïques are the same alternatives.)

#### Search Hints

EN (K. 465): "Mozart Dissonance Quartet K 465 Quartetto Italiano" or "Mozart K 465 Hagen Quartet" EN (K. 387): "Mozart String Quartet 14 K 387 Quartetto Italiano" or "Mozart K 387 Alban Berg Quartet" ZH (K. 465): "莫扎特 不协和弦四重奏 K465 意大利四重奏" 或 "莫扎特 K465 哈根四重奏" ZH (K. 387): "莫扎特 第14号弦乐四重奏 K387 意大利四重奏" 或 "莫扎特 K387 阿尔班·贝尔格四重奏" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

### 16.2 The Listening Window — K. 465 First Movement

Timestamps below refer to the Quartetto Italiano Primary Recording.

**0:00-1:00 — The Adagio slow introduction.** Cello enters first with a steady accompaniment figure (a repeated note pattern in the lower register); viola enters with a chromatic line that crosses against the cello; second violin enters with another chromatic line that crosses against the viola; first violin enters on top with a chromatic line that crosses against the second violin. The four entries produce a series of sharply dissonant chords — moments where the four notes do not fit the standard harmonic patterns of late-eighteenth-century music. The dissonances are what gave the quartet its nickname and what later analysts return to first.

**The resolution.** The dissonant chords gradually resolve as the introduction progresses. By the end of the Adagio, the harmonic field has settled into the C-major dominant preparation that launches the Allegro.

**The Allegro main movement.** The Allegro launches in clean C-major harmony. The first-theme group is presented; the texture is the standard four-instrument quartet dialogue model. Mozart in the main movement uses mainstream Classical-period harmonic language; the dissonant Adagio was the special feature, and the Allegro is the work in mainstream style.

**The exposition's second-theme group.** Mozart moves to G major (the dominant) for the second theme. The contrast in texture and key is brief; the music returns to C major for the closing material.

**The development.** Mozart takes the first-theme material through several keys; the contrapuntal dialogue between the four instruments continues throughout.

**The recapitulation and coda.** First-theme group returns in C major; second-theme group also returns in C major (the home-key rule). The movement closes in C major.

### 16.3 The Listening Window — K. 387 Finale

Timestamps below refer to the Quartetto Italiano Primary Recording.

**0:00-0:20 — The first subject.** A clean stepwise melodic figure stated by the first violin; the other instruments respond. The figure is brief and balanced.

**The introduction of subsequent subjects.** Across the first few minutes of the finale, Mozart introduces additional melodic subjects. The instruments share the material; the texture is the standard four-instrument quartet dialogue.

**The fugal development.** Mozart takes the subjects into fugal counterpoint — extended passages where the subjects are stated by one instrument and answered by the others in imitation. The contrapuntal density is one of the principal compositional features of the movement; K. 387's finale carries the most elaborate counterpoint Mozart wrote for string quartet to that point.

**The closing combination.** At the end of the finale, Mozart combines the subjects in multi-voice counterpoint. The combination is the structural climax of the movement and anticipates the K. 551 Jupiter finale's similar but more elaborate combination (Chapter 10). The K. 387 finale is in many ways the chamber-music ancestor of the Jupiter.

**The coda.** Brief affirmation of G major and the closing of the work.

### 16.4 What's Happening in the Music

#### Why the Haydn Quartets are a unified set

The six Haydn Quartets — K. 387, K. 421, K. 428, K. 458, K. 464, K. 465 — were composed across approximately thirty months (December 1782 through January 1785). They were published together as a set in September 1785 by the Vienna publisher Artaria with a formal printed dedication to Joseph Haydn. The dedication was an unusual public gesture; composers of the period typically dedicated works to aristocratic patrons (who would pay for the privilege of being named in the dedication) rather than to fellow composers. Mozart's choice to dedicate the set to Haydn was a public acknowledgment of his admiration and of the creative debt the quartets owed to Haydn's example.

The Haydn quartet tradition that Mozart was responding to: Haydn had been composing string quartets since the 1750s and by 1782 had completed around forty-five quartets, including the Op. 33 set of 1781 (six quartets that Haydn himself described in correspondence as composed "in a new and special way" — emphasizing thematic development across all four instruments equally rather than the older first-violin-plus-accompaniment model). Mozart's Haydn Quartets respond to Haydn's Op. 33 directly — adopting the equal-voice dialogue model, the thematic-development emphasis, and the formal structures Haydn had refined.

What Mozart added: more chromatic harmonic language (K. 465's dissonant introduction is the principal extreme example), denser contrapuntal writing in some movements (K. 387's finale particularly), and the operatic-style ensemble register in slow movements (K. 421's slow movement and K. 458's slow movement particularly). The Mozart Haydn Quartets are not derivative imitations of Haydn but Mozart's own contributions to the genre Haydn had defined.

The six quartets were not normally performed as a connected cycle in Mozart's lifetime (the Haydn-Mozart quartet evenings played them individually as the evening's repertoire required); they have been recorded as a connected cycle by most major modern quartets (the Quartetto Italiano cycle, the Alban Berg cycle, the Hagen Quartett cycle, the Quatuor Mosaïques cycle). The cycle as a publication set with a dedication is the form in which the six are now understood.

#### The K. 465 dissonance — what it is and what it is not

The dissonant Adagio introduction to K. 465 has drawn sustained analytical attention since its publication. Contemporary reception (1785-1800) was uncertain about the dissonances — some commentators considered them daring innovations; others considered them composition errors. By the early nineteenth century the dissonances were widely understood as deliberate compositional choice; by the late twentieth century they were a standard analytical topic in any treatment of Mozart's chamber music.

What the dissonances are: a series of moments in the slow introduction where the four instruments produce chords that contain notes that do not fit the standard harmonic patterns of late-eighteenth-century music. The dissonances are not random — they arise from the chromatic lines the four instruments are playing, which cross against each other in specific moments that produce the dissonance. The dissonance is therefore the result of contrapuntal procedure (the four chromatic lines moving in counterpoint) rather than the result of arbitrary harmonic choice.

What the dissonances do compositionally: they establish a moment of harmonic uncertainty at the work's opening that the Allegro's clean C-major resolution then dispels. The dramatic gesture is the contrast — dissonant introduction, then clean main movement — and the dissonant introduction's strangeness makes the Allegro's normalcy feel like a hard-won resolution rather than a default starting point.

What the dissonances are not: they are not the principal compositional language of K. 465. The dissonances are confined to the slow introduction (about one minute of music); the rest of the quartet (about twenty-nine minutes) is in mainstream Classical-period harmonic language. K. 465 is sometimes mistakenly characterized as a generally dissonant or experimental work; the characterization mistakes the slow introduction's specific dissonances for the general harmonic character of the work.

A practical tip: the K. 465 dissonant introduction is the work's most distinctive single feature, and it deserves repeated listening to hear how the chromatic lines produce the specific dissonant moments. But the work as a whole is mainstream Mozart chamber-music writing in the Haydn Quartet style.

#### Mozart as violist

The Mozart-Haydn quartet evenings at Mozart's apartment had Mozart playing viola — his lifelong instrument in chamber music. His preference for the viola over the violin (the more conventional choice for a leading composer-performer) is one of the small but consistent biographical facts about him.

What the viola gave Mozart in chamber music: the inner voice — the middle of the four-instrument texture, between the violins on top and the cello on the bottom. The inner-voice perspective is the chamber-music position that sees the harmonic and contrapuntal architecture most clearly; the violist hears both the upper lines (the violins' melodic and figurative material) and the lower lines (the cello's bass and the harmonic foundation) and is responsible for the harmonic middle that connects them.

Mozart's viola playing is one of the principal pieces of evidence for his chamber-music thinking. The Sinfonia Concertante K. 364 (Chapter 4) gives the viola equal weight with the violin; the late string quintets K. 515, K. 516, K. 593, K. 614 (Chapter 17) use two violas (rather than the more conventional two cellos) in the five-instrument ensemble; the late quartets' viola parts give the instrument substantial melodic material. Throughout the catalog, the viola is present as a serious chamber-music instrument rather than as a secondary supporting voice.

#### Cross-reference to K. 551 (the Jupiter finale)

The K. 387 finale's contrapuntal procedures connect forward to the K. 551 Jupiter finale (Chapter 10). Both movements use multi-subject counterpoint with the subjects combined at the closing climax; the Jupiter's five-subject invertible counterpoint is the more elaborate version of what K. 387's finale had explored.

Mozart's contrapuntal writing is part of his late-Classical compositional thinking; the connection between the K. 387 quartet finale (1782) and the K. 551 symphony finale (1788) suggests Mozart was developing the multi-subject combination technique across six years and across multiple genres. The chamber-music context (the quartet) and the orchestral context (the symphony) require different compositional means, but the underlying technique is the same.

If you have heard the K. 551 Jupiter finale (Chapter 10) and now hear K. 387 finale, the family resemblance is direct. The K. 387 is the chamber-music ancestor; the Jupiter is the orchestral descendant.

### 16.5 The Stories

#### The Mozart-Haydn relationship

The Mozart-Haydn friendship began in 1781-84 Vienna (Chapter 5 introduced the relationship). The two composers met in Vienna in the early 1780s — Haydn at this point had been Esterházy court Kapellmeister for two decades and was the leading composer in Europe; Mozart had just established himself as a Vienna freelance composer. The relationship across 1781-91 was warm and creatively productive.

The principal evidence for the relationship: Haydn's praise of Mozart in the February 1785 statement to Leopold; Mozart's dedication of the six Haydn Quartets in September 1785; Haydn's reported tears at the news of Mozart's death in December 1791; the dedications and warmth in the surviving Haydn correspondence about Mozart after 1791.

After Mozart's death, Haydn lived another eighteen years (he died May 31, 1809). Haydn's later compositional output (the late symphonies for London in 1791-95, the late string quartets, the oratorios _The Creation_ of 1798 and _The Seasons_ of 1801) sometimes shows Mozart influence — the late symphonies' wind writing, the late chamber music's harmonic adventurousness, certain compositional gestures that scholars have traced to Haydn's awareness of Mozart's late works.

The Mozart-Haydn relationship is a touchstone friendship in the history of Western art music. Two composers of comparable greatness who knew each other personally, who admired each other publicly, who influenced each other creatively, and who maintained the relationship across the difference in age, status, and life-circumstance — the friendship is the kind of professional generosity that the history of any art form rarely supplies.

#### The string quartet as a genre in the 1780s

The string quartet as a publishable and performable genre had been developed primarily by Joseph Haydn from the 1750s through the 1780s. Haydn's quartet output (Op. 1 through Op. 17 in the early years, Op. 20 of 1772, Op. 33 of 1781, Op. 50 of 1787, the later sets through Op. 77 of 1799) established the four-movement form, the equal-voice dialogue model, and the structural conventions that became standard for the late-eighteenth-century string quartet.

By 1782, when Mozart began the Haydn Quartets, the genre had a recognized form and a recognized scholar-composer (Haydn). Mozart's Haydn Quartets enter the genre as Mozart's contribution to the form Haydn had defined; the dedication acknowledges this directly. The six quartets are Mozart's principal work in the genre during his Vienna freelance years.

Other Vienna composers were also writing in the genre: Karl Ditters von Dittersdorf, Johann Baptist Vanhal, Leopold Hofmann, and others published quartet sets through the 1780s and 1790s. The genre was active and competitive; Mozart's contributions were among the leading entries but were not alone in the field.

Beethoven would enter the genre with the six Op. 18 quartets of 1798-1800 (after Haydn's instruction and after Mozart's death) and would dominate the genre across the early nineteenth century through the late quartets Op. 127, 130, 131, 132, 133, 135 of the 1820s. The chamber music for string quartet would remain one of the principal serious-music genres through the nineteenth century into the twentieth.

#### Recording history of the Haydn Quartets

The complete-cycle references for the six Haydn Quartets:

The Quartetto Italiano (Philips, 1966-71, the chapter's Primary Recording) is the leading older modern-instrument reference. The Quartetto Italiano cycle covers all twenty-three Mozart quartets; the readings are warm, structurally clear, and emotionally direct.

The Alban Berg Quartett (EMI, 1980s) is the next-generation modern reference. The readings are more precisely controlled than the Quartetto Italiano readings; the structural detail is sharper.

The Hagen Quartett (Deutsche Grammophon, 1991) is the contemporary modern reference. The Hagen readings are admired for the chamber-music intimacy and the careful phrasing.

The Quatuor Mosaïques (Astrée / Naïve, 1990s) is the period-instrument complete reference. Period-instrument quartet playing reveals the chromatic lines in K. 465's introduction more clearly than modern-instrument playing tends to do; the gut strings and lighter bowing give the texture transparency.

The Smetana Quartet (Supraphon, 1970s) is the older Eastern European reference; the readings are warmly expressive in the central European chamber-music tradition.

The Amadeus Quartet (Deutsche Grammophon, 1960s-70s) is another older reference; the readings emphasize structural clarity and balance.

The Talich Quartet (Calliope, 1990s) is another period-instrument-aware modern reading worth knowing.

The Lindsay Quartet (ASV, 1980s-90s) is another modern reference.

For listeners new to the Haydn Quartets: the Quartetto Italiano cycle remains the most-recommended starting point. The cycle is widely available, the readings are accessible, and the structural sense of the cycle as a unified compositional project comes through clearly.

Why does he then resolve into mainstream Classical harmony for the rest of the quartet rather than continuing the dissonant language?"_

What does the statement cost Haydn to make, and what does it give Mozart? How does the Mozart-Haydn relationship compare to other senior-junior composer relationships you know about?"_

Why does Mozart return to the technique at the symphonic scale after first exploring it in the chamber-music scale?"_

What does it tell you about Mozart and Haydn that the acknowledgment was given and accepted in the warm spirit it was?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find Quartetto Italiano (Philips, 1966-71) or Alban Berg Quartett (EMI, 1980s). Listen to K. 465 first movement and K. 387 finale and attend to four concrete moments:

  1. **K. 465 opening Adagio (the first thirty seconds).** Confirm: cello enters with a steady accompaniment figure; viola enters with a chromatic line that crosses against the cello; second violin enters with another chromatic line; first violin enters on top. The four chromatic lines crossing produce the sharply dissonant chords that give the work its "Dissonance" nickname.

  2. **K. 465 transition into the Allegro.** Listen for the moment Mozart shifts from the dissonant Adagio into the clean C-major Allegro. The contrast between dissonance and resolution is the structural hinge of the movement.

  3. **K. 387 finale opening.** Listen for the first subject's clean stepwise melodic figure stated by the first violin; the other instruments respond. The figure is brief and balanced; it will combine with subsequent subjects across the movement.

  4. **K. 387 finale closing combination.** Listen for the multi-voice counterpoint at the closing. Mozart combines the subjects in dense contrapuntal texture; the procedure anticipates the K. 551 Jupiter finale's similar combination.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : pair K. 387 and K. 465 with the Joseph Haydn String Quartet Op. 33 No. 3 in C major "The Bird" (1781) — one of the Haydn Op. 33 quartets that the Mozart Haydn Quartets were responding to. The "Bird" is in C major (the same key as K. 465); the structural model is the equal-voice dialogue that Mozart adopted. Hearing the Haydn alongside the Mozart gives the fullest sense of what Mozart absorbed from Haydn and what he added. The Lindsay Quartet, the Hagen Quartett, and various period-instrument quartets have recorded both Mozart and Haydn quartet cycles for comparison.

**Read** : Daniel Heartz, _Mozart, Haydn and Early Beethoven, 1781-1802_ (Norton, 2009). Heartz's treatment of the Haydn-Mozart relationship and of the chamber-music context of the early-mid 1780s is the principal scholarly treatment of the period. Heartz on the Op. 33 → Mozart Haydn Quartets transmission is the most-detailed account. About 900 pages total; the Haydn-Mozart and chamber-music chapters are roughly 200 pages of focused reading.

## Chapter 17: The Late String Quintets + Clarinet Quintet + Wind Concertos

Mozart's chamber-music peak occupies a specific stretch of years, roughly 1787 to 1791, during which he composed the four late string quintets (K. 515 in C major and K. 516 in G minor of 1787; K. 593 in D major of 1790; K. 614 in E♭ of 1791), the Clarinet Quintet K. 581 in A major of 1789, and the late wind concertos including the Clarinet Concerto K. 622 of October 1791. The chapter also briefly surveys the earlier wind concertos — the Horn Concertos K. 412/417/447/495 composed across 1781-86 for the Salzburg-born horn player Joseph Leutgeb, the Flute and Harp Concerto K. 299 of 1778, and the Bassoon Concerto K. 191 of 1774.

This chapter anchors most closely on the **String Quintet in G minor K. 516** (1787), first movement (the third major G-minor anchor in this volume after K. 183 of Chapter 2 and K. 550 of Chapter 10). The second anchor is the **Clarinet Quintet in A major K. 581** (1789), second-movement Larghetto — the serene chamber-music peer of the K. 488 Adagio and the K. 595 Larghetto (Chapter 8). The wind concertos are covered briefly as a survey rather than as individual anchors.

### 17.1 Before You Listen — Two Works in Context

#### Hook Anecdote (K. 516)

May 16, 1787. Vienna. Mozart entered the String Quintet in G minor K. 516 into his thematic catalog (_Verzeichnüss aller meiner Werke_). It is the second of the two 1787 quintets — K. 515 in C major was catalogued on April 19, and K. 516 followed roughly a month later. The two works belong to the same compressed Spring 1787 stretch. Mozart was thirty-one years old. The Don Giovanni commission from Prague had been received; the Don Giovanni composition would occupy Mozart through the summer and into the autumn (premiere October 29, 1787). Leopold Mozart's final illness was in progress in Salzburg; Leopold died on May 28, 1787 — twelve days after K. 516's catalog entry.

K. 516 is one of the principal G-minor works in Mozart's catalog. It joins the early "little G minor" symphony K. 183 (Chapter 2, 1773) and the "great G minor" symphony K. 550 (Chapter 10, 1788) as the three major G-minor works in the catalog. The matched set across fifteen years, K. 183 (1773), K. 516 (1787), and K. 550 (1788), gives the fullest evidence of what G minor meant to Mozart compositionally across his life.

K. 516 is conventionally heard as the chamber-music peer of K. 550. Both works share the dark-key character, the chromatic-descending material, and the structural seriousness of mature minor-key Mozart writing. K. 516 was composed about fifteen months before K. 550; the chamber-music exploration of the G-minor mood in K. 516 may have prepared the ground for the larger orchestral exploration in K. 550. The chamber-and-symphony pairing, the chamber-music ancestor that prepared the symphonic mature work, is one of the structural patterns in Mozart's catalog.

#### Hook Anecdote (K. 581)

September 29, 1789. Vienna. Mozart completed the autograph of the Clarinet Quintet in A major K. 581, his principal chamber-music work for the clarinet. The work was composed for Anton Stadler (1753-1812), the leading Vienna clarinetist of the period and Mozart's preferred clarinet-soloist collaborator. The quintet was premiered at a benefit concert for the Tonkünstler-Societät (a Vienna musicians' charitable society) on December 22, 1789; Stadler played the clarinet part; the other parts were performed by Vienna chamber musicians.

K. 581 is the principal clarinet chamber-music work in Mozart's catalog. The four other late chamber-music works that feature wind instruments — the Quintet for Piano and Winds K. 452 (1784, Chapter 6), the Horn Quintet K. 407 (1782), the Oboe Quartet K. 370 (1781) — are smaller and earlier; K. 581 is the mature chamber-music wind writing.

K. 581 has been a standard-repertoire chamber work since the mid-nineteenth century. The Larghetto second movement, about seven minutes long in D major (the subdominant of the home A-major key), is the work's central slow movement and is often programmed as a stand-alone recital piece. The Larghetto is the chamber-music peer of the K. 488 Adagio and the K. 595 Larghetto (Chapter 8); the three slow movements together form the principal evidence for the slow-movement-as-heart pattern this volume has been tracking.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen (K. 516 first movement)

  1. **The opening.** First violin enters with a syncopated leaping figure in G minor; the other strings (second violin, two violas, cello) provide accompaniment. The texture is dark and rhythmically agitated; the chromatic-descending material is present from the opening. Listen for the family resemblance to K. 550's first movement (Chapter 10) — the syncopation, the dark-key texture, the chromatic descents are shared between the two G-minor works.

  2. **The five-instrument texture.** K. 516 uses two violins, two violas, and one cello — Mozart's preferred quintet ensemble (the more common five-instrument quintet would have two violins, one viola, and two cellos). The two-viola texture gives the work its darker overall color; the violas in the inner voices contribute a sustained darker harmonic foundation that two cellos would not have provided.

  3. **The structural ambition.** The first movement is substantial — about ten minutes — and uses sonata-form with extensive development. The contrapuntal interplay between the five instruments is the structural argument; each instrument carries melodic and accompanimental material in alternation.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen (K. 581 Larghetto)

  1. **The opening orchestral statement.** The four strings (two violins, viola, cello) play a quiet sustained chord in D major; the clarinet enters with the principal melodic line. The texture is the chamber-music equivalent of the K. 595 Larghetto opening — strings provide quiet harmonic support; the wind instrument (clarinet here, full orchestra in K. 595) carries the lyric line.

  2. **The clarinet writing.** The Anton Stadler clarinet writing in K. 581 uses the instrument's full register and exploits its dynamic flexibility. The clarinet can sustain long phrases at very quiet dynamics (a capacity the oboe of the period had less reliably); Mozart writes for this dynamic range. Listen for the very quiet sustained notes in the upper register.

  3. **The dialogue between clarinet and strings.** Across the movement, the clarinet and the four strings trade material. The clarinet is the leading voice but is not the soloist-against-accompaniment; the writing is genuine chamber music with all five instruments as equal partners. Listen for the moments where the first violin or viola takes over the lyric line and the clarinet provides accompanying figuration.

#### One-sentence background (K. 516)

Catalogued May 16, 1787 in Mozart's _Verzeichnüss_ ; about thirty-five minutes across four movements, the first movement runs about ten minutes; the **Primary Recording** for K. 516 in this chapter is the Hagen Quartett with Gérard Caussé as the second viola (Deutsche Grammophon, 1998), part of the Hagen complete Mozart string quintets cycle. (The Grumiaux Trio with Arpad Gerecz and Max Lesueur on Philips, 1973, is the older modern alternative; the Borodin Quartet with various second violists has recorded the quintets; the Smetana Quartet has recorded the quintets in the older Eastern European tradition.)

#### One-sentence background (K. 581)

Completed September 29, 1789; about thirty minutes across four movements, the Larghetto runs about seven minutes; the **Primary Recording** for K. 581 in this chapter is Karl Leister (clarinet) with members of the Berliner Solisten / Amadeus Quartet (Deutsche Grammophon, 1965), a long-standing reference. (Anthony Pay on basset clarinet with the Academy of Ancient Music ensemble on L'Oiseau-Lyre / Decca, 1990, is the period-instrument alternative; Sabine Meyer with the Vienna String Sextet on EMI, 1994, is the modern alternative; David Shifrin with the Emerson String Quartet on Deutsche Grammophon, 1990s, is another modern reference.)

#### Search Hints

EN (K. 516): "Mozart String Quintet 4 K 516 G minor Hagen" or "Mozart K 516 Grumiaux" EN (K. 581): "Mozart Clarinet Quintet K 581 Leister Amadeus" or "Mozart K 581 Pay Academy Ancient Music" ZH (K. 516): "莫扎特 g小调弦乐五重奏 K516 哈根" 或 "莫扎特 K516 格鲁米欧" ZH (K. 581): "莫扎特 单簧管五重奏 K581 莱斯特" 或 "莫扎特 K581 佩" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

### 17.2 The Listening Window — K. 516 First Movement

Timestamps below refer to the Hagen Quartett Primary Recording.

**0:00-0:20 — The opening.** First violin enters with a syncopated leaping figure in G minor; second violin enters with a complementary line; the two violas pulse beneath with steady eighth notes; the cello provides the bass. The texture establishes the dark-key character immediately; the syncopation and the chromatic-descending material in the violin lines are the principal compositional elements.

**The first-theme group's continuation.** The first theme is restated and developed across the opening section. The five instruments share the material; the dialogue between them is the structural argument.

**The exposition's second-theme group.** Mozart moves to B♭ major (the relative major) for the second theme. The mood lifts briefly; the music returns to G minor for the closing material.

**The development.** Mozart takes the first-theme material through several keys; the chromatic-descending material dominates the development. The contrapuntal density increases as the development progresses.

**The recapitulation and coda.** First-theme group returns in G minor; second-theme group returns in G minor (the home-key rule). The coda is brief; the movement closes in G minor without major-mode resolution.

### 17.3 The Listening Window — K. 581 Larghetto

Timestamps below refer to the Leister / Amadeus Primary Recording.

**0:00-0:30 — The opening.** The four strings play a quiet sustained chord in D major; the clarinet enters with the principal melodic line — a long lyric phrase in the clarinet's upper-middle register. The harmonic field is settled; the tempo is Larghetto (slow but not extremely slow); the texture is chamber-music transparent.

**The continuation of the first section.** The clarinet's principal melodic line is developed across the first section. The strings provide harmonic support; the first violin occasionally enters into dialogue with the clarinet but the clarinet carries the leading voice through most of the section.

**The middle section.** The harmonic language modulates briefly; the texture lightens further. The clarinet's writing exploits the instrument's dynamic flexibility — long sustained notes at very quiet dynamics, contrasted with brief moments of greater dynamic emphasis.

**The first violin's solo passage.** Mozart gives the first violin a brief solo moment in which the clarinet provides accompanying figuration. The role-reversal is a chamber-music gesture: the leading voice and the supporting voice exchange positions momentarily, and the listener hears the underlying chamber-music partnership.

**The return to the opening material.** The clarinet returns with the principal melodic line; the strings provide the quiet sustained accompaniment of the opening.

**The closing.** The movement settles into the closing D-major cadence. The clarinet's final sustained notes recede into silence; the strings settle into the final chord.

The Larghetto is approximately seven minutes long. Its emotional weight is built on the chamber-music transparency and on the clarinet's capacity for sustained quiet singing. The work is a standard-repertoire chamber piece; the Larghetto specifically is among the most-cited slow movements in the chamber-music literature.

### 17.4 What's Happening in the Music

#### Why two violas

Mozart's late string quintets (K. 515, K. 516, K. 593, K. 614) all use the same five-instrument ensemble: two violins, two violas, one cello. This was an unconventional choice. The more common late-eighteenth-century string quintet ensemble (used by Boccherini and others) had two violins, one viola, and two cellos — a darker texture overall, with the two cellos providing extended bass support. Mozart's two-viola choice gives a different sound: the two violas in the inner voices provide a sustained darker harmonic middle, while the single cello provides the bass line.

What the two-viola texture gives Mozart's quintets: a denser inner-voice writing, with the two violas often in close harmony or in contrapuntal interplay with each other. The texture is somewhat darker than a two-cello ensemble would have been (the violas' middle register is closer to a sustained darker color than the cellos' lower register would be), and the inner-voice writing has more compositional possibilities (the violas can carry melodic material that two cellos would not be able to project).

Mozart's choice of the two-viola quintet was personal — he played the viola himself (as we noted in Chapter 16) and may have preferred the texture from the violist's perspective. The quintets are sometimes called "viola quintets" to distinguish them from the more common cello-quintet ensembles.

#### The chamber-and-symphony pairing pattern

K. 516 (G-minor String Quintet, April 1787) was composed about fifteen months before K. 550 (G-minor Symphony, July 1788). The chamber-music exploration of the G-minor mood in K. 516 may have prepared the ground for the larger orchestral exploration in K. 550. The pattern — chamber-music ancestor followed by symphonic mature work in the same key — is one of the recurring structural patterns in Mozart's catalog.

Other instances of the pattern: the Piano Quartet in G minor K. 478 (October 1785) precedes the K. 516 quintet by eighteen months and may have been the first chamber-music G-minor exploration that fed into K. 516. The K. 478 piano quartet, K. 516 string quintet, and K. 550 symphony together form a G-minor cluster across 1785-88.

The pattern reflects Mozart's compositional process: chamber-music writing was the laboratory for compositional ideas that would later receive their symphonic or operatic mature working. The Mozart catalog is full of similar genealogical chains (the K. 452 piano-and-winds quintet feeding into the K. 491 piano concerto's wind writing; the Haydn Quartets' contrapuntal procedures feeding into the K. 551 Jupiter finale; the late string quintets feeding into the late chamber music more generally).

Hearing K. 516 alongside K. 550 (Chapter 10) gives the fullest sense of what Mozart did with G minor across the chamber-and-symphony pair.

#### Anton Stadler and the basset clarinet

Anton Stadler (1753-1812) was the principal clarinetist of the Vienna Imperial-Royal Court Orchestra from the 1780s through the 1790s. He was a Mason and a personal friend of Mozart's (the two were members of the same Vienna Lodge). Both the Clarinet Quintet K. 581 and the Clarinet Concerto K. 622 were composed for him.

Stadler was also the developer of the basset clarinet — an extended-range clarinet with additional keys allowing the instrument to play four notes below the standard clarinet's lowest note. The basset clarinet was Stadler's personal instrument; Mozart composed both K. 581 and K. 622 for the extended range. The original autograph of K. 622 (which has been lost) used the basset clarinet's extended range; the standard published edition (which was edited to fit the regular clarinet's range) is the version normally performed, but the original basset-clarinet version has been reconstructed by modern scholars and is performed in period-instrument editions.

The K. 581 was apparently also composed for the basset clarinet; some passages in the autograph sit below the standard clarinet's range, and modern editions either transpose these passages up to fit the regular clarinet or restore them to their original position for performance on basset clarinet. Anthony Pay's period-instrument recording (Academy of Ancient Music, 1990) uses the basset clarinet for K. 581 and K. 622; other period-instrument recordings make similar choices.

The basset clarinet's lower range gives the writing a richer bass color in certain passages; the difference is audible but not dramatic. Modern-instrument readings (Leister, Sabine Meyer, David Shifrin) play the standard clarinet; period-instrument readings (Pay, others) play the basset clarinet. Both options are legitimate.

#### The wind concertos briefly

The Mozart wind concertos are a small but central body of work:

**Horn Concerto K. 412/514** (1791); **Horn Concerto K. 417** (1783); **Horn Concerto K. 447** (probably 1787); **Horn Concerto K. 495** (1786). The four horn concertos were composed for Joseph Leutgeb (1732-1811), the Salzburg-born horn player who had moved to Vienna and become a personal friend of Mozart's. The concertos are mainstream Mozart concerto writing in the smaller-scale wind-concerto register; they are not on the structural ambition scale of the great Vienna piano concertos but are warmly lyric and idiomatic for the natural horn. K. 447 is often considered the finest of the four.

**Flute Concertos K. 313 in G major and K. 314 in D major** (both 1778, the Mannheim months of the Paris-and-Mannheim journey). Mozart wrote the flute concertos for Ferdinand Dejean, a Dutch amateur flutist. The concertos are not central in the catalog (Mozart was not enthusiastic about the flute as a solo instrument and the concertos are partly transcriptions or arrangements of earlier material) but are in the standard flute repertoire.

**Flute and Harp Concerto K. 299** (1778, Paris). Composed for the Duke of Guines (a French amateur flutist) and his daughter (a harpist). The work has remained in the standard flute-and-harp recital and concert repertoire.

**Oboe Concerto K. 314** (1777, Salzburg). The C-major oboe concerto composed for the oboist Giuseppe Ferlendis. The work is the principal oboe concerto in the standard repertoire (K. 314 was later reworked by Mozart as the D-major Flute Concerto, also K. 314 — the same Köchel number applied to two distinct works in different transposing-instrument versions; the oboe original and the flute reworking are both performed).

**Bassoon Concerto K. 191** (1774, Salzburg, composed when Mozart was eighteen). The principal bassoon concerto in the standard repertoire; the work is in nearly every bassoonist's repertoire.

**Clarinet Concerto K. 622** (October 1791). The principal late-Mozart wind concerto, composed for Anton Stadler. We name it here as the chamber-music peer to the Clarinet Quintet K. 581 and as part of the late-style transparency cluster (along with K. 595 of Chapter 8 and the Magic Flute of Chapter 14). The work uses Stadler's basset clarinet in its original autograph and is the last orchestral concerto Mozart completed before his death; if you have not already heard it, this is one of the natural exit points from this volume into continued late-Mozart listening.

#### Cross-reference to the late-style transparency

The Clarinet Quintet K. 581's Larghetto and the Clarinet Concerto K. 622 together extend the late-style transparency feature into the chamber and concerto registers. The K. 595 Larghetto (Chapter 8) is the piano-concerto version; the Magic Flute's wind writing (Chapter 14) is the operatic version; K. 581 Larghetto and K. 622 are the chamber and solo-wind versions.

The four late-style works (K. 595, K. 622, K. 581, Magic Flute) together constitute the late-style cluster collected in Chapter 19 §19.7.

When you sit down with the recordings, hearing the K. 581 Larghetto alongside the K. 595 Larghetto and the K. 622 second-movement Adagio gives the fullest sense of what the late-style transparency accomplished across different ensemble contexts. Concrete shared markers to listen for: in all three, a single wind line (clarinet in K. 581 and K. 622, one wind player at a time over piano in K. 595) carries the principal melody while strings provide quiet sustained harmonic support; the principal melodic lines are long-breathed and lightly ornamented rather than rapidly figured; and the modulations across each movement arrive under unchanged dynamics rather than being announced by dominant-seventh pushes.

### 17.5 The Stories

#### The 1787 G-minor cluster

The April 1787 completion of K. 516 sits in the middle of a productive period in Mozart's career. Leopold Mozart's final illness was in progress; Leopold would die on May 28, 1787. Mozart had received the Don Giovanni commission from Prague; the composition would occupy him through the summer and autumn. The Vienna freelance economics were still favorable (the contraction would begin in 1788); the family was comfortable in their Vienna household.

The dark-key character of K. 516 has sometimes been connected biographically to Leopold's illness; the connection is not directly annotated and is interpretive. The April 1787 letter from Mozart to Leopold (the famous letter on death) was written approximately a week before the autograph of K. 516 was completed. The letter is widely quoted in Mozart biographies and is sometimes read as evidence for Mozart's contemplation of death in the months around K. 516's composition.

What the letter says (translated and paraphrased): Mozart writes to his dying father about death, calling it "the true goal of our existence" and "the best and truest friend of mankind." The tone is contemplative and reconciled rather than morbid; Mozart appears to be sharing his philosophical view with his father at a moment of his father's mortality. The letter is one of the principal pieces of evidence for Mozart's Masonic philosophical perspective (the Vienna Lodge tradition included philosophical reflection on death as one of its meditative subjects).

Whether K. 516 carries the philosophical weight of the April 1787 letter is interpretive. The work's dark-key character is real and is built into the composition; the biographical connection to Leopold's death is plausible and conventional but not directly annotated.

#### The 1789 Stadler collaboration

The Clarinet Quintet K. 581 was completed September 29, 1789. The composition context: Mozart had returned from a trip to Berlin and Dresden (April-June 1789) during which he had hoped for court appointment opportunities that did not materialize; the Vienna economy was contracting; the household was under financial pressure. The benefit concert at which K. 581 premiered (December 22, 1789, the Tonkünstler-Societät concert) was one of the principal Vienna chamber music events of late 1789.

Anton Stadler's playing was widely admired in Vienna. Contemporary accounts describe his tone as exceptionally warm and his dynamic control as remarkable. Mozart wrote for Stadler's specific capacities; the long quiet sustained passages in K. 581's Larghetto are calibrated to Stadler's breath control and tone production. The collaboration between composer and performer is one of the principal examples in Mozart's catalog of writing tailored to a specific player.

#### Recording history of the late chamber works

**K. 516** principal references: the Hagen Quartett with Gérard Caussé (Deutsche Grammophon, 1998, the chapter's Primary Recording); the Grumiaux Trio with Arpad Gerecz and Max Lesueur (Philips, 1973); the Borodin Quartet with various second violists; the Smetana Quartet (Supraphon, 1980s); the Talich Quartet (Calliope, 1990s); the Quatuor Mosaïques with Erich Höbarth (Astrée / Naïve, period instruments).

**K. 581** principal references: Karl Leister with members of the Amadeus Quartet (Deutsche Grammophon, 1965, the chapter's Primary Recording); Anthony Pay on basset clarinet with the Academy of Ancient Music ensemble (L'Oiseau-Lyre / Decca, 1990, the period-instrument reference); Sabine Meyer with the Vienna String Sextet (EMI, 1994); David Shifrin with the Emerson String Quartet (Deutsche Grammophon, 1990s); Reginald Kell with the Fine Arts Quartet (Concert-Disc, 1950s, the historic reference); Jacques Lancelot with the Quatuor Pascal (older modern alternative).

**Late string quintets cycle** complete references: the Grumiaux Trio (Philips, 1970s) is the leading older modern-instrument cycle; the Hagen Quartett (Deutsche Grammophon, late 1990s) is the leading contemporary modern reference; the Borodin Quartet (Chandos, various decades) is another reference; the Quatuor Mosaïques (Astrée / Naïve, 1990s) is the period-instrument cycle.

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find Hagen Quartett K. 516 (Deutsche Grammophon, 1998) and Leister / Amadeus K. 581 (Deutsche Grammophon, 1965). Listen to both works and attend to four concrete moments:

  1. **K. 516 opening (the first thirty seconds).** Confirm: first violin enters with the syncopated leaping figure in G minor; second violin enters with a complementary line; the two violas pulse beneath with steady eighth notes; the cello provides the bass. The five-instrument texture establishes the dark-key character immediately; the family resemblance to K. 550's first movement is direct.

  2. **K. 516 development section.** Listen for the chromatic-descending material as it dominates the development through extended modulation. The contrapuntal density between the five instruments increases as the development progresses.

  3. **K. 581 Larghetto opening.** Confirm: the four strings play a quiet sustained chord in D major; the clarinet enters with the principal melodic line. The chamber-music transparency is immediate; the texture is the chamber-music equivalent of the K. 595 Larghetto opening.

  4. **K. 581 Larghetto's first violin solo passage.** Listen for the moment Mozart gives the first violin a brief solo while the clarinet provides accompanying figuration. The role-reversal is the chamber-music gesture that confirms the partnership-rather-than-soloist character of the work.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : pair K. 581 Larghetto with the K. 622 Clarinet Concerto second movement. Both movements were composed for Anton Stadler; both use the basset clarinet's extended range in the original autographs; both stand among the central slow movements in the Mozart wind literature. Hearing the two slow movements in proximity gives the fullest sense of what Mozart wrote for Stadler in his last two years.

**Read** : Christoph Wolff, _Mozart at the Gateway to His Fortune: Serving the Emperor, 1788-1791_ (Norton, 2012). Wolff's treatment of the late chamber works — the late string quintets, the Clarinet Quintet, the Prussian Quartets — places the works in the context of Mozart's repositioning of his compositional life in the late freelance period. About forty pages on the late chamber music; the full book is roughly 280 pages.

## Chapter 18: Great Mass in C minor K. 427 (1782-83) + Requiem in D minor K. 626 (1791)

Mozart's two great sacred works are both unfinished. The Great Mass in C minor K. 427 was begun in autumn 1782 and worked on across 1783; it was never completed (Mozart wrote the Kyrie, the Gloria, the Sanctus, and parts of the Credo and Agnus Dei but did not produce a complete Mass setting). The Requiem in D minor K. 626 was begun in summer 1791 and worked on through Mozart's final illness; he died on December 5, 1791 with the work partially complete (Introit complete; Kyrie nearly complete; Sequence from Dies Irae through Confutatis sketched with vocal lines and bass; Lacrimosa sketched only eight bars in; Offertorium and Sanctus and Agnus Dei either fragmentary or absent).

The two unfinished sacred works are the principal evidence for Mozart's sacred-music writing in his Vienna freelance years. The Salzburg years had produced extensive sacred output (Mozart's contractual obligation as Salzburg court musician included regular sacred composition for the cathedral liturgy); the Vienna freelance years produced almost no sacred music apart from these two great unfinished projects. The K. 427 Mass was an offering for a Salzburg performance (probably for Constanze's vow to have a sacred work performed in Salzburg if her childbirth went well — the autograph context suggests this); the K. 626 Requiem was a paid commission from Count Walsegg (anonymous through Walsegg's intermediary). The two works represent the sacred-music aspirations of Mozart's Vienna years that the freelance career's commercial pressures otherwise displaced.

This chapter anchors most closely on the **Requiem's Lacrimosa** , with the **Kyrie + Confutatis** as additional anchor moments. Brief audition: the **Great Mass K. 427 Kyrie + "Et incarnatus est"**. The chapter is among the most scholarly-weighty in the volume — the Requiem completion problem is one of the principal contested topics in Mozart scholarship and deserves careful treatment.

### 18.1 Before You Listen — The Two Works in Context

#### Hook Anecdote (K. 626)

Summer 1791. Vienna. A messenger from Count Franz von Walsegg-Stuppach (an Austrian aristocrat with a country estate at Stuppach in Lower Austria) approached Mozart with an anonymous commission for a Requiem Mass. The commission's secrecy was Walsegg's habit — Walsegg liked to commission compositions anonymously from professional composers and then perform them at his estate as his own work. Walsegg's wife Anna had died on February 14, 1791; the Requiem was for an annual memorial Mass for her.

The anonymity created some psychological strain on Mozart, particularly as his health deteriorated in late November and December 1791. According to the Mozart family accounts collected by Constanze Mozart and her sources after his death (the principal source is the late-life recollection by Sophie Haibel, Constanze's younger sister, who was with Mozart during his final illness), Mozart in his last weeks sometimes spoke of the Requiem as if it were for himself. The detail is partly attested in Sophie Haibel's recollection (recorded in correspondence with Friedrich Rochlitz in the early 1800s) and partly elaborated in later popular biography. Solomon's treatment is the standard cautious one — the Sophie Haibel recollection is one of the principal sources but is at a remove of years and may have been shaped by Constanze's interest in shaping Mozart's posthumous image.

The chronology of the Requiem composition is partly contested. Some scholars argue that Mozart began the Requiem in summer 1791 and worked on it intermittently across autumn 1791 alongside Magic Flute and the Clarinet Concerto K. 622. Other scholars place the principal composition in October-November 1791, after K. 622 was completed. The autograph and the surviving sketches do not definitively settle the question; both timelines are consistent with the surviving material.

What is established: Mozart died on December 5, 1791 with the Requiem partially complete. The Introit ("Requiem aeternam") was complete in full score. The Kyrie was nearly complete in full score. The Sequence (Dies Irae, Tuba mirum, Rex tremendae, Recordare, Confutatis) was sketched with vocal lines and bass — Mozart had drafted the vocal parts and the bass line but had not completed the orchestration. The Lacrimosa was sketched only eight bars in — Mozart had begun the Lacrimosa but had completed only the opening before dying. The Offertorium (Domine Jesu and Hostias) was partially sketched. The Sanctus, Benedictus, and Agnus Dei were either fragmentary or absent — no surviving Mozart material exists for these movements.

#### Hook Anecdote (K. 427)

Autumn 1782 through 1783. Vienna and Salzburg. Mozart had married Constanze Weber on August 4, 1782 (Chapter 5). Sometime in 1782, Mozart made a vow that he would compose a major sacred work in fulfillment of a personal pledge — the surviving evidence suggests the vow was connected to Constanze's safe delivery of their first child (Raimund Leopold Mozart, born June 17, 1783; died August 19, 1783, only two months old). Mozart's letter to Leopold of January 4, 1783 mentions the vow and the Mass-in-progress; the same letter contains the principal surviving evidence about the K. 427 Mass project.

The Mass was performed in Salzburg on October 26, 1783 during Mozart's only visit to Salzburg with Constanze (the visit Chapter 15 mentioned as the probable context for K. 331). The Salzburg performance was at St. Peter's Abbey (Stift Sankt Peter), the Benedictine abbey in Salzburg where Constanze sang one of the soprano solo parts. The performance used what Mozart had completed of the Mass — the Kyrie, the Gloria, the Sanctus, and probably the Benedictus — with the missing portions of the Credo and the Agnus Dei substituted with other settings or simply omitted.

After the Salzburg performance, Mozart never returned to the Mass. The K. 427 Mass remained incomplete; no surviving Mozart material exists for the missing Credo movements and the Agnus Dei. The Mass has been completed by various twentieth-century editors (the Helmut Eder completion of 1985 is the standard modern completion when one is used); it is normally performed as Mozart left it (Kyrie + Gloria + Sanctus + Benedictus + the partial Credo) rather than in completion.

The K. 427 and K. 626 share the structural feature of being unfinished. They also share a tonal language — both works are in C minor or D minor (K. 427 in C minor, K. 626 in D minor) and both use the late-Mozart chromatic-descending material extensively. The two works together constitute Mozart's principal sacred-music output of his Vienna years and his deepest engagement with the choral-orchestral sacred genre.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen (Requiem Lacrimosa)

  1. **The opening orchestral material (the first ten seconds).** Strings play a quiet sustained chord in D minor; the lower strings begin a chromatic descending figure underneath. The chromatic-descending material — the rhetorical convention of grief and mourning carried across this volume — is the principal compositional element of the Lacrimosa.

  2. **The choral entry.** The chorus enters with the words "Lacrimosa dies illa" — "Tearful that day." The choral writing is hushed and lyric; the texture is the operatic-style mourning vocabulary in liturgical context. Listen for how the chorus and orchestra interact — the orchestral chromatic descents continue beneath the choral lines.

  3. **The climactic moment.** Near the middle of the Lacrimosa, the music builds to a brief climactic moment ("dona eis requiem" — "grant them rest") and then recedes back into the mourning material. The climactic moment is one of the principal emotional peaks in the Requiem.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen (Requiem Kyrie + Confutatis)

  1. **The Kyrie's double-fugue texture.** The Kyrie is structured as a double fugue — two melodic subjects ("Kyrie eleison" and "Christe eleison") combined contrapuntally. The fugal writing is dense and structurally rigorous; the texture is the most contrapuntally elaborate in the Requiem. Listen for the two subjects entering separately and then combining.

  2. **The Confutatis opening.** The Confutatis opens with a dramatic male-voice chorus ("Confutatis maledictis" — "When the cursed are confounded") in a rhythmic, almost violent register; the female voices respond with "Voca me cum benedictis" ("Call me with the blessed") in a contrasting lyric register. The alternation between male and female voices is the principal dramatic device of the movement.

  3. **The Confutatis closing.** The movement closes with the chromatic descending material returning in preparation for the Lacrimosa. The transition from Confutatis to Lacrimosa is the structural hinge of the Sequence; the chromatic descents prepare the Lacrimosa's mourning material.

#### One-sentence background

Composed in Vienna, the Requiem from summer to early December 1791 (Mozart died December 5, 1791 leaving the work unfinished); the K. 427 Great Mass composed across autumn 1782 through 1783 (also unfinished); the complete Requiem (in the Süssmayr completion) runs about fifty minutes; the Lacrimosa runs about three minutes; the **Primary Recording** for the Requiem is Sir John Eliot Gardiner with the Monteverdi Choir and the English Baroque Soloists (Philips, 1986), a period-instrument reading widely held as one of the modern references. (Christopher Hogwood with the Choir of Westminster Abbey and the Academy of Ancient Music on L'Oiseau-Lyre / Decca, 1984, is the alternative period-instrument reading; Karl Böhm with the Vienna Philharmonic on Deutsche Grammophon, 1971, is the warmer modern-instrument alternative; Nikolaus Harnoncourt with the Concentus Musicus Wien on Teldec, 1981, is another modern alternative; Sir Neville Marriner with the Academy of St Martin in the Fields on Philips, 1990, is another reference.)

#### Search Hints

EN (Requiem): "Mozart Requiem K 626 Gardiner Monteverdi" or "Mozart Requiem Böhm Vienna Philharmonic" or "Mozart Requiem Hogwood Academy Ancient Music" EN (K. 427): "Mozart Great Mass C minor K 427 Bernstein" or "Mozart K 427 Marriner" ZH (Requiem): "莫扎特 安魂曲 K626 加德纳" 或 "莫扎特 安魂曲 贝姆 维也纳爱乐" ZH (K. 427): "莫扎特 c小调大弥撒 K427 伯恩斯坦" 或 "莫扎特 K427 马里纳" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

### 18.2 The Listening Window — Requiem Lacrimosa

Timestamps below refer to the Gardiner Primary Recording. The Lacrimosa runs approximately three minutes.

**0:00-0:10 — The opening.** Strings play a quiet sustained chord in D minor; lower strings begin the chromatic descending figure. The harmonic field is the dark D-minor of the Requiem's overall framing. The chromatic-descending material — the principal Mozart use of the figure in the sacred-music register — is established immediately.

**The choral entry.** The chorus enters with "Lacrimosa dies illa" — "Tearful that day." The choral writing is hushed and lyric; the four-voice texture (soprano, alto, tenor, bass) is structurally compact. The orchestral chromatic descents continue beneath the choral lines.

**The continuation through "Qua resurget ex favilla / judicandus homo reus."** The chorus continues with the text — "From which the guilty man, to be judged, will rise." The harmonic language remains dark; the chromatic descents intensify.

**The climactic moment.** Near the middle of the Lacrimosa, the music builds to "Huic ergo parce, Deus / Pie Jesu Domine / dona eis requiem" — "Spare him, then, O God / Merciful Lord Jesus / grant them rest." The climactic moment is harmonically the most intense in the movement; the chromatic descents reach their dynamic peak; the choral texture opens to its fullest dynamic.

**The Süssmayr boundary.** Around bar nine of the Lacrimosa, Mozart's autograph ends; the remainder of the movement — about two-and-a-half minutes — is Süssmayr's completion. Some attentive listeners can hear the seam, particularly in the orchestration's slight change of weight; others cannot. The Conversation Seed below invites you to test your own ear. (§18.4 takes up the completion problem at length.)

**The closing.** The Lacrimosa closes with a return to the mourning material and a final sustained chord in D minor.

The Lacrimosa is approximately three minutes long. Its emotional weight is built on the chromatic descending material, the hushed choral writing, and the dramatic timing of the climactic moment. It is one of the most-loved single passages in the sacred-music literature.

### 18.3 The Listening Window — Requiem Kyrie and Confutatis

Timestamps below refer to the Gardiner Primary Recording.

#### The Kyrie

The Kyrie follows the Introit (the opening "Requiem aeternam") which is the only fully Mozart-completed movement of the Requiem. The Kyrie is structured as a double fugue — two melodic subjects combined contrapuntally across the movement.

**The first subject ("Kyrie eleison").** Stated by the bass voice; answered by the tenor; then by the alto; then by the soprano. The four voices enter in sequence with the same melodic material — the standard fugal exposition procedure.

**The second subject ("Christe eleison").** Enters in counterpoint with the first subject. The two subjects are combined across the movement in various pairings.

**The development of the double fugue.** Mozart takes the two subjects through several keys and develops them in dense contrapuntal texture. The fugal writing is the most rigorous in the Requiem.

**The closing.** The double fugue closes with the two subjects in their final combination. The harmonic language remains in D minor throughout; the closing is structurally one of the most powerful in the Requiem.

The Kyrie is approximately three minutes long. It is the work in which Mozart's contrapuntal command — the same compositional skills that produced the K. 387 quartet finale (Chapter 16) and the K. 551 Jupiter finale (Chapter 10) — operates in the sacred-music register. The Kyrie is considered one of the principal examples of Mozart's mature fugal writing.

#### The Confutatis

The Confutatis is from the Sequence movements (Dies Irae through Confutatis) that Mozart sketched with vocal lines and bass but did not fully orchestrate. Süssmayr completed the orchestration based on the Mozart sketch.

**The opening.** Male voices (tenors and basses) enter dramatically on "Confutatis maledictis / flammis acribus addictis" — "When the cursed are confounded / damned to the bitter flames." The texture is rhythmic and almost violent; the harmonic language is harshly dissonant.

**The female-voice response.** Female voices (sopranos and altos) respond with "Voca me cum benedictis" — "Call me with the blessed." The female voices' texture is contrastingly lyric and quiet; the dynamic contrast between male and female voices is the principal dramatic device.

**The alternation.** The two textures alternate across the movement — male voices' violent damnation, female voices' lyric prayer. The alternation builds dramatic tension.

**The closing.** The movement closes with the chromatic descending material in preparation for the Lacrimosa. The transition from Confutatis to Lacrimosa is structurally connected — the chromatic descents prepare the Lacrimosa's mourning material; the listener moves from the violent damnation imagery into the tearful prayer of the Lacrimosa.

### 18.4 What's Happening in the Music

#### The Requiem completion problem

The Requiem completion problem is one of the principal contested topics in Mozart scholarship. Mozart died December 5, 1791 with the Requiem partially complete; the completion was undertaken in stages by several hands.

**(a) What Süssmayr did.** Constanze first asked **Joseph Eybler** (Mozart's friend and contemporary) to complete the work.
