Eybler completed some Sequence orchestration (the Dies Irae and parts of the Tuba mirum) before withdrawing. **Franz Xaver Süssmayr** (1766-1803), Mozart's pupil and assistant in the last months, was then commissioned and produced the completion delivered to Walsegg in 1792. The Süssmayr completion: completed the Sequence orchestration that Mozart had sketched (incorporating or replacing Eybler's work); completed the Lacrimosa from Mozart's eight-bar sketch; composed the Offertorium (Domine Jesu and Hostias) using Mozart's partial sketches; composed the Sanctus, Benedictus, and Agnus Dei outright; and repeated the opening Introit and Kyrie material as the closing Communio with new text.

What is Mozart and what is not in the standard Süssmayr version:

* **Introit** ("Requiem aeternam" + "Te decet hymnus"): Mozart in full score — the only fully Mozart-completed movement.
* **Kyrie** : Mozart in nearly full score (some orchestration details may be Eybler or Süssmayr).
* **Sequence** (Dies Irae through Lacrimosa): Mozart sketched vocal lines and bass; Süssmayr (or Eybler) completed orchestration. The Lacrimosa is Mozart for the first eight bars and Süssmayr thereafter.
* **Offertorium** : partially sketched by Mozart; substantially completed by Süssmayr.
* **Sanctus, Benedictus, Agnus Dei** : composed by Süssmayr without significant Mozart material.
* **Communio** : Süssmayr's adaptation of the Introit and Kyrie material with new text.

**(b) Why Süssmayr's completion has authority despite its limitations.** Süssmayr's completion has been criticized on familiar grounds: his compositional skills were not at Mozart's level; some orchestration choices are clumsy; the entirely new movements (Sanctus, Benedictus, Agnus Dei) are stylistically thinner than the surviving Mozart material. But Süssmayr was Mozart's pupil and assistant in the last months, present during the Requiem composition, and apparently the principal person (with Constanze) to whom Mozart spoke about the intended completion. He worked under impossible time pressure to deliver a complete score to the patron shortly after his teacher's death. His version is what the world has heard for more than two centuries and has accumulated its own performance tradition.

**(c) Modern alternatives.** **Robert Levin** (American Mozart scholar and pianist, born 1947) published an alternative completion in 1991 (Stuttgart premiere; Carus-Verlag, 1993), based on more recent scholarship and drawing on musical material from other Mozart works. The Levin differences are most audible in the Sanctus, Benedictus, and Agnus Dei (entirely re-composed using Mozart's other sacred-music vocabulary); the differences in the Introit, Kyrie, and Sequence are smaller. Further completions by **Franz Beyer** , **Richard Maunder** , and **Duncan Druce** have been produced since the late twentieth century, each based on different scholarly assumptions.

**(d) Current performance choices.** The Süssmayr version is what you will hear in nearly every standard recording (Gardiner, Böhm, Hogwood, Harnoncourt, Marriner). The Levin completion is the principal modern scholarly alternative — Helmuth Rilling (Hänssler, 1994) and Christoph Spering (Opus 111, 2002) are the references. Hearing the Levin Sanctus and Benedictus against the Süssmayr is illuminating: the difference is noticeable. Neither has displaced the other from the repertoire; the Requiem is best understood as a Mozart-Süssmayr collaboration in which Mozart's contributions are the principal material and Süssmayr's are the structural completion that allows the work to be performed whole.

#### The K. 427 Great Mass's compositional context

The K. 427 Great Mass in C minor was composed across autumn 1782 through 1783, in the same period as Mozart was establishing himself as a Vienna freelance composer (Chapter 5) and composing the early Vienna piano concertos (Chapter 6). The Mass was probably connected to a personal vow — the Mozart family circumstances of 1782-83 included Constanze's pregnancy and the birth of their first child Raimund Leopold (June 17, 1783; he died August 19, 1783, only two months old).

The Mass is incomplete. The surviving material includes:

* **Kyrie** : complete.
* **Gloria** : substantially complete (Mozart set the Gloria in eight sections, including the choral _Gloria_ , the soprano-bass duet _Domine Deus_ , the chorus _Qui tollis_ , the trio _Quoniam_ , the chorus _Jesu Christe — Cum sancto spiritu_).
* **Credo** : partial. Mozart set the opening _Credo in unum Deum_ chorus and the soprano aria _Et incarnatus est_ — the latter a Mozart sacred-music aria that has held a steady place in the recital repertoire, often performed on its own. The rest of the Credo text from _Crucifixus_ onward is missing.
* **Sanctus + Benedictus** : complete.
* **Agnus Dei** : missing (no surviving Mozart material).

The Salzburg performance on October 26, 1783 was the only documented performance of the work in Mozart's lifetime. After the Salzburg performance, Mozart never returned to the Mass. Why he did not complete it is one of the principal questions about the work; the surviving evidence does not provide a clear answer. Possibilities: the personal vow connected to Constanze's safe childbirth was discharged by the Salzburg performance and Mozart no longer felt the urgency; the Vienna freelance economics did not provide opportunity for further work on a piece that had no commission backing; Mozart's compositional focus shifted to the operatic and concerto projects of 1784-86.

The K. 427 is normally performed as Mozart left it — the Kyrie, the Gloria, the Sanctus, the Benedictus, plus whatever Credo material the editor chooses to include. The Helmut Eder completion of 1985 supplies the missing Credo movements and the Agnus Dei; some performances use the Eder completion, others perform the work as a fragment.

#### The "Et incarnatus est"

The "Et incarnatus est" — the soprano aria within the Credo of the K. 427 Mass — is the section of K. 427 that has held its own as a concert piece. The aria is structurally unusual for a Mass Credo movement; Mozart writes it as a _concertante_ aria with three solo wind instruments (flute, oboe, bassoon) interacting with the soprano in chamber-music dialogue. The vocal writing exploits the soprano's upper register; the wind writing is independently melodic. The aria is approximately seven minutes long and is often programmed as a stand-alone concert piece for soprano with chamber ensemble.

The "Et incarnatus est" is the principal anchor moment in the K. 427 Mass and the section recorded most often on its own. Constanze Mozart sang the soprano part at the Salzburg performance in 1783; Mozart was apparently writing for her specific vocal capacities. The aria has been recorded by every major lyric soprano in the standard repertoire.

#### Cross-reference to the chromatic-descent figure and the dark-key signature

The Requiem extends both principal Thread B features. **Dark-key signature** : D minor, joining K. 466 (Chapter 7) and Don Giovanni (Chapter 12) as the principal D-minor works in the catalog; the sustained D-minor presence across the work's fifty minutes is the longest single dark-key span in Mozart. **Chromatic-descent figure** : the Lacrimosa is one of the two principal Mozart uses of the figure (the other being the Don Giovanni Commendatore scene), here in the sacred-music register — the same convention used for supernatural judgment in Don Giovanni now used for grief and prayer. The two features together constitute the Requiem's compositional vocabulary, integrated more comprehensively than in any other Mozart work.

### 18.5 The Stories

#### Mozart's death and the Requiem

The conventional biographical reading of Mozart's last weeks — that Mozart in his final illness spoke of the Requiem as if it were for himself; that he sang through the Lacrimosa with Süssmayr and a small group of friends on the morning of his death — is partly attested in the Sophie Haibel and Constanze Mozart recollections and partly elaborated in later popular biography. Sophie Haibel's letter to Friedrich Rochlitz (1825, thirty-four years after the events) is the principal source for the bedside scene. Solomon's treatment is the standard cautious one: the recollections are consistent with Mozart's known character and with the Requiem's central place in his last months, but the specific details cannot be securely attested.

Mozart died on December 5, 1791 at age thirty-five. Süssmayr's completion was delivered to Walsegg in 1792 and performed at his estate in 1793 as Walsegg's own composition (per Walsegg's anonymity habit); it was also performed in Vienna in 1793 in benefit concerts for Constanze and the children, and within several years had entered the standard sacred-music repertoire. The _Amadeus_ play and film (Chapter 20) dramatize the story with various inventions — most notably the suggestion that Salieri was the anonymous commissioner, which is film fiction with no historical foundation.

#### Recording history

**Requiem** (all use Süssmayr unless noted). Period-instrument: Gardiner / Monteverdi Choir / English Baroque Soloists (Philips, 1986, Primary Recording); Hogwood / Westminster Abbey / Academy of Ancient Music (L'Oiseau-Lyre, 1984). Modern-instrument: Böhm / Vienna Philharmonic (DG, 1971), warmer Vienna-tradition reference; Harnoncourt / Concentus Musicus Wien (Teldec, 1981); Marriner / ASMF (Philips, 1990); Giulini / Philharmonia (EMI, 1979). Historic: Bruno Walter / New York Philharmonic (Columbia/Sony, 1956). Levin completion: Helmuth Rilling / Stuttgart Bach Collegium (Hänssler, 1994), the principal Levin reference. The René Jacobs / Akademie für Alte Musik Berlin recording (Harmonia Mundi, 2017) is the more recent period-instrument reading.

**K. 427 Great Mass**. Modern: Sir Colin Davis / LSO (Philips, 1977); Marriner / ASMF (Decca, 1986); Bernstein / Bavarian Radio SO (DG, 1990). Period-instrument: Gardiner / Monteverdi Choir (Philips, 1986); Hogwood / Academy of Ancient Music (L'Oiseau-Lyre, 1989). German choral tradition: Rilling / Stuttgart Bach Collegium. For "Et incarnatus est" as stand-alone, nearly every major lyric soprano has recorded it (Schwarzkopf, Price, Popp, Mathis, Bonney, Bartoli, Fleming).

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The Requiem completion problem is one of the principal contested topics in Mozart scholarship. The Süssmayr completion has been the standard performance version for more than two centuries; the Robert Levin completion of 1991 is the modern scholarly alternative. Listen to a Süssmayr performance (Gardiner, Böhm, Hogwood) and identify whether you can hear the seam between Mozart's eight bars of the Lacrimosa and Süssmayr's continuation. If you cannot, what does that tell you about Süssmayr's skill? If you can, what specifically gives the seam away?"_
* _"The Lacrimosa is built on the chromatic-descending figure — the rhetorical convention of grief and mourning that this volume has tracked through the wind concertos and operas. The same figure operates in the Don Giovanni Commendatore scene (Chapter 12) as the supernatural-judgment vocabulary. What does the chromatic-descent convention give Mozart in the sacred-music register that purely original material would not have given him? Is the music's emotional power dependent on the listener recognizing the convention, or does the convention work even for listeners with no familiarity with eighteenth-century musical rhetoric?"_
* _"The 'Et incarnatus est' soprano aria in the K. 427 Great Mass is structurally unusual for a Credo movement — Mozart writes it as a _concertante_ aria with three solo wind instruments interacting with the soprano in chamber-music dialogue. What does the chamber-music ensemble texture accomplish in a liturgical context? Why does Mozart take this approach to the Credo's 'and was incarnate of the Virgin Mary' text rather than the more conventional choral-with-organ setting?"_
* _"The conventional biographical reading of Mozart's last weeks — that he spoke of the Requiem as if it were for himself — is partly attested in primary sources (Sophie Haibel's late-life recollection) and partly elaborated in later popular biography. The dramatic appeal of the dying composer working on his own death music has made the Requiem one of the most culturally weighted works in the Western tradition. How do you hold the biographical romance? Is it useful for listening to the music, or does it distract from the music's compositional substance?"_
* _"Mozart's two great sacred works — the K. 427 Great Mass and the K. 626 Requiem — are both unfinished. Both were composed in the Vienna freelance years, when Mozart had limited compositional opportunity for sacred music (his Salzburg years had produced extensive sacred output under contractual obligation). Why did Mozart attempt these two large unfinished sacred projects in the Vienna years, given the lack of commercial opportunity? What does the attempt tell you about his compositional ambition outside the freelance-economic motivations?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find Gardiner / Monteverdi Choir / English Baroque Soloists (Philips, 1986) or Böhm / Vienna Philharmonic (Deutsche Grammophon, 1971). Listen to the Lacrimosa, the Kyrie, and the Confutatis and attend to four concrete moments:

  1. **Lacrimosa opening (the first ten seconds).** Confirm: strings play a quiet sustained chord in D minor; lower strings begin the chromatic descending figure. The chromatic-descending material is established immediately and dominates the movement.

  2. **Lacrimosa climactic moment.** Listen for the moment when the music builds to "Huic ergo parce, Deus / Pie Jesu Domine / dona eis requiem." The harmonic intensity reaches its peak; the choral texture opens to its fullest dynamic.

  3. **Kyrie double-fugue exposition.** Listen for the two melodic subjects entering separately. The first subject ("Kyrie eleison") enters in one voice; the second subject ("Christe eleison") enters in another. The subjects then combine in contrapuntal texture across the movement.

  4. **Confutatis male-female alternation.** Listen for the alternation between the male-voice "Confutatis maledictis" (dramatic, almost violent) and the female-voice "Voca me cum benedictis" (lyric, prayer-like). The alternation is the principal dramatic device of the movement.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : pair the Requiem with the K. 477 _Maurerische Trauermusik_ ("Masonic Funeral Music," 1785) — Mozart's principal Masonic funeral work, composed for a memorial ceremony for two Masonic brothers. The work is about six minutes long and uses the same solemn wind writing that appears in _Magic Flute_ (Chapter 14) and in parts of the Requiem. Hearing K. 477 alongside the Requiem gives a fuller sense of Mozart's funeral-music vocabulary and the Masonic context of his sacred writing.

**Read** : Christoph Wolff, _Mozart's Requiem: Historical and Analytical Studies, Documents, Score_ (University of California Press, 1994). Wolff's study is the principal scholarly treatment of the Requiem completion problem and gives the most detailed account of what is Mozart and what is Süssmayr in the standard performance version. The book includes the surviving Mozart sketches and the Süssmayr completion side by side. About 280 pages; the principal academic reference on the Requiem.

---

## Chapter 19: Mozart's Compositional Features — Spot Them Across the Catalog

Across Chapters 2 through 18 of this volume, we have been tracking Thread B, Mozart's recurring compositional features, with forward pointers at each first appearance to this chapter, where the features are collected into a single synthesis. This chapter is the convergence payoff. Seven features have been introduced; each is now revisited with a brief recapitulation of what the feature is, where in the catalog it appears most clearly, and what the listener can learn to spot when hearing future Mozart works.

The seven features are not Mozart's inventions; each is attested in the scholarly literature (Solomon, Rosen, Sadie, Eisen, Heartz, Wolff) as a recurring compositional concern. The volume's contribution is to organize them into a coherent listening framework. The framework is what the Socratopia companion (who reads each chapter to teach you) can use to make connections across the catalog when guiding your future Mozart listening.

This is a synthesis chapter, less anchor-heavy than Chapters 7-18 — substantial enough to cover the seven features with care, but more compact than the operatic and concerto chapters that lived inside specific works.

### 19.1 The Dark-Key Signature

**The feature** : Mozart's preference for D minor and G minor (with C minor as a third dark key) as vehicles for emotionally weighted material. The dark-key signature is the most-cited Mozart compositional feature in the scholarly literature and is the principal evidence for the "deep Mozart" that Chapter 1 named as the corrective to the "Mozart as wallpaper" misreception.

**Where it appears in this volume** :

* **K. 183** (G minor symphony, 1773): the early Sturm und Drang launch (Chapter 2). The seventeen-year-old Mozart's first symphonic statement in a minor key.
* **K. 466** (D minor piano concerto, 1785): the first minor-key Vienna concerto (Chapter 7). The Beethoven-cadenza concerto.
* **K. 491** (C minor piano concerto, 1786): the second minor-key Vienna concerto (Chapter 7). The most heavily orchestrated Mozart piano concerto.
* **K. 516** (G minor string quintet, 1787): the chamber-music ancestor of K. 550 (Chapter 17).
* **K. 550** (G minor symphony, 1788): the "great G minor" symphony (Chapter 10). The matched pair with K. 183 across fifteen years.
* **Don Giovanni Overture and Commendatore scene** (D minor, 1787): the operatic application (Chapter 12).
* **Requiem K. 626** (D minor, 1791): the sacred-music application (Chapter 18).

**What to listen for** : When you encounter a Mozart work in D minor, G minor, or C minor, expect the dark-key vocabulary — chromatic descending figures, syncopated rhythmic agitation, dramatic dynamic contrasts, structurally weighted slow introductions or openings, conditional rather than triumphant major-mode relief sections. The dark keys are Mozart's vocabulary for weight; the works in these keys carry weight by genre convention as much as by individual compositional choice.

**The matched pair K. 183 + K. 550** is the most direct evidence in the catalog for the dark-key signature. The two G-minor symphonies separated by fifteen years across Mozart's compositional life share the chromatic descending material, the syncopated rhythmic openings, the structural seriousness, the absence of major-mode resolution in the closing. The seventeen-year-old who wrote K. 183 and the thirty-two-year-old who wrote K. 550 were the same Mozart returning to the same key with different compositional maturity.

**Charles Rosen's argument** : G minor for Mozart was the key in which he wrote when the work required emotional weight. Whether the choice was conscious or unconscious is unknowable; the choice is consistent across the catalog and is among the most-attested Mozart compositional patterns in the scholarly literature.

### 19.2 The Wind Writing

**The feature** : Mozart's elevation of the wind section (clarinets and oboes especially, with horns and bassoons) from supporting role to melodically independent role. The wind writing is one of the principal features by which Mozart's mature orchestration is distinguishable from his contemporaries' orchestration.

**Where it appears in this volume** :

* **Sinfonia Concertante K. 364** (1779): the launch of the substantial wind writing in the orchestral exposition (Chapter 4).
* **Die Entführung K. 384** (1782): the "Martern aller Arten" four-instrument concertante (Chapter 5).
* **K. 452 Quintet for Piano and Winds** (1784): Mozart's own self-judgment of "the best work I have so far written" (Chapter 6).
* **K. 491 Piano Concerto** (1786): both oboes and clarinets in the wind section, the densest Mozart concerto wind writing (Chapter 7).
* **K. 595 Larghetto** (1791): the transparent late-style wind writing (Chapter 8).
* **K. 543 Symphony** (1788): the first late symphony with substantial clarinet writing (Chapter 9).
* **Clarinet Quintet K. 581** (1789) and **Clarinet Concerto K. 622** (1791): the principal late wind chamber and concerto works (Chapter 17).

**What to listen for** : When you encounter a Mozart orchestral or chamber work, listen specifically for the wind instruments. Are they doubling string lines (the conventional supporting role), or are they carrying independent melodic material? The Mozart wind writing typically has the winds in dialogue with strings rather than as accompaniment to strings; the dialogue is the signature.

**Anton Stadler and the late clarinet works** are the clearest case of composer-performer collaboration shaping the writing. K. 581 and K. 622 were both composed for Stadler's specific capacities (his tone, his breath control, his basset clarinet's extended range); the writing is calibrated to a specific instrument played by a specific musician.

**Charles Rosen and Stanley Sadie** both identify the wind writing as one of the principal markers of mature Mozart orchestration. The feature is attested across the scholarly literature and is not a construction of this volume.

### 19.3 The Slow-Movement-as-Work's-Heart Pattern

**The feature** : Mozart's frequent placement of the emotional center of a work in the slow movement (the Andante, Adagio, or Larghetto) rather than in the dramatic outer movements. The pattern contrasts with the Beethoven tradition (which often places the emotional center in the dramatic first movement or in the heroic finale) and is one of the distinctive markers of Mozart's compositional approach to multi-movement structure.

**Where it appears in this volume** :

* **K. 271** (Piano Concerto No. 9 "Jeunehomme," 1777): the C-minor Andante as the work's emotional center (Chapter 4).
* **K. 364** (Sinfonia Concertante, 1779): the C-minor Andante as the work's emotional center (Chapter 4).
* **K. 488** (Piano Concerto, 1786): the F-sharp minor Adagio as the work's emotional center — Mozart's only major slow movement in F-sharp minor and a key choice he uses almost nowhere else.
* **K. 595** (Piano Concerto, 1791): the B-flat major Larghetto in the late-style transparency register (Chapter 8).
* **K. 581** (Clarinet Quintet, 1789): the D-major Larghetto as the chamber-music peer of K. 488 and K. 595 (Chapter 17).
* **K. 622** (Clarinet Concerto, 1791): the slow-movement Adagio in the late-style register.

**What to listen for** : When you encounter a Mozart multi-movement work, give particular attention to the slow movement. The slow movement may carry more emotional weight than the outer movements; the structural and harmonic ambition may peak there rather than in the dramatic first movement.

**The triplet K. 488 + K. 595 + K. 581** is the clearest example of the slow-movement-as-heart pattern. All three works have major-key home keys (A major for K. 488, B♭ major for K. 595, A major for K. 581), but the slow movements all move into more reflective registers (F-sharp minor for K. 488, sustained E♭ major Larghetto for K. 595, D major Larghetto for K. 581) where the principal emotional weight resides.

### 19.4 The Theme-and-Variations Form

**The feature** : Mozart's use of the theme-and-variations form for finales and for first movements where the variation procedure suited the material. The form is one of Mozart's recurring structural choices and is the principal form he used for sets of variations on popular tunes (the standalone variation sets) and for variation movements within sonatas, concertos, and chamber works.

**Where it appears in this volume** :

* **K. 453** finale (Piano Concerto No. 17, 1784): the starling-imitable theme as the variation subject (Chapter 6).
* **K. 331** first movement (Piano Sonata No. 11, 1783): the theme-and-variations first movement, paired with the "Alla Turca" finale (Chapter 15).

**Other catalog examples** (not anchored in this volume): the "Ah, vous dirai-je, Maman" variations K. 265 (the "Twinkle, Twinkle, Little Star" variations of 1781-82); variations within the Haydn Quartets; variations within the Clarinet Quintet; variations within several late chamber works.

**What to listen for** : When you encounter a theme-and-variations movement in Mozart, listen for the theme's first statement (the theme is typically simple, balanced, and memorable so that the listener can follow it through the variations); then identify each variation's principal compositional technique (right-hand ornamentation, left-hand figuration, mode change, tempo change, contrapuntal elaboration). The variation form's accessibility is one of its compositional strengths — the listener can follow the structure clearly.

**The pedagogical centrality** of the theme-and-variations form in Mozart's catalog reflects the form's importance in eighteenth-century keyboard music. The form was a standard skill for keyboard composers and improvisers; Mozart's variation sets and variation movements are among the principal repertoire entries in the form.

### 19.5 The Opera Ensemble Finale

**The feature** : The multi-character interlocking continuous-musical-span finale that Mozart developed in the late operas, particularly in the Da Ponte collaborations. The form was an Italian _opera buffa_ innovation that Mozart and Da Ponte brought to its mature compositional development. The form is now one of the principal distinctive features of late-eighteenth-century opera and is one of Mozart's principal contributions to operatic structure.

**Where it appears in this volume** :

* **Le nozze di Figaro Act II finale** (1786): the seven-voice climax of accumulating tension (Chapter 11).
* **Le nozze di Figaro Act IV finale** (1786): the Countess's forgiveness as the structural reversal (Chapter 11).
* **Don Giovanni Act II finale (supper scene)** (1787): the form extended into supernatural-dramatic content (Chapter 12).
* **Così fan tutte ensembles** (1790): the form in Da Ponte's third collaboration (Chapter 13, briefly).
* **Die Zauberflöte Act II finale** (1791): the form modified by the _Singspiel_ tradition (Chapter 14).

**What to listen for** : When you encounter a Mozart operatic finale, follow the entrances and exits of characters. The finale typically begins with a small ensemble (duet or trio) and expands as new characters arrive; each new character's entry produces a musical shift (new tempo, new key, new texture); by the finale's climax, all the principal characters are singing simultaneously in interlocking parts. The form's distinctive feature is the continuous musical span — there are no breaks between numbers, no recitative interruptions, no opportunities to applaud individual sections. The finale runs as a single dramatic and musical arc.

**The Figaro Act II finale** is the clearest single example of the form. The finale runs about twenty minutes continuous; characters enter the dramatic situation one by one; the seven-voice closing texture is the form at its fullest. The Don Giovanni Act II finale extends the form into supernatural-judgment content; the Magic Flute Act II finale modifies the form into the _Singspiel_ tradition.

**Daniel Heartz and Cliff Eisen** both identify the Mozart ensemble finale as one of the principal structural innovations in late-eighteenth-century opera. The form's antecedents in Pasquale Anfossi, Giovanni Paisiello, Domenico Cimarosa, and other Italian _opera buffa_ composers are well-attested; Mozart's contribution is the form's mature compositional development.

### 19.6 The Chromatic-Descent Figure

**The feature** : The descending sequence of half-steps, often in the lower strings or bassoons, that Mozart inherited from the Bach-Purcell-Baroque tradition as the conventional musical vocabulary of grief, mourning, and supernatural judgment. The figure is one of the rhetorical conventions of late-eighteenth-century music; Mozart's deployment of the convention is one of the principal Classical-period applications.

**Where it appears in this volume** :

* **K. 491** Piano Concerto (1786): the wind chromatic descents in the first-movement opening (Chapter 7).
* **Don Giovanni Commendatore scene** (1787): the supernatural-judgment vocabulary in operatic context (Chapter 12).
* **K. 550** Symphony (1788): the chromatic descending material as the principal first-movement theme (Chapter 10).
* **Requiem Lacrimosa** (1791): the operatic-style mourning vocabulary in liturgical context (Chapter 18).

**What to listen for** : When you encounter a Mozart work in a dark key (D minor, G minor, C minor), listen specifically for the chromatic descents in the lower strings or bassoons. The figure is often present as the underlying compositional material — sometimes as the principal theme (K. 550 first movement), sometimes as accompanying material that adds the conventional grief or judgment register (the Commendatore scene), sometimes as the orchestral material that prepares a choral mourning entrance (the Lacrimosa's opening).

**The convention's historical depth** : Johann Sebastian Bach used the chromatic-descent figure extensively in the cantatas, the Passions, and the B-minor Mass (the "Crucifixus" is the Bach instance analysts reach for first). Henry Purcell used it in _Dido and Aeneas_ (Dido's "When I am laid in earth"). The figure was a shared convention across the Baroque and into the Classical period; the listener trained in the convention recognizes it immediately and feels its emotional charge.

**Mozart's specific contributions** : the convention's application in the Classical-period symphonic, concerto, operatic, and sacred-music registers. K. 550 takes the figure as primary thematic material in symphonic context; the Commendatore scene uses it in the operatic supernatural-judgment register; the Requiem Lacrimosa uses it in the liturgical mourning register. The same convention serves three different generic contexts in Mozart's catalog.

### 19.7 The Late-Style Transparency

**The feature** : The transparency of orchestral writing, the simplicity of melodic material, and the reduced dramatic urgency that characterizes Mozart's works of his last year and a half (roughly mid-1790 through December 1791). The late-style transparency is the principal Mozart late-style feature, well-attested across Wolff, Solomon, and the modern late-period scholarship.

**Where it appears in this volume** :

* **K. 595 Larghetto** (Piano Concerto No. 27, January 1791): the principal piano-concerto late-style work (Chapter 8).
* **Die Zauberflöte** (Magic Flute, September 1791): the principal operatic late-style work (Chapter 14).
* **Clarinet Quintet K. 581** (1789, slightly earlier): the chamber-music ancestor of the late style (Chapter 17).
* **Clarinet Concerto K. 622** (October 1791): the principal solo-wind late-style work.
* **Requiem K. 626 Introit** (the only fully Mozart-completed Requiem movement, autumn 1791): late-style transparency in liturgical context (Chapter 18).

**What to listen for** : When you encounter a Mozart work from 1790-91 (the last year and a half of his life), listen for the texture — is the orchestration lighter than the high-peak Vienna writing? Is the melodic material simpler and more immediately memorable? Is the harmonic language more settled and less modulatory? The late-style transparency is the compositional thinning that distinguishes the last works from the dense Vienna peak of 1784-86.

**The cluster of K. 595 + K. 622 + Magic Flute + Requiem Introit** is the principal evidence for the late-style transparency. The four works were composed in Mozart's last year and share the transparency features. The late-style is not the work of a dying composer in the literal sense (Mozart in early 1791 was not yet ill); it is a stylistic shift that began around 1789-90 and reached its mature form in 1791.

**Christoph Wolff's argument** in _Mozart at the Gateway to His Fortune_ is that the late style was Mozart's compositional thinking arriving at a transparent register that contrasted with the dense Vienna peak. The late style is not decline but compositional development; Mozart's late works are not lesser but different from his peak works.

### 19.8 How the Seven Features Combine

The seven features are not independent compositional concerns. They combine in specific patterns across the catalog. Some examples:

**K. 466 (D-minor Piano Concerto)** combines the dark-key signature (D minor) with the chromatic-descent figure (in the orchestral opening) and with the wind writing (the dialogue between piano and wind section in the first movement). Three features in one work.

**K. 550 (G-minor Symphony)** combines the dark-key signature (G minor) with the chromatic-descent figure (the first-movement principal theme) and with the leaner orchestration that contrasts with the wind-rich K. 543 of the same summer. The work is dark-key and chromatic-descent driven; the wind writing is restrained rather than expansive.

**Don Giovanni Act II finale (Commendatore scene)** combines the dark-key signature (D minor) with the chromatic-descent figure (the supernatural-judgment vocabulary) with the opera ensemble finale form (the continuous musical span). Three features in one finale.

**Requiem K. 626 Lacrimosa** combines the dark-key signature (D minor) with the chromatic-descent figure (the principal compositional material) with the slow-movement-as-heart pattern (the Lacrimosa being the emotional center of the Requiem) with the late-style transparency (the choral writing's hushed lyric register). Four features in one movement.

**K. 595 Larghetto (Piano Concerto No. 27, second movement)** combines the slow-movement-as-heart pattern (the Larghetto is the work's emotional center) with the late-style transparency (the lightest orchestral texture in the late piano concertos) with the wind writing (the wind section carries substantial material). Three features in one movement.

**Die Zauberflöte** combines the late-style transparency (the work's principal orchestral character) with the opera ensemble finale form (the Act II finale) with the wind writing (the priests' chorus and the Sarastro arias). Three features across the work.

The features are not a checklist; they are patterns that recur across the catalog and that combine in different works in different proportions. A given Mozart work may show one feature prominently, several features simultaneously, or none of the seven features in any prominent way (some of Mozart's early or occasional works do not display any of the features). The synthesis here is descriptive rather than prescriptive — the features are what to listen for, not what every Mozart work must contain.

### 19.9 The Synthesis as a Listening Framework

The Socratopia companion that uses this volume to teach Mozart will use the seven-feature framework as a structural guide for connecting works across the catalog. When you encounter K. 466 (D minor concerto) in a Socratopia session and the companion is teaching from the K. 466 chapter, the companion knows from this synthesis chapter that K. 466 is structurally connected to K. 491 (the other minor-key concerto), to K. 550 (the dark-key symphony), to the Requiem (the dark-key sacred work), and to _Don Giovanni_ 's Commendatore scene (the dark-key operatic moment). The companion can make these connections in the conversation with you.

When you encounter K. 595 Larghetto and the companion is teaching from Chapter 8, the companion knows from this synthesis that K. 595 is structurally connected to K. 622 (the chamber-style late wind concerto), to the Magic Flute (the operatic late-style work), to the Clarinet Quintet K. 581 (the chamber-music late-style work), and to the slow-movement-as-heart pattern that runs across the late concerto cycle. The companion can move the conversation across these works as the dialogue develops.

The framework's value for the listener (you) is the same value it has for the Socratopia companion — the framework makes the catalog cohere as a single compositional life rather than as a sequence of unrelated works. The Mozart you have been hearing across the volume is not many composers writing in many styles; he is one Mozart, with seven recurring compositional concerns that organize the catalog's variety.

### 19.10 Bridge to Chapter 20

Chapter 20 turns from the music to the biographical questions that have most occupied popular reception of Mozart — specifically the Mozart-Salieri question and the _Amadeus_ -film inventions. The chapter is the principal place in this volume where the popular-culture image of Mozart is directly engaged.

The bridge from Chapter 19 to Chapter 20: the synthesis chapter has shown what the music does. The next chapter shows how the music has been received and how the biographical record has been shaped, and sometimes distorted, by the popular reception. The two chapters together complete the volume's account of Mozart in scholarly and popular contexts. Chapter 19 is the music itself; Chapter 20 is the music's reception.

The central tension Chapter 20 develops is the distance between the documented historical record — Salieri as respected Imperial Kapellmeister, the Mozart-Salieri professional competition as real but not murderous — and the popular image of Mozart as the persecuted genius of Shaffer's play and Forman's film. Chapter 20 engages it through a three-column observational frame: scholarly record / _Amadeus_ -film invention / genuine historical gray zone.

The principal figures Chapter 20 will engage by name: Antonio Salieri (1750-1825) as Imperial Kapellmeister 1788-1824 and teacher of Beethoven, Schubert, and Liszt; Peter Shaffer (1926-2016) as the playwright whose 1979 _Amadeus_ shaped the popular Mozart-Salieri narrative; Miloš Forman (1932-2018) as the director whose 1984 film extended Shaffer's narrative to mass audiences; Alexander Pushkin (1799-1837) as the Russian poet whose 1830 _Mozart and Salieri_ play preceded Shaffer's by 150 years and established the Salieri-as-poisoner narrative in literary culture.

The bridge also names the principal historiographical question: how does a careful reader hold a popular-culture narrative that has shaped public imagination for fifty years and that has detached from the scholarly record? Chapter 20 offers no verdict; the chapter presents the documented record and the popular invention side by side and leaves the listener to decide what to do with the difference.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The seven Mozart compositional features collected in this chapter — dark-key signature, wind writing, slow-movement-as-heart, theme-and-variations, opera ensemble finale, chromatic-descent figure, late-style transparency — are not Mozart's inventions but the patterns Mozart's mature writing returns to most consistently. Which of the seven features do you find most useful as a listening framework? Pick one and describe how it has changed the way you hear a specific Mozart work since reading the synthesis."_
* _"The Requiem Lacrimosa combines four of the seven features simultaneously (dark-key signature, chromatic-descent figure, slow-movement-as-heart pattern, late-style transparency). Most movements in the catalog combine only two or three features. Why does the Lacrimosa concentrate so many features at once? Is the combination a function of the work's late composition date, the sacred genre, the specific compositional moment, or something else?"_
* _"Charles Rosen, Stanley Sadie, Cliff Eisen, Daniel Heartz, and Christoph Wolff are the principal scholarly voices in the documentation of Mozart's compositional features. The seven features in this synthesis are organized into a listening framework that this volume's contribution, but each feature is documented in the scholarship as Mozart's recurring compositional concern. What does the scholarly documentation give the listener that direct listening alone would not give? Is the framework useful for listening, or is the listening more important than the framework?"_
* _"The dark-key signature, the slow-movement-as-heart pattern, and the late-style transparency are compositional concerns that distinguish Mozart from his contemporaries (Haydn and the lesser Vienna composers) and from his successors (Beethoven and the early Romantics). What does the distinctiveness tell you about Mozart's place in the late-eighteenth-century compositional landscape? Is Mozart the central composer of the Classical period, one of several major composers, or a singular figure whose style does not fit comfortably with any of his peers?"_
* _"The chromatic-descent figure and the opera ensemble finale form are compositional conventions that Mozart inherited from earlier traditions (the Baroque chromatic descent; the Italian _opera buffa_ ensemble). What does it mean for a great composer's signature features to be largely inherited conventions used in distinctive ways rather than wholly original inventions? Is originality the right framework for thinking about Mozart's contributions, or is the convention-and-elaboration framework more accurate?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

This is a synthesis chapter with no single anchor work; the listening exercise is a comparative session across multiple chapters' anchor works. Pick three of the seven features and listen for them in two contrasting works each:

  1. **The dark-key signature** : listen to K. 466 (D minor) and K. 550 (G minor). Identify the chromatic-descending material in each; notice how the same dark-key vocabulary appears in different specific keys with different specific compositional consequences.

  2. **The slow-movement-as-heart pattern** : listen to the K. 488 Adagio (F-sharp minor) and the K. 595 Larghetto (E-flat major; Chapter 8). Identify what makes each slow movement the emotional center of its work; notice the differences in key, tempo, and texture.

  3. **The opera ensemble finale form** : listen to the Figaro Act II finale (Chapter 11) and the Don Giovanni Act II finale supper scene (Chapter 12). Identify the structural pattern (entrances, exits, accumulating ensemble, climactic combination); notice how the same form serves the comic Figaro material and the supernatural Don Giovanni material.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : a comparative session across the dark-key cluster — K. 183 (Chapter 2), K. 466 (Chapter 7), K. 491 (Chapter 7), K. 516 (Chapter 17), K. 550 (Chapter 10), Don Giovanni Overture (Chapter 12), Requiem Introit (Chapter 18). The seven works span Mozart's life and demonstrate the dark-key signature's continuity across thirty years. Listening to all seven in sequence (or across a week) gives the fullest sense of the dark-key vocabulary as a Mozart compositional life-pattern.

**Read** : Charles Rosen, _The Classical Style: Haydn, Mozart, Beethoven_ (Norton, 1971; expanded edition 1997). Chapter 5 ("Mozart") is the principal analytical treatment of the compositional features collected in this synthesis chapter. Rosen's framework — the concerto-as-dialogue, the symphony-as-architecture, the ensemble-finale form, the harmonic vocabulary — is the analytical voice the synthesis chapter draws on. Read selectively; the Mozart chapter is roughly eighty pages.

---

## Chapter 20: The Documented Mozart-Salieri Question

This chapter is the principal place in this volume where we engage the popular-culture image of Mozart directly. The image is dominated by Peter Shaffer's 1979 play _Amadeus_ and Miloš Forman's 1984 film of the same title — two dramatic works that together have shaped the public imagination of Mozart and Antonio Salieri for fifty years and have largely displaced the scholarly record in popular consciousness. The chapter offers no verdict; the popular-culture invention has its own dramatic merits and is not the same kind of artifact as a scholarly history. But the scholarly record and the popular invention are different things, and a careful Mozart listener benefits from knowing where they agree and where they diverge.

The chapter is organized as a three-column observational frame: **scholarly record** (what the historical evidence supports), **_Amadeus_ -film invention** (what Shaffer and Forman created dramatically without historical foundation), and **historical gray zone** (questions where the evidence is genuinely uncertain and reasonable scholars disagree). The frame is meant to help you hold the popular Mozart-Salieri story alongside the historical Mozart-Salieri record without forcing a choice between them — both are legitimate cultural objects, but they are not the same kind of object.

This is a biographical and historiographical chapter rather than a music-focused one. There is no anchor work; the chapter is about the Mozart-Salieri relationship and its reception, not about specific compositions.

### 20.1 The Scholarly Record on Antonio Salieri

Antonio Salieri (August 18, 1750 – May 7, 1825) was an Italian composer who lived nearly the whole of his adult life in Vienna. The principal facts of his career, well-attested in the scholarly literature (the standard reference is Volkmar Braunbehrens's _Maligned Master: The Real Story of Antonio Salieri_ of 1989; Cliff Eisen and others have published shorter studies):

**Education and early career.** Born in Legnago, Italy (a small town in the Venetian Republic). His parents died in his early teens; he was taken to Venice by the music patron Giovanni Mocenigo and then to Vienna in 1766 by Florian Leopold Gassmann, the Imperial Court Composer. Gassmann was Salieri's principal teacher and mentor; Gassmann died in 1774 and Salieri inherited some of his official positions.

**Vienna appointments.** Salieri became Imperial Chamber Composer in 1774 (at age 24, an extraordinary appointment). He became director of the Italian opera at the Burgtheater in 1774 and held the position through 1790. In 1788, he was promoted to Imperial Kapellmeister (the principal music position at the Vienna imperial court), succeeding Giuseppe Bonno. He held the Kapellmeister position from 1788 until his retirement in 1824 — thirty-six years.

**Compositional output.** Salieri composed more than forty operas (Italian, German, and French), substantial sacred music (masses, requiems, oratorios), chamber music, concertos, and pedagogical works. The principal operatic successes were _Armida_ (1771, Vienna), _La fiera di Venezia_ (1772, Vienna), _La grotta di Trofonio_ (1785, Vienna), _Axur, re d'Ormus_ (1788, Vienna, with libretto by Lorenzo Da Ponte — the same Da Ponte who had collaborated with Mozart on _Figaro_), and _Falstaff_ (1799, Vienna). _Axur_ was Salieri's most successful opera; it remained in the Vienna repertoire for decades and was performed across Europe through the early nineteenth century.

**Teaching.** Salieri was one of the principal composition teachers of his generation. His pupils included Ludwig van Beethoven (who studied with Salieri in Vienna from approximately 1792 through 1802, primarily on Italian vocal composition), Franz Schubert (who studied with Salieri from 1808 through 1813 or 1814), Franz Liszt (who studied briefly with Salieri in 1821-22), Carl Czerny (who studied with Salieri 1801-12), Johann Nepomuk Hummel (briefly), and many others. The pupil list is one of the principal pieces of evidence for Salieri's contemporary professional standing — the leading Vienna composer-pupils of three successive generations sought him out as a teacher.

**Death.** Salieri died in Vienna on May 7, 1825 at age 74. His funeral was attended by Vienna musical society; no specific Salieri-funeral composition by Schubert is securely attested. Salieri was buried at the Matzleinsdorf Cemetery in Vienna.

What this record establishes: Salieri was the principal Italian-opera and court-composer figure in Vienna from the 1770s through the early 1820s. He was a successful composer in his own right (more than forty operas, multiple Vienna successes), a teacher of three generations of leading Vienna composers, and the holder of the most senior music position at the imperial court for thirty-six years. He was not a marginal figure; he was the central Vienna court music establishment for a half-century.

### 20.2 The Scholarly Record on the Mozart-Salieri Relationship

The historical record of the Mozart-Salieri relationship is partial but substantive. The principal pieces of evidence:

**The competition for court patronage in the 1780s.** Salieri held the senior Italian opera position at the Burgtheater from 1774 through 1790. Mozart in the Vienna freelance years (1781-91) competed with Salieri for opera commissions, court favor, and the limited number of available court music positions. The competition was real and was at times acrimonious. Mozart's letters to Leopold across the 1780s contain occasional complaints about "Italian cabals" at the Vienna court — references to the Italian composers (Salieri among them) who Mozart felt were favored over German-style composers like himself.

**The 1786 _Prima la musica e poi le parole_ \+ _Der Schauspieldirektor_ twin commission.** In February 1786, the Habsburg court commissioned simultaneous one-act musical theater works from Salieri and Mozart for a court entertainment at Schönbrunn Palace. Salieri composed _Prima la musica e poi le parole_ (a satirical piece about the precedence of music over words in opera); Mozart composed _Der Schauspieldirektor_ (a German _Singspiel_ on theatrical management). The two works were performed at the same court event on February 7, 1786. The dual commission is evidence that the court treated Salieri and Mozart as peers; both were considered worthy of the simultaneous high-profile commission.

**Salieri attending Mozart performances.** Mozart's letters to Constanze from late 1791 (the autumn of Magic Flute) mention Salieri attending a performance of Magic Flute and expressing enthusiasm for the work. The October 14, 1791 letter to Constanze describes Salieri's attendance and his "bravo!" responses to the music. The letter is evidence of professional cordiality between the two composers in the last months of Mozart's life.

**Salieri teaching Mozart's son Franz Xaver Wolfgang Mozart.** After Mozart's death, Constanze placed her surviving son Franz Xaver Wolfgang Mozart (born July 26, 1791, four months before Mozart's death) with various Vienna teachers for his musical education. Salieri was among the teachers; the choice is evidence that Constanze did not consider Salieri an enemy of the Mozart family.

**Salieri's public denial of the poisoning rumor.** In the early 1820s, after Salieri had suffered a mental decline (likely senile dementia or related conditions), rumors began to circulate in Vienna that he had confessed on his deathbed to having poisoned Mozart. Salieri, before his death, publicly denied the rumor. The denial was witnessed by his pupil Ignaz Moscheles (the pianist and composer, 1794-1870) in a visit to Salieri in 1823, two years before Salieri's death. Moscheles recorded the visit in his later memoirs; Salieri's denial was direct and emphatic.

What this record establishes: Mozart and Salieri were professional competitors in the Vienna court music establishment of the 1780s and early 1790s. The competition was real and at times tense. But Mozart was not poisoned by Salieri; the poisoning rumor emerged after Salieri's mental decline in the 1820s and was publicly denied by Salieri before his death. The Mozart-Salieri relationship was that of competitive professional colleagues in a tight Vienna music economy, not that of murderer and victim.

### 20.3 The _Amadeus_ Invention

Peter Shaffer's _Amadeus_ (the play, premiered November 2, 1979 at the National Theatre in London) and Miloš Forman's 1984 film of the same title together constitute one of the most successful pieces of historical-fiction theater and cinema of the late twentieth century. The play won the Tony Award for Best Play in 1981; the film won eight Academy Awards including Best Picture in 1985. The work has shaped the popular image of Mozart and Salieri for two generations.

The dramatic premise: Salieri, the established Vienna court composer, recognizes Mozart as a divinely-gifted genius and is consumed by jealousy. He resolves to destroy Mozart, partly through professional sabotage and partly through a campaign that drives Mozart to compose the Requiem for himself (Salieri, in disguise, commissions the Requiem and pressures Mozart to complete it under conditions that hasten his death). The play and film are structured as a retrospective confession by an elderly Salieri (the film opens with the suicide attempt and confession of an old Salieri in a Vienna asylum in 1823).

The principal _Amadeus_ -film inventions:

**Salieri as poisoner / death-causer.** The play and film present Salieri as actively involved in causing Mozart's death — through professional sabotage, through the Requiem commission as psychological torture, and (in the film's most direct dramatic interpretation) through the deliberate effort to drive Mozart into the overwork and stress that contributed to his fatal illness. The historical record gives no support for any of these. Salieri did not commission the Requiem (Count Walsegg did, anonymously); Salieri did not pressure Mozart professionally in ways that contributed to his death; Salieri did not poison Mozart.

**Mozart's giggle and personality.** The film's Mozart (played by Tom Hulce in the Forman film) has a high-pitched giggle that has become one of the most-imitated features in popular culture. The giggle is the film's invention; no historical source records Mozart having a distinctive giggle, and the personality the film portrays (the immature genius incapable of appropriate social behavior) is the film's dramatic creation rather than the historical Mozart's actual personality. The historical Mozart was, by all evidence (the surviving letters, the contemporary accounts), a normal adult socially — articulate, witty in correspondence, professionally competent in his dealings with patrons and colleagues, devoted to his wife and family.

**Constanze's portrayal.** The film's Constanze (played by Elizabeth Berridge) is portrayed as somewhat naive and overwhelmed by the household pressures. The historical Constanze was a competent household manager, an accomplished singer in her own right (she sang the soprano solos in the 1783 Salzburg performance of the K. 427 Great Mass), and an able advocate for Mozart's posthumous reputation (she worked with the early biographers Niemetschek and Nissen to shape the historical record; she organized publications of Mozart's works to provide for the Mozart children).

**The Requiem composition scene.** The film's climactic scene — Salieri sitting at Mozart's bedside in the final hours, taking down Mozart's dictation of the Requiem material — is the film's central dramatic invention. The scene does not correspond to any historical event. Süssmayr was Mozart's pupil and assistant during the Requiem composition and was present for some of the late discussions; Salieri was not present at Mozart's deathbed and had no role in the Requiem's composition or completion.

**The supposed Salieri-Mozart rivalry as the central dramatic conflict.** The play and film organize Mozart's biography around the Salieri-Mozart antagonism as the principal dramatic engine. The historical Mozart's life had many principal relationships and conflicts — the Leopold relationship, the Vienna patrons, the operatic collaborators, the Vienna economic conditions of 1788-91 — and the Salieri relationship was one among several rather than the central organizing fact. The _Amadeus_ -film concentration on the Salieri rivalry is dramatic construction rather than historical proportion.

What the _Amadeus_ -film accomplishes as drama: a powerful theatrical and cinematic exploration of mediocrity confronting genius, of the human cost of artistic exceptionalism, of envy as a corrosive force. The work is one of the great twentieth-century theatrical and cinematic works. Its dramatic power is real and is not diminished by its historical inaccuracies.

What the _Amadeus_ -film does not accomplish: a historical account of Mozart's life or of the Mozart-Salieri relationship. Treating the play and film as historical documentation is a category error; they are dramatic fiction with a real-historical setting, not history.

### 20.4 The Historical Gray Zone

Some questions in the Mozart-Salieri relationship are genuinely uncertain. The principal gray-zone questions:

**Did Salieri actively sabotage Mozart professionally?** The Mozart letters contain occasional complaints about "Italian cabals" at the Vienna court; some specific incidents in the operatic competition of the 1780s have been read as Salieri-orchestrated obstacles (the rumored delays in _Figaro_ 's premiere, the alleged interference with the _Don Giovanni_ Vienna premiere). The historical evidence is partial and ambiguous; some incidents may reflect Salieri's competitive maneuvers, others may reflect normal Vienna court politics not specifically Salieri-driven. The honest scholarly position: there was professional competition; whether it included specific sabotage is uncertain.

**What did Salieri think of Mozart's music privately?** The historical record gives mostly public statements from Salieri about Mozart (the October 1791 Magic Flute praise that Mozart records in his letter to Constanze; some other public expressions of admiration). Salieri's private opinion of Mozart's music is largely unknown; the few surviving private references are reserved-positive rather than enthusiastic, but the sample is small and the inference is uncertain.

**How did Constanze and the Mozart family regard Salieri after Mozart's death?** Constanze entrusted Franz Xaver Wolfgang Mozart's education partly to Salieri, suggesting she did not consider Salieri an enemy. But Constanze's later memoirs (filtered through Niemetschek and Nissen) sometimes contain reserved or critical references to Salieri. The Mozart family's overall posthumous attitude toward Salieri appears to have been ambivalent rather than hostile, but the evidence is fragmentary.

**The death rumor's exact origin.** The 1820s poisoning rumor emerged after Salieri's mental decline. Whether Salieri himself, in his confused state, made statements that started the rumor, or whether the rumor was external invention that Salieri's mental condition prevented him from effectively refuting, is uncertain. Moscheles's 1823 visit recorded Salieri's emphatic denial of the rumor; the rumor's persistence after Salieri's emphatic denial suggests it took on independent cultural life.

**The Pushkin-to-Shaffer literary genealogy.** Alexander Pushkin's 1830 verse play _Mozart and Salieri_ established the Salieri-as-poisoner narrative in Russian literary culture. Nikolai Rimsky-Korsakov set the play as a chamber opera in 1898, extending the narrative into musical-theatrical reception. Peter Shaffer's 1979 _Amadeus_ drew on the Pushkin-Rimsky-Korsakov tradition but transformed it through twentieth-century dramatic invention. The genealogical chain — Pushkin 1830 → Rimsky-Korsakov 1898 → Shaffer 1979 → Forman 1984 — is the principal cultural transmission of the Salieri-poisoner narrative. The Russian literary origin matters because Pushkin was working from rumor and dramatic possibility rather than historical research; the narrative has therefore been a literary-dramatic tradition for 195 years rather than a historical claim.

### 20.5 How to Hold the Popular Story Alongside the Historical Record

The Mozart-Salieri question is the principal place where the popular-culture image of Mozart most sharply diverges from the scholarly record. The divergence is unusual in its scale and cultural persistence — most popular-culture images of historical figures eventually align (more or less) with the scholarly record over time, but the _Amadeus_ -film Mozart and Salieri have persisted alongside the scholarly record for fifty years without significant convergence. The play and film are too successful as drama to be displaced by historical correction; the historical Mozart and Salieri are too factually documented to be displaced by dramatic invention.

For listeners and readers of Mozart's music: the practical answer is to hold both. The _Amadeus_ -film Mozart and Salieri are valid cultural objects — Peter Shaffer's play and Miloš Forman's film are great works of late-twentieth-century theater and cinema, and engaging them as drama is legitimate. The historical Mozart and Salieri are also valid cultural objects — the scholarly record is well-attested, the Mozart-Salieri relationship was real and complex, and engaging the historical record gives a different and more accurate picture of what the actual relationship was. The two pictures are not the same; both have their place; neither requires the other to be displaced.

When engaging the music: the historical Mozart is the relevant figure. The dark-key signature, the wind writing, the late-style transparency, the opera ensemble finale form — these are features of the actual compositional life of Wolfgang Amadeus Mozart (1756-1791), not of the _Amadeus_ -film character. The music has its own coherence as the production of the actual historical composer; engaging the music does not require engaging the _Amadeus_ -film picture.

When engaging the popular culture: the _Amadeus_ -film Mozart and Salieri are the relevant figures. The play and film are about something different from the historical Mozart-Salieri relationship — they are about envy, mediocrity, the cost of genius, the human response to encountering artistic exceptionalism. These are genuine human questions that Shaffer's drama treats powerfully. Engaging the play and film does not require dismissing them as historical fraud; they are doing something different from history.

The practical hold: when you watch _Amadeus_ , watch it as drama. When you listen to Mozart, listen to him as the historical composer he was. When the two pictures conflict, the historical record is what describes what actually happened; the dramatic work is what extends Mozart and Salieri into the cultural imagination of subsequent centuries. Both are part of how Mozart now lives in the world; neither displaces the other.

### 20.6 The Salieri Music Question

A separate question that follows from the _Amadeus_ -film reception: what about Salieri's actual music?

Salieri's compositional output has been largely forgotten in mainstream classical-music performance. His more than forty operas are now rarely staged; his sacred music and chamber music are recorded occasionally but not in the standard repertoire; his concertos and symphonic works are mostly out of print. The reasons for the eclipse are partly aesthetic (Mozart's music is, by nearly all judgments, better than Salieri's) and partly cultural (the _Amadeus_ -film image of Salieri as the mediocre court composer has reinforced the eclipse rather than provoked the curiosity that would lead to renewed performance).

What Salieri's music actually sounds like: serviceable Italian-tradition Vienna-court composition of the late eighteenth century. The operas have moments of dramatic interest and some genuinely accomplished arias; the sacred music is competent in the Italian liturgical tradition; the chamber and concerto works are pleasant if not distinctive. Salieri was not a great composer in the sense Mozart was; he was a thoroughly competent professional Italian-tradition Vienna composer who held the principal court position for thirty-six years on the basis of his professional competence rather than his compositional genius.

The recent Salieri-revival recordings have been limited but worth knowing:

The Cecilia Bartoli _Salieri Album_ (Decca, 2003) is the principal contemporary recording of Salieri arias; Bartoli's vocal performances are stunning and the album makes the case for Salieri as a competent Italian-tradition aria composer.

The René Jacobs / Concerto Köln _Axur, re d'Ormus_ (Harmonia Mundi, 1991) and _Falstaff_ (Harmonia Mundi, 1995) recordings are the principal complete-opera Salieri recordings of recent decades.

The Christophe Rousset / Les Talens Lyriques Salieri _Les Danaïdes_ (Aparté, 2014) is the contemporary period-instrument Salieri reference for the French operatic side of Salieri's output.

For most listeners these recordings are interesting curiosities rather than essential repertoire; Salieri does not displace the time you might spend with Mozart, Haydn, Beethoven, or Schubert. But hearing some Salieri once provides a context for the Mozart-Salieri relationship — you can hear what Salieri actually wrote rather than imagining him as the _Amadeus_ -film character.

### 20.7 What Chapter 20 Does and Does Not Do

This chapter does:

* Lay out the historical record on Antonio Salieri (1750-1825) as Imperial Kapellmeister 1788-1824 and as composer-teacher of Beethoven, Schubert, Liszt, and others.
* Lay out the historical record on the Mozart-Salieri relationship as professional competition with occasional cordiality in a tight Vienna music economy.
* Distinguish the _Amadeus_ -film inventions (the poisoning, the giggle, Constanze's caricature, the Requiem deathbed scene, the centrality of the Salieri rivalry to Mozart's life) from the historical record.
* Identify the genuinely uncertain gray-zone questions where reasonable scholars disagree.
* Trace the literary genealogy of the Salieri-poisoner narrative from Pushkin 1830 through Rimsky-Korsakov 1898 to Shaffer 1979 and Forman 1984.

This chapter does not:

* Offer a verdict on the Mozart-Salieri relationship beyond the historical record's partial conclusions.
* Dismiss the _Amadeus_ -film as a cultural object.
* Recommend that readers abandon the popular Mozart image and replace it with a scholarly one.
* Adjudicate the gray-zone questions where the evidence is genuinely uncertain.

The chapter's posture is observational. The historical record and the popular invention are different kinds of cultural objects; both have their legitimate place; the careful Mozart listener benefits from knowing where they agree and where they diverge, but the listener is not required to choose between them.

### 20.8 Bridge to Chapter 21

Chapter 21 turns to a different kind of reception question: the recording history of Mozart's music across the past 125 years. Where Chapter 20 has engaged the Mozart-as-popular-culture-figure question, Chapter 21 engages the Mozart-as-performed-music question. Both chapters belong to Part VII (Synthesis) and both look at how Mozart has been received rather than at what Mozart composed. The transition: from the cultural reception (this chapter) to the performance reception (next chapter).

The recording history of Mozart's music is a parallel reception alongside the _Amadeus_ -film cultural one — it tells us how Mozart has actually been heard over the past century: the early phonograph era of the 1900s-1920s; the Walter / Klemperer / Furtwängler mid-century tradition; the Böhm / Szell architectural tradition of the 1960s-70s; the Hogwood / Gardiner / Pinnock / Brüggen Historically Informed Performance revolution of the 1970s-90s; the Uchida / Perahia / Barenboim / Pires modern-instrument reference tradition; and the contemporary readings of Savall, Koopman, Currentzis, and others.

The recording history has shaped how Mozart sounds to modern listeners. The pre-HIP modern-instrument tradition produced a Mozart of warm strings, vibrato-rich orchestral tone, broader tempos, and architectural weight — a Mozart in the late-Romantic performing tradition's terms. The HIP revolution produced a different Mozart: leaner orchestral textures, period-instrument timbres (natural horns, gut strings, basset clarinets), faster tempos closer to the original metronome marks, less vibrato. Modern listeners often have heard both traditions and may have strong preferences; both are legitimate ways of receiving Mozart's music, and the choice between them is partly aesthetic preference and partly historical-philosophical position about what "authentic" performance means.

Chapter 21 will trace the major lineages of Mozart performance from the 1900s through 2026, identify the principal conductors and performers in each lineage, and discuss how the different traditions have shaped the Mozart we hear today. The chapter will compare multiple recordings of the same works (K. 550 and K. 488 and Figaro Act II finale are good case-study works) to demonstrate how different performance traditions produce different Mozart experiences from the same scores. The chapter is structurally the parallel to this one — both are reception chapters, looking at Mozart through different reception modes.

The bridge also names the principal historiographical question: how does a listener decide which performance tradition gives the most-authentic Mozart? The answer is that there is no single most-authentic Mozart; the various traditions give different Mozarts, and the listener's choice depends on what kind of Mozart experience the listener wants. The chapter will engage this question through specific case studies of multiple recordings of the same works.

What does it mean for a culture to have two parallel pictures of a historical figure, the scholarly and the dramatic, that both persist? Is the _Amadeus_ -film picture harmful to Mozart's actual reception, neutral to it, or beneficial in some way (by keeping Mozart culturally alive)?"_

Why has the cultural memory shifted so completely toward the _Amadeus_ -film image? What does the shift tell you about how cultural narratives form and persist?"_

Is uncertainty a satisfying intellectual position, or do you find yourself wanting to take a side even when the evidence does not support one?"_

What would have to happen culturally for Salieri's music to enter mainstream performance?
