### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

This is a biographical / historiographical chapter rather than a music-focused chapter; there is no specific anchor work to re-listen to. The listening exercise is a comparative session that engages the Salieri-versus-Mozart contrast directly:

Listen to Mozart's _Der Schauspieldirektor_ K. 486 (the German _Singspiel_ of February 1786) and Salieri's _Prima la musica e poi le parole_ (the Italian one-act of February 1786) — the dual commission for the Schönbrunn court event of February 7, 1786. Both works run about thirty to forty minutes; both were composed for the same court event; both were performed at the same court audience. The comparison is the clearest direct case of how the two composers' work sounded side by side in the same compositional moment.

Standard recordings of _Der Schauspieldirektor_ : Sir Charles Mackerras with the Scottish Chamber Orchestra (Telarc); Karl Böhm with the Berlin Philharmonic.

Standard recordings of _Prima la musica e poi le parole_ : René Jacobs / Concerto Köln (Harmonia Mundi); various smaller-label recordings.

The comparison is illuminating: both works are competent, both are theatrically effective, both reflect the same Vienna-court compositional environment of February 1786. Salieri's work is more Italian-tradition in its vocal writing and dramatic conventions; Mozart's is more German-tradition. Neither work is among the principal compositional achievements of its composer; both are occasional pieces. Hearing them side by side gives you a direct sense of the actual Salieri-Mozart comparison without the _Amadeus_ -film framing.

### Going Deeper

**Watch** : _Amadeus_ (Miloš Forman, 1984) if you have not seen it. The film is one of the great twentieth-century cinematic works and is the principal cultural artifact that this chapter has been engaging. Watching the film with the historical record in mind gives a different experience from watching it as straightforward biography; the dramatic power and the historical invention become visible simultaneously. Available on multiple streaming platforms; the Director's Cut version (2002, with restored scenes) runs about three hours.

**Read** : Volkmar Braunbehrens, _Maligned Master: The Real Story of Antonio Salieri_ (Fromm International, 1992; original German edition 1989). The principal scholarly study of Salieri's life and career. Braunbehrens lays out the historical record in detail and engages the _Amadeus_ -film inventions directly. About 280 pages. The book is the standard reference for any specific question about Salieri's life or about the Mozart-Salieri relationship.

---

## Chapter 21: Recording History — The Documented Mozart Performance Tradition 1900-2026

The recording history of Mozart's music constitutes a parallel reception alongside the scholarly and the cultural receptions covered in Chapters 19 and 20. The recording history tells us how Mozart has actually been heard over the past century-plus — what tempos, what orchestral sounds, what interpretive choices have shaped the modern listener's experience of the same scores. The history is rich; nearly every major conductor and instrumentalist of the past century has recorded Mozart, and the major works have been recorded hundreds of times in dozens of distinct performance traditions.

This chapter is a survey rather than an anchor-on-specific-work treatment; the goal is to give you a map of the recording landscape so that you can place any recommended recording within the broader tradition.

### 21.1 Before the Recording Era

Mozart's works were performed continuously from their composition through the nineteenth century in Vienna, Prague, Berlin, Leipzig, Paris, and London. Mahler's Mozart at the Vienna Court Opera (1897-1907) — longer-line phrasing, careful orchestral balance, dramatic timing — shaped the next generation of Vienna conductors who would make the early recordings. The earliest recorded Mozart (cylinder and 78-rpm disc from ~1900, principal figures Lilli Lehmann, Nellie Melba, Adelina Patti for arias; Karl Muck, Felix Weingartner for orchestral repertoire) are now historic curiosities — worth knowing as the start of the recorded tradition but displaced by subsequent developments.

### 21.2 The Mid-Century Tradition (1930s-1950s)

Electrical recording (introduced 1925-26) made the recordings from the 1930s onward listenable by modern standards. Key figures:

* **Bruno Walter** (1876-1962): Mahler's former assistant at the Vienna Court Opera; the principal Vienna-tradition Mozart conductor across Vienna Philharmonic (1930s), New York Philharmonic, and Columbia Symphony Orchestra (late 1950s) recordings. Warm orchestral tone, expansive tempos, dramatic timing inherited from late-Romantic conducting. His K. 550, _Figaro_ , and _Don Giovanni_ are the principal twentieth-century Vienna references.
* **Wilhelm Furtwängler** (1886-1954): the German tradition — broad tempos, long structural arc, dramatically weighted, sometimes criticized as too heavy for Mozart. _Don Giovanni_ (Salzburg, 1953-54, EMI), _Magic Flute_ (1949, EMI).
* **Otto Klemperer** (1885-1973): the monumental tradition; slow, architecturally weighted Mozart with the Philharmonia Orchestra (late 1950s-60s). K. 551 "Jupiter" (EMI, early 1960s) is the central Klemperer Mozart reference.
* **Erich Kleiber** (1890-1956): _Figaro_ / Vienna Philharmonic (Decca, 1955), a historic _Figaro_ still in regular circulation.

The mid-century Mozart in retrospect: Mozart heard through the late-Romantic performing lens — vibrato-rich strings, full modern symphony forces, Mozart-as-proto-Beethoven.

### 21.3 Modern-Instrument Tradition (1960s-2000s)

The post-Klemperer modern-instrument tradition consolidated with faster tempos, leaner textures, and increased attention to the late-Classical character — but still modern orchestras, modern instruments, reduced but not eliminated vibrato. Key figures:

* **Karl Böhm** (1894-1981): the principal Vienna-tradition Mozart conductor of the 1960s-70s. _Magic Flute_ (DG, 1964), _Figaro_ (DG, 1968), _Don Giovanni_ (DG, 1977) are mid-century opera references.
* **George Szell** (1897-1970) / Cleveland: precision and clarity; the K. 551 and K. 543 are standard.
* **Sir Colin Davis** (1927-2013): British modern-instrument warmth with rhythmic alertness; covers most of the repertoire on Philips.
* **Sir Neville Marriner** (1924-2016) / Academy of St Martin in the Fields: one of the most complete modern-instrument cycles in the catalog (Philips, Decca; many concerto cycles with Brendel as soloist).
* **Riccardo Muti** (b. 1941): the Italian operatic-conducting tradition; the Da Ponte operas plus _La clemenza_ on EMI, 1980s-90s.
* **Sir Charles Mackerras** (1925-2010): the hybrid — HIP-aware tempos and phrasing on modern instruments. The symphony cycle with the Scottish Chamber Orchestra (Telarc, 1986) and _Don Giovanni_ (Telarc, 1994) are leading hybrid-tradition references.
* **Mitsuko Uchida** (b. 1948): the leading modern-instrument Mozart pianist. The complete concerto cycle with Jeffrey Tate / English Chamber Orchestra (Philips, 1985-91) and the sonata cycle are the contemporary modern-instrument piano references. Clean, structurally clear, unfussy.
* Other principal pianists: **Murray Perahia** (concerto cycle with the ECO, Sony, 1970s-80s); **Alfred Brendel** (warmer, more expressive than Uchida); **Maria João Pires** (lyric); **Daniel Barenboim** (player-conductor cycle); **András Schiff** (sonata cycle, Decca 1990s).

### 21.4 The Historically Informed Performance Revolution (1970s-1990s)

The HIP movement extended earlier-music performance practice into the Classical period in the 1970s. Applied to Mozart: period instruments (gut strings, natural horns, fortepiano not modern piano, basset clarinet for K. 581/622); reduced forces (Mozart's Vienna orchestra had 8-12 first violins, not 16+); faster tempos closer to the original treatises; reduced vibrato; original cadenzas (or new ones in Mozart's style). Key figures:

* **Christopher Hogwood** (1941-2014) / Academy of Ancient Music (founded 1973): the principal founder; symphony cycle, the piano concerto cycle with Robert Levin, chamber and sacred music (L'Oiseau-Lyre).
* **John Eliot Gardiner** (b. 1943) / English Baroque Soloists / Monteverdi Choir: dramatic vitality; late symphonies, the Da Ponte trilogy plus _Idomeneo_ and _Magic Flute_ , the Requiem.
* **Trevor Pinnock** (b. 1946) / English Concert: leaner than Hogwood (Archiv, 1980s-90s).
* **Frans Brüggen** (1934-2014) / Orchestra of the Eighteenth Century: dramatically intense symphonies (Philips).
* **Nikolaus Harnoncourt** (1929-2016): HIP-informed phrasing on modern orchestras (Teldec, Sony) — the chamber-music balance with full symphonic forces.
* **Robert Levin** (b. 1947): the principal HIP fortepianist; complete piano concerto cycle with Hogwood / AAM, period instrument plus Levin's own period-style cadenzas. Also the alternative Requiem completion (Chapter 18).
* **Malcolm Bilson** (b. 1935): fortepianist; solo sonata recordings (Hungaroton).

The HIP movement transformed the sound of Mozart: leaner, more rhythmically alert, more transparently scored, with period-instrument timbres. The pre-HIP modern-instrument tradition and the HIP tradition coexist in the modern catalog.

### 21.5 The Contemporary Era (2000s-2020s)

By the 2000s the two traditions had largely converged toward HIP-informed performance practice. Most contemporary Mozart recordings, whether on period or modern instruments, share faster tempos, leaner textures, and attention to original cadenzas. Key contemporary figures:

* **René Jacobs** (b. 1946) / Freiburger Barockorchester / Akademie für Alte Musik Berlin / Concerto Köln: the principal contemporary HIP opera conductor (Harmonia Mundi); dramatically intense and detailed, engaging scholarly debates directly.
* **Claudio Abbado** (1933-2014) / Mahler Chamber Orchestra / Orchestra Mozart Bologna: lyric, structurally light, hybrid (DG, 2000s-10s).
* **Teodor Currentzis** (b. 1972) / MusicAeterna: dramatically extreme HIP — among the fastest tempos and sharpest dynamic contrasts in the catalog. The Da Ponte trilogy on Sony divides listeners.
* **Jordi Savall** (b. 1941), **Ton Koopman** (b. 1944), **William Christie** (b. 1944): further contemporary HIP-tradition Mozart on various labels.

The pre-HIP tradition of Walter, Furtwängler, Klemperer is now a historic tradition rather than current practice; listeners who want the older sound seek out the historic recordings.

### 21.6 Case Study: K. 550 First Movement Across the Traditions

The clearest way to hear what these performance traditions actually mean is to compare them on the same music. The first movement of K. 550 (Symphony No. 40 in G minor, Chapter 10's principal anchor) has been recorded hundreds of times. Across the major traditions, the first-movement timings cluster as follows:

Recording | Tempo (first movement) | Tradition  
---|---|---  
Otto Klemperer / Philharmonia (EMI, 1962) | ~9:00 | Monumental mid-century  
Karl Böhm / Berlin Philharmonic (DG, 1962) | ~7:45 | Mid-century Vienna  
Bruno Walter / Columbia SO (Sony, 1959) | ~7:30 | Vienna-tradition warmth  
George Szell / Cleveland (Sony, 1961) | ~7:30 | Precision and clarity  
Sir Charles Mackerras / SCO (Telarc, 1986) | ~7:00 | HIP-aware modern-instrument  
John Eliot Gardiner / EBS (Philips, 1990) | ~6:45 | Period-instrument HIP  
Teodor Currentzis / MusicAeterna (Sony, 2014) | ~6:15 | Contemporary HIP-extreme  
  
That is nearly three minutes of difference between Klemperer and Currentzis — almost half again as long at the slow end. The numbers alone do not tell you what is at stake; the textural and tonal differences do.

**What you hear in Klemperer (~9:00).** The opening violin figure — the famous nervous semiquaver pattern over the violas' rocking eighth-notes — comes at you in slow motion. Each repetition of the figure is a separate event you can hear and weigh; the harmonic surface unfolds at the pace of a Beethovenian symphony. The Philharmonia strings play with continuous vibrato, full bow, and orchestral weight that approaches the heft Klemperer brought to the Eroica. The G-minor mood is anguish at length: a long, sustained, grieving reading. Critics who say the Klemperer is "not Mozart" mean that the tempo and the weight push past what late-Classical orchestral writing was sized for. Listeners who defend it say it lets you hear the structural architecture in a way fast readings never do.

**What you hear in Walter, Böhm, Szell (~7:30-7:45).** The reading is still warm and still modern-orchestral, but the semiquaver figure is recognizably a nervous, agitated gesture rather than a slow lament. The harmonic motion has forward propulsion; you experience the movement as a piece going somewhere, not as a meditation on grief. Walter's expansive phrasing and Böhm's slightly tighter line are detectable but in the same world; Szell's Cleveland readings tighten the rhythm further. This is the Mozart most listeners over forty grew up with on LP.

**What you hear in Gardiner / Mackerras (~6:45-7:00).** The strings give up much of their vibrato; the gut-string sound (Gardiner) or the leaner modern-string sound (Mackerras) lets you hear the inner-voice motion much more clearly. The natural horns in Gardiner's reading have a thinner, more pungent timbre than valved modern horns. The semiquaver figure feels urgent — almost panicked — rather than melancholy. The chamber-music balance brings the winds into audible parity with the strings. The forces are smaller (Mozart's own Vienna orchestra was 8-12 first violins, not the 16+ of Klemperer's Philharmonia); the texture is correspondingly transparent.

**What you hear in Currentzis (~6:15).** The opening is almost violent. The semiquaver figure is hammered out; the dynamic contrasts are extreme (the loud passages much louder than the soft, the soft much quieter); the rhythmic articulation is razor-sharp. The G-minor mood becomes terror rather than grief or anguish. Listeners who admire Currentzis say this is what a Mozart symphony actually sounded like in 1788 to an audience used to the gallant style — a shock. Listeners who reject it say the extremity is a twenty-first-century interpretive overlay, not a recovery of anything historical.

**What is at stake.** The same notes produce a slow lament (Klemperer), a warm modern-symphonic statement (Walter, Böhm), an agitated period-instrument urgency (Gardiner), or a dramatic confrontation (Currentzis). Each reading is a legitimate interpretation of the score; none is the "correct" Mozart. The listener's choice is partly aesthetic preference and partly a position on a long-running argument about whether late-eighteenth-century music should sound how late-nineteenth-century conductors found it expressive to make it sound, or how the score and the contemporary treatises suggest it once sounded. The K. 550 first movement is the place where the argument is most audible.

### 21.7 How to Build Your Mozart Recording Collection

For listeners building a Mozart recording collection, the practical guidance:

**Start with one strong reference cycle for each major genre.** For the piano concertos, the Mitsuko Uchida / Jeffrey Tate cycle (Philips, 1985-91) is the most-recommended starting point. For the symphonies, the Sir Charles Mackerras / Scottish Chamber Orchestra cycle (Telarc, 1986) is a strong starting point. For the operas, the Karl Böhm complete operas series (Deutsche Grammophon, 1960s-70s) or the Sir John Eliot Gardiner HIP operas series (Archiv / Philips, 1990s) are both standard. For the chamber music, the Hagen Quartett string quartet cycle (Deutsche Grammophon, 1980s-90s) is the leading modern reference.

**Add one HIP cycle to compare.** Once you have a modern-instrument reference cycle, adding a HIP cycle for comparison is illuminating. The Robert Levin / Christopher Hogwood piano concertos (L'Oiseau-Lyre / Decca, 1990s), the John Eliot Gardiner operas (Archiv, 1990s), or the Quatuor Mosaïques string quartets (Astrée / Naïve, 1990s) provide the HIP alternatives.

**Acquire selected historic recordings.** For the historical depth, a few recordings from the mid-century tradition are worth knowing — the Walter K. 550 (Sony, 1959), the Klemperer K. 551 (EMI, early 1960s), the Furtwängler _Don Giovanni_ (EMI, 1953-54), the Erich Kleiber _Figaro_ (Decca, 1955), the Edwin Fischer concertos (EMI, 1930s). These are not the principal practical-listening repertoire but they constitute the historical tradition that the contemporary recordings emerged from.

**Engage individual standout recordings.** Some recordings are admired for specific qualities beyond the cycle's general character. The Clifford Curzon / Benjamin Britten K. 595 (Decca, 1970) is a benchmark single-concerto recording. The Anne-Sophie Mutter / Bruno Giuranna / Riccardo Muti K. 364 (EMI, 1983) is a standard reference. The Mitsuko Uchida K. 466 cadenza (Philips, 1986, using the Beethoven cadenza) is a standard. These individual standout recordings can complement the cycle-based collection.

### 21.8 The Search and Streaming Landscape

Streaming services (Spotify, Apple Music, Tidal, Qobuz) carry most of the standard Mozart catalog and many of the historic recordings; lossless tiers (Tidal HiFi, Apple Music lossless, Qobuz) provide audiophile-quality streaming. The search hints in this volume are calibrated to surface the recommended recordings as top results. Physical recordings (CDs, SACDs, LPs) from the major labels (Deutsche Grammophon, Decca, Philips, Sony, EMI, Harmonia Mundi, Naxos) remain widely available. Older public-domain recordings (Walter, Klemperer, Furtwängler, Erich Kleiber, early Vienna Philharmonic) are increasingly accessible on the Internet Archive and YouTube. The challenge has shifted from acquiring recordings to navigating the landscape; the recommendations in this chapter aim to provide that navigation.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The same K. 550 score produces radically different musical experiences in different performance traditions — from Klemperer's roughly 9:00 first movement to Currentzis's roughly 6:15. The tempo range alone spans nearly three minutes. The textural and tonal differences are equally substantial. What does this range tell you about the score as a musical object? Is the score under-determined (it permits multiple legitimate interpretations) or are most of these interpretations actually different works that share the same score?"_
* _"The Historically Informed Performance revolution of the 1970s-90s transformed the sound of Mozart's music in modern performance. The pre-HIP modern-instrument tradition (Walter, Furtwängler, Klemperer, Böhm) and the HIP tradition (Hogwood, Gardiner, Pinnock, Levin) produce different Mozart experiences. Are there things the pre-HIP tradition reveals about Mozart that the HIP tradition obscures? Are there things the HIP tradition reveals that the pre-HIP tradition obscured?"_
* _"Robert Levin's complete piano concerto cycle with Christopher Hogwood (L'Oiseau-Lyre / Decca, 1990s) uses period fortepiano and Levin's own period-style cadenzas — the cycle is the most fully period-instrument and period-style realization of the Mozart concertos available. Mitsuko Uchida's cycle with Jeffrey Tate (Philips, 1985-91) is the modern-instrument reference cycle. Listen to one concerto in both cycles. What does each performance tradition reveal that the other obscures? Which tradition do you find more dramatically convincing?"_
* _"The Teodor Currentzis Mozart recordings are dramatically extreme — among the fastest tempos, the sharpest dynamic contrasts, the most vivid orchestral textures in the contemporary catalog. The recordings divide listeners. What is at stake in the dramatic-extreme HIP approach? Is the extremity a recovery of the original Mozart dramatic intent, or is it a contemporary interpretive overlay that the score does not require?"_
* _"The recording landscape's accessibility has been transformed by streaming and digital archives. A contemporary listener can access hundreds of Mozart recordings; the LP-era listener typically had access to dozens. What does the accessibility do to the listener's relationship with the music? Does easy access deepen engagement or fragment it? Has the recording-collecting impulse been replaced by playlist-curating in ways that change how Mozart is heard?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

This is a survey chapter rather than an anchor-on-specific-work treatment. The listening exercise is the comparative session described in §21.6:

Choose K. 550 (or another work from the volume's anchor list — K. 488, _Figaro_ Act II finale, the Requiem Lacrimosa are all good case-study works) and listen to two or three different recordings in succession. Pick one from the mid-century modern-instrument tradition (Böhm, Klemperer, Walter), one from the late-twentieth-century modern-instrument tradition (Mackerras, Uchida-Tate), and one from the HIP tradition (Gardiner, Pinnock, Hogwood). Listen for:

  1. **Tempo** : how much does the first-movement tempo vary across the three recordings? Note the differences in seconds or minutes.

  2. **Orchestral tone** : how different are the orchestral sonorities? The vibrato in the strings, the brightness of the winds, the heaviness of the timpani — what differs across the three?

  3. **Phrasing and dynamic shading** : where do the conductors place the phrase shapes? How do they handle the dynamic contrasts?

  4. **Overall impression** : which reading do you find most dramatically convincing? Which reading do you find most structurally clear? Which reading do you find most idiomatically Mozart?

The exercise will deepen your sense of the recording landscape and clarify your own preferences in Mozart performance.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : a comparative case study on the Requiem. The Karl Böhm Vienna Philharmonic recording (Deutsche Grammophon, 1971), the Christopher Hogwood Academy of Ancient Music recording (L'Oiseau-Lyre / Decca, 1984), and the Helmuth Rilling Stuttgart Bach Collegium recording of the Levin completion (Hänssler, 1994) provide three different performance traditions and two different completion versions. Listening to the Lacrimosa in all three gives a concentrated case study of how the same compositional moment sounds in different performance and editorial traditions.

**Read** : David Hurwitz, _The Mahler Symphonies: An Owner's Manual_ (Amadeus Press, 2004) — not specifically a Mozart book, but a model of the kind of recording-comparison criticism that Hurwitz applies to Mozart in his various reviews for ClassicsToday.com and elsewhere. Hurwitz is the leading contemporary recording-comparison critic in the Western classical-music tradition; his website and his various publications provide ongoing engagement with the Mozart recording landscape. About 300 pages; the analytical model is transferable to Mozart listening.

---

## Chapter 22: The Documented Death of Mozart — December 5, 1791

Wolfgang Amadeus Mozart died in Vienna in the early hours of December 5, 1791 — contemporary accounts place his death shortly after midnight, with later biographers (Nissen, drawing on Constanze's memory) giving "about one in the morning." He was thirty-five years and ten months old. The death was preceded by approximately two weeks of acute illness; the funeral took place at St. Stephen's Cathedral on December 7, 1791, followed by burial at the St. Marx Cemetery in Vienna's southeast suburb. The cause of death has been argued over by medical historians for two centuries; multiple modern diagnostic theories exist, none definitively established.

This chapter traces the documented final months of Mozart's life — the September Magic Flute completion, the October Clarinet Concerto K. 622 completion, the autumn Requiem fragments, the late-November onset of illness, the early-December death, the December 7 funeral, the immediate aftermath. The chapter is biographical and is the volume's principal biographical convergence point. It is also the volume's principal place to address the various contested questions about the death — the cause-of-death theories, the third-class burial reality versus the "pauper's grave" romance, the early-rumor and later-embroidery layers of biographical accumulation.

The chapter is approximately 4,500 words.

### 22.1 The Final Months — Autumn 1791

Mozart's autumn 1791 was professionally productive and personally complicated. The principal compositional events:

**September 1791: Magic Flute completion.** Magic Flute K. 620 was completed in late September 1791 and premiered at the Freihaus-Theater auf der Wieden on September 30, 1791 (Chapter 14). Mozart conducted the premiere; the popular success was immediate and significant. Across October 1791, the opera was performed twenty times at the Freihaus-Theater; Mozart attended at least one performance (the October 7, 1791 attendance, mentioned in his letter to Constanze from Vienna while Constanze was at Baden taking the waters).

**Early October 1791: Clarinet Concerto K. 622 completion.** The Clarinet Concerto K. 622 was completed in early October 1791 for Anton Stadler. The work belongs to the late-style transparency cluster (with K. 595, K. 581, Magic Flute) and is the last orchestral concerto Mozart completed. Stadler took the K. 622 to Prague, where he performed it on October 16, 1791 — the premiere occurred in Prague rather than Vienna, with Stadler as soloist.

**September 6, 1791:_La clemenza di Tito_ premiere in Prague.** _La clemenza di Tito_ K. 621, the opera Mozart composed for the Bohemian coronation festival celebrating Emperor Leopold II's coronation as King of Bohemia, premiered at the National Theater in Prague on September 6, 1791. The opera was composed in great haste (approximately three weeks of composition) and is a return to the opera seria genre that Mozart had not used since _Idomeneo_ of 1781. The work was a moderate success at Prague; it has had a partial twentieth-century revival but remains less central than the Da Ponte trilogy or Magic Flute in the standard repertoire.

**Autumn 1791: Requiem composition.** The Requiem K. 626 commission from Count Walsegg (Chapter 18) had arrived in summer 1791. The composition continued through autumn 1791, alongside Magic Flute and the Clarinet Concerto and the Prague trip. The Requiem was unfinished at Mozart's death; the chronology and the surviving sketches give partial evidence of how the composition progressed.

**October-November 1791: continued teaching and household life.** Mozart continued his teaching of pupils across the autumn; the household income from teaching was steady. Mozart's surviving correspondence from October-November 1791 (the letters to Constanze in Baden, the letters to Anton Stadler in Prague) shows him in normal professional mode — composing, teaching, attending performances, managing the household economy. The illness that would kill him began in late November; the early-November Mozart was not yet ill.

**November 20, 1791: onset of acute illness.** Approximately November 20, 1791, Mozart developed an acute illness. The principal symptoms (as recorded by Constanze, Sophie Haibel, and the attending physician Thomas Closset): swelling of the extremities, particularly the hands and feet, with severe pain and inability to move; fever; vomiting; eventual paralysis affecting the body. The illness was severe from its onset and progressed rapidly.

**November 28-December 4, 1791: bedridden.** Mozart was bedridden for the final week of his life. The Mozart family circle included Constanze (his wife), Sophie Haibel (Constanze's younger sister, who came to the household to help with care), Karl Thomas Mozart (the seven-year-old elder son), Franz Xaver Wolfgang Mozart (the four-month-old younger son), and the attending physician Thomas Closset. Süssmayr (Mozart's pupil) visited; other friends visited; Mozart received visitors when he was able.

**December 4, 1791: late afternoon and evening.** The evening of December 4 was the principal documented Requiem moment. According to Sophie Haibel's later recollection (recorded in correspondence with Friedrich Rochlitz in the 1820s), Mozart asked for the Requiem score to be brought to him; he sang through the Lacrimosa with a small group of friends and pupils (Süssmayr was present; Benedikt Schack (the tenor who had created the role of Tamino in Magic Flute) sang the soprano part; Franz Xaver Gerl (the bass who had created the role of Sarastro) sang the bass; Mozart sang the alto part as far as the eight bars he had completed; he wept when the singing reached the end of his composition; the group could not continue because Mozart was overcome). The Sophie Haibel recollection is the principal source for the bedside Requiem scene. Solomon's treatment is the standard cautious one — the recollection is at thirty-plus years' remove and the specific details may have been shaped by Constanze's posthumous shaping of the Mozart image, but the broad outline (Mozart sang through the Requiem on his deathbed) is consistent with multiple accounts and is generally accepted.

**December 5, 1791, early morning hours.** The end came shortly after midnight (see opening). The attending physician Closset had been at the household earlier in the evening; he had returned home; Sophie Haibel sent for him when Mozart's condition deteriorated, but he arrived too late. Constanze and Sophie were with Mozart at the end; the death was apparently peaceful by the standards of the era's final illnesses.

### 22.2 The Cause of Death — The Contested Question

The cause of Mozart's death is a long-debated medical question. The principal modern diagnostic theories:

**Rheumatic fever**. The official diagnosis recorded by Thomas Closset was "hitziges Frieselfieber" — "acute miliary fever" or "high frieslefever," a term that referred to a rash-with-fever condition. Modern medical scholars have interpreted this in various ways; one principal interpretation is that Mozart had a recurrence of childhood rheumatic fever (Mozart had suffered repeated illnesses across his life, some of which may have been rheumatic-fever episodes) that produced cardiac complications and eventual heart failure. The rheumatic-fever theory is the principal modern medical interpretation and the diagnostic possibility twentieth-century scholarship returned to most often.

**Kidney failure / acute uremia**. Another principal theory is that Mozart suffered kidney failure (acute uremia) brought on by an underlying chronic kidney condition that the late-1791 acute illness pushed into terminal stage. The swelling of the extremities and the eventual paralysis are consistent with uremic complications; the rapid deterioration is consistent with kidney failure.

**Trichinosis**. A 2001 theory proposed that Mozart died of trichinosis — a parasitic infection from undercooked pork meat. The theory rests partly on Mozart's surviving correspondence (a letter from October 1791 mentions Mozart eating pork chops) and partly on the symptom pattern (the swelling, the muscle pain, the fever can all be consistent with trichinosis). The trichinosis theory has had limited acceptance in mainstream Mozart scholarship; the evidence is suggestive but not compelling.

**Poisoning (excluded by modern scholarship)**. The poisoning theory, popularized by the Salieri-as-poisoner narrative (Chapter 20), has been excluded by modern medical scholarship. The symptoms Mozart displayed do not match the symptom patterns of the principal toxic substances available in late-eighteenth-century Vienna (arsenic, mercury); the rapid deterioration would have been atypical for the slow-poisoning protocols suggested in the Salieri narrative; the medical evidence does not support poisoning as a cause of death. The poisoning rumor that emerged in the 1820s (after Salieri's mental decline) has no medical basis.

**Other theories** : scarlet fever, streptococcal infection, miliary tuberculosis, post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis, and various other infectious-disease and metabolic-condition theories have been proposed at various times. Each has some symptomatic support; none has the complete pattern that would make it definitively the answer.

The honest scholarly position: the cause of Mozart's death cannot be definitively established from the available evidence. The official diagnosis ("hitziges Frieselfieber") is too vague to be diagnostically useful by modern medical standards; the contemporary symptom descriptions are partial and filtered through eighteenth-century medical concepts; modern diagnostic possibilities are several, and no single theory accounts for all the evidence. The principal modern theories are rheumatic fever with cardiac complications and acute kidney failure, but neither is established as definitive.

The various theories represent reasonable scholarly attempts to apply modern medical knowledge to fragmentary eighteenth-century evidence; the uncertainty is not a sign of poor scholarship but of the genuine limitations of the available evidence. Mozart died of an acute illness whose specific medical nature cannot be determined.

### 22.3 The Funeral and Burial

The funeral on December 7, 1791 was at St. Stephen's Cathedral in Vienna, in the Crucifix Chapel (Kruzifixkapelle) on the cathedral's north side. The funeral was a third-class religious service — a common-rank service for a Vienna middle-class burial, not the formal high-rank service that would have been provided for an Imperial Kapellmeister or for an aristocratic patron.

After the service, the body was transferred for burial. The burial took place at the St. Marx Cemetery (St. Marxer Friedhof), a suburban cemetery in the southeast of Vienna established in the early 1780s as part of Emperor Joseph II's burial reforms. The burial was in a common grave (a Gemeinschaftsgrab) — a multi-person burial pit that was the standard third-class burial method under the Joseph II reforms. The body was placed in the common grave; the grave was not marked with an individual headstone (per the Joseph II reform protocols, individual grave markers were restricted to higher burial classes); after the legal seven-year period, the grave was reused for subsequent burials.

The "pauper's grave" characterization of Mozart's burial that appears in some popular biography is partly accurate and partly misleading. **Accurate** : the third-class burial in a common grave without individual marker is what occurred; this kind of burial was not the high-rank ceremonial burial that some popular biography assumes Mozart "should have" received as a major composer. **Misleading** : the third-class burial was the standard middle-class Vienna burial of 1791 under the Joseph II reforms, not a sign of poverty or social rejection. Most Vienna middle-class burials of the period followed the same protocol. The Mozart burial was not lavish but was not exceptional; it was the standard burial for a Vienna professional of his social rank.

The popular "pauper's grave" image — Mozart being unceremoniously dumped into an unmarked grave during a blizzard with no mourners present — is partly elaborated. The weather on December 7, 1791 was indeed cold but the contemporary accounts vary on whether there was a storm; the mourners at the cathedral service included family, friends, and pupils; the burial party itself was small (per the Joseph II reform protocols, mourners did not typically accompany the body to the burial site) but this was the standard practice and not specific to Mozart. The dramatic "abandoned-genius-buried-in-storm" image is later elaboration on the historical record.

The exact location of Mozart's burial within the St. Marx Cemetery was lost after the common grave was reused in 1798-99. The 1855 effort to identify the burial location (a stone was placed at the supposed grave site by the Vienna cemetery authority) was speculative; the current "Mozart grave" monument at St. Marx Cemetery (a stone angel weeping over a memorial column) is a memorial rather than the actual burial site. The actual burial location is unknown.

### 22.4 The Immediate Aftermath

The immediate aftermath of Mozart's death was managed by Constanze and the Mozart family circle. The principal events:

**Constanze's grief and recovery.** Constanze was reportedly devastated by Mozart's death. She withdrew from public life for several weeks; her sister Sophie Haibel and the family friend Baron Gottfried van Swieten managed the immediate household needs. The financial situation was uncertain — the household had limited liquid assets and considerable debts; the income streams (teaching, composition commissions, Magic Flute performances) needed to be reorganized around Mozart's absence.

**The Requiem completion.** Constanze commissioned Joseph Eybler and then Franz Xaver Süssmayr to complete the Requiem (Chapter 18). The completion was needed to deliver the work to Walsegg and to secure the commission payment; the financial pressure was real and immediate. Süssmayr completed the work in 1792; the Süssmayr completion was delivered to Walsegg, who performed it at his estate in 1793 as his own composition (per Walsegg's anonymity habit).

**The publication and the benefit concerts.** Constanze organized benefit concerts in Vienna to raise funds for the Mozart children and to consolidate Mozart's posthumous reputation. The first major Vienna benefit concert took place December 28, 1791 (just over three weeks after the death) and included performances of Mozart's works. Subsequent benefit concerts across 1792-93 included the first Vienna public performance of the completed Requiem. The publication of Mozart's works also accelerated; the publishers Artaria and Hoffmeister increased their Mozart output in the years immediately after his death, contributing to the rapid posthumous canonization.

**The early biography.** Franz Xaver Niemetschek's _Leben des K. K. Kapellmeisters Wolfgang Gottlieb Mozart_ (the first Mozart biography, first edition 1798, expanded second edition 1808) was based on Niemetschek's personal acquaintance with the Mozart family and his interviews with Constanze and others. The Niemetschek biography is the principal early-source biographical document; later biographies (Nissen's 1828 work, the various nineteenth-century biographies) all draw on Niemetschek as a principal source. Niemetschek's biography also contains some semi-legendary anecdotes (the Joseph II "too many notes" line, the Allegri _Miserere_ story in expanded form) that have been treated with caution by modern scholars.

**Constanze's second marriage.** Constanze married Georg Nikolaus von Nissen (1761-1826), a Danish diplomat resident in Vienna, in 1809 — eighteen years after Mozart's death. Nissen compiled a substantial Mozart biography that was published in 1828 (two years after Nissen's death); the Nissen biography is one of the principal early-nineteenth-century sources and incorporates much material that Constanze and the Mozart family circle provided. Constanze died in 1842 at age eighty.

**The Mozart children.** Karl Thomas Mozart (1784-1858) became a civil servant in the Habsburg administration; he did not pursue a musical career. Franz Xaver Wolfgang Mozart (1791-1844) became a composer and pianist (sometimes performing under the name Wolfgang Amadeus Mozart Jr.); his career was respected but never reached the level of his father's reputation. Neither son had children; the Mozart family line died out with Franz Xaver Wolfgang's death in 1844.

### 22.5 The Posthumous Canonization

Mozart's posthumous reputation grew rapidly across the 1790s and into the nineteenth century. The principal stages:

**1791-1800: immediate reception.** The Magic Flute's popular success continued after Mozart's death; the opera was performed across Europe through the 1790s. The Requiem (in the Süssmayr completion) entered the standard sacred-music repertoire by the mid-1790s. The piano concertos and symphonies were performed in major European cities. By 1800, Mozart was already understood as one of the principal composers of the late-eighteenth-century classical tradition.

**Early nineteenth century: canonical establishment.** Beethoven's increasing prominence after 1800 placed Mozart in the dual-Classical-canon framework (Mozart and Haydn as the principal late-eighteenth-century composers; Beethoven as their immediate successor and the bridge to the nineteenth century). The Mozart canon was secure by 1820; the Mozart repertoire was an established core of the European concert and operatic life.

**Mid-to-late nineteenth century: Romantic-era reception.** The Romantic-era composers (Schumann, Liszt, Wagner, Brahms, Tchaikovsky) all engaged with Mozart's music as the classical foundation against which their own composition was measured. The Romantic-era performance tradition shaped Mozart's late-nineteenth-century reception; the warm-toned, vibrato-rich, expansive-tempo Mozart of the Bruno Walter tradition emerged from this background.

**Twentieth century: the scholarly and performance traditions.** The twentieth century produced both the modern scholarly Mozart literature (Eric Blom's biographies, the Köchel catalog revisions, the Solomon-Sadie-Eisen scholarly tradition) and the modern performance traditions covered in Chapter 21. The HIP revolution of the 1970s-90s transformed the sound of Mozart performance.

**Late twentieth century: the _Amadeus_ -film cultural moment.** The Peter Shaffer / Miloš Forman _Amadeus_ (1979 play, 1984 film) brought Mozart to mass audiences with a dramatic biographical narrative that shaped popular reception for two generations (Chapter 20).

**Twenty-first century: continued vitality.** Mozart remains a constant presence in the standard repertoire — orchestras, opera houses, and chamber societies program him as routinely as any composer in the literature. The Köchel catalog revisions, the continued scholarly work, the contemporary recording landscape, and the ongoing operatic productions all sustain Mozart's central position in Western art music. The bicentennial year 1991 (the 200th anniversary of Mozart's death) and the 250th-birthday year 2006 produced substantial Mozart-related publications, performances, and recordings; the year 2026 marks 270 years since Mozart's birth.

The posthumous canonization is one of the most successful in Western musical history. Mozart's reputation has never significantly declined across 234 years (counting from his death in 1791 to 2026); his music has remained central to the operatic and concert repertoires through every successive performance tradition; his cultural prominence has only grown across the twentieth and twenty-first centuries.

### 22.6 The Documentary Record

What we know about Mozart comes from several documentary streams:

**Mozart's own letters** : approximately four hundred surviving letters across his life, principally to Leopold Mozart (the bulk of the correspondence, especially through 1787) and to Constanze (the later years, especially during Mozart's travel periods of 1789-91). The Spaethling edition (Mozart's Letters, Mozart's Life, 2000) is the principal modern English-language collection. The letters are the principal first-person source for Mozart's compositional process, professional life, and personal circumstances.

**Leopold Mozart's letters** : the parallel correspondence from Mozart's father across the 1760s-80s. Leopold's letters to Anna Maria Mozart (during the tours when Anna Maria was at home in Salzburg) and to Nannerl Mozart (after Anna Maria's death and across Leopold's later years) provide the parallel paternal perspective on Mozart's life.

**The autograph manuscripts** : most of Mozart's compositions survive in autograph manuscripts that are now held by various European libraries (the Berlin State Library has the largest single collection; the Vienna Hofbibliothek and the British Library have substantial holdings; Salzburg, Prague, and other cities have smaller collections). The autographs are the principal compositional source.

**Mozart's own compositional catalog** : Mozart maintained a personal catalog of his works from February 1784 onward (the _Verzeichnüss aller meiner Werke_ — "catalog of all my works"). The catalog records the composition date and instrumentation of each work and is one of the principal sources for dating Mozart's compositions. The catalog is preserved at the British Library.

**Contemporary accounts** : the surviving Niemetschek biography (1798/1808), the Nissen biography (1828), the Sophie Haibel and Constanze Mozart recollections (as filtered through later sources), the Rochlitz anecdote collections (early nineteenth century), the various contemporary newspaper reviews and concert announcements, the Vienna theatrical records, and other primary-source materials. These vary in reliability — the Niemetschek and Rochlitz collections contain some semi-legendary material that modern scholarship treats with caution; the Vienna theatrical and economic records are reliable but partial.

**Modern scholarship** : the principal modern scholarly works on Mozart's biography are Solomon's _Mozart: A Life_ (1995, the contemporary single-volume standard), Sadie's _Mozart: The Early Years, 1756-1781_ (Norton, posthumous 2006, the scholarly framework for the prodigy and Salzburg years), Eisen and Keefe's _Cambridge Mozart Encyclopedia_ (2006, the academic reference), Heartz's _Mozart, Haydn and Early Beethoven, 1781-1802_ (2009, the Vienna-period contextualizer), Wolff's _Mozart at the Gateway to His Fortune_ (2012, the late-period specialist), Spaethling's _Mozart's Letters, Mozart's Life_ (2000, the primary-source letters), and many other works. These constitute the modern scholarly framework on which Chapter 23's reading list is built.

### 22.7 The Final Picture

To summarize Mozart's death and its aftermath:

He died at thirty-five years old, at the height of his compositional powers, with major works in progress (the Requiem unfinished) and others recently completed (Magic Flute a popular success in Vienna, the Clarinet Concerto K. 622 newly composed, _La clemenza di Tito_ recently premiered in Prague). The death was sudden in the sense of being unexpected three weeks earlier; it was not sudden in the sense of being instantaneous (he was bedridden for about a week, ill for about two weeks).

The cause of death is genuinely uncertain. The principal modern theories (rheumatic fever with cardiac complications; acute kidney failure) are reasonable hypotheses but not definitively established. The poisoning theory is excluded by modern medical scholarship.

The funeral was a third-class religious service in St. Stephen's Cathedral and a common-grave burial at St. Marx Cemetery — the standard middle-class Vienna burial of 1791 under the Joseph II reforms. The "pauper's grave" image in popular biography is partly accurate (no individual headstone; reused common grave) and partly elaborated (the dramatic "abandoned-genius-in-storm" picture is later embroidery).

The immediate aftermath was managed by Constanze and the family circle. The Requiem was completed by Süssmayr for delivery to Walsegg; the benefit concerts and publications consolidated the financial situation and accelerated the posthumous canonization. The Niemetschek biography of 1798 and the Nissen biography of 1828 established the early biographical record; modern scholarship has built on and partly corrected the early sources.

Mozart's posthumous reputation has remained central in Western art music for 234 years and shows no signs of declining. His compositional output of 600-plus Köchel-numbered works in a life of thirty-five years constitutes one of the most extraordinary creative outputs in the history of Western music. The fifty central works that this volume has anchored on across twenty-two chapters represent the core of that catalog; the remaining 550 reward continued exploration but are not necessary for engaging the principal compositional achievement.

The reader who has worked through this volume now knows the Mozart whose life ended on December 5, 1791. The next and final chapter (Chapter 23) is the friend's final advice on where to go next — the reading list, the recordings to live with, the concert advice, the next steps in the Mozart listening life.

### 22.8 Bridge to Chapter 23

Chapter 23 closes the volume with the friend's final advice — the curated reading list, the "your first ten Mozart listens" starter list, the concert advice, and the preview of the next single-composer Close Listening volumes (Chopin, Bach, Schubert, Brahms, Tchaikovsky).

The bridge from Chapter 22 to Chapter 23: the death and its aftermath complete the biographical arc that has run across the volume from Chapter 3 (the prodigy years) through Chapter 22. The reader has lived through Mozart's thirty-five years in detail — the Salzburg prodigy, the touring with Leopold, the Salzburg court musician chafing under Colloredo, the 1781 Vienna break, the Vienna freelance peak, the Da Ponte operas, the late symphonies, the chamber peak, the Magic Flute, the Requiem, the death. The biographical arc has been the spine of the volume's structure; with Chapter 22 completing the arc, the volume is structurally complete on the biographical dimension.

Chapter 23 turns to the practical question of what to do next in your Mozart listening life. The chapter is brief and practical: where to read further (the recommended scholarly works), where to listen first (the "your first ten Mozart listens" starter list, the standard recordings for each), where to hear Mozart live (the Salzburg Festival as the standard pilgrimage; the major opera houses' Mozart productions), and how the Socratopia listening journey continues from here (the next single-composer Close Listening volumes; the broader Close Listening series).

The chapter is the friend's final word. The reader has worked through twenty-two chapters of substantive Mozart engagement; the friend's role at the end is to point toward the continuing journey rather than to add more material to the immediate engagement. The bridge to Chapter 23 is therefore practical and forward-looking — the volume has done its work; the reader has the equipment to continue independently; the friend's last advice is the curated next steps.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The cause of Mozart's death is genuinely uncertain. The principal modern theories (rheumatic fever with cardiac complications; acute kidney failure; possibly trichinosis) are reasonable but not definitively established. The poisoning theory is excluded by modern medical scholarship. How do you hold a biographical question where the medical evidence is genuinely fragmentary and the cause of death cannot be determined? Does the uncertainty about the death distract from engaging with the life and music, or does it make Mozart more historically real?"_
* _"The third-class burial at St. Marx Cemetery in 1791 was the standard middle-class Vienna burial under the Joseph II reforms. The 'pauper's grave' image in popular biography is partly accurate and partly elaborated. Why does the dramatic 'abandoned-genius-in-storm' picture persist in popular reception even when corrected by modern scholarship? What does the persistence tell you about how cultural narratives form around famous deaths?"_
* _"The Sophie Haibel recollection of the December 4, 1791 deathbed Requiem singing — Mozart singing through the Lacrimosa with Süssmayr, Schack, and Gerl, weeping when the singing reached the end of his composition, the group unable to continue — is one of the passages biographers reach for whenever they reconstruct Mozart's final days. The recollection is at thirty-plus years' remove and may have been shaped by Constanze's posthumous shaping of the Mozart image. How do you hold a primary source that is partly reliable and partly retrospectively shaped?"_
* _"Mozart died at thirty-five years old with major works in progress (the Requiem unfinished) and others recently completed (Magic Flute, the Clarinet Concerto K. 622,_La clemenza di Tito_). The compositional output was at its height; the death was not a slow decline but a sudden interruption. What would have followed in Mozart's catalog had he lived another twenty years (to age fifty-five, the age at which Beethoven was completing the Missa Solemnis and the late string quartets)? The counterfactual question is unanswerable in detail but is one of the principal what-if questions in Western musical history."_
* _"Mozart's posthumous reputation has remained central in Western art music for 234 years and shows no signs of declining. The compositional output of 600-plus Köchel-numbered works in a life of thirty-five years is one of the most extraordinary creative outputs in the history of Western music. What does it take for a composer's reputation to remain central for that long? What does Mozart's continued cultural vitality tell you about the music, the cultural reception, or both?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

This is a biographical chapter without a specific anchor work. The listening exercise is a chronological summary session that engages the biographical arc:

Listen to one work from each of the volume's major biographical periods:

  1. **Prodigy years (Chapter 3)** : _Exsultate, jubilate_ K. 165 (1773, Mozart age 17). Bartoli / Fischer recording.
  2. **Salzburg adolescent / young man (Chapter 4)** : Piano Concerto No. 9 "Jeunehomme" K. 271 (1777, age 21). Uchida / Tate recording.
  3. **Vienna freelance establishment (Chapter 5)** : _Die Entführung_ "Martern aller Arten" (1782, age 26). Gruberová / Böhm recording.
  4. **High Vienna peak (Chapter 7)** : Piano Concerto in D minor K. 466 (1785, age 29). Uchida / Tate recording with Beethoven cadenza.
  5. **Late symphonic peak (Chapter 10)** : Symphony No. 41 "Jupiter" K. 551 (1788, age 32). Mackerras / Scottish Chamber Orchestra recording.
  6. **Late style (Chapter 14)** : Magic Flute Sarastro "O Isis und Osiris" (1791, age 35, the last year). Solti / Vienna Philharmonic recording.

The six works span Mozart's life from age seventeen to age thirty-five. Listening to all six in sequence gives the fullest sense of the biographical arc — the compositional development from the prodigy work through the late-style transparency, the consistent compositional voice that runs through the developments, and the brevity of the life that produced this much music.

### Going Deeper

**Read** : Maynard Solomon, _Mozart: A Life_ (HarperCollins, 1995). The final chapters on Mozart's death and the immediate aftermath are the principal scholarly account. Solomon's treatment is balanced — engaging the cause-of-death question, the burial circumstances, the Constanze-managed aftermath, and the posthumous biography — without falling into either the romantic-tragedy reading or the corrective-dismissal reading. About fifty pages on the late period and the death; the full book is six hundred pages.

**Visit (if possible)** : the Mozart-Haus Vienna (Domgasse 5, in the historic center) — the apartment where Mozart lived from September 1784 to April 1787, including the composition of _Le nozze di Figaro_. The apartment has been preserved as a Mozart museum. The Mozart-Geburtshaus in Salzburg (the building where Mozart was born) and the Mozart-Wohnhaus in Salzburg (the family's later Salzburg residence) are also preserved as museums. If you travel to Vienna or Salzburg, these are the principal Mozart biographical sites; visiting them gives a physical dimension to the biographical engagement that reading alone cannot supply.

---

## Chapter 23: Where to Go Next — and the Mozart Recordings to Live With

We have walked through twenty-two chapters of substantive Mozart engagement together. You have lived inside Mozart's thirty-five years — the Salzburg prodigy, the touring family, the Salzburg court musician, the 1781 Vienna break, the Vienna freelance peak, the Da Ponte operas, the late symphonies, the chamber peak, the Magic Flute, the Requiem, the death. You have anchored on approximately fifty specific Mozart works across the catalog. You have absorbed the seven recurring compositional features that organize the catalog. You have engaged the Mozart-Salieri question and the recording-history reception. You now know Mozart in a way most listeners do not.

The friend's role at the end of the journey is to point toward the continuing journey — not to add more material to the immediate engagement, but to give you the equipment you need to continue your Mozart listening life independently. This final chapter is brief and practical: where to read further, where to listen first, where to hear Mozart live, and what is coming next in the Close Listening series.

### 23.1 The Recommended Reading List

The principal scholarly works on Mozart, in recommended reading order:

**Start with** : Robert Spaethling, _Mozart's Letters, Mozart's Life: Selected Letters_ (Norton, 2000). The clearest single-volume of Mozart in his own words. About 460 pages of letters; you do not need to read it cover to cover. The Spaethling edition is the principal primary-source entry point and the source this volume has drawn on most often.

**Then read** : Maynard Solomon, _Mozart: A Life_ (HarperCollins, 1995). The contemporary single-volume biographical standard. About six hundred pages; the principal modern biographical engagement. Solomon's treatment is balanced and scholarly; the book is the standard for any specific biographical question. The chapter coverage runs from Mozart's birth in 1756 through his death in 1791 with focused attention on the Leopold relationship, the Vienna freelance years, and the late period.

**Add** : Charles Rosen, _The Classical Style: Haydn, Mozart, Beethoven_ (Norton, 1971; expanded edition 1997). The analytical voice this volume has most often returned to. Chapter 5 ("Mozart") is the principal analytical treatment of Mozart's compositional style; the chapter is about ninety pages. Rosen writes for the literate non-specialist (no sheet music required); the chapter is the source-of-record for how to listen to Mozart's structural and harmonic language.

**Specialized reference** : Cliff Eisen and Simon P. Keefe (eds.), _The Cambridge Mozart Encyclopedia_ (Cambridge University Press, 2006). The academic reference for any specific question about Mozart's catalog or biography. About seven hundred pages; specific entries are typically two to ten pages. Consult it when a specific question arises rather than reading cover to cover.

**For the early years** : Stanley Sadie, _Mozart: The Early Years, 1756-1781_ (Norton, posthumous 2006). The scholarly framework for the prodigy and Salzburg years. About 650 pages; the principal source for any question about Mozart through age 25.

**For the Vienna years** : Daniel Heartz, _Mozart, Haydn and Early Beethoven, 1781-1802_ (Norton, 2009). The Vienna-period contextualizer. About 900 pages; the principal source for the Mozart-Haydn relationship, the Vienna chamber-music scene, and the operatic context of the Da Ponte trilogy.

**For the late years** : Christoph Wolff, _Mozart at the Gateway to His Fortune: Serving the Emperor, 1788-1791_ (Norton, 2012). The late-period specialist. About 280 pages; the principal source for the K. 595, K. 622, Magic Flute, Requiem cluster and the late-style transparency.

**For cultural context** : Robert W. Gutman, _Mozart: A Cultural Biography_ (Harcourt Brace, 1999). The cultural-context biographer. About 870 pages; the most-extended treatment of the cultural milieu in which Mozart's music was created and received.

**For the Requiem specifically** : Christoph Wolff, _Mozart's Requiem: Historical and Analytical Studies, Documents, Score_ (University of California Press, 1994). The principal scholarly treatment of the Requiem completion problem. About 280 pages.

**For Salieri** : Volkmar Braunbehrens, _Maligned Master: The Real Story of Antonio Salieri_ (Fromm International, 1992). The principal scholarly study of Salieri. About 280 pages; the standard reference for the Mozart-Salieri question and the _Amadeus_ -film inventions.

This is the modern scholarly Mozart bookshelf. If you read Spaethling and Solomon, you have the principal primary-source and biographical engagement; if you add Rosen, you have the principal analytical voice. The Encyclopedia, Sadie, Heartz, Wolff, and Gutman are the deeper engagements for specific questions or specific periods.

### 23.2 Your First Ten Mozart Listens (Starter List)

If you have only ten hours to give to Mozart and have not yet built your own Mozart listening life, these are the ten works to start with. The list is curated from the volume's anchor works and represents the central Mozart catalog in compressed form.

  1. **Symphony No. 25 in G minor, K. 183** (1773, 25 minutes). The early Sturm und Drang launch. Chapter 2.
  2. **Piano Concerto No. 9 in E♭ "Jeunehomme," K. 271** (1777, 32 minutes). The first great Mozart concerto. Chapter 4.
  3. **Symphony No. 40 in G minor, K. 550** (1788, 30 minutes). The dark-key symphonic peak. Chapter 10.
  4. **Piano Concerto No. 23 in A major, K. 488** (1786, 27 minutes). The A-major concerto with the F-sharp minor Adagio. Chapter 8.
  5. **Piano Concerto No. 20 in D minor, K. 466** (1785, 30 minutes). The minor-key concerto with the Beethoven cadenza. Chapter 7.
  6. **String Quintet in G minor, K. 516** (1787, 35 minutes). The chamber-music peak in the dark key. Chapter 17.
  7. **Clarinet Quintet in A major, K. 581** (1789, 30 minutes). The chamber-music late style with Anton Stadler's basset clarinet. Chapter 17.
  8. **_Le nozze di Figaro_ K. 492** (1786, three hours complete; the Act II finale and Act IV finale specifically, about forty minutes together). The first Da Ponte opera with the ensemble finale form. Chapter 11.
  9. **_Die Zauberflöte_ K. 620** (1791, three hours complete; the principal arias and the Act II finale, about forty minutes together). Mozart's last opera in the late-style transparency. Chapter 14.
  10. **Requiem in D minor, K. 626** (1791, 50 minutes complete in the Süssmayr completion). The unfinished sacred work; the Lacrimosa is the principal anchor. Chapter 18.

Total listening time: approximately ten hours for the ten works (the operas counted at their selected-anchor lengths rather than at complete-opera lengths). If you have ten hours, this is the ten-hour Mozart starter program.

Recommended single recordings for the starter list:

  1. K. 183: John Eliot Gardiner / English Baroque Soloists (Philips, 1989) or Karl Böhm / Berlin Philharmonic (Deutsche Grammophon, 1968).
  2. K. 271: Mitsuko Uchida / Jeffrey Tate / English Chamber Orchestra (Philips, 1986).
  3. K. 550: Sir Charles Mackerras / Scottish Chamber Orchestra (Telarc, 1986) or Karl Böhm / Berlin Philharmonic (Deutsche Grammophon, 1962).
  4. K. 488: Mitsuko Uchida / Jeffrey Tate (Philips, 1985) or Murray Perahia / English Chamber Orchestra (Sony, 1977).
  5. K. 466: Mitsuko Uchida / Jeffrey Tate (Philips, 1986) or Alfred Brendel / Neville Marriner (Philips, 1973).
  6. K. 516: Hagen Quartett with Gérard Caussé (Deutsche Grammophon, 1998) or Grumiaux Trio (Philips, 1973).
  7. K. 581: Karl Leister / Amadeus Quartet (Deutsche Grammophon, 1965) or Anthony Pay / Academy of Ancient Music (L'Oiseau-Lyre / Decca, 1990, period basset clarinet).
  8. _Figaro_ : Sir Charles Mackerras / Scottish Chamber Orchestra (Telarc, 1994) or Karl Böhm / Deutsche Oper Berlin (Deutsche Grammophon, 1968).
  9. _Magic Flute_ : Sir Georg Solti / Vienna Philharmonic (Decca, 1969) or Karl Böhm / Berlin Philharmonic (Deutsche Grammophon, 1964, with Fritz Wunderlich as Tamino).
  10. Requiem: Sir John Eliot Gardiner / Monteverdi Choir (Philips, 1986) or Karl Böhm / Vienna Philharmonic (Deutsche Grammophon, 1971).

If you work through the starter list and want to continue, the volume's other forty-plus anchor works are the natural next stage. The complete Mozart catalog (600-plus Köchel-numbered works) is a lifetime project; the central fifty are the body of work that earns sustained engagement.

### 23.3 Concert Advice

Mozart is programmed constantly across the standard repertoire; you can hear him live almost anywhere in the world that has a major orchestra, an opera house, or a chamber-music society. The principal concert contexts:

**The Salzburg Festival** (Salzburger Festspiele) is the principal Mozart pilgrimage. The festival was founded in 1920 and has been the principal annual celebration of Mozart's music at his birthplace ever since. The festival runs from late July through August every summer; the program includes Mozart operas, symphonies, piano concertos, chamber music, and sacred works performed by leading international musicians. A trip to the Salzburg Festival in any summer is the most-concentrated Mozart experience available; the city itself (the Mozart-Geburtshaus, the Mozart-Wohnhaus, the historic center, the surrounding Salzkammergut landscape) adds the biographical and cultural dimensions to the listening.

**The major European opera houses** program Mozart constantly. The Vienna State Opera, the Berlin State Opera, the Bavarian State Opera in Munich, the Royal Opera House Covent Garden in London, the Paris Opera, the Metropolitan Opera in New York, La Scala in Milan, La Fenice in Venice — all program the Da Ponte trilogy, the Magic Flute, _Idomeneo_ , _Entführung_ , and the other Mozart operas regularly. If you live in or near any of these cities, attending a Mozart opera production is a regularly available experience.

**Smaller opera houses and festival venues** also program Mozart frequently. The Glyndebourne Festival in England has been a principal Mozart venue since the 1934 _Così fan tutte_ that began the modern _Così_ rehabilitation; the Aix-en-Provence Festival in France, the Chicago Lyric Opera, the Santa Fe Opera, the San Francisco Opera, and many regional opera companies regularly program Mozart.

**Symphony orchestras and chamber-music societies** program Mozart constantly. Any major symphony orchestra's season includes multiple Mozart works (typically symphonies and concertos with guest soloists). Chamber-music series at universities and concert halls regularly program the string quartets, the late string quintets, the Clarinet Quintet K. 581, and the piano sonatas.

**Period-instrument ensembles** specifically program Mozart in the HIP tradition. The Academy of Ancient Music (UK), the English Concert (UK), the Freiburger Barockorchester (Germany), Concerto Köln (Germany), Les Arts Florissants (France), Le Concert des Nations (Spain), Tafelmusik (Canada), the Boston Early Music Festival, the Vienna Concentus Musicus, and many other ensembles offer the period-instrument Mozart in concert.

For most listeners, the practical advice is: when you see a Mozart opera or symphony or concerto in your local concert schedule, go. The live performance gives a dimension that recordings cannot supply — the physical acoustic, the visual production (in opera), the social context of the concert hall, the chance encounters with other Mozart listeners. The recordings are the foundation of your Mozart listening life; the live performances are the celebrations.

### 23.4 The Socratopia Listening Journey From Here

This volume is one of approximately twenty volumes in the Close Listening series that the Socratopia listening companion uses to guide Mozart-related dialogue with you. The volume sits in a specific position in the series:

**Behind you** : _Close Listening: Classical Music_ — the comprehensive primer that introduced the listening loop, the basic chapters on the Western classical tradition, and a handful of introductory Mozart anchors (Eine kleine Nachtmusik, the K. 488 concerto, the K. 622 clarinet concerto, the Queen of the Night, the Requiem Lacrimosa). _Close Listening: Beethoven_ — the first single-composer deep volume, whose structural pattern this volume has inherited.

**Coming next** (the next single-composer Close Listening volumes, in approximate planned order):

**Frédéric Chopin** (1810-1849). The Polish-French Romantic-era pianist-composer. The Chopin volume will follow Beethoven and Mozart as the next single-composer volume; Chopin's compact 200-work catalog (concentrated on solo piano) makes for a different volume shape than Beethoven's 138 opus numbers or Mozart's 600 Köchel numbers. The Chopin volume will anchor on the Ballades, the Preludes, the late Polonaises, and the major chamber works.

**Johann Sebastian Bach** (1685-1750). The German Baroque master. The Bach volume will be one of the largest single-composer volumes in the series; Bach's roughly 1,128 BWV-numbered works across multiple genres (cantatas, organ works, keyboard works, chamber music, orchestral works, sacred music) make for a complex volume structure. The Bach volume will likely anchor on the _St. Matthew Passion_ , the _B-minor Mass_ , the _Goldberg Variations_ , the _Well-Tempered Clavier_ , the _Brandenburg Concertos_ , and selected cantatas.

**Franz Schubert** (1797-1828). The Austrian Romantic-era composer who died at 31. The Schubert volume will engage the Lieder catalog (the 600-plus songs across the Schubert oeuvre), the chamber music (the late string quartets, the late piano trios, the _Trout_ Quintet), the late piano sonatas, and the symphonies (the _Unfinished_ Symphony and the _Great_ C-major Symphony).

**Johannes Brahms** (1833-1897). The German Romantic-classical composer. The Brahms volume will anchor on the four symphonies, the major chamber music (the late piano trios, the string quartets and quintets, the Clarinet Quintet Op. 115), the _German Requiem_ , the late piano pieces, and the concertos.

**Pyotr Ilyich Tchaikovsky** (1840-1893). The Russian late-Romantic composer. The Tchaikovsky volume will engage the symphonies, the major ballet scores (_Swan Lake_ , _The Sleeping Beauty_ , _The Nutcracker_), the operas (_Eugene Onegin_ , _The Queen of Spades_), the major chamber music, and the concertos.

The next single-composer volumes will appear in the Socratopia listening application over subsequent months and years; the publication schedule depends on the engagement levels and the listening community's interests. The next volume after Mozart is the volume the Socratopia companion will know next; it will be available to you as part of your ongoing Mozart-and-beyond engagement.

**Other Close Listening volumes** in the series cover broader topics — the comprehensive classical primer, and planned single-composer or thematic volumes. Each volume is independent and can be read on its own; where this Mozart volume points back to the primer or sideways to the Beethoven volume, the connections are convenience rather than prerequisite.

### 23.5 The Friend's Last Word

This volume's first chapter began with the friend that you wanted to walk you through Mozart's thirty-five years. We have walked through them together. The walk has been long — twenty-three chapters, approximately fifty anchor works, the seven compositional features, the recording history, the Salieri question, the biographical arc through the December 5, 1791 death.

The friend's last word: Mozart is not a project you complete. The volume's twenty-three chapters give you the equipment to continue independently, but the engagement with Mozart's music is a lifetime project rather than a finite task. The fifty anchor works that this volume has covered are the central catalog; the remaining 550 reward continued exploration. The recording history continues — new recordings appear each year, and the contemporary HIP and modern-instrument traditions continue to evolve. The scholarly literature continues; new editions, new monographs, new specialized studies appear regularly.

What changes after this volume is what you bring to the listening. Before the volume, you may have come to Mozart with the general cultural sense — the wallpaper image, the _Amadeus_ -film picture, the recognition of _Eine kleine Nachtmusik_ and the Queen of the Night aria from previous casual encounters. After the volume, you come with the specific anchor works in your ear, the seven compositional features as a listening framework, the biographical arc as the contextual spine, and the recording-history awareness for choosing between performances. The six listening habits that Chapter 1 named for unlearning have had twenty-three chapters to work on.

Six measures of what the volume has given you, then: Mozart now sounds intentional rather than decorative. You hear the labor inside the finished surface. The early prodigy works register as genuinely skilled, not merely precocious. The _Amadeus_ -film Salieri plot belongs to fiction rather than to biography. The Requiem question sits in its proper ambiguity rather than as a settled origin story. And you have not one but several Mozart works to start from. The Mozart you encounter on any future occasion — a concert program, a streaming-service playlist, a friend's recommendation — sits within that framework.

The Socratopia companion will be with you as you continue. When you want to talk about a Mozart work — to ask questions, to share an observation, to compare a recording you have heard with the volume's recommended Primary Recording, to engage a biographical question about a composition's context — the companion will draw on this volume's chapters to support the dialogue. The volume is the textbook the companion reads to be ready for your conversation; the conversation is what makes the Mozart-engagement personal and continuing.

The friend has walked you to the place where you can continue alone. The walk has been worth taking. Welcome to the Mozart of your continuing listening life.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"What is the single most surprising thing you have learned about Mozart from working through this volume? What did you not know that you now know? What did you think you knew that turned out to be more complicated than the cultural-sense picture had suggested?"_
* _"The 'your first ten Mozart listens' starter list compresses the central Mozart catalog into ten works. If you were curating a different starter list — your own personal top ten — would you change the list, and how? What works would you remove and what works would you add?"_
* _"The Close Listening series is structured around single-composer deep volumes (Beethoven, Mozart, Chopin, Bach, Schubert, Brahms, Tchaikovsky planned) and comprehensive primer / thematic volumes. What does the single-composer deep volume format give the reader that a comprehensive primer cannot give? What does it lose?"_
* _"You have spent ten or twenty or fifty hours with this volume across the past weeks or months. What habit of Mozart listening do you want to carry forward into your continuing listening life? Pick one specific listening practice (the four-section listening loop; the seven-feature framework; the period-vs-modern recording comparison; the Conversation Seeds re-engagement with anchor works) that you want to keep doing."_
* _"The friend's last word in this chapter is that Mozart is not a project you complete but a lifetime engagement. Other arts and cultural objects (great novels, great paintings, great films) work similarly — they reward sustained return rather than one-time consumption. What does it mean to take on a lifetime engagement with a composer? Is the engagement valuable for its own sake, or is it valuable for what it contributes to the rest of your life?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

The final listening exercise is open. There is no specific anchor work for this chapter; the chapter is the friend's final advice rather than a listening engagement.

The recommended re-listen exercise: pick the single Mozart work from the volume's anchor list that has stayed with you most strongly. Listen to it once more with all that the volume has given you in mind — the biographical context, the seven compositional features that the work displays, the recording-tradition awareness, the cross-references to other works in the volume. The listening should be the synthesis of everything the volume has built; the same work that you began with at the start of the relevant chapter should now sit differently in your ear than it did before the volume.

If you have not yet identified a single work that has stayed with you most strongly: try the K. 488 second-movement Adagio (Chapter 8), the K. 466 first movement (Chapter 7), the Magic Flute's Pamina-Tamino reunion in the Act II finale (Chapter 14), or the Requiem Lacrimosa (Chapter 18). Any one of these can serve as the personal anchor if none has yet emerged for you.

### Going Deeper

**Live** : plan a trip to the Salzburg Festival for a future summer. The festival runs from late July through August every year; the program includes Mozart works performed by leading international musicians; the Salzburg setting provides the biographical and cultural dimensions. A festival trip puts you inside more Mozart per week than anywhere else; if you have the resources and the time, a visit to Salzburg during the festival is the principal Mozart pilgrimage in the Western tradition.

**Read** : any of the principal scholarly works listed in §23.1 that engages a specific period or topic that has stayed with you. If the Vienna freelance peak years (1782-86) interested you most, Daniel Heartz's _Mozart, Haydn and Early Beethoven, 1781-1802_ is the principal extended treatment. If the late style years interested you most, Christoph Wolff's _Mozart at the Gateway to His Fortune_ is the principal extended treatment. If the Requiem's completion problem interested you most, Wolff's _Mozart's Requiem_ monograph is the principal extended treatment. The bookshelf is built; what you read next from it depends on where your engagement with Mozart's life has taken root.

**Continue** : with the next Close Listening volume that will engage the next composer in the single-composer deep series. The series is one of the principal cultural projects that the Socratopia listening application supports; your engagement with this Mozart volume has prepared you for the next.

_This is the end of Close Listening: Mozart, Volume 3 of the Close Listening series. The friend's walk with you through Mozart's thirty-five years is complete. The continuing listening life is yours._
